Open FJRW Theory and Mirror Symmetry
Mark Gross, Tyler L. Kelly, Ran J. Tessler

Abstract
. We construct an open enumerative theory for the Landau-Ginzburg (LG) model ( ℂ 2 , μ r × μ s , x r + y s ) (\mathbb{C}^{2},\mu_{r}\times\mu_{s},x^{r}+y^{s}) . The invariants are defined as integrals of multisections of a Witten bundle with descendents over a moduli space that is a real orbifold with corners. In turn, a generating function for these open invariants yields the mirror LG model and a versal deformation of it with flat coordinates. After establishing an open topological recursion result, we prove an LG/LG open mirror symmetry theorem in dimension two with all descendents. The open invariants we define are not unique but depend on boundary conditions that, when altered, exhibit wall-crossing phenomena for the invariants. We describe an LG wall-crossing group classifying the wall-crossing transformations that can occur.



Introduction

In a sequence of papers, Abramovich, Chiodo, Jarvis, Kimura and Vaintrob developed an enumerative theory for the moduli space of
r
-spin curves, that is, stable curves with an
r
th root of the canonical bundle
[
51
,
1
,
28
,
27
,
26
,
9
]
. The intersection numbers of this moduli space can be encapsulated in a generating function related to the Gelfand-Dickey hierarchies. This is predicted by the
r
-spin version of Witten’s conjecture, generalizing the original proof of Witten’s conjecture by Kontsevich
[
31
]
. Nowadays, their theory can be seen as a special case of FJRW theory, an enumerative theory for a Landau-Ginzburg
A
-model introduced by Fan, Jarvis and Ruan in another sequence of
papers
[
15
,
13
,
12
]
. FJRW theory introduces an
A
-model
enumerative invariant associated to the Landau-Ginzburg model
(W,G)
where

W:\mathbb{C}^{a}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a weighted homogeneous polynomial
and
G
is a subgroup of the automorphism group of
W
. FJRW theory is
equivalent to
r
-spin theory when considering the case
(x^{r},\mu_{r})

[
14
]
.

Here, there should be a form
of mirror symmetry between Landau-Ginzburg models

(W,G)\leftrightarrow(\check{W},\check{G}).

One such example, which shall be explored in this paper, is the
mirror pair

(0.1)

(x_{1}^{r_{1}}+\cdots+x_{a}^{r_{a}},\mu_{r_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mu_{r_{a}})\leftrightarrow(x_{1}^{r_{1}}+\cdots+x_{a}^{r_{a}},1).

This mirror pair at its onset was predicted as a case of Berglund-Hübsch duality
[
2
]
, a combinatorial mirror construction that provides mirror pairs of Landau-Ginzburg models. In this paper, we consider the left-hand side as the
A
-model and the right hand side as the
B
-model. Many aspects of mirror symmetry for this mirror pair are understood. For example it is known that their corresponding Frobenius algebras are isomorphic
[
32
]
and the (closed) FJRW theory of
(x_{1}^{r_{1}}+\cdots+x_{a}^{r_{a}},\mu_{r_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mu_{r_{a}})
corresponds to the (closed) Saito-Givental theory of
(x_{1}^{r_{1}}+\cdots+x_{a}^{r_{a}},1)

[
24
]
.

The closed
A
-model on the left of (
0.1
) involves moduli spaces of pointed
stable
orbicurves

(C,z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})

where
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
are marked points with non-trivial stabilizer,
and the only other points on
C
with non-trivial stabilizer are
nodes, satisfying the so-called balancing condition.
Further, one also includes data of
spin bundles

S_{1},\ldots,S_{a}

on
C
, with the data of an isomorphism
S_{i}^{\otimes r_{i}}\cong\omega_{C,\log}
, the logarithmic dualizing sheaf. Such a curve
is called a
W
-spin curve
. In this particular (closed) case,
the genus zero FJRW invariants we are interested in are defined
relatively easily from the top Chern class of the
Witten bundle

on the moduli space, whose fibre at a point in moduli space is

\bigoplus_{i}H^{1}(C,S_{i})^{\vee}
. In more general situations, such
as higher genus or more complex potentials, the definition of
FJRW invariants is considerably more difficult, as one might
have
H^{0}(C,S_{i})\not=0
.
Those cases may require either a more in-depth study of analytic properties of the Witten equation as carried out in
[
12
]
or the more recent
approaches
[
16
,
11
,
17
]
for general algebraic enumerative theories for gauged linear sigma models.
Thus we avoid such situations in this paper.

The closed
B
-model side of the story, at least for the right-hand side of
the correspondence (
0.1
), was clarified in work
of Li, Li, Saito and Shen
[
33
]
, with mirror symmetry for closed invariants being established in
[
15
,
24
,
37
]
. The
B
-model side is
a Saito-Givental
theory, which, put as simply as possible, involves calculating
oscillatory integrals of the form

(0.2)

\int_{\Gamma}e^{W_{\bf t}/\hbar}f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{a},{\bf t})dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{a}.

Here
x_{1},\ldots,x_{a}
are coordinates on
\mathbb{C}^{a}
,
\hbar
is a coordinate
on an auxiliary
\mathbb{C}^{*}
,
f

is a carefully chosen complex-valued regular function, and
{\bf t}=\{t_{i_{1}\cdots i_{a}}\,|\,1\leq i_{j}\leq r_{j}-2\}
is
a set of coordinates on the parameter space for a universal unfolding

W_{\bf t}
of
W
.
Finally
\Gamma
runs over some suitable non-compact cycles in
\mathbb{C}^{a}
.
The requirement on
f
is that the form
fdx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{a}

is a so-called
primitive form
in the sense of Saito-Givental theory.

While Saito-Givental theory in general gives a Frobenius manifold
structure on the parametrizing space of a universal unfolding of
W
,
determining this structure
can be quite difficult. However, experience with mirror
symmetry for toric Fano varieties
[
10
,
22
,
18
]
suggests that
mirror symmetry becomes much more transparent when a specific perturbation

W_{\bf t}
of the original potential is used. In particular,
in the Fano case, the mirror Landau-Ginzburg model
W_{\bf t}
should be taken to be a generating function
for certain
open
Gromov-Witten invariants. Slightly more precisely,
one considers a Lagrangian torus fibre
F
of an SYZ fibration on a given
Fano manifold and counts holomorphic disks with interior marked points realizing
some set of constraints and with boundary lying on
F
. The correct choice
of
W_{\bf t}
is then a generating function for these counts. Importantly,
these open invariants are not well-defined. However, the oscillatory
integrals analogous to (
0.2
) are well-defined,
and furthermore, if properly set up, the function
f
becomes identically

1
and the analogous coordinates
{\bf t}
become the so-called
flat
coordinates
of Saito-Givental theory. As a consequence, the entirely
well-defined closed Gromov-Witten invariants can be immediately read
off from the oscillatory integrals. Unfortunately, an enumerative theory for a Landau-Ginzburg model does not have a target space, hence there is no associated SYZ fibration; therefore, a new approach is needed to create an analogous story.

We take this as motivation for providing
an open enumerative theory for the Landau-Ginzburg model
(x_{1}^{r_{1}}+x_{2}^{r_{2}},\mu_{r_{1}}\times\mu_{r_{2}})
. We now summarize the key points of the paper:

•

We define open FJRW invariants
as integrals of multisections of (descendent) Witten bundles over moduli
spaces which are real orbifolds
with corners.

•

These open invariants depend on choices of boundary conditions for
sections of the (descendent) Witten bundles on the boundaries of moduli spaces.
However, such choices may be made (Theorem
0.1
).

•

Some boundary conditions can be fixed via a geometrically natural
positivity condition. However, there are degrees of freedom in the choice
of boundary conditions which cannot be constrained. This leads to the first example of wall-crossing for an open enumerative theory for a Landau-Ginzburg model.

•

If the boundary conditions not determined by positivity
are chosen with an inductive
structure, certain combinations of open FJRW invariants satisfy a form
of topological recursion: see Theorem
0.5
. This type of topological recursion is new in a sense as we can only establish recursions between certain polynomials of invariants rather than a single invariant due to the wall-crossing phenomenon.

•

Given a system of open FJRW invariants determined by a family
of boundary
conditions satisfying this inductive structure, we construct
a perturbation of
W
as a generating function for these open invariants.
The oscillatory integrals (
0.2
) then become
generating functions for closed extended FJRW invariants, see Theorem

0.3
. Besides proving mirror symmetry at
genus zero, we can construct the mirror entirely in terms of the
original open
A
-model, as opposed to relying on previous combinatorial
constructions such as Berglund-Hübsch.

•

We then show there is a wall-crossing group
which acts faithfully and transitively
on (i) the collection of all possible systems of open FJRW invariants; and
(ii) the set of all perturbations
of the potential
W
satisfying certain homogeneity constraints for
which Theorem
0.3
holds. Thus our method of
choosing boundary conditions is geometrically natural, allowing us to
match the
A
- and
B
-models for FJRW theory on the nose. See
Theorem
0.7
.

We now give more details of different aspects of this.

0.1.
The open
A
-model invariants

Our construction of the open
A
-model invariants generalizes
the construction of open
r
-spin invariants of
Buryak, Clader and Tessler
[
5
,
6
]
.
This in turn builds to a certain extent on the open descendent theory
of Pandharipande, Solomon and Tessler
[
42
]
.

Given a potential
W=\sum_{i=1}^{a}x_{i}^{r_{i}}
, we review in
§
1

the notion of a stable (twisted)
W
-spin curve and give the
definition of a stable (twisted)
W
-spin disk.
In slightly more detail, a
W
-spin curve involves data
(C,\{z_{i}\},S_{1},\ldots,S_{a},\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{a})
.
Here
C
is a stable marked orbicurve, with all marked points and
nodes having the same stabilizer group
\mu_{d}
, where
d
is the
least common multiple of the
r_{i}
. Further,
\tau_{i}
is an isomorphism

\tau_{i}:S_{i}^{\otimes r_{i}}\rightarrow\omega_{C,\log}(-\sum_{i\in I_{0}}r_{i}z_{i})

where
I_{0}
is a subset of indices of the marked points. It is
this twist by
z_{i}
which leads to the phrase “twisted”, and only Ramond
points are allowed to be twisted. Each marked point comes with

twists

\text{tw}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq a
, where
\text{tw}_{i}\in\{-1,\ldots,r_{i}-1\}

records the action of the stabilizer group of the marked
point on the fibre of the twisted spin
bundle
S_{i}
. See §
1.1

for details
on our conventions for these twists. The case of
\text{tw}_{i}=-1
or
r_{i}-1
corresponds to the Ramond
case (where the action of the stabilizer on the fibre of
S_{i}
is
trivial), with the two possibilities corresponding to the cases that

i\not\in I_{0}
or
i\in I_{0}
respectively.

To have a compact moduli space of
W
-spin curves, we must consider
W
-spin curves whose underlying orbicurves
C
have nodal singularities. If
C
has a nodal point
n
, then take a partial normalization
\nu:\widetilde{C}\rightarrow C
at the node
n
. This yields two
half-nodes
on
\widetilde{C}
, the inverse image of
n
, which we call
z_{1},z_{2}
. These half-nodes will have twists,
with the standard balancing condition
at nodes dictated by Equations (
1.1
) and (
1.2
)
implying that these twists satisfy

\text{tw}_{i}(z_{1})+\text{tw}_{i}(z_{2})\equiv r_{i}-2\mod r_{i}.

Closed FJRW invariants, in this setup, may be defined from the Euler class
of the Witten bundle on the moduli space of twisted
W
-spin curves when
the underlying curves have genus
0
and, for each
1\leq i\leq n
, there
is at most one marked point with
\text{tw}_{i}=-1
; otherwise the spin bundle

S_{i}
may have sections and defining FJRW invariants becomes more complicated.
In the
r
-spin case, such invariants were introduced in
[
26
]
, and are called
closed extended
FJRW invariants
. See §
3.3.1
for details.
They were often not studied in the subsequent literature as the
\text{tw}_{i}=-1
case is not directly involved in statements of closed mirror symmetry; however, the extended theory is required to establish a natural relationship between open and closed extended invariants through open topological recursion as seen here and in
[
4
,
5
,
6
]
.

A
W
-spin disk
is a genus zero
W
-spin curve

(C,\{z_{i}\}_{i\in I},\{\bar{z}_{i}\}_{i\in I},\{x_{i}\}_{i\in B},S_{1},\ldots,S_{a},\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{a})

equipped with the additional data of an anti-holomorphic involution

\phi:C\rightarrow C
along with liftings of
\phi
to involutions of
S_{i}
:
see §
1.2
for details and more precise notation.
We usually write
\Sigma
for a choice
of fundamental domain of the anti-holomorphic involution
\phi
,
so that
C=\Sigma\cup\phi(\Sigma)
and
\partial\Sigma=\Sigma\cap\phi(\Sigma)
. The marked points
z_{i}
are
interior
marked points
indexed by a set
I
, i.e.,

z_{i}\in\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma
for
i\in I
, with
complex conjugate marked points
\bar{z}_{i}=\phi(z_{i})
. The marked points

x_{i}

are
boundary marked points
indexed by a set
B
, i.e.,
x_{i}\in\partial\Sigma
for
i\in B
.
While the twists of internal marked points are unrestricted,
we place the restriction on boundary marked points that

\text{tw}_{i}\in\{0,r_{i}-2\}
(see Remark
1.30
for discussion).
We further exclude the possibility that
\text{tw}_{i}=0
for all
i
: these
are in fact points which may be forgotten; forgetting such points
will play an important role.
As in
[
5
,
6
]
,
we also require some additional choice of data called a
grading

involving the restriction of the spin bundles to
\partial\Sigma
.
While this is technical and we omit further mention of
this data in the introduction, gradings are crucial for imposing the necessary
boundary conditions to define open invariants.

W
-spin
disks with this additional data and constraints on boundary twists
are called
graded
W
-spin disks
.

In §
1
, we first give the definition of
(closed)
W
-spin curves, followed by
W
-spin disks. This definition is a slight generalization of the definitions of the corresponding

r
-spin curves and disks of
[
5
,
6
]
. We then introduce a language
of dual intersection graphs to describe various ways that graded

W
-spin curves or disks may degenerate. Unfortunately, there are a large number
of ways
W
-spin disks may degenerate, and this is where
combinatorial complexities appear. However, these generate
interesting features.
We define the notion of a
graded
W
-spin graph
,
which records the combinatorial data of a stable graded
W
-spin curve or
disk. This data consists essentially of its dual intersection graph,
the twists of marked points and nodes, and the ordering (or collection of orderings) of boundary marked points.

In §
2
we commence a study of moduli spaces
of stable graded
W
-spin disks. In Proposition
2.4

we construct a compact moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
where
\Gamma
denotes
a graded
W
-spin graph as above corresponding to a smooth disk with
specified numbers of boundary and internal marked points along with
their twists. This compact moduli space includes all
degenerations of smooth disks described by the graph
\Gamma
.

Having constructed these moduli spaces, we may define the Witten
and descendent bundles on these moduli spaces. This is
carried out as in
[
5
,
6
]
. For
1\leq i\leq a
,
there is a bundle
{\mathcal{W}}_{i}
on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
whose fibre at a point
corresponding to a
W
-spin curve
C
with
anti-holomorphic involution
\phi
is the eigenspace of the
action of
\phi
on
H^{1}(C,S_{i})^{\vee}
with eigenvalue
-1
.
Further, for each internal marked point there is a descendent
bundle
{\mathbb{L}}_{i}
, a rank one complex vector bundle, whose fibre is
the cotangent space at the
i
th marked point. If
\Gamma
is a

W
-spin graph corresponding to a smooth disk
with internal marked points indexed by a set
J
, then we may consider
a
descendent vector

\mathbf{d}=(d_{j})_{j\in J}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
.
We then obtain a real vector bundle

E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d}):=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{a}\mathcal{W}_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j\in J}{\mathbb{L}}_{j}^{\oplus d_{j}},

the
descendent Witten bundle
.
We say
\Gamma
is
balanced
for
\mathbf{d}
if

\dim{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}^{W}=\operatorname{rank}E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d}).

It is precisely this case where we will extract invariants.

The very first step in defining invariants is to orient the moduli
spaces and the descendent Witten bundles. This is carried out in
§
2.5
using results of
[
42
,
5
]
. It is here where for the first time we restrict to the rank
2
case. There is little to say here about this process,
other than that it is a necessary but unpleasant step. Because we are restricting to rank two, we now change notation and write

W=x^{r}+y^{s}.

Since now a marked point or half-node has two twists
\text{tw}_{1},\text{tw}_{2}
,
we typically write the twist as a pair
(\text{tw}_{1},\text{tw}_{2})
.

The second step in defining invariants is to have a suitable
set of boundary conditions.
Because
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}^{W}
is an orbifold with corners,
one does not define the invariants simply by integrating Euler classes of the vector
bundle
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
. Rather, one has to impose boundary conditions.

The general setup is as follows; see §
2.2.3

for a brief but more detailed review of these notions.
If given a compact orbifold with corners
M
of dimension
n

and a rank
n
orbibundle
E
on
M
,
along with a nowhere vanishing multisection
\mathbf{s}
of
E|_{\partial M}
, the
signed number of zeroes of any extension of
\mathbf{s}
to a transverse multisection

\tilde{\mathbf{s}}
of
E
over all of
M
is well-defined, see
[
42
]
,
Theorem A.14. This is the
relative Euler class
of
E
with respect
to
M
and we write this as

\int_{M}e(E;\mathbf{s})=\#Z(\tilde{\mathbf{s}}).

In
[
6
]
, Appendix A, a variant of this is given, where
M

need not be compact. Rather, one assumes given an open subset
U\subseteq M

such that
M\setminus U
is compact, and a nowhere vanishing multisection
\mathbf{s}
of

E|_{U\cup\partial M}
. Then, the signed number of zeroes of any
transverse extension of
\mathbf{s}
is well-defined, again giving the relative
Euler class. We still write this using the notation above.

Thus, to define open invariants, we need to make a choice of boundary
conditions for the descendent Witten bundle for every balanced

\Gamma
. To be of any use and to have any hope of proving relationships
between the different invariants, such as open topological recursion,
there has to be some inductive structure to this choice of boundary
conditions. Here, the approach to such an inductive structure follows
the approach of
[
5
,
6
,
42
]
.

We now give an impressionistic description of how this is done. The
reader should be aware that the definitions given here in the introduction aren’t
precisely correct, and should refer to the main text for the complete
definitions.
1
1

1

For example, we elide the notion of alternating for boundary marked points and nodes, see Definition
1.23
.

Consider a
W
-spin graph
\Gamma
corresponding to a smooth
W
-spin
disk, thus giving a moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}^{W}
of such disks
and their degenerations. The boundary
of this moduli space is a union of codimension one strata, which arise
in several different ways. See Figure
3
for a pictorial description of the various degenerations possible. We note that strata depicted in Figure
3
(A) have codimension 2, so such strata are not on the boundary
\partial\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{W}
. Thus we now focus on the cases in Figure
3
(B) and
3
(C). We broadly distinguish here between three
types of boundary strata:

(1)

There are strata where the corresponding
stable disk has a
contracted boundary node
, i.e.,
C
has a node which
is a fixed point of the anti-holomorphic involution.

(2)

There are strata with a boundary node where the corresponding stable disk
is a union of two disks, with the resulting half-nodes having twists

(a,b)
,
(r-a-2,s-b-2)
, with either
a\in\{1,\ldots,r-3\}

or
b\in\{1,\ldots,s-3\}
. Such a stratum is called
positive
.

(3)

We have a similar degeneration to two disks with a boundary node, but this time

a\in\{0,r-2\}
and
b\in\{0,s-2\}
. We call such a stratum

relevant
.

For case (1), we impose a boundary condition on multisections of the Witten bundle along
boundary strata arising from contracted boundary nodes. This is done
via a positivity condition and
guarantees non-vanishing of these multisections along such boundary
strata.

For case (2), we deal with the positive strata by removing them and imposing conditions
near such strata.
We define a new moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
obtained from

{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}^{W}
by deleting the positive boundary strata. If
V\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}^{W}
is a suitably small open neighbourhood of the union
of positive boundary strata, we take
U=V\cap{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
. We then
only consider multisections of
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
which satisfy a certain
positivity condition on
U
. These sections are then non-vanishing
on
U
, by definition.

A key point in the construction of the boundary conditions is to define the positivity boundary conditions in a consistent way. A more naive strategy is consistent only in low codimension strata and requires modifications (see Remark
3.19
). We continue to use the modification used by Buryak, Clader and Tessler in
[
5
,
6
]
, where
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
is introduced to construct an open
r
-spin intersection theory. The crucial point is that the zero locus of the section of Witten’s bundle avoids nodal strata with certain twists at so-called non-alternating marked points in
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
(see Definition
1.23
).

We call those multisections of
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}

which satisfy these positivity
conditions at contracted boundary node strata and near positive
strata
strongly positive multisections
, see
Definition
3.18
. We note that the notion of
grading, brushed over earlier in this introduction, is necessary to
define positivity. Given such a section
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
, we
are then able to define the
open
W
-spin invariant

\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{12}^{k_{12}(\Gamma)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma},o}:=\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}e(E;\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}|_{U\cup\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})=\#Z(\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma})\in\mathbb{Q}.

Here
I(\Gamma)
denotes the set of tails of
\Gamma

corresponding to internal marked points of the corresponding disk,
with

(a_{i},b_{i})
denoting the twist at the internal point labelled by
i

and
d_{i}
the component of the descendent vector. On the other hand,

k_{1}(\Gamma),k_{2}(\Gamma)
and
k_{12}(\Gamma)
denote the number of
boundary points with twists
(r-2,0)
,
(0,s-2)
and
(r-2,s-2)

respectively. We note that we do not label the boundary points, but the invariants may depend on the labels of the internal points and not just their twists.

This construction has now produced a single invariant associated
to a
W
-spin graph
\Gamma
corresponding to a smooth
W
-spin disk
and a descendent vector
\mathbf{d}=(d_{i})_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{I(\Gamma)}
. However, the range of boundary conditions allowed by strongly positivity is still too flexible to provide meaningful systems of invariants.

The key punchline here is that, in case (3) above, there is an inductive
structure for the relevant strata. Here, the normalization
of a stable disk in the interior of a relevant stratum is a disjoint
union of two disks, each of which only have boundary points with
twists
(0,0)
,
(r-2,0)
,
(0,s-2)
or
(r-2,s-2)
.
However, after forgetting those boundary points of twist
(0,0)
,
these are precisely
the types of boundary twists we allow in graded
W
-spin disks. We call points with twist
(r-2,0)

r
-points,
(0,s-2)

s
-points, and
(r-2,s-2)
fully twisted points. In particular,
if
{\mathcal{M}}
denotes such a boundary stratum and
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{M}}_{2}

are the moduli spaces of the corresponding normalized disks (with
boundary points of twist
(0,0)
forgotten),
we have a natural map
{\mathcal{M}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
. We will call this moduli
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
the
base
of the stratum
{\mathcal{M}}
,
and it will be a central object for this paper. Further,
it follows from gluing properties of the Witten bundle (see Proposition

2.14
) that

E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})|_{{\mathcal{M}}}
is the pull-back of a corresponding
descendent Witten bundle on
{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{M}}_{2}
, a sum
E_{1}\boxplus E_{2}
of the pullbacks of descendent Witten bundles
E_{i}

on
{\mathcal{M}}_{i}
.
As a consequence, if we have already fixed multisections of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
,
we obtain via pullback under the map
{\mathcal{M}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{M}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{M}}_{2}

a multisection of
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})|_{{\mathcal{M}}}
. This allows us to inductively
determine boundary conditions along relevant strata.

To be useful, and to take into account inductively
defined boundary conditions, we organize this data as follows.
We will fix the set
2
2

2

We remark this is
a slight simplification of what is necessary in the main text.

\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,\ldots\}
to be the set
of all possible labels of interior marked points, so that every
W
-spin
graph
\Gamma
comes with an injective marking function
m^{I}:I(\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
. We also fix a twist
function

\text{tw}:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\{0,\ldots,r-1\}\times\{0,\ldots,s-1\}

with the property that
\text{tw}^{-1}(a,b)
is an infinite set for
any
(a,b)\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\}\times\{0,\ldots,s-1\}
. We often write

\text{tw}(i)=(a_{i},b_{i})
for
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We will thus always assume that an internal
marked point labeled by
i
has twist
(a_{i},b_{i})
.
We typically will further fix a finite subset
I\subset\mathbb{N}

of possible labels and focus attention on those graphs
\Gamma

with
I(\Gamma)\subseteq I
.
This will help deal with a number of convergence issues in formal
power series rings when we define various potentials later.

Given the above data,
we define the notion of a
family of
canonical multisections bounded by
I
(see
Definition
3.22
). This consists of
a choice of multisection
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
of
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})

for each graph
\Gamma
associated to a smooth graded
W
-spin disk
and descendent vector
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{I(\Gamma)}
, with
I(\Gamma)\subseteq I
.
Further, these multisections satisfy the inductive boundary conditions
described above.
Note these sections must be defined even in the non-balanced case to be
useful; indeed, in general boundary strata involved in the inductive
definition of canonical families involve moduli spaces which are not
balanced.

Given such a family, we may now assemble a system of invariants. Let

J\subseteq I
and
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
. Denote by
{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})

the set of graded

W
-spin graphs
\Gamma
with
I(\Gamma)=J
which are balanced for

\mathbf{d}
. Then define

(0.3)

{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)=\prod_{J\subseteq I}\prod_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}}\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})}.

A family
\mathbf{s}
of canonical multisections bounded by
I
then produces an
element
{\nu}
of
{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)
whose entry corresponding to a
graph
\Gamma
balanced for
\mathbf{d}
is

(0.4)

{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}=\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{12}^{k_{12}(\Gamma)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s},o}.

We remark here that we do not assume
our canonical multisections are invariant under a twist-preserving change
of labels of internal marked points. This will make many arguments easier,
and we may, if we wish,
pass to symmetric families by an averaging process, see
§
3.5
. We say a family
\mathbf{s}
of canonical multisections (bounded by
I
) is
symmetric
if it is invariant under the natural lift of a twist-preserving bijection on
I
to the level of moduli (see §
3.5
for a more precise treatment).
Analogously, we call an element
{\nu}\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)

symmetric
if
{\nu}
is invariant under all twist-preserving
automorphisms of the set
I
. Explicitly, let
\sigma:I\rightarrow I

be a permutation with
\text{tw}\circ\sigma=\text{tw}
, and for a graph
\Gamma

with internal tails labeled by elements of a set
J\subseteq I
,
let
\sigma(\Gamma)
denote the same graph with an internal label

i
replaced by
\sigma(i)
. Then
{\nu}
is symmetric if

{\nu}_{\Gamma,(d_{j})_{j\in J}}={\nu}_{\sigma(\Gamma),(d_{\sigma(j)})_{j\in J}}

for all such
\sigma
. We let
{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)\subseteq{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)
denote the subset of symmetric
elements. With these definitions in hand, we can state our first theorem, which is a combination of Theorem
3.29
and Observation
3.44
.

Theorem 0.1
.

Open FJRW invariants for the LG model
(x^{r}+y^{s},\mu_{r}\times\mu_{s})
exist. That is, we can construct a family (bounded by
I
) of canonical transverse multisections. Moreover, one can construct a family (bounded by
I
) of symmetric canonical transverse multisections.

We now turn to describing the systems of open FJRW invariants for the LG model
(x^{r}+y^{s},\mu_{r}\times\mu_{s})
. Define

\Omega_{r,s}(I)=\{{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}}\,|\,\hbox{$\mathbf{s}$ a family of canonical multisections
bounded by $I$}\}\subseteq{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)

to be the set of all systems of open FJRW invariants arising from
families of canonical multisections. We also define

{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)=\Omega_{r,s}(I)\cap{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)\subseteq{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I),

which we will see consists of systems of open FJRW invariants
arising from symmetric families of canonical multisections.
A priori, Theorem
0.1
only shows the nonemptiness
\Omega_{r,s}(I)
and
{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
.
Thus the real
task is to understand the structure of the sets
\Omega_{r,s}(I)
and
{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
.

Unlike the
r
-spin case of
[
5
,
6
]
, it is very important
to keep in mind that these open
W
-spin invariants are dependent on boundary
conditions. It is precisely this dependence which we will explore
in more detail below in §
0.5
.
In general, given two families of canonical
multisections
\mathbf{s}_{0}
and
\mathbf{s}_{1}
,
one can construct homotopies between them, and the change of
open invariants is then seen by counting
zeroes escaping off the boundary of moduli space, or coming in from the
boundary of moduli space. In other words, what matters is the number of
zeros of these homotopies restricted to the boundary of the various moduli
spaces. To harness this, we define

families of canonical homotopies
in Definition
3.36
. It is important for the homotopies to have a rich structure for us to control their contributions to the invariants. In particular, they must be strongly positive and be pulled back from the base when restricted to relevant boundary strata.
In Lemma
3.40
, we show that we can construct families of canonical homotopies in such a way that we can control their non-vanishing on various boundary strata.

We then are able to provide a structure theorem for the sets
\Omega_{r,s}(I)
and
{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
after taking inspiration from the structure of the
B
-model.

0.2.
The
B
-model

In §
4
we study the period integrals which yield the

B
-model side of our story. We refer the reader to §
4.1
and
[
19
]

for more details of these period integrals. The key point
is to study integrals of the form (
0.2
)
where
\Gamma
runs over a so-called
good basis
of cycles

\Xi_{\mu}
of unbounded
a
-dimensional cycles in
\mathbb{C}^{a}

with the property that
\re(W/\hbar)
tends to
-\infty
in the
unbounded directions. Here,
\mu
runs over tuples

(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{a})
with
0\leq\mu_{i}\leq r_{i}-2
.
This basis is described explicitly in
[
19
]
.
These cycles have a multi-valued dependence on
\hbar
, hence exhibit
monodromy as

\hbar
varies in
\mathbb{C}^{*}
. The good basis is determined by the requirement that

\int_{\Xi_{\mu}}x^{\mu^{\prime}}e^{W/\hbar}dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{a}=\delta_{\mu\mu^{\prime}},

where
\delta_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}
is the Kronecker delta function,
x^{\mu^{\prime}}
denotes the monomial
\prod_{i}x_{i}^{\mu^{\prime}_{i}}
,
and
W=\sum_{i}x_{i}^{r_{i}}
.

While the full
B
-model description gives a semi-infinite variation of
Hodge structure and a Frobenius manifold, we will ignore this structure,
other than making a connection between the description of this
B
-model
data in
[
33
,
24
]
and the explicit period integrals we consider
here. We briefly review the setup in terms of period integrals, and
send the reader to §
4.1
for more details.

One considers a universal unfolding
W_{\mathbf{y}}
of
W
,
where
\mathbf{y}
denotes a set of coordinates
\{y_{\mu}\}

on the universal unfolding parameter space. One then would like
to identify a
primitive form
in the sense of Saito, i.e., a form

f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{a},\mathbf{y})dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{a}
with the
property that

\int_{\Xi_{\mu}}e^{W_{\mathbf{y}}/\hbar}fdx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{a}=\delta_{\mu 0}+t_{\mu}(\mathbf{y})\hbar^{-1}+O(\hbar^{-2})

for some collection of functions
t_{\mu}
. These functions then
form a system of coordinates on the universal unfolding moduli space
known as
flat coordinates
. Once the above integrals are
written in terms of these flat coordinates, they become generating
functions for closed FJRW invariants. The precise form of this
generating function may be found in the statement of Theorem

0.3
.

To be explicit, we now return to the rank two case with
W=x^{r}+y^{s}
.
Fix
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
a finite set of allowable markings as in the
previous subsection, and recall the twist function
tw
assigning
a specific twist to each label in
I
. Consider the
ring

A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}:=\mathbb{Q}[t_{\alpha,\beta,d}\,|\,0\leq\alpha\leq r-2,0\leq\beta\leq s-2,d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}]/\mathrm{Ideal}(I),

where
\mathrm{Ideal}(I)
is the ideal generated by monomials of the
form

\prod_{d}t_{\alpha,\beta,d}^{n_{d}}

such that
\sum n_{d}>|(\text{tw}|_{I})^{-1}(\alpha,\beta)|
.
Note that the definition of
\mathrm{Ideal}(I)
is
designed so that in the potential defined below, we only consider graphs
with at most
|(\text{tw}|_{I})^{-1}(\alpha,\beta)|
internal marked points with twist

(\alpha,\beta)
.

Given a symmetric element
{\nu}=({\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}})\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
, we may then define the

potential
of
{\nu}
to be

W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}=\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 0,l\geq 0}\,\,\sum_{A=\{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i})\}\in\mathcal{A}_{l}}(-1)^{l-1}\frac{{\nu}_{\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})\}},\mathbf{d}}}{|\text{Aut}(A)|}(\textstyle{\prod}_{i=1}^{l}t_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i}})x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}\in A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}[[x,y]].

Here:

•

\mathcal{A}_{l}
denotes the set of cardinality
l
multisets of tuples

(\alpha,\beta,d)
with
0\leq\alpha\leq r-2
,
0\leq\beta\leq s-2
,
d\geq 0
;

•

\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})\}}
denotes
the graph corresponding to a smooth disk with:

–

k_{1}
boundary
r
-points,

–

k_{2}
boundary
s
-points,

–

1 boundary point that is fully twisted, and

–

l
internal marked points with twists
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})
,

1\leq i\leq l
;

•

\text{Aut}(A)
denotes the automorphism group
of the multiset
A
, i.e., permutations
\sigma
on
l
letters such that

(\alpha_{\sigma(i)},\beta_{\sigma(i)},d_{\sigma(i)})=(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq l
; and

•

\mathbf{d}=(d_{i})_{1\leq i\leq l}
denotes the
descendent vector.

We take the number
\nu_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}
to be
zero if
\Gamma
is not balanced for

\mathbf{d}
.

We make three remarks about this potential, assuming that

{\nu}={\nu}^{\mathbf{s}}
, i.e.,
\nu
is enumerative, arising from a symmetric canonical family of
multisections
\mathbf{s}

via (
0.4
). First, in fact we will show
(see Theorem
5.1
) that

(0.5)

W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}\equiv x^{r}+y^{s}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha\leq r-2\\
0\leq\beta\leq s-2\end{subarray}}t_{\alpha,\beta,0}x^{\alpha}y^{\beta}

modulo all monomials quadratic in the variables
t_{\alpha,\beta,0}
or linear
in
t_{\alpha,\beta,d}
for
d>0
. This agrees
with a natural choice of universal unfolding of
W=x^{r}+y^{s}
.
However, in general there will be a possibly infinite number of additional
terms which are higher order in the coordinates
t_{\alpha,\beta,d}
.

Second, the condition that a graph
\Gamma
must be balanced with
respect to
\mathbf{d}
to contribute
to the potential makes
W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}
homogeneous of
degree
rs
with respect to an Euler vector field

(0.6)

E:=sx\partial_{x}+ry\partial_{y}-\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0\leq\alpha\leq r-2,\ 0\leq\beta\leq s-2,}}{{d\geq 0}}}(s\beta+r\alpha+rs(d-1))t_{\alpha,\beta,d}\partial_{t_{\alpha,\beta,d}}.

In other words,

E(W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}})=rsW^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}.

Third, integrality considerations for degrees of spin bundle show that

W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}
is invariant under the action

x\mapsto\xi_{r}x
,
y\mapsto\xi_{s}y
,
t_{a,b,d}\mapsto\xi^{-a}_{r}\xi^{-b}_{s}t_{a,b,d}
, where
\xi_{r},\xi_{s}
are primitive

r^{th}
and
s^{th}
roots of unity respectively.

The following theorem, which is Corollary
4.16
,
is fairly straightforward via integration by parts:

Theorem 0.2
.

Fix
{\nu}\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
such that

W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}\equiv x^{r}+y^{s}\mod\langle t_{\alpha,\beta,d}\ |\ 0\leq\alpha\leq r-2,\ 0\leq\beta\leq s-2,\ 0\leq d\rangle.

For a multiset
A\in\bigcup_{l}\mathcal{A}_{l}
,
A=\{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i})\}
,
define
r(A)
to be the unique element of
\{0,\ldots,r-1\}

congruent to
\sum_{i=1}^{l}\alpha_{i}
modulo
r
, and similarly
s(A)
the
unique element of
\{0,\ldots,s-1\}
congruent to
\sum_{i=1}^{l}\beta_{i}
modulo
s
.
Finally, let

d(A)={sr(A)+rs(A)-\sum_{i=1}^{l}(s\alpha_{i}+r\beta_{i}+rs(d_{i}-1))\over rs}-2.

Then

(0.7)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\int_{\Xi_{(a,b)}}e^{W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}/\hbar}dx\wedge dy

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\delta_{a,0}\delta_{b,0}+\sum_{l\geq 1}\sum_{A=\{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i})\}\in\mathcal{A}_{l}}(-1)^{l}(-\hbar)^{-d(A)-2}\mathcal{A}(A,{\nu})\delta_{r(A),a}\delta_{s(A),b}\left({\prod_{j=1}^{l}t_{\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},d_{j}}\over|\textup{Aut}(A)|}\right).

Here
\mathcal{A}(A,{\nu})\in\mathbb{Q}
is defined in Notation
3.42
and Definition
4.11
.

We do not repeat the definition of
\mathcal{A}(A,{\nu})
in
the introduction as it is slightly involved, but it involves
a sum over products of the
{\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}
and is obtained quite
naturally from evaluating the integral by integration by parts
after a power series
expansion of
e^{(W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}-x^{r}-y^{s})/\hbar}
. Nevertheless, these
invariants play a crucial role in the paper, with our form of open
topological recursion being a recursion involving a combination
of these
\mathcal{A}(A,{\nu})
and closed extended FJRW
invariants.

0.3.
The mirror theorem and open topological recursion

The key mirror symmetry result in our paper is the following. A priori, if we choose a deformed potential
W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}
with coefficients determined by an arbitrary
{\nu}\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
, we can compute the oscillatory integrals in Theorem
0.2
, but the quantities
\mathcal{A}(A,\nu)
in (
0.7
) contain no enumerative meaning. However, when we use a system of open FJRW invariants for
{\nu}
to construct
W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}
, the variables
t_{a,b,0}
are flat coordinates for the underlying Frobenius manifold and the oscillatory integrals are then generating functions for all closed extended FJRW invariants with descendents with internal markings bounded by
I
. Moreover, this only happens when
\nu
is enumerative, that is, a system of open FJRW invariants.

We can state this precisely as follows.
We continue with a choice of finite set
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
. The following is
Corollary
5.12
.

Theorem 0.3

(Open Mirror Symmetry for 2-dimensional Fermat polynomials)
.

Let
{\nu}\in{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{\Xi_{a,b}}e^{W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}/\hbar}dx\wedge dy={}\delta_{a,0}\delta_{b,0}+\sum_{d\geq 0}t_{a,b,d}\hbar^{d-1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{l\geq 2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\in\mathcal{A}_{l}\\
d(A)\geq 0\end{subarray}}(-1)^{l}(-\hbar)^{-d(A)-2}{\delta_{r(A),a}\delta_{s(A),b}\over|\textup{Aut}(A)|}\left\langle\tau_{d(A)}^{(r-r(A)-2,s-s(A)-2)}\prod_{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i})\in A}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})}\right\rangle^{\textup{ext}}\prod_{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i})\in A}t_{\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},d_{j}}.

Here
r(A)
and
s(A)
are as defined in Theorem
0.2
and
\left<\cdots\right>^{\mathrm{ext}}
denotes the closed extended FJRW
invariants, as
r-r(A)-2
or
s-s(A)-2
may be
-1
.

In fact, if one removes the descendent part of the potential
W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}

by working modulo the ideal
\langle t_{\alpha,\beta,d}\,|\,0\leq\alpha\leq r-2,\ 0\leq\beta\leq s-2,\ 0<d\rangle
, then
dx\wedge dy
is a primitive form
and the
t_{a,b,0}
are flat coordinates.

Remark 0.4
.

(1)
The above theorem matches with the philosophy of the open Gromov-Witten
framework for Fano manifolds. For example, work of Cho–Oh, Gross and
Fukaya–Oh–Ohta–Ono
[
10
,
22
,
18
]
showed how counts of
Maslov index two disks with boundaries on Lagrangian tori naturally
give rise to the correct mirror potentials. Our framework then provides
an analogue of Maslov index two disks in FJRW theory.

(2) When working modulo the ideal
\langle t_{\alpha,\beta,d}\,|\,0\leq\alpha\leq r-2,\ 0\leq\beta\leq s-2,\ 0<d\rangle
, we are in the setting of standard Saito-Givental theory. Here we recover the (closed) mirror symmetry results of
[
33
,
24
]
in the case of
W=x^{r}+y^{s}
. The proofs are fundamentally different. There, the authors consider the integrand
e^{W^{\nu,\mathrm{sym}}}fdx\wedge dy
where
W^{\nu,\mathrm{sym}}
is the versal deformation given by (
0.5
) and perturbatively correct the coordinates through the function
f
(Theorem 3.7 of
[
33
]
) up to quadratic terms and then use reconstruction arguments. Our approach finds a precise versal deformation
W^{\nu,\mathrm{sym}}
using open FJRW invariants so that
f=1
and the coordinates for the versal deformation are already flat.

(3) In the context of open mirror symmetry for Fano manifolds, Overholser
[
41
]
generalized the oscillatory integral framework to include open descendent invariants for
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Theorem
0.3
creates a similar framework for Landau-Ginzburg mirror symmetry, deducing all genus zero FJRW invariants with descendents from the oscillatory integrals.

In light of Theorem
0.2
, the proof of Theorem

0.3
follows from
a calculation of the complicated invariants
\mathcal{A}(A,{\nu})
,
which are shown in Theorem
5.4
to be independent
of the choice of
{\nu}\in{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
. It also provides a large amount of structure for the open FJRW invariants due to the sheer number of constraints given by the relations in Theorem
0.3
. This is summarized in Corollary
5.11
.

The key tool for calculating these quantities is open topological recursion. To express it, we consider the case where the open FJRW invariants are defined using a family of canonical multisections bounded by
I
that need not be symmetric. If
{\nu}\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)
, we have a expression
\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d},\nu)
similar to
\mathcal{A}(A,\nu)
given by the oscillatory integrals, as defined in Notation
3.42
. In Theorem
5.4
, we show that the expression
\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d},\nu)
is independent of
\nu
whenever
\nu\in\Omega_{r,s}(I)
. For this reason, we write
\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d}):=\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d},\nu)
for
\nu\in\Omega_{r,s}(I)
. We are now ready to state open topological recursion:

Theorem 0.5

(Open topological recursion for 2-dimensional Fermat polynomials)
.

Given
J\subseteq I
non-empty, choose
j_{1}\in J

and
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
. Denote by
\mathbf{e}_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
the vector
whose entry corresponding to
j_{1}
is
1
and all other entries zero.
Then the following identities hold.

(1)

If
|J|\geq 1
, then

(0.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle(J,\mathbf{d}+{{\mathbf{e}}}_{1})=

\displaystyle\quad\quad\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-2\}\\
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-2\}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\coprod B=J\setminus\{j_{1}\}\\
A\not=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\tau_{d_{j_{1}}}^{(a_{1},b_{1})}\prod_{i\in A}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\textup{ext}}_{0}\mathcal{A}(B\cup\{z_{a,b}\},\mathbf{d})-\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d}).

Here
z_{a,b}
is an additional internal marking with twist
(r-2-a,s-2-b)
,
with
d_{z_{a,b}}=0
.

(2)

If
|J|\geq 2
, let
j_{2}\in J\setminus\{j_{1}\}
. Then

(0.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle(J,\mathbf{d}+{{\mathbf{e}}}_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-2\}\\
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-2\}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\coprod B=J\setminus\{j_{1}\},j_{2}\in B\\
A\not=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\tau_{d_{j_{1}}}^{(a_{1},b_{1})}\prod_{i\in A}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\textup{ext}}_{0}\mathcal{A}(B\cup\{z_{a,b}\},\mathbf{d}).

where
z_{a,b}
and
d_{z_{a,b}}
are as in (1).

The proof of open topological recursion occupies
§
6
and is the heart of the paper.
The proof works by choosing a suitable multisection of
{\mathbb{L}}_{j_{1}}

whose vanishing locus consists of strata in which closed spheres bubble
off from disks. Most importantly this vanishing locus is easy to
describe. However, this multisection
does not belong to a family of canonical multisections, and hence a detailed study of zeroes arising
in homotoping this multisection to a canonical multisection is required. This
is the hardest part of the proof.

Another key difference in the open topological recursion relations in our paper and the previous ones found in the literature is that there does not exist an open topological recursion relation for a single open invariant. Rather one must use the polynomial
\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d}+{{\mathbf{e}}}_{1},{\nu})
in open invariants in order to obtain a topological recursion relation. This is unlike what is found in the open
r
-spin case in
[
6
]
and the topological recursion structures found by higher Airy structures (see, e.g., §6 of
[
3
]
).

Furthermore, the quantities
\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d})
are familiar. We can crucially use Theorem
0.5
to establish in Corollary
5.11
(3) an open-closed correspondence that links polynomials of open invariants to closed extended invariants.

Corollary 0.6

(Open-Closed Correspondence)
.

Given
J\subseteq I
non-empty
and
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
. Suppose
|J|\geq 2
.
Let
d(J,\mathbf{d})
be as defined in Definition
4.8
. Then

\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d})=\begin{cases}0&d(J,\mathbf{d})<0\\
(-1)^{d(J,\mathbf{d})-1}\left\langle\tau_{d(J,\mathbf{d})}^{(r-r(I)-2,s-s(I)-2)}\prod_{i\in J}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\textup{ext}}&d(J,\mathbf{d})\geq 0.\end{cases}

0.4.
Wall-crossing formulae

The remaining question is to understand the subset
{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)

giving all possible sets of symmetric open
W
-spin invariants.
As mentioned earlier in this introduction, given two families
of canonical multisections
\mathbf{s}_{0}
and
\mathbf{s}_{1}
, there exists
a canonical family of homotopies
H^{*}
between them, and the change
of invariants associated to a
W
-spin graph
\Gamma

requires understanding the number of zeros
\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}})
.
Crucially, these zeros will occur on boundary strata corresponding
to
W
-spin graphs
\Lambda
representing stable
W
-spin disks which
are unions of two disks. These disks meet at a node with the twists at the half-nodes satisfying certain constraints. Further, we have the relation

\dim{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda}=\operatorname{rank}E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})-1

so that
H^{\Gamma}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{W}_{\Lambda}}
will have a well-defined
number of zeroes. Such boundary strata are called
critical
boundaries
, and are responsible for wall-crossing phenomena. Given a graph
\Lambda
, we denote by
\Lambda_{v}
the subgraph consisting of all half-edges adjacent to the vertex
v
in
\Lambda
. A graph
\Lambda
corresponding to a critical boundary will have a vertex
v

such that the subgraph
\Lambda_{v}
will be graded, have no fully twisted boundary marked points, and

\dim{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda_{v}}=\operatorname{rank}E_{\Lambda_{v}}(\mathbf{d})-1.

We will call such a (connected) subgraph
\Lambda_{v}

critical for
\mathbf{d}
, see Definition
2.33
(1).

We define the
Landau-Ginzburg wall-crossing group
(Definition
4.19
) to be the subgroup
G_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}\subseteq\text{Aut}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}(A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}[[x,y]])
of the group of continuous automorphisms of

A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}[[x,y]]
as an
A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}
-algebra consisting of elements that (1) are the identity modulo the ideal generated by the
t_{\alpha,\beta,d}
;
(2) preserve
dx\wedge dy
; (3) preserve the ideal generated by
xy
. These properties are natural for our framework. The first property arises as all
critical boundaries involve disks with internal marked points.
The second property ensures that, if
g\in G_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
,
then replacing a potential

W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}
with
g(W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}})
does
not change the result of the period integrals. Finally, the third condition is more subtle,
but reflects that for certain boundary strata, we are able to define
positivity of multisections of the Witten bundle to guarantee that
zeroes never occur on these boundary strata. This issue arises in
Definitions
1.57
(3) and

3.18
(3).

It turns out that the wall-crossing is governed by a Lie subgroup

G^{r,s}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}\subset G_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}},

which is specified via its corresponding Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{r,s}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
. This Lie algebra is a subalgebra of the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
of
G_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
consisting
of vector fields on which
[E,\cdot]
acts by multiplication by
rs
,
where
E
is as in
(
0.6
), and are invariant under the action

x\mapsto\xi_{r}x
,
y\mapsto\xi_{s},y
,
t_{a,b,d}\mapsto\xi^{-a}_{r}\xi^{-b}_{s}t_{a,b,d}
, where
\xi_{r},\xi_{s}
are primitive

r^{th}
and
s^{th}
roots of unity respectively.

The underlying vector space of the Lie algebra is a direct sum of one-dimensional
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces indexed by all critical graphs.

Theorem
4.27
shows that the group
G^{r,s}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
acts on
{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
via its action
on potentials: for
g\in G^{r,s}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
and
{\nu}\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
, we define
g({\nu})
by the formula

W^{g({\nu}),\mathrm{sym}}=g(W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}).

The main wall-crossing theorem (see Corollary
5.14
)
then states:

Theorem 0.7
.

The group
G^{r,s}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
acts faithfully and transitively
on
{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
. Further,
{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)\subseteq{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
is characterized
as the subset of those
{\nu}\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

If
J\subseteq I
and

\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{0})
, then

{\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{0}}=1
if
|J|=1
and

{\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{0}}=-1
if
|J|=0
.

(2)

For
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
with
|I^{\prime}|=1
,
\mathbf{d}=(d)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{I^{\prime}}
,
we have

\mathcal{A}(I^{\prime},\mathbf{d},{\nu})=(-1)^{d}.

(3)

For any
J\subseteq I
,
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
with

d(J,\mathbf{d})<0
, we have
\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},{\nu})=0
.

In fact, the three conditions in the theorem characterizing

{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
are precisely the conditions guaranteeing
that the oscillatory integral
\int e^{W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}/\hbar}\Omega

of Theorem
0.3
takes the form given there up to terms of
the form
\hbar^{-n}
,
n\geq 2
.

Remark 0.8
.

The Landau-Ginzburg wall crossing group is very similar in spirit to the tropical vertex group,
a group of automorphisms
of a two-dimensional algebraic torus preserving
{dx\over x}\wedge{dy\over y}

introduced by Kontsevich and Soibelman in
[
30
]

and given an enumerative interpretation for Fano manifolds in
[
20
]
.
Generalizations of the tropical vertex group have been used extensively in the literature
in a large number of wall-crossing situations. For Fano manifolds, elements of the wall-crossing group for a Fano manifold correspond to Maslov index zero disks. In our case, a
\mathbb{Q}
-summand of automorphisms in
G^{r,s}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
correspond to a critical graph, which is crucial in the proof of Theorem
0.7
.

Remark 0.9
.

For pairs
(r,s)\in\{(n,2),(2,n),(3,3),(3,4),(4,3)\ |\ n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}\}
, the potentials
W=x^{r}+y^{s}
are simple singularities
A_{n-1}
,
E_{6}
, and
E_{8}
. Here, there are no critical graphs with respect to descendent vector
\mathbf{d}=\mathbf{0}
, and thus the open FJRW invariants with no descendents are well-defined. The fact that the primitive form is unique in these cases is well-established
[
43
,
40
]
. However, in these cases there are indeed critical graphs for non-trivial
\mathbf{d}
and wall-crossing for the theory with descendents. We predict that the only open FJRW invariants that do not enjoy wall-crossing phenomena will be the simple singularities when considered with no descendents.

0.5.
Context and comparison to existing literature

Mirror symmetry for open Gromov Witten (OGW) theories with Landau-Ginzburg (LG) mirrors were first considered for Fano manifolds in
[
10
,
22
,
18
]
Here, the open Gromov-Witten invariants corresponded to compact toric manifolds paired with a Lagrangian torus
T^{n}
. The toric
n=1
case generalizes nicely to some more complicated Lagrangians, essentially to Lagrangians whose rational cohomology is spanned by the unit and the point class, and certain generalizations of this case (see, for example,
[
46
]
). It turns out that such OGW theories
[
46
,
39
,
7
]
give rise to intersection numbers which are independent of choices and are governed by a universal recursion, the
open WDVV (OWDVV) equation

[
48
]
,
[
47
, Theorem 3]
. Moreover, they satisfy other interesting properties such as an extension of the quantum cohomology to relative quantum cohomology, a correspondence between open invariants with a certain internal relative constraint, and open invariants in which this constraint is replaced by a boundary constraint
[
47
, Theorem 6]
. In the case of the target pair
(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\mathbb{RP}^{1})
the OWDVV extends also to descendant theory
[
7
]
, and such an extension is expected to hold more generally.

The OGW theory in the case
n\geq 2
is less understood. Here, Gross and Overholser studied the tropical open
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
case with descendants
[
22
,
41
]
, while Fukaya, Oh, Ohta, and Ono studies the case
n\geq 2
without descendants in detail and obtained mirror symmetry for
n=2
and under additional assumptions also for
n>2
targets
[
18
]
. In the general case where
n\geq 2
, invariants are defined only up to wall crossings. These wall crossings are conjectured to be governed by the Kontsevich-Soibelman wall crossing group
[
30
]
, and this conjecture is proven in some tropical or symplectic cases
[
22
,
41
,
35
,
34
]
.

Open FJRW
theory (OFJRW) is a theory which is still under development. The first construction of an OFJRW theory, the open
r
-spin theories, has appeared in
[
5
,
6
]
, where the main difficulty was identifying a natural choice of boundary conditions to the
open Witten bundle
. These boundary conditions rely on a hidden notion of positivity in this theory. Open
r
-spin are precisely the OFJRW analog of the
n=1
OGW case described above.
As such they have well defined invariants
[
6
, Theorem 3.17]
, satisfy the OWDVV equations
[
6
, Section 4]
, involve an extension of the closed state space (where the Ramond states play the role of relative classes)
[
4
]
and a correspondence between open and closed extended intersection numbers.

This work addresses the construction and calculation of the OFJRW analog of the
n=2
OGW case. The first sections of the paper up to Section
2.4
provide a geometric construction of the open moduli spaces in all dimension; however, we specialize to the dimension
n=2
case exactly when the theory diverges from the situations found in
n<2
and
n>2
. We then geometrically construct the rank
2
theory, including descendants, characterize all invariant quantities, which this time do not satisfy the OWDVV equations, but a different universal recursion. We then identify the wall crossing group and realize it geometrically. Our geometric construction involves the boundary conditions found in
[
6
]
. Interestingly, there are certain boundaries for which the boundary conditions are not specified. They turn out to be exactly the source for the wall crossing. The calculation of invariants and geometric realization of the wall crossings involve new ideas which are then used to establish mirror symmetry in this case.
