1.
Graded
W
-spin surfaces

1.1.
Closed graded
W
-structures

We begin by defining closed
W
-spin Riemann surfaces. These are needed
for two reasons. First, an open
W
-spin surface is a closed surface
equipped with additional structure, including an anti-holomorphic involution.
Second, even in the study of open surfaces, disks may bubble off spheres
on the boundary of moduli space. Hence moduli of closed
W
-spin
surfaces naturally occur
in the boundary of open
W
-spin surfaces. We first consider the special
case of an
r
-spin surface, reviewing terminology from
[
5
]
.

A
closed marked genus
0
orbifold Riemann surface
C

is a proper, one-dimensional Deligne–Mumford stack whose coarse
space
|C|
is a genus
0
Riemann surface with at worst nodal singularities, and such that the morphism
\pi:C\to|C|
is an
isomorphism away from the finitely many marked points and nodes.
We often allow disconnected surfaces, in which case it must be a disjoint
union of such genus
0
surfaces.
We collectively call these marked points and nodes the
special points
. Any special point may have a non-trivial stabilizer which is required to be a finite cyclic group. Any node must be
balanced
, i.e.,
the local picture at any node is

(1.1)

\{xy=0\}/\mu_{d},

where
\mu_{d}
is the group of
d^{th}
roots of unity, and generator
\zeta
acts by

(1.2)

\zeta\cdot(x,y)=(\zeta x,\zeta^{-1}y).

The curve
C
is
d
-stable
if all special points have isotropy
group
\mu_{d}
.

As in
[
5
]
, we introduce a set for markings: let
\Omega

denote the set of all finite subsets of
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}
.
We identify the element
\{i\}\in\Omega
with
i
, for
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
A marking of a set
A
is a function

m:A\rightarrow\Omega,

such that whenever
a,a^{\prime}
are distinct elements of
A\setminus m^{-1}(\emptyset)
,
we have
m(a)\cap m(a^{\prime})=\emptyset
. Such functions are used in what
follows to label the marked points on the curve. The possibility of marking
some points by
\emptyset
or with a set is needed for two reasons. First, we use marking by
\emptyset
to handle boundary marked points, whose cyclic order will often serve as a substitute for their markings. Second, we use
\emptyset
and subsets of
\mathbb{N}
in a consistent way to mark new internal marked
points that arise via normalization of a nodal curve, see Definitions
1.3
and
1.17
. An element of
A
which is mapped to
\emptyset
will sometimes be referred as
unlabeled
.

Explicitly, the markings on the curve
C
are indexed by a set
I

and are labeled by a marking function
m^{I}:I\to\Omega
.
We denote the marked points of
C
by
z_{i},~i\in I
. There is another distinction we have for marked points:

Definition 1.1

(Anchors)
.

We consider a subset of the marked points of
C

to be
anchors
. We allow at most one anchor in each connected component
of
C

and require that this point be labeled
\emptyset
.
A connected component of
C
is
anchored
if it contains an anchor.

Remark 1.2
.

The anchor will serve as a book-keeping mechanism to deal with closed
W
-spin
surfaces which arise as a connected component in a partial normalization
of an open surface.
The anchor should be thought of as the marked point in a closed connected component which, before normalization, was a half-node of an open surface. In this context, such a half-node connected the closed part to the open part or is a contracted boundary half-node. Note that as we only consider genus zero in this
paper, the anchor must be unique.

We define the slightly complicated labeling above in order to keep track of this information and to avoid adding symmetries when we normalize.
Intersection theory on moduli spaces of
r
-spin Riemann surfaces without boundary is, of course, not affected by the anchor, and is equivalent to the classical one defined in
[
51
]
. The same comment holds for closed
W
-spin surfaces, to be defined below.

This yoga of anchors thus requires some care in defining the normalization
of a curve.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
q
be a node of
C
.
The
normalization
of
C
at
q
is the usual normalization at
q,

{\nu}_{q}:C^{\prime}\to C,
with additional care concerning anchors. Both points of
the preimage of
q
become marked points.
Suppose a connected component
C_{1}
of
C
containing
q
is separated into
two connected components
C_{1}^{\prime},C_{1}^{\prime\prime}
by the normalization. If
C_{1}
is anchored and
C^{\prime}_{1}
does not contain the anchor, we define the preimage of
q
in

C^{\prime}_{1}
as its anchor and label it
\emptyset
(See Figure
1
). The other preimage of
q
will be labeled by the union of labels of the other marked points of
C_{1}^{\prime}
. In all other cases, the preimages of
q
are labeled
\emptyset
. Observe that the resulting labeling is still a marking. We denote by

{\nu}:\widehat{C}\to C

the result of the normalization at all nodes.

A
half-node
is a point of the normalization
\widehat{C}

mapping to a node
q
in
C
. We often refer to a half-node also as a node
q\in C

along with a choice of branch of
C
at
q
.

\nu_{q}^{-1}(q)
\nu_{q}
q

Figure 1
.
The normalization
\nu_{q}:C^{\prime}\rightarrow C
of the connected component
C_{1}
at a node
q
. Anchors are color-coded as blue.

Suppose that
C
is
d
-stable and
r
divides
d
.
An
r
-spin
structure on
C
is an orbifold line bundle
L\to C
, called the
r
-spin bundle
, together with an isomorphism

(1.3)

\tau:L^{\otimes r}\to\omega_{C,\log}.

The local structure of
L
at a marked point
p
looks like the quotient stack
[\mathbb{C}^{2}/\mu_{d}]
where the generator
\zeta=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{d}}\in\mu_{d}
acts by

(1.4)

\zeta\cdot(X,T)=(e^{\frac{2\pi i}{d}}X,e^{m\frac{2\pi i}{d}}T).

Here
X
is a local coordinate on the curve,
T
a coordinate on the
fibres of
L
, and
the
multiplicity
(of
L
) is some integer
m=\text{mult}_{p}(L)
modulo
d
. We impose here that
\text{mult}_{p}(L)\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
. Moreover, since the multiplicity of
\omega_{C,\log}
at any point is
0,
for each marking
p
it must hold that

{\text{mult}_{p}(L)\over d/r}\in\mathbb{Z}.

A
twisted
r
-spin
structure on a closed marked genus
0
orbifold Riemann surface
C
is an orbifold bundle of the form

(1.5)

S=L\otimes\mathcal{O}\left(-\sum_{i\in I_{0}}[z_{i}]\right),

where
L
is an
r
-spin bundle and
I_{0}
is a subset of the markings of
L
-multiplicity zero which contains
every marking whose
L
-multiplicity is
0,
except, possibly,
anchors. Note that a priori the subset
I_{0}
is a choice and part of the data of the
r
-spin structure, but we will see below that there is a standard choice of
I_{0}
determined by the rest of the
r
-spin structure. Here, the twist

\mathcal{O}([z_{i}])
is the orbifold line bundle associated to the degree
1
divisor

[z_{i}]
, i.e.,
\mathcal{O}([z_{i}])
is the pull-back of a line bundle on the
coarse moduli space.
The
twist
of the
i^{th}
marking (with respect to
S
) is defined to be

l_{i}-1+r\mathbf{1}_{i\in I_{0}}

where
l_{i}
is the unique solution of

\text{mult}_{z_{i}}(L)=\left(\frac{d}{r}\right)l_{i},~~l_{i}\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\}

and
\mathbf{1}_{i\in I_{0}}
denotes the characteristic function of

I_{0}
. Thus in particular:

(1.6)

If a marked point has twist
-1
, it is an anchor.

Using (
1.3
), note that there is an isomorphism
\tau
so that

(1.7)

\tau:S^{\otimes r}\simeq\omega_{C,\log}(-\sum_{i\in I_{0}}r[z_{i}]).

Definition 1.4
.

A
closed twisted genus
0

r
-spin surface
is a closed genus
0

d
-stable surface together with a twisted
r
-spin structure.
The notion of isomorphism is the standard one.

Given the normalization
\nu:\widehat{C}\rightarrow C
,
the orbifold line bundle
{\nu}^{*}L\to\widehat{C}
is automatically an
r
-spin bundle. However,
{\nu}^{*}S
need not be a twisted
r
-spin bundle. Instead,
if we take

(1.8)

\widehat{S}={\nu}^{*}S\otimes\mathcal{O}\left(-\sum_{q\in\mathcal{R}}[q]\right),

where

(1.9)

\mathcal{R}:=\{\hbox{$q$ a half-node of $\widehat{C}$ which is of
multiplicity $0$ but not an anchor}\},

then
\widehat{S}
is a twisted
r
-spin bundle, with the set of
marked points where the spin structure is twisted being

\widehat{I}_{0}=I_{0}\cup\mathcal{R}.

The
twists
of the half-nodes of
\widehat{C}
are defined to be the twists of

\widehat{S}
at these points. We often use this notion for the twist of a half-node
viewed as a nodal point in
C
along with a choice
of branch of
C
through this node.

Note in analogy with (
1.6
), we have

(1.10)

The only half-nodes of
(\widehat{C},\widehat{S})

of twist
-1
are anchors.

For twisted
r
-spin structures, we will impose that
I_{0}
consists of all markings of
L
-multiplicity zero except anchors.
3
3

3

Moving forwards, we require a rule for twisted
r
-spin structures in the open case. Here, closed
r
-spin curves are connected components of the normalization of some stable marked genus 0 orbifold Riemann surfaces with boundary. We exclude from
I_{0}
the anchors that come from a contracted boundary (see Definition
1.16
).

Definition 1.5
.

A special point whose twist is
-1\mod r
is called
Ramond
, while the other markings are called
Neveu-Schwarz
(with respect to
S
).

Consider the coarsification map
\pi:C\rightarrow|C|
and the pushforward
\pi_{*}L
. Here, we can see that locally at the marked point
p
, the sections of this sheaf will be given by
\mu_{d}
-invariant sections of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}}
with respect to the action in Equation (
1.4
). The generator acts locally on a section
f
by taking
\zeta\cdot(X,f(X))=(\zeta X,\zeta^{m}f(X))
. For invariance, we need that
\zeta^{m}f(X)=f(\zeta X)
, which implies that
f(X)=X^{m}g(X^{d})
for some polynomial
g
. Note that
there is a local coordinate
x
on
|C|
with

\pi^{*}(x)=X^{d}
, hence
\pi_{*}L
is a line bundle. We then have that
(\pi_{*}L)^{\otimes d}
near
p
is generated by
X^{md}=x^{m}
, which is used to prove the following proposition:

Proposition 1.6
.

For any connected component
C_{l}
of
|\widehat{C}|,
with markings also denoted by
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in I_{l}}
and half-nodes
\{p_{h}\}_{h\in N_{l}},
it holds that
|\widehat{S}|
is a line bundle and

(1.11)

\left(|\widehat{S}|\big|_{C_{l}}\right)^{\otimes r}\cong\omega_{|C_{l}|}\otimes\mathcal{O}\left(-\sum_{i\in I_{l}}a_{i}[z_{i}]-\sum_{h\in N_{l}}c_{h}[p_{h}]\right),

a_{i},c_{h}\in\{-1,0,\ldots,r-1\},

where
a_{i},c_{h}
are the twists.

Proof.

The corresponding claim for
|{\nu}^{*}L|
is well-known and can be seen through the argument in the preceding paragraph.
Since
\widehat{S}
differs from
{\nu}^{*}L
by twisting down at a divisor pulled back from the coarse curve, the twisting and coarsening commute which allows us to deduce the claim.
∎

We can also define the orbifold line bundle

J:=\omega_{C}\otimes S^{\vee}

on
C
, which has a natural pairing with
S
given by

(1.12)

\langle-,-\rangle:S\otimes J\rightarrow\omega_{C}.

We now require the notion of a
grading
.

Definition 1.7

(Grading)
.

A
grading
of a twisted closed genus
0

r
-spin surface
(C,S)
consists
of the following additional structure at each anchor
z_{j}
with
twist
r-1
.
Recall that we have a map
\tau^{\prime}
defined as the composition

\tau^{\prime}:\left(S\otimes\mathcal{O}\left([z_{j}]\right)\right)^{\otimes r}\big|_{z_{j}}\rightarrow\omega_{C,\log}\big|_{z_{j}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},

where the second map is an isomorphism given by the residue map. Then
the data of a grading at
z_{j}
is, first, the choice of
an
\mathbb{R}
-linear involution
\widetilde{\phi}
on the fiber
\left(S\otimes\mathcal{O}\left([z_{j}]\right)\right)_{z_{j}}
which is required to satisfy two properties:

(1)

For all
v\in\left(S\otimes\mathcal{O}\left([z_{j}]\right)\right)_{z_{j}}
,
we have
\tau^{\prime}(\widetilde{\phi}(v)^{\otimes r})=-\overline{\tau^{\prime}(v^{\otimes r})}

where
w\mapsto\overline{w}
is the standard conjugation.

(2)

We have

\{\tau^{\prime}(v^{\otimes r})\,|\,v\in\left(S\otimes\mathcal{O}\left([z_{j}]\right)\right)^{\widetilde{\phi}}_{z_{j}}\}\supseteq i\mathbb{R}_{+}
, where
i
is the root of
-1
in the upper half plane.

Second, we choose a non-zero vector
v_{j}
in the fiber
(S\otimes\mathcal{O}([z_{j}]))^{\tilde{\phi}}_{z_{j}}
so that
\tau^{\prime}(v_{j}^{\otimes r})\in i\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Two gradings
\{v_{j}\},\{v_{j}^{\prime}\}
are
equivalent
if
v_{j}=\lambda_{j}v_{j}^{\prime}
for some collection of positive real numbers
\lambda_{j}
.

We call the connected component of
\left(S\otimes\mathcal{O}\left([z_{j}]\right)\right)^{\widetilde{\phi}}_{z_{j}}\setminus\{0\}
containing
v_{j}
the
positive direction
on
\left(S\otimes\mathcal{O}\left([z_{j}]\right)\right)^{\widetilde{\phi}}_{z_{j}}
and call any
v
in that connected component
positive
. Note that if
v
is positive then
\tau^{\prime}(v^{\otimes r})\in i\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Since

J_{z_{j}}\otimes\left(S\otimes\mathcal{O}\left([z_{j}]\right)\right)_{z_{j}}\simeq\omega_{C,\log}\big|_{z_{j}},

\widetilde{\phi}
induces a conjugation on the fiber
J_{z_{j}},
also denoted by
\widetilde{\phi}
, by the requirement that under the identification of
\omega_{C,\log}\big|_{z_{j}}
with
\mathbb{C}
, we have

\langle\widetilde{\phi}(w),\widetilde{\phi}(v)\rangle=-\overline{\langle w,v\rangle}

for
w\in J_{z_{j}}
,
v\in\left(S\otimes\mathcal{O}\left([z_{j}]\right)\right)_{z_{j}}
.
Similarly, using the positive direction on
\left(S\otimes\mathcal{O}\left([z_{j}]\right)\right)^{\widetilde{\phi}}_{z_{j}}
, we define a positive direction on
J^{\widetilde{\phi}}_{z_{j}}
in the following way. We say that
w\in J_{z_{j}}
is positive if, for any positive
v\in\left(S\otimes\mathcal{O}\left([z_{j}]\right)\right)_{z_{j}}
, it holds that

\langle w,v\rangle\in i\mathbb{R}_{+}.

Remark 1.8
.

Gradings arise naturally from the open
r
-spin disk case. Indeed,
as we shall see, such disks may acquire contracted boundary nodes,
i.e., nodes obtained by shrinking the boundary to a point, see Figure

3
. The
stalk of the spin bundle at these points then naturally carry
an involution
\tilde{\phi}
and the grading can be seen as the
limiting data of a lifting on the open disks in the sense of Definition

1.21
,(2).

Definition 1.9
.

A
closed genus
0
graded
r
-spin surface
is a twisted closed genus
0

r
-spin surface all of whose connected components are anchored, together with a choice of grading, up to equivalence. An
isomorphism
of closed genus
0
graded
r
-spin surfaces is an isomorphism of closed genus
0
twisted
r
-spin surfaces that preserves the anchors and, in the case where an anchor has twist
r-1
, also the involution
\widetilde{\phi}
and the positive direction.

Remark 1.10
.

As already mentioned in Remark
1.2
, once we start to
normalize open
r
-spin surfaces, the
anchor of a sphere component should be thought of as the half-node
at which the component met a disk component, or met a simple
path of sphere components connecting it to a disk component. The additional
data in the grading in the case of a twist
r-1
anchor arises as in Remark
1.8
.

The properties of the twists and grading can be summarized in the following well-known observation below.

Observation 1.11
.

It follows immediately from
Proposition
1.6
that if
C
is a smooth
r
-spin curve,
then the degree of the line bundle
|S|\to|C|
is

(1.13)

\frac{-2-\sum a_{i}}{r}\in\mathbb{Z},

where
a_{i}
are the twists. This number must be an integer.
Thus, also

(1.14)

\frac{\sum a_{i}-(r-2)}{r}\in\mathbb{Z}.

If
C
is a
d
-stable
r
-spin curve and

p
and
p^{\prime}
are the two branches of a node, then

(1.15)

c_{p}+c_{p^{\prime}}\equiv r-2\mod r.

Moreover, in the notation of (
1.11
), for any component
C_{l}
of
|\widehat{C}|

with markings also denoted by
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in I_{l}}
and half-nodes
\{p_{h}\}_{h\in N_{l}},
we have

r\Big|\sum_{i\in I_{l}}a_{i}+\sum_{h\in N_{l}}c_{h}+2-r.

When (
1.14
) holds, there is, up to isomorphism,
a unique graded structure on
C
with the given twists.

Remark 1.12
.

The only automorphisms of genus
0
graded
r
-spin surfaces with injective marking
m

are the
ghost automorphisms
. There are two types of such automorphisms: (i) scaling fiberwise the bundle
S
and (ii) “rotating”
C
at a node. In type (i), we scale the bundle
S
fiberwise by an
r^{th}
root of unity. Note that we can do this independently on each of the connected components; however, as explained
in Proposition 2.15 of
[
5
]
, the extra data of a grading at
anchors of twist
r-1
eliminates the automorphism given by scaling
the spin bundle by an
r^{th}
root of unity on the connected component
of
C
containing the anchor. This provides a factor of
r^{|\pi_{0}(C)|-A}
to the group of ghost automorphisms, where
|\pi_{0}(C)|
is the number
of connected components of
C
and
A
is the number of anchors whose
twist is
r-1
. In type (ii), recall that the stabilizer group is
\mu_{d}
at a node, so
this contributes a factor of
\mu_{d}
to the automorphism group of
C
.
In terms of the local picture (
1.1
), the action of

\mu_{d}
is given by

\zeta\cdot(x,y)=(\zeta x,y),~~\zeta~\text{is an $d^{th}$ root of unity.}

One can check as in
[
8
]
that the pull back of the spin bundle

S
via this automorphism gives an isomorphic spin structure, and hence
induces an automorphism. See also
[
5
]
, Remark 2.12 and Proposition
2.15. However, in that reference,
d=r
. Consequently, we obtain a factor of
d^{N}
where
N
is the number of nodes. Putting together, the group of ghost automorphisms is of order
r^{|\pi_{0}(C)|-A}d^{N}
.

We now generalize to the
W
-spin case.
Let
W
be any Fermat polynomial in
n
variables,

(1.16)

W(x_{1},\ldots,x_{a})=\sum_{i=1}^{a}x_{i}^{r_{i}},

and let
d={\mathrm{lcm}}(r_{1},\ldots,r_{a})
.

Definition 1.13
.

A
closed graded genus
0

W
-spin Riemann surface
is a tuple

(C,S_{1},\ldots,S_{a};\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{a})

where
C
is
d
-stable marked genus
0
orbifold Riemann surface, all of whose components are anchored, and for all
i\in[a]:=\{1,\ldots,a\}
,
(S_{i},\tau_{i})
is a graded twisted
r_{i}
-spin structure on
C
.

The
twist
of a marking or a half-node is the
a
-tuple of its twists with respect to each
S_{i}
. We write
\text{tw}(q)
for the twist of
q,
and
\text{tw}_{i}(q)
for its
i^{th}
component,
i\in[a]
. A special point
q
is
broad
if it is Ramond with respect to one of the bundles
S_{i},
otherwise
q
is
narrow
.

Remark 1.14
.

Recall from (
1.10
) that the only half-nodes of twist
-1
are anchors. In (closed) genus
0

W
-spin theory, it is possible to allow at most one marked point with twist
-1
for each
r_{i}
-spin bundle,
i=1,\ldots,a
. This is observed in
[
25
]
, and studied in detail in
[
4
]
for a single spin bundle. So long as all other marked points have non-negative twist, the degree of the spin bundle is negative which implies that the Witten bundle defined in Section
2.2.1
is indeed a bundle.

Observation 1.15
.

The number of automorphisms is
d^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{a}r_{i}^{|\pi_{0}(C)|-A_{i}}
, where
A_{i}
is the number of anchors at which the bundle
S_{i}
has twist
r_{i}-1

and
N
is the number of nodes.

1.2.
Open
r
-spin structures

Definition 1.16
.

(1)

A
connected marked genus
0
orbifold Riemann surface with boundary
or
disk
is a tuple

(C,\phi,\Sigma,\{z_{i}\}_{i\in I},\{x_{j}\}_{j\in B},m^{I},m^{B}),

in which:

(i)

C
is a connected genus
0
orbifold Riemann surface.

(ii)

\phi:C\rightarrow C
is an anti-holomorphic involution (
conjugation
).

(iii)

\Sigma
is a fundamental domain for the induced conjugation
on
|C|
. We also write
\phi:|C|\rightarrow|C|
for this induced
conjugation. Here
\Sigma
is a Riemann surface, usually, but not
always, with boundary.

(iv)

z_{i}\in C
are a collection of distinct points
(the
internal marked points
) labeled by the set
I
, whose images in
|C|
lie in
\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma
, with
conjugate marked points

\bar{z}_{i}:=\phi(z_{i})
.

(v)

x_{j}\in\text{Fix}(\phi)
are a collection of distinct points (the
boundary marked points
) labeled by the set
B
, whose images in
|C|
lie in
\partial\Sigma
.

(vi)

m^{I}:I\to\Omega
and
m^{B}:B\to\Omega
are markings.

A typical such surface with the various marked points labeled can be seen in Figure
2
. We shall often denote the object only by
C
or by the preferred half
\Sigma
.

Note there can be at most one isolated
\phi
-invariant point. If it exists, it is called a
contracted boundary node
and it is regarded as an
anchor
. In this case,
\Sigma
has no boundary. In all other cases,
\Sigma
is a
nodal marked disk
and has no anchor. Those
\phi
-invariant nodes which are not a contracted boundary are called
boundary nodes
. The remaining nodes whose image under the coarsification map falls in the interior of
\Sigma
are the
internal nodes
. Their conjugate points are the
conjugate nodes
. We call
\partial\Sigma
the
boundary of the open surface
. It is oriented as the boundary of
\Sigma
. See Figure
3
.

(2)

A
marked genus
0
orbifold Riemann surface with boundary
is a finite union of connected marked genus
0
orbifold Riemann surfaces with boundary and connected marked genus
0
orbifold Riemann surfaces without boundary. It is
smooth
if there are no nodes. See Figure
2
.

(3)

A marked genus
0
orbifold Riemann surface
C
(with or without boundary) is
stable
if each connected component of the normalization has at least three marked points. We say that
C
is
partially stable
if
C
is connected and open, and either (i)
|I|=1
and
|B|=0
or (ii)
|I|=0
and
|B|=2
.

(4)

We usually suppress
\phi
from the notation and write
\overline{w}
for
\phi(w)
when
w
lies in the preimage of
\Sigma
in
C
. In what follows we usually identify a special point with its image in
\Sigma
.

(5)

An
isomorphism
of marked orbifold Riemann surfaces with boundary

(C_{1},\phi_{1},\Sigma_{1},\{z_{1,i}\}_{i\in I_{1}},\{x_{1,j}\}_{j\in B_{1}},m_{1}^{I_{1}},m_{1}^{B_{1}})\cong(C_{2},\phi_{2},\Sigma_{2},\{z_{2,i}\}_{i\in I_{2}},\{x_{2,j}\}_{j\in B_{2}},m_{2}^{I_{2}},m_{2}^{B_{2}})

consists of an isomorphism
s:C_{1}\rightarrow C_{2}
and bijections
f^{I}:I_{1}\rightarrow I_{2}
and
f^{B}:B_{1}\rightarrow B_{2}
such that

(i)

s\circ\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}\circ s
,

(ii)

s(\Sigma_{1})=\Sigma_{2}
,

(iii)

s(x_{1,j})=x_{2,f^{B}(j)}
for all
j\in B_{1}
and
s(z_{1,i})=z_{2,f^{I}(i)}
for all
i\in I_{1}
, and

(iv)

m_{1}^{I_{1}}=m_{2}^{I_{2}}\circ f^{I}
and
m_{1}^{B_{1}}=m_{2}^{B_{2}}\circ f^{B}
.

Note that one can write

|C|=\left(\Sigma\coprod_{\partial\Sigma}\overline{\Sigma}\right)/\sim_{CB},

where
\bar{\Sigma}
is obtained from
\Sigma
by reversing the complex structure, and
\sim_{CB}
is the equivalence relation on
\Sigma\cup_{\partial\Sigma}\overline{\Sigma}
defined by
x\sim_{CB}y
precisely if
x=\phi(y)
and one of them is a contracted boundary node.

C
\partial\Sigma
\bullet
z_{1}
\bullet
\bar{z}_{1}
\bullet
x_{1}
\Sigma
\bullet
z_{2}
\bullet
\bar{z}_{2}

Figure 2
.
A smooth connected marked genus 0 orbifold Riemann surface with boundary with two internal markings
z_{1},z_{2}
and a boundary marking
x_{1}
.

We next take care in defining normalization of open spin surfaces.
As already mentioned in Remarks
1.2
and
1.10
,
anchors play a key role in bookkeeping in the normalization process.
Precisely:

Definition 1.17
.

Let
(C,\phi,\Sigma,\{z_{i}\}_{i\in I},\{x_{j}\}_{j\in B},m^{I},m^{B})

be a marked genus
0
orbifold Riemann surface with boundary.
The
partial normalization

{\nu}_{q}:C^{\prime}\to C
at the node
q
is defined by
taking the usual partial normalization
{\nu}_{q}:C^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow C
at
q
and
its conjugate node

\phi(q)
and then taking
C^{\prime}\subseteq C^{\prime\prime}
to be the union of
connected components whose image in
|C|
intersects
\Sigma
in more
than one point. For example, in the examples in
Figure
3
, the light gray spheres are discarded in
cases (A) and (C), if the normalization occurs at the internal node
and the contracted boundary node respectively. However, nothing is
discarded if one normalizes (B) at the boundary node.

We then define
{\nu}_{q}:C^{\prime}\rightarrow C
to be
{\nu}_{q}|_{C^{\prime}}
. In the normalization, we label the resulting half-nodes and choose
anchors, generalizing Definition
1.3
, as follows:

(1)

If
q
is an internal node, suppose without loss of generality that its image in
|C|
belongs to
\Sigma
. In this case
C^{\prime}
will have two connected components: one component will be closed and one component will be open or have
a contracted boundary node. The preimage
q_{c}
of
q
in the closed component is declared to be the anchor, and is marked by
\emptyset\in\Omega
. The other half-node is marked by the union of the labels of the remaining points in the component of
q_{c}
.

(2)

If
q
is a boundary node, then we label each of its half-nodes by
\emptyset\in\Omega
. Note that both connected components after the partial normalization are still nodal marked disks and consequently do not have anchors.

(3)

If
q
is a contracted boundary node, we declare its preimage in
C^{\prime}

to be an anchor, and we mark it by
\emptyset\in\Omega
.

(a)

Internal node

(b)

Boundary node

(c)

Contracted boundary

Figure 3
.
The closed curve
|C|
, with the nodal disk
\Sigma
shaded.

The normalization
{\nu}:\widehat{C}\to C
for a marked genus 0 orbifold Riemann surface with boundary
C
is similarly defined by iterating the partial normalization definition over all nodes.

Definition 1.18
.

Let
C
be a
d
-stable marked genus 0 orbifold Riemann surface with boundary. Let
r
divide
d
. An
r
-spin
(resp.
twisted
r
-spin
) structure on
C
is an
r
-spin structure
L
(resp. twisted
r
-spin structure
S
) on
C
together with an involution
\widetilde{\phi}
on
L
(resp.
S
) lifting

\phi
and such that
\widetilde{\phi}^{\otimes r}
agrees under
\tau

with the involution of
\omega_{C,\log}
induced by
\phi
.

Multiplicities of marked points and half-nodes, as well as twists of marked points, are defined as in the closed case. Note that this implies that, for any
r
-spin structure on a
d
-stable marked genus 0 orbifold Riemann surface with boundary, the multiplicities and twists at an internal marked point
z_{i}
(called the
internal twist

a_{i}
) must be the same at its conjugate marked point
\bar{z}_{i}
. We note that we analogously call the twist
b_{i}
at a boundary special point the
boundary twist
.

Observation 1.19
.

There is a constraint on the allowable twists of the marked points,
arising from Observation
1.11
. Indeed,
let
S
be a twisted
r
-spin structure with internal twists
a_{i}
and boundary twists
b_{j}
. Then

(1.17)

\frac{2\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}+\sum_{j\in B}b_{j}-(r-2)}{r}\in\mathbb{Z}.

As in the closed case,
{\nu}^{*}L\to\widehat{C}
is itself an
r
-spin structure. We define the twisted
r
-spin structure
\widehat{S}\to\widehat{C}
as

(1.18)

\widehat{S}={\nu}^{*}S\otimes\mathcal{O}\left(-\sum_{q\in\mathcal{R}}[q]\right).

Here

(1.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{R}:=&\{\hbox{$q$ is a contracted boundary node of multiplicity $0$}\}\\
&\cup\{\hbox{$q$ is a half-node of $\widehat{C}$ which is of
multiplicity $0$ but not an anchor}\}.\end{split}

We recall that
\mathcal{O}([q])
denotes the pull-back of the corresponding
line bundle from
|\widehat{C}|
.
The twists of half-nodes with respect to
S
are defined as the twists of the corresponding half-nodes with respect to
\widehat{S}
. The notions of Ramond and Neveu-Schwarz points are as in the closed case.

We remark that (
1.17
) also induces constraints
on the twists of half-nodes. Indeed, when applied to a connected
component of the normalization
\widehat{C}
, one must sum over twists
of the half-nodes as well as the twists of the marked points.
Note that the definition of
\mathcal{R}
determines the twists of
all half-nodes.

Remark 1.20
.

In the course of the proof of
[
5
]
, Proposition 2.15, it is shown that
every contracted boundary node is Ramond.

1.2.1.
Lifting and alternations

Definition 1.21
.

Let
S
be a twisted
r
-spin structure on a connected marked genus 0 orbifold Riemann surface with boundary
C
.

(1)

Suppose that
C
has no contracted boundary node. Let
A
be the complement in
C^{\phi}
of the set of special points. In this case a
lifting of
S
is a choice (if it exists) of an orientation for the real line bundle

S^{\widetilde{\phi}}\to A

satisfying the following property. If we take any vector
v\in S^{\widetilde{\phi}}_{a}
at any
a\in A
such that
v
is positive with respect to the orientation specified by the choice of lifting, then its image under the map
v\mapsto\tau(v^{\otimes r})
is positive with respect to the natural orientation of
\omega_{C}^{\phi}|_{A}
. Two sections
v
and
v^{\prime}
of
S|_{A}
are
equivalent
if
v=cv^{\prime}
for a continuous function
c:A\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
. We write
[v]
for the equivalence class of
v
. When a section
v
is positive with respect to the lifting, we denote a lifting of
S
over
A
by
[v]
.

A
lifting of
J
over
A
is an orientation of the real line bundle

J^{\widetilde{\phi}}\to A,

satisfying the following property. If we take any vector
w\in J^{\widetilde{\phi}}_{a}
at any
a\in A
such that
w
is positive with respect to the orientation, then there exists a vector
v\in S^{\widetilde{\phi}}_{a}
with
\tau(v^{\otimes r})
and
\langle w,v\rangle
positive with respect to the natural orientation of
\omega_{C}^{\phi}|_{A}
, where
\langle-,-\rangle
is as in (
1.12
). We similarly define an equivalence class
[w]
for sections of
J
over
A
as above.

(2)

Suppose that
C
has a contracted boundary node
q
. In this case a
lifting of
S
is a choice (if it exists) of an orientation for the real line
(S_{q})^{\tilde{\phi}}

satisfying a different set of properties as follows.
The fiber
\omega_{C,q}
is canonically identified with
\mathbb{C}
via the residue, and the involution
\phi
is sent, under this identification, to the involution
z\to-\bar{z},
whose fixed points are the purely imaginary numbers.
4
4

4

The residue of a conjugation invariant form
\zeta
can be calculated as
\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{L}\zeta
where
L\subset\Sigma
is a small loop surrounding
q
whose orientation is such that
q
is to the left of
L
. The behaviour under conjugation shows that the residue of an invariant section is purely imaginary.

The choice of orientation must satisfy the property that for any vector
v\in S_{q}^{\tilde{\phi}}
positive with respect to the orientation, the image of
v^{\otimes r}
under the map

S^{\otimes r}_{q}\rightarrow\omega_{C,q}

is
positive imaginary
, meaning that it lies in
i\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We then write a lifting of
S
at
q
as an equivalence class
[v]
of such positive elements
v
under the equivalence relation of multiplication by a positive real number. In the contracted boundary case,
there always exists a
\widetilde{\phi}
-invariant
w\in J|_{q}
such that
\langle w,v\rangle
is positive imaginary, and we refer to such
w
as a
grading at
q
.

We say that a twisted
r
-spin structure
S
on a marked genus 0 orbifold Riemann surface with boundary
C
is
compatible
if each connected component of
C
has a lifting. A
lifting on
C
is a choice of lifting on each connected component.

Remark 1.22
.

Consider the case where
C
has a contracted boundary node. Recall by Remark
1.20
that
q
is Ramond and by Observation
1.19
that the twist at
q
on the partial normalization at
q
will be
r-1
. Then the choice of a lifting
q
will induce a grading (Definition
1.7
) on the closed genus 0
r
-spin surface given by the partial normalization at
q
, as
q
will become the anchor (Definition
1.17
(3)).

In the case where there are no contracted boundaries, observe that a twisted
r
-spin structure admits a lifting of
S
over
A
precisely if it admits a lifting of
J
over
A
. Moreover, there is a bijection between equivalence classes of liftings of
S
and of
J
, in which
[v]
corresponds to
[w]
if
\langle w,v\rangle
is everywhere positive for all representatives
v
and
w
of
[v]
and
[w]
.

Now, a lifting of
J
induces a lifting of
\widehat{J}:=\omega_{\widehat{C}}\otimes\widehat{S}^{\vee}
. Take
q
to be a boundary marked point or boundary half-node of
\widehat{C}
. Then let
U_{q}
be some neighborhood of
|{\nu}^{-1}(q)|
in
\partial\widehat{\Sigma}
, where
\widehat{\Sigma}
is the normalisation of
\Sigma
. The lifting of
J
induces an orientation for the real line bundle
|\widehat{J}|^{\widetilde{\phi}}|_{U_{q}\setminus|\nu^{-1}(q)|}
. Here, we use the notation
\widetilde{\phi}
also for the involutions on
\widehat{J}
and its coarsening
|\widehat{J}|
.

Definition 1.23

(Alternating nodes or marked boundary points)
.

Let
S
be a compatible twisted
r
-spin structure on a connected marked genus
0
orbifold Riemann surface with boundary. Suppose further that
C
has no contracted boundary node. Take
q
to be a boundary marked point or boundary half-node of
\widehat{C}
and take
U_{q}
to be some neighborhood of
|{\nu}^{-1}(q)|
as above. We say that the
lifting alternates in
q
and that
q
is
alternating
with respect to the lifting if the orientation
cannot be extended
to an orientation of
|\widehat{J}|^{\widetilde{\phi}}\big|_{U_{q}}
. Otherwise the lifting
does not alternate
or is
non-alternating
and
q
is
non-alternating
.

We write
{\text{alt}}(q)=1
if the lifting alternates in
q
and
{\text{alt}}(q)=0
if it does not alternate.

Remark 1.24
.

In
[
5
]
, the terms
legal
and
illegal
are used
instead of alternating and non-alternating, respectively.

We summarize the properties of
r
-spin structures with a lifting in the following propositions. We start with Proposition 2.5 of
[
5
]
:

Proposition 1.25
.

Suppose that
(C,S)
is a smooth connected genus
0
twisted
r
-spin Riemann surface with boundary.

(1)

When
r
is odd, any twisted
r
-spin structure is compatible, and there is a unique equivalence class of liftings.

(2)

When
r
is even, the boundary twists
b_{j}
in a compatible twisted
r
-spin structure must be even. Whenever the boundary twists are even, either the
r
-spin structure is compatible or it becomes compatible after replacing
\widetilde{\phi}
by
\xi\circ\widetilde{\phi}\circ\xi^{-1}
for
\xi
an
r^{th}
root of
-1
, which yields an isomorphic
r
-spin structure.

(3)

Suppose
r
is odd and
v
is a lifting. Then
x_{j}
is alternating if and only if its twist is odd.

(4)

Suppose
r
is even. If a lifting alternates precisely at a subset
D\subset\{x_{j}\}_{j\in B}
, then

(1.20)

\frac{2\sum a_{i}+\sum b_{j}+2}{r}\equiv|D|\mod 2.

If (
1.20
) holds, then there exist exactly two liftings up to equivalence, one the negative of the other, which alternate precisely at
D\subset\{x_{j}\}_{j\in B}
.

In particular, in the nodal case the analogous claims hold for the connected components of
(\widehat{C},\widehat{S})
.

The following is a combination of
[
5
]
, Propositions 2.11 and 2.15,
with a slight change to the number of automorphisms as indicated in
Remark
1.12
.

Proposition 1.26
.

Suppose
(C,\phi,\Sigma,\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}\},\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}\})
is a connected genus
0
marked orbifold Riemann surface with boundary, and

a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}\in\{0,1,\ldots,r-1\},\quad b_{1},\ldots,b_{k}\in\{0,1,\ldots,r-2\}

are such that (
1.17
) holds. If
r
is even, suppose the
b_{i}
are even for all
i
and let
D\subseteq[k]
be an arbitrary set for which (
1.20
) holds. If
r
is odd, let
D=\{i\in[k]\,|\,~2\nmid b_{i}\}
. Then, up to isomorphism,
there exists a unique twisted
r
-spin structure with a lifting on this disk with internal twists given by the integers
a_{i},
boundary twists by the integers
b_{j}
, and
D
being the set of alternating boundary marked points. The automorphism group of the
r
-spin disk with lifting is of size
d^{N}
,
where
N
is the number of internal nodes.

Remark 1.27
.

Note that if
r
is even, the two choices of liftings
in Proposition
1.25
are related by an isomorphism
which is the identity on the underlying Riemann surface and rescales the
spin bundle by
-1
.

Definition 1.28
.

Consider a Fermat polynomial
W
as in (
1.16
), with
d=\mathrm{lcm}(r_{1},\ldots,r_{a})
.
A
W
-spin Riemann surface with boundary
is a tuple

(C,S_{1},\ldots,S_{a};\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{a};\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{a})

where

(1)

C
is an orbifold marked Riemann surface with boundary such that:

(a)

all of the special points of
C
have isotropy group
\mu_{d}
;

(b)

all the closed connected components of
C
are anchored;

(2)

each
(S_{i},\tau_{i},\phi_{i})
are twisted
r_{i}
-spin structures.

The
W
-spin structure on
C
is
compatible
if each
r_{i}
-spin structure is.

A
lifting
for a
W
-spin Riemann surface with boundary is an
a
-tuple
([v_{1}],\ldots,[v_{a}])
of liftings for each
J_{i}=\omega_{C}\otimes S_{i}^{\vee}
(or equivalently liftings of
S_{i}
). For any boundary special marking or half-node
q
we write
{\text{alt}}(q)
for the
a
-tuple whose
i^{th}
component is
{\text{alt}}_{i}(q),
the alternation with respect to the
i^{th}
lifting.

We now consider restrictions on the behaviour of boundary marked points.
The kinds of open surfaces considered below will be the ones for which
we define open
W
-spin invariants, as Euler classes of certain bundles
on their moduli spaces:

Definition 1.29
.

(1)

A
connected pre-graded open
W
-spin surface
is a connected, compatible,
W
-spin Riemann surface with boundary, together with a lifting which satisfies

(a)

For any boundary marking
p
and any
i\in[a]
, either

\text{tw}_{i}(p)=r_{i}-2,{\text{alt}}_{i}(p)=1~~\text{or }\text{tw}_{i}(p)={\text{alt}}_{i}(p)=0.

(b)

If
q
is a boundary node and
i\in[a]
where
q
is Neveu-Schwarz
with respect to
S_{i}
, then the lifting alternates at precisely one of
the half-nodes of
q
.

We call the lifting the
grading
.

(2)

A
connected graded
W
-spin surface
is a connected pre-graded

W
-spin surface such that in addition
there is no boundary marking
p
with
\text{tw}(p)={\text{alt}}(p)=\vec{0}
.
For
T\subseteq[a]
denote by
k_{T}
the number of boundary points
p
with

(\text{tw}_{i}(p),{\text{alt}}_{i}(p))=(r_{i}-2,1)\Leftrightarrow i\in T.

k_{\{j\}}
is also denoted by
k_{j}
.

(3)

A boundary marked point
p
of a graded connected
W
-spin surface is
singly twisted
if there exists an
i\in[a]
such that

\text{tw}_{j}(p)=(r_{j}-2)\delta_{ij}
. In this case
p
is alternating only for the
i^{th}
bundle.

(4)

A
rooted connected
W
-spin surface
is a graded connected
W
-spin
surface such that all boundary marked points but one are singly twisted, and the
remaining point, called the
root
, is
fully twisted
, i.e.,
\text{tw}(p)=(r_{1}-2,\ldots,r_{a}-2)
.

Remark 1.30
.

The imposition of the twists and alternation dictated in Definition
1.29
is required to define open
W
-spin invariants in the way we do below.
The existence of a lifting was used in
[
5
]
to define canonical relative orientation for the Witten bundle. It was used further in
[
6
]
to define the notion of
positivity
for boundary conditions of the Witten bundle,
which is a crucial condition in their open
r
-spin construction (and our generalization), see §
3.1.2
. It is nontrivial to ensure that multisections which satisfy the positivity boundary conditions even exist. For this one needs in the
r
-spin case the boundary twists to be
r-2
, see
[
6
, Proposition 3.20]
.

Moreover, by ensuring positivity, the graphs that will be important to define open
W
-spin intersection numbers will be precisely the pre-graded ones and their degenerations. The boundary strata of the moduli space corresponding to
relevant
graphs (see Definition
1.57
) will correspond to pre-graded open
W
-spin surfaces, after normalization. Here, we will be able to forget the (untwisted) boundary markings
p
with
\text{tw}(p)={\text{alt}}(p)=\vec{0}
, and build boundary conditions recursively from lower-dimensional graded
W
-spin surfaces, whose boundary twists are again as above. This is used to establish open topological recursion
[
6
, Theorem 4.1]
, which can be thought of as an open
r
-spin, descendent, analog of Solomon’s Open WDVV
[
48
]
.

Algebraically, the enumerative results from imposing the choice of boundary twist being
r-2
and the modulo 2 condition fit naturally into other enumerative structures, including the nonzero coefficients of the Gelfand-Dickey wave function
[
6
]
or of the perturbation of the mirror superpotential
[
19
]
.

Recent work
[
49
,
50
]
constructs a new collection of open
r
-spin theories which still require a lifting, but allow more general boundary twists. It is not yet known how these theories are related to mirror symmetry.

Remark 1.31
.

The grading at a contracted boundary, defined in Definition
1.21
, is a limit case of the grading in Definition
1.29
that is obtained when there are no boundary markings or nodes and the boundary itself contracts.

Remark 1.32
.

In order to obtain compact moduli spaces of connected
pre-graded open
W
-spin surfaces, we need to know that
given a degeneration of a smooth connected pre-graded

W
-spin surface, condition (1)(b) in the above definition
holds for the degenerate surface.
When
r
is odd, it follows immediately from (
1.15
)
and Proposition
1.25
, (3) that
condition (1)(b) holds for any compatible

W
-spin surface with boundary.
For
r
even, a priori we have that
(2\sum_{i}a_{i}+\sum_{j}b_{j}+2)/r\equiv|D|\pmod{2}
as in Proposition
1.25
, (4).
When we degenerate, we obtain twists
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
at a newly
acquired boundary node. If we normalize at the node, the corresponding
half-nodes lie in different
connected components of the normalization. Then the internal and boundary marked points are partitioned into the two different components. Thus we have
partitions
I=I_{1}\sqcup I_{2}
and
B=B_{1}\sqcup B_{2}
. We then have

\displaystyle\frac{2\sum_{i\in I_{1}}a_{i}+\sum_{j\in B_{1}}b_{j}+t_{1}+2}{r}+\frac{2\sum_{i\in I_{2}}a_{i}+\sum_{j\in B_{2}}b_{j}+t_{2}+2}{r}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{2\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}+\sum_{j\in B}b_{j}+2+t_{1}+t_{2}+2}{r}\equiv|D|+1\pmod{2},

as
t_{1}+t_{2}\equiv r-2\pmod{2}
.
Thus, if the two half-nodes are either both non-alternating or both alternating,
we will obtain a contradiction.
Hence one must be alternating and the other non-alternating.

In the disconnected case, we define:

Definition 1.33
.

A
pre-graded genus
0

W
-spin surface
is the disjoint union of several closed graded and open connected pre-graded
W
-spin surfaces. It is
graded/open/smooth/rooted
if all components are.

Remark 1.34
.

In
[
45
]
the case of graded
2
-spin surfaces is considered. There, another equivalent definition of open graded surfaces is given purely in terms of the half
\Sigma,
considered as an
orbifold surface with corners
. This definition is shown to be equivalent to the definition in terms of the doubled surface
C
. We shall not take this path, but we should note that the definition in the language of orbifold surfaces with corners can be generalized to the general
r
-spin, or even
W
-spin case.

By combining Observation
1.19
and Proposition
1.25
, we obtain the following.

Observation 1.35
.

Suppose given a smooth pre-graded
W
-spin genus
0
marked orbifold Riemann
surface with boundary with:

•

internal marked points
z_{1},\ldots,z_{l}
with
twist

\vec{a}_{j}=(a_{j,1},\ldots,a_{j,a}),~j\in[l]
;

•

the numbers
k_{T},~T\subseteq[a]
governing the twists of the
boundary points.

Then the following congruence conditions are satisfied:

(1.21)

e_{i}:=\frac{2\sum_{j}a_{j,i}+(-1+\sum_{T:i\in T}k_{T})(r_{i}-2)}{r_{i}}\in\mathbb{Z},~~i=1,\ldots,a,

(1.22)

e_{i}\equiv-1+\sum_{T:i\in T}k_{T}~(\text{mod}~2).

Here (
1.22
) follows from (
1.21
) if

r_{i}
is odd and from (
1.20
) if
r_{i}
is even.

We now define an equivalence relation on graded genus
0

W
-spin surfaces
which will simplify some definitions and computations in what follows.

Definition 1.36
.

Let
C
be a graded genus
0

W
-spin surface.
Suppose
C_{1},\ldots,C_{m}
are a collection of irreducible components of
C

such that each
C_{i}
is a disk with three special points, namely a boundary
half-node and two boundary marked points
x_{i},x^{\prime}_{i}
, with

\text{tw}_{k}(x_{i})=(r_{k}-2)\mathbf{1}_{k\in T_{i}},~{\text{alt}}_{k}(x_{i})=\mathbf{1}_{k\in T_{i}}\quad\text{tw}_{k}(x^{\prime}_{i})=(r_{k}-2)\mathbf{1}_{k\in T^{\prime}_{i}},~{\text{alt}}_{k}(x^{\prime}_{i})=\mathbf{1}_{k\in T^{\prime}_{i}},

for some arbitrary disjoint sets
T_{i},T^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq[a]
. We obtain a new
graded
W
-spin curve
C^{\prime}
by replacing

C_{i}
with the disk
C_{i}^{\prime}
which simply transposes
x_{i}
and
x_{i}^{\prime}

in the cyclic order (see Figure
4
). In this case, we write
C{\sim_{\textup{Exchange}}}C^{\prime}
, and this
is an equivalence relation on graded genus
0

W
-spin disks.

Intuitively, equivalence classes of this relation can be thought of as graded
genus
0

W
-spin surfaces
whose representatives share pairs of boundary points which have collided.
Equivalently,
C^{\prime}
is being obtained from
C
by replacing
each
C_{i}
with its complex conjugate, thus reversing the boundary orientation.
We then use Proposition
1.25
to possibly modify
the involution on the spin bundles to obtain a grading.

n_{\sigma_{1}h}

with
C_{1}

.
Two graded genus 0

-spin surfaces
C
and

C{\sim_{\textup{Exchange}}}C^{\prime}
. Only the fundamental domains

and

are drawn.

1.3.
Graded and rooted graphs

Here we consider the combinatorial data which will be used to keep track of
boundary strata of moduli spaces of graded
W
-spin disks.
Of course, there is a standard dual graph construction associated
to a stable curve with marked points. In our case, we have
more structure which we encode
in decorated dual graphs. Our graphs generalize
the
r
-spin graphs of
[
5
]
, with one difference: our graphs will also carry a collection of cyclic orders of boundary tails.

Definition 1.37
.

A
genus zero, anchored, pre-stable dual graph
is a tuple

\Gamma=(V,H,\sigma_{0},\sim,\hat{\Pi},H^{CB},T^{\mathrm{anch}},m),

in which

(i)

V
is a finite set (the
vertices
) equipped with a decomposition
V=V^{O}\sqcup V^{C}
into
open
and
closed vertices
;

(ii)

H
is a finite set (the
half-edges
) equipped with a decomposition
H=H^{B}\cup H^{I}
into
boundary
and
internal half-edges
;

(iii)

\sigma_{0}:H\rightarrow V
is a function, viewed as associating to each half-edge the vertex from which it emanates;

(iv)

\sim
is an equivalence relation on
H
, which decomposes as a pair of equivalence relations
\sim_{B}
on
H^{B}
and
\sim_{I}
on
H^{I}
. The equivalence classes are required to be of size
1
or
2
, and those of size
1
are referred to as
tails
. We denote by
T^{B}\subseteq H^{B}
and
T^{I}\subseteq H^{I}
the sets of equivalence classes of size
1
in
H^{B}
and
H^{I}
, respectively;

(v)

\hat{\Pi}=(\hat{\Pi}_{v})_{v\in V^{O}},
where
\hat{\Pi}_{v}
is a collection of cyclic orders on
\sigma_{0}^{-1}(v)\cap H^{B}
.

(vi)

H^{CB}\subseteq T^{\mathrm{anch}}\subseteq T^{I}
. Here
H^{CB}
is the set of
contracted boundary tails
and
T^{\mathrm{anch}}
is the set of
anchors
;

(vii)

m
is a function given by

m=m^{B}\sqcup m^{I}:T^{B}\sqcup(T^{I}\setminus H^{CB})\rightarrow\Omega,

where
m^{B}
and
m^{I}
(the
boundary
and
internal markings
) satisfy the definition of a marking when restricted to any connected component of
\Gamma
.

Note that
(V,H,\sigma_{0},\sim)
defines a graph, and that we do not require this graph to be connected; denote its connected components by
\{\Gamma_{i}\}
. We require the above data to satisfy the following conditions:

(1)

For each boundary half-edge
h\in H^{B}
, we have
\sigma_{0}(h)\in V^{O}
;

(2)

For each
\Gamma_{i}
, the obvious topological realisation of the
graph
\Gamma_{i}
has first Betti number
0
;

(3)

Each
open component

\Gamma_{i}
(a component which has open vertices) has no half-edge in
T^{\mathrm{anch}}
. Any
closed component
(
V^{O}(\Gamma_{i})=\emptyset
) contains exactly one half-edge in
T^{\mathrm{anch}}
.

(4)

For each
\Gamma_{i}
, the sub-graph formed by its open vertices (if any exist) and their incident boundary edges is connected.

Example 1.38
.

In Figure
5
, we provide a graded
W
-spin graph and its corresponding dual graph
\Gamma
. Here,
V^{O}=\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
,
V^{C}=\{v_{3}\}
,
H^{B}
contains all the
x_{i}
and the two half-edges corresponding to the edge
q_{2}
, and
H^{I}
contains all the half-edges
z_{i}
and the two half-edges corresponding to the edge
q_{1}
.

-spin disk

.

-spin disk and its corresponding dual graph
\Gamma

Notation 1.39
.

(1) We refer to elements of
T^{B}
as
boundary tails
and elements of
T^{I}\setminus H^{CB}
as
internal tails
, and we denote

T:=T^{I}\sqcup T^{B}.

Note that
\sim
induces a fixed-point-free involution on
H\setminus T
, which we denote by
\sigma_{1}
. We denote

E^{B}:=(H^{B}\setminus T^{B})/\sim_{B},~~E^{I}:=(H^{I}\setminus T^{I})/\sim_{I}

and refer to these as
boundary edges
and
internal edges
.
The set of
edges
is

E:=E^{B}\sqcup E^{I}.

Denote by
\sigma_{0}^{B}
the restriction of
\sigma_{0}
to
H^{B}
, and similarly for
\sigma_{0}^{I}
.

For each vertex
v
, set

k(v):=|(\sigma_{0}^{B})^{-1}(v)|,~~B(v)=m^{B}(T^{B}\cap(\sigma_{0}^{B})^{-1}(v)).

Analogously, set

l(v):=|(\sigma_{0}^{I})^{-1}(v)|,~~I(v)=m^{I}((T^{I}\setminus H^{CB})\cap(\sigma_{0}^{I})^{-1}(v)).

We say that an open vertex
v\in V^{O}
is
stable
if
k(v)+2l(v)>2
, and we say that a closed vertex
v\in V^{C}
is
stable
if
l(v)>2
. An open vertex is
partially stable
if
k(v)=0,l(v)=1
or
k(v)=2,l(v)=0
,
i.e.,
k(v)+2l(v)=2
.

(2) A genus zero pre-stable dual graph is said to be
connected
if it is connected as a graph, and we write
{{\textup{Conn}}}(\Gamma)
for the set of connected components of
\Gamma
. The graph is said to be
stable
(
partially stable
) if all of its vertices are stable (stable or partially stable), and it is said to be
closed
if
V^{O}=\emptyset
. The graph is
smooth
if there are no edges or contracted boundary tails. An
isomorphism
of genus
0
pre-stable dual graphs is a graph isomorphism which respects all additional structure. We denote by
\text{Aut}(\Gamma)
the group of automorphisms of
\Gamma
.

(3) When each set
\hat{\Pi}_{v}
is a singleton
\{\hat{\pi}_{v}\}
, we denote
\hat{\Pi},\hat{\Pi}_{v}
by
\hat{\pi},\hat{\pi}_{v},
respectively. When
\hat{\Pi}
is omitted from the notation we mean that for all
v\in V^{O},~\hat{\Pi}_{v}
consists of all possible cyclic orders of the boundary half edges of
v
.

Observe that
\hat{\Pi}
induces a set of cyclic orders of the boundary tails of each connected component
\Lambda
of
\Gamma
obtained as follows.
For each vertex
v
of
\Lambda
, choose
\hat{\pi}_{v}\in\hat{\Pi}_{v}
. We may then
follow the boundary tails of
\Lambda
in the unique cyclic way compatible with
\hat{\pi}_{v}
. We denote the set of all cyclic orders of the
boundary tails of
\Gamma
obtained in this way by
\hat{\Pi}_{\Gamma},
or,
in case of a singleton,
\hat{\pi}_{\Gamma}
. The data of
\hat{\Pi}_{\Gamma}
and
\hat{\Pi}
is equivalent and will be referred to as the cyclic orderings of
\Gamma
.

While pre-stable dual graphs encode the discrete data of an anchored orbifold Riemann surface with boundary, the following decorated version encodes the additional data of a graded
W
-spin structure. We require many properties
of the data, but they all arise from corresponding properties of
surfaces. For each condition, we will indicate the corresponding property of
surfaces to motivate the definition.

Definition 1.40
.

A
genus zero
W
-spin dual graph with a lifting
is a genus zero pre-stable anchored dual graph
\Gamma
as above, together with two
a
-tuples of maps,

\text{tw}=(\text{tw}_{i})_{i\in[a]},~~\text{tw}_{i}:H\rightarrow\{-1,0,1,\ldots,r_{i}-1\}

(the
twist
) and

{\text{alt}}=({\text{alt}}_{i})_{i\in[a]},~~{\text{alt}}_{i}:H^{B}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z},

satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

Any connected component of
\Gamma
is either: (1) stable, i.e., all
vertices are stable, or (2) consists of a single partially stable vertex.

(ii)

All tails
t
with
\text{tw}_{i}(t)=-1
for some
i
belong to
T^{\mathrm{anch}}
:
this arises from (
1.6
).
Recalling
H^{CB}\subseteq T^{\mathrm{anch}}
, any element of
T^{\mathrm{anch}}\setminus H^{CB}
is marked
\emptyset
and is the only tail marked
\emptyset
in its connected component: this condition arises from the behaviour of labeling of anchors under
normalization, Definition
1.17
.

(iii)

For any vertex
v
, there exists at most one incident half-edge
h
with
either
h\in T^{\mathrm{anch}}
or
\text{tw}_{i}(h)=-1
for some
i
. Note that the latter case only consists of half-edges that, after normalization, will become an anchor.

(iv)

For any contracted boundary tail
t\in H^{CB}
, we have
\text{tw}(t)=(r_{1}-1,\ldots,r_{a}-1)
. This condition arises from Remark
1.20
.

(v)

For any open vertex
v\in V^{O},~i\in[a]
,

2\sum_{h\in(\sigma_{0}^{I})^{-1}(v)}\text{tw}_{i}(h)+\sum_{h\in(\sigma_{0}^{B})^{-1}(v)}\text{tw}_{i}(h)\equiv r_{i}-2\mod r_{i}

and

\frac{2\sum_{h\in(\sigma_{0}^{I})^{-1}(v)}\ \text{tw}_{i}(h)+\sum_{h\in(\sigma_{0}^{B})^{-1}(v)}\text{tw}_{i}(h)+2}{r_{i}}\equiv\sum_{h\in(\sigma_{0}^{B})^{-1}(v)}{\text{alt}}_{i}(h)\mod 2.

These conditions arise from (
1.17
) and
Proposition
1.25
, (3), (4) respectively.

(vi)

For any closed vertex
v\in V^{C},~i\in[a]
,

\sum_{h\in\sigma_{0}^{-1}(v)}\text{tw}_{i}(h)\equiv r_{i}-2\mod r_{i}.

This condition arises from (
1.14
).

(vii)

(a)

For any half-edge
h\in H\setminus T,~i\in[a]
, we have

\text{tw}_{i}(h)+\text{tw}_{i}(\sigma_{1}(h))\equiv r_{i}-2\mod r_{i},

and
at most one of
\text{tw}_{i}(h)
and
\text{tw}_{i}(\sigma_{1}(h))
equals
-1
.
This condition arises from (
1.15
) and (
1.10
).

(b)

No boundary half-edge
h
satisfies
\text{tw}_{i}(h)=-1
, as all anchors
are internal.

(c)

In case
h\in H^{I}\setminus T^{I}
satisfies
\text{tw}_{i}(h)\equiv-1\mod~r_{i},

then
\text{tw}_{i}(h)=r_{i}-1
precisely when the corresponding half-node does not become an anchor in the partial normalisation following the rules given in Definition
1.17
. More precisely, in terms of graphs, this can determined algorithmically as follows.
Remove the edge of
\Gamma
determined by
h
and replace it
with two tails,
h
and
\sigma(h)
, to obtain a graph

\Gamma^{\prime}
with one more connected component than
\Gamma
.
5
5

5

This will be called the
detaching of
\Gamma
at
e
: see
Definition
1.52
.
Note that
one of these connected components can be viewed as containing

h
. Then either (a)

h
belongs to a connected component of
\Gamma^{\prime}
containing an anchor of
\Gamma
,
or (b)
h

belongs to a connected connected component of
\Gamma^{\prime}
containing
an open vertex.

(viii)

For any boundary half-edge
h\in H^{B}\setminus T^{B}
, if
\text{tw}_{i}(h)\neq r_{i}-1
we have

{\text{alt}}_{i}(h)+{\text{alt}}_{i}(\sigma_{1}(h))=1

and if
\text{tw}_{i}(h)=r_{i}-1
we have

{\text{alt}}_{i}(h)={\text{alt}}_{i}(\sigma_{1}(h))=0.

The first condition arises from Definition
1.29
(1)(b), and the
second from Proposition
1.25
(2) and (3). Note that this proposition also implies that the second case only occurs for odd
r
.

(ix)

If
r_{i}
is odd, then for any
h\in H^{B}
,

{\text{alt}}_{i}(h)\equiv\text{tw}_{i}(h)\mod 2,

and if
r_{i}
is even, then for any
h\in H^{B}
,

2|\text{tw}_{i}(h).

These conditions arise from Proposition
1.25
,
(3) and (2) respectively.

We define
\text{for}_{\text{spin}}(\Gamma)
to be the dual graph obtained from
\Gamma
by forgetting the additional data
\text{tw},{\text{alt}}
. We similarly define
\text{for}_{\text{spin}\neq i}(\Gamma),
to be the
W_{i}
-dual graph with a lifting obtained by forgetting all
\text{tw}_{j},{\text{alt}}_{j}
for
j\neq i,
where
W_{i}=x_{i}^{r_{i}}
.

A genus
0

W
-spin surface with boundary and lifting
\Sigma
induces
a genus
0

W
-spin dual graph with a lifting, denoted by
\Gamma(\Sigma)
,
in the standard way, see Figure
6
.

In what follows we shall be interested only in pre-graded, graded and
rooted graphs
analogous to Definition
1.29
,
which we now define. More general graphs with a lifting will appear only
as boundary strata of graded graphs.

Definition 1.41
.

(1)

A
pre-graded
W
-spin graph
is a genus zero
W
-spin dual graph with a lifting, with the additional requirement that

(a)

For any boundary marking
p,
and any
i\in[a]
, either

\text{tw}_{i}(p)=r_{i}-2,{\text{alt}}_{i}(p)=1,~~\text{or }\text{tw}_{i}(p)={\text{alt}}_{i}(p)=0.

(b)

If
e
is a boundary edge with half-edges
h,h^{\prime}
and
i\in[a]
with

\text{tw}_{i}(h)\in\{0,\ldots,r_{i}-2\}
,
then the lifting alternates at precisely one of
h,h^{\prime}
.

(2)

A
graded
W
-spin graph
is a pre-graded
W
-spin graph,
such that in addition
there is no boundary tail
t
with
\text{tw}(t)={\text{alt}}(t)=\vec{0}
.

(3)

A
rooted
W
-spin graph
is a graded
W
-spin graph
such that for every
open connected component all boundary tails are singly twisted,
i.e., there exists an
i\in[a]
such that
\text{tw}_{j}(p)=(r_{j}-2)\delta_{ij}
,
except for one which has twist
(r_{1}-2,\ldots,r_{a}-2)
.

Definition 1.42
.

Let
\Gamma
be a genus zero
W
-spin dual graph with a lifting.

(1)

Boundary half-edges
h
with
{\text{alt}}_{i}(h)=1
are called
alternating for the
i^{th}
bundle
, and those with
{\text{alt}}_{i}(h)=0
are called
non-alternating for the
i^{th}
bundle
.

(2)

Half-edges
h
with
\text{tw}_{i}(h)\in\{-1,r_{i}-1\}
are called
Ramond for the
i^{th}
bundle
, and those with
\text{tw}_{i}(h)\in\{0,\ldots,r_{i}-2\}
are called
Neveu–Schwarz for the
i^{th}
bundle
. An edge is Ramond or
Neveu–Schwarz with respect to the
i^{th}
bundle if its half-edges
are.
6
6

6

We note that if a half-edge is Ramond for some

i\in[a]
, it is standard in the literature to refer to this as a

broad half-edge
, and otherwise it is called
narrow
. The
same terminology holds for edges.

(3)

A half-edge
h
is
singly twisted
if there exists an
i\in[a]
such that

\text{tw}_{j}(h)=(r_{j}-2)\delta_{ij}
for all
j\in[a]
.

Definition 1.43
.

We say that a genus zero pre-graded
W
-spin graph is
stable
if the underlying dual graph is stable, in the sense specified above. An
isomorphism
between pre-graded
W
-spin dual graphs with liftings consists of an isomorphism as dual graphs that respects
\text{tw},~{\text{alt}}
.

In the special case
W=x^{r}
we call the (pre-)graded
W
-spin graph a
(pre-)graded
r
-spin graph
.

Notation 1.44
.

When
\Gamma=\Gamma(\Sigma),
we write
n_{h}=n_{h}(\Sigma)
for the half-node in the normalization of
\Sigma
that corresponds to the half-edge
h\in(H\setminus T)\cup H^{CB}
. We write
n_{e}
for the node in
\Sigma
that corresponds to the edge
e
. When
h
is an internal half-edge or a contracted boundary tail, we sometimes write
z_{h}
instead of
n_{h}
. When
h
is a boundary half-edge, we sometimes write
x_{h}
instead of
n_{h}
.

We end this subsection by providing notation for some special graphs that will appear throughout the remainder of the paper.

Notation 1.45
.

We denote by
\Gamma_{0,B,I}
the genus zero, anchored, pre-stable dual graph

\Gamma_{0,B,I}=(V,H,\sigma_{0},\sim,\hat{\Pi},H^{CB},T^{\mathrm{anch}},m)

where

•

V
consists of a single open vertex
v
;

•

H
is a finite set of half-edges
H=B\sqcup I
, where
H^{B}=B
are the boundary half-edges and
H^{I}=I
are the interior half-edges;

•

\sigma_{0}:H\to V
is the constant map to
v
;

•

\sim
is the trivial equivalence relation;

•

\hat{\Pi}=\hat{\Pi}_{v}
is the collection of all possible cyclic orders of the set of boundary half-edges
B
;

•

H^{CB}=T^{\mathrm{anch}}=\emptyset
;

•

the marking function
m=m^{B}\sqcup m^{I}:B\sqcup I\to\Omega
is defined in such a way so that
m^{B}
is the constant map mapping all elements to
\emptyset\in\Omega
and
m^{I}
is a bijection with
\{1,\ldots,|I|\}\subset\Omega
.

Note that all half-edges in
\Gamma_{0,B,I}
are also tails. We write
\Gamma_{0,B,I}^{\text{labeled}}
for the same graph, except we replace
m^{B}
with a marking function
m^{B,\text{labeled}}
that is a bijection with
\{1,\ldots,|B|\}\subset\Omega
.
In the special case where
B=[k]
we write
\Gamma_{0,k,I}
. If we further have that
I=[l]
, we write
\Gamma_{0,k,l}
. In the labeled version, we write
\Gamma_{0,k,I}^{\text{labeled}}
and
\Gamma_{0,k,l}^{\text{labeled}}
for the graphs with the same alteration of the marking function.
7
7

7

Frequently we want to choose
some master index set
I\subseteq\Omega
, typically
I=[l]
, and then
consider graphs whose internal tails are marked by subsets
J\subseteq I
.
The choice of index set is chosen depending on what marking is natural in the context.

We also write

\Gamma_{0,\{B_{J}\}_{J\subseteq{[a]}},\{\vec{a}_{j}\}_{j\in[l]}}^{W}
for the (smooth, connected) pre-graded
W
-spin graph given by taking
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0,B,I}
where
B=\sqcup_{J\subseteq[a]}B_{J}
and
I=[l]
with the
a
-tuples of maps
tw
and
alt
defined as follows. For the internal tails, enumerated from
1
to
l
, the
j
th internal tail point has twist
\vec{a}_{j}
. For the set of unlabeled boundary tails
t_{j}
in the set
B_{J}
for some
J\subseteq[a]
, we have that

(\text{tw}_{i}(t_{j}),{\text{alt}}_{i}(t_{j}))=\begin{cases}(r_{i}-2,1)&\text{ if $i\in J$}\\
(0,0)&\text{ if $i\notin J$.}\end{cases}

We write
\Gamma_{0,\{B_{J}\}_{J\subseteq{[a]}},\{\vec{a}_{j}\}_{j\in[l]}}^{W,\text{labeled}}
for the labeled analogue with the aforementioned change to the marking function. We write
\Gamma_{0,\{k_{J}\}_{J\subseteq{[a]}},\{\vec{a}_{j}\}_{j\in[l]}}^{W}
for the case
B_{J}=[k_{J}]
, noting that
B
is the disjoint union of the sets
B_{J}
.

Note that when
B_{\emptyset}=\emptyset
, the graph is graded. In this case, we omit
B_{\emptyset}
from the notations.

Example 1.46
.

The dual graph to the smooth connected marked genus 0 Riemann surface with
k
boundary markings and
l
internal markings is
\Gamma_{0,k,l}
.

Definition 1.47
.

Let
\Gamma=(V,H,\sigma_{0},\sim,\hat{\Pi},H^{CB},T^{\mathrm{anch}},m)
be a genus zero, anchored, pre-stable dual graph. Take another genus zero, anchored, pre-stable dual graph
\Lambda
. We say that
\Lambda\equiv\Gamma

modulo cyclic orders
if
\Lambda
can be written in the form

\Lambda=(V,H,\sigma_{0},\sim,\hat{\Pi}_{\Lambda},H^{CB},T^{\mathrm{anch}},m)

for some new set of cyclic orders
\hat{\Pi}_{\Lambda}
. If, moreover, we have the inclusion
\hat{\Pi}_{\Lambda}\subset\hat{\Pi}
then we say
\Lambda
is a
subordinate graph
of
\Gamma
and denote this relation by
\Lambda\prec\Gamma
.

Remark 1.48
.

Geometrically, the relation
\Lambda\prec\Gamma
will later mean that the moduli space of disks corresponding to
\Lambda
consists of certain connected components of the moduli space of disks corresponding to
\Gamma
.

1.4.
Graphs associated to non-smooth graded disks

Several graph operations play a role in what follows.

Definition 1.49
.

Let
\Gamma
be a pre-graded
W
-spin graph.

(1)

Suppose
\Gamma
has an edge
e
connecting vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. The
smoothing
of
\Gamma
along
e
is the graph
{\text{sm}}_{e}\,\Gamma

obtained by contracting
e
and replacing the two vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
with a single vertex
v_{12}
. This vertex is closed if and only if both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are closed. If
v_{1},v_{2}
are both open,
we obtain a collection of cyclic orderings for
v_{12}
as follows.
Take any
\hat{\pi}_{v_{1}}\in\hat{\Pi}_{v_{1}},\hat{\pi}_{v_{2}}\in\hat{\Pi}_{v_{2}}
. Let
e=\{h_{1},h_{2}\}
where
h_{i}
is attached to
v_{i}
. Construct the ordering induced by the cyclic ordering
\hat{\pi}_{v_{2}}\big|_{\sigma_{0}^{-1}(v_{2})\cap H^{B}\setminus\{h_{2}\}}
, obtained by starting at the half-edge that directly follows
h_{2}
. When one reaches
h_{2}
again, we now concatenate with the analogous ordering induced from the cyclic ordering
\hat{\pi}_{v_{1}}\big|_{\sigma_{0}^{-1}(v_{1})\cap H^{B}\setminus\{h_{1}\}}
. The set
\hat{\Pi}_{v_{12}}
is the
collection of cyclic orders that can be obtained this way.

(2)

Suppose
\Gamma
has a contracted boundary tail
h\in H^{CB}
.
The
smoothing
of
\Gamma
along
h

is the graph
{\text{sm}}_{h}\Gamma
obtained by erasing
h
and moving the vertex
v=\sigma_{0}^{-1}(h)
from
V^{C}
to
V^{O}
.

(3)

For a set
F
of edges and contracted boundary tails, one can perform a sequence of smoothings, and the graph obtained is independent of the order in which those smoothings are performed; denote the result by
{\text{sm}}_{F}\Gamma
. Write also
{\text{sm}}\,\Gamma
for the smooth graph
{\text{sm}}_{E(\Gamma)\cup H^{CB}(\Gamma)}\Gamma
which we will call the
smoothing of
\Gamma
.

(4)

We say a pre-graded
W
-spin graph
\Lambda
is a
degeneration
of
\Gamma
if both of the following hold:

(a)

{\text{sm}}_{F}\Lambda\prec\Gamma
for some set
F
of edges and contracted boundary tails of
\Lambda
.

(b)

If
\Lambda\prec\Lambda^{\prime}
and
{\text{sm}}_{F}\Lambda^{\prime}\prec\Gamma
, then
\Lambda=\Lambda^{\prime}
, i.e.,
\Lambda
is maximal among orderings satisfying
(a).

Example 1.50
.

In Figure
6
, we take the smoothing at one of the edges
e_{q_{1}}
in the dual graph
\Gamma
in Figure
5
. This graded
W
-spin disk corresponding to
{\text{sm}}_{e_{q_{1}}}\Gamma
has two open irreducible components. We shall see in the next section that the moduli of graded
W
-spin disks with dual graph
\Gamma
will be a boundary component for the moduli space of graded
W
-spin disks with dual graph
{\text{sm}}_{e_{q_{1}}}\Gamma
. For this reason, we call
\Gamma
a degeneration of
{\text{sm}}_{e_{q_{1}}}\Gamma
.

Definition 1.51
.

We set

(1.23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial^{!}\Gamma&=\{\Lambda\;|\;\Lambda\text{ is a degeneration of $\Gamma$}\},\\
\partial\Gamma&=\partial^{!}\Gamma\setminus\{\Gamma\}.\end{split}

Here
\partial^{!}\Gamma
is the set of all graphs that are degenerations of
\Gamma
and
\partial\Gamma
is the set of all graphs that are non-trivial degenerations of
\Gamma
.

Definition 1.52
.

Let
\Gamma
be a genus zero pre-graded
W
-spin graph, and let
e
be an edge of
\Gamma
or a contracted boundary tail. Then the
detaching
of
\Gamma
at
e
is the graph
\textup{detach}_{e}(\Gamma)=\Gamma(\Sigma_{n})
where

\Sigma
is any pre-graded
W
-spin disk with
\Gamma=\Gamma(\Sigma)

and
\Sigma_{n}
is the partial normalization of
\Sigma
at the node
n

corresponding to
e
.
8
8

8

The detaching operation can also be stated purely in terms of graphs, in the expected way. For example, when
e
is an edge, the graph
\textup{detach}_{e}(\Gamma)
is obtained from
\Gamma
by cutting
e
into the two half edges which form it, and then updating both
\sim
and the marking data. Note that the half-edges that were originally in the edge
e
are now tails.

There is a canonical identification of
E(\Gamma)\setminus\{e\}
with the edges of
\textup{detach}_{e}(\Gamma)
when
e
is an edge of
\Gamma
.
Similarly there is a canonical identification of
E(\Gamma)
with the edges of
\textup{detach}_{t}(\Gamma)
when
t
is a contracted boundary tail. As the set of boundary half-edges incident to any vertex
v
remains unchanged, the detaching does not change the set
\hat{\Pi}
of cyclic orderings. One can also iterate this detaching process. For any subset
N\subset E(\Gamma)\cup H^{CB}(\Gamma)
, we denote by
\textup{detach}_{N}(\Gamma)
the graph obtained by performing
\textup{detach}_{f}
for each element
f\in N
; the result is independent of the order in which the detachings are performed. When we write
\textup{detach}(\Gamma)
without any subscript, we mean
\textup{detach}_{E(\Gamma)}(\Gamma)
.

Definition 1.53
.

Let
\Gamma
be a genus zero pre-graded
W
-spin graph with a lifting.
A boundary half-edge
h
with
{\text{alt}}_{i}(h)=0,\text{tw}_{i}(h)>0
for
i\in[a]
will be called
positive for the
i^{th}
grading
. A boundary half-edge which is positive for one of the gradings is called
positive
. Write
{H^{+}}_{i}(\Gamma)
for the set of half-edges
h
of the graph
\Gamma
such that either
h
or
\sigma_{1}(h)
is positive for the
i^{th}
grading. We set

{H^{+}}(\Gamma)=\bigcup_{i\in[a]}{H^{+}}_{i}(\Gamma).

A half-node in a stable pre-graded
W
-spin surface with a lifting which corresponds to a positive half-edge will be called positive as well.

Definition 1.54
.

Let
\Gamma
be a pre-graded
W
-spin graph. Let
\partial^{+}\Gamma
be the subset of
\partial^{!}\Gamma
with at least one positive half-edge.
Write
\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
for the subset of
\partial\Gamma\setminus\partial^{+}\Gamma
made of graphs
\Lambda
such that
\Lambda
has at least one boundary half-edge
h
with the following property. Let
\Xi
be the subgraph of
\textup{detach}_{\mathrm{edge}(h)}\Lambda

which contains
h
. Then
\Xi
is the graph with precisely one vertex and three boundary tails
t
,
t^{\prime}
, and
h
, such that

\text{tw}_{k}(t)=(r_{k}-2)\mathbf{1}_{k\in I},\quad\text{tw}_{k}(t^{\prime})=(r_{k}-2)\mathbf{1}_{k\in J},~~\text{tw}_{k}(h)=(r_{k}-2)\mathbf{1}_{k\notin I\cup J},

for some arbitrary disjoint sets
I,J\subseteq[a]
. We call such graphs
exchangeable
, and if
v\in V^{O}
is the vertex which after detaching forms the graph
\Xi,
then
v
is called an
exchangeable vertex
.

Define the equivalence relation
{\sim_{\textup{exchange}}}
on
\partial\Gamma\setminus\partial^{+}\Gamma
by saying that
\Lambda{\sim_{\textup{exchange}}}\Lambda^{\prime}
if
V^{O}(\Lambda)
contains exchangeable vertices
v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}
whose sets of cyclic orders are singletons,
\hat{\pi}_{v_{1}},\ldots,\hat{\pi}_{v_{m}}
, such that the graph obtained by reversing those cyclic orders is isomorphic to
\Lambda^{\prime}
. If
\Gamma
is an exchangeable graph and
v
an exchangeable vertex, we denote by
\textup{exchange}_{v}(\Gamma)
the graph obtained from
\Gamma
by replacing the cyclic orders in
\hat{\Pi}_{v}
with their opposite cyclic orders. If
\Gamma
has a single exchangeable vertex
v
we write
\textup{exchange}(\Gamma)
for
\textup{exchange}_{v}(\Gamma)
. This arises from Definition
1.36
.

Remark 1.55
.

Note that in rank
a=2
, a graph in
\partial\Gamma\setminus\partial^{+}\Gamma
will be exchangeable if there exist
h,\Xi,t,t^{\prime}
as in the previous definition such that either (i) one of
t,t^{\prime}
has
(\text{tw},{\text{alt}})=(\vec{0},\vec{0})
. or (ii) both are singly twisted and one of them has
(\text{tw}_{1},{\text{alt}}_{1})=(0,0)
and the other has
(\text{tw}_{2},{\text{alt}}_{2})=(0,0)
. Note that if the graph is graded,
case (i) does not occur.

Definition 1.56
.

Let
\partial^{0}\Gamma

be the subset of all graphs
\Lambda\in\partial\Gamma\setminus\left(\partial^{+}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma\right)
satisfying one of the following:

(1)

\Lambda
contains a contracted boundary half-edge.

(2)

\Lambda
contains a boundary edge.

The motivation for these definitions and the upcoming definition
of irrelevant graphs is as follows.
The reader may skip this upon first reading if they do not enjoy spoilers.
In Section
2
, we will define a moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}

compactifying the moduli space
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
parameterizing all open
W
-spin curves with dual graph
\Gamma
(Definition
2.6
).
Graphs
\Lambda\in\partial\Gamma
parameterize strata of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
.
At the same time,
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
carries the
Witten bundle

\mathcal{W}

arising from the spin structure, also defined in Section
2
.

The open FJRW invariants we define here will
be counts of zeroes of sections of
\mathcal{W}
satisfying certain boundary
conditions. How these conditions are treated depends on the type
of boundary stratum. Strata corresponding to
\Lambda\in\partial^{+}\Gamma

are simply removed from
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma},
giving a partial
compactification
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
.

On the other hand, the graphs of
\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma

precisely correspond to strata of
\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
, see
the discussion following Definition
3.2
. Among these graphs, boundary components labeled by graphs that are
{\sim_{\textup{exchange}}}
-equivalent but distinct will have similar
boundary counditions; however, an analysis of orientations will
show the contribution of such boundary strata to the zero count
cancels.
One can think of this as if we were gluing exchangeable boundaries of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
, and counting the number of zeroes in the glued space. This is indeed possible, and equivalent to the approach we take.

The
irrelevant
graphs
\Lambda
defined below
are called irrelevant as they are irrelevant to the wall-crossing and enumerative invariants in the following sense. We will
require (Definition
3.18
) that all sections of the
Witten bundle we consider satisfy a notion of positivity either on
or near the strata corresponding to irrelevant graphs.
Hence as we vary sections, zeroes
cannot escape off of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
through these boundary strata.
The sections of
\mathcal{W}
we consider must satisfy an inductive boundary
condition over the remaining strata indexed by elements of
\partial^{0}\Gamma
which
are relevant. This is encoded in the notion of a
family of canonical multisections, see Definition
3.22
.

Definition 1.57
.

Let
\Gamma
be a pre-graded
W
-spin graph. It is
irrelevant

if one of the following holds:

(1)

H^{CB}(\Gamma)\neq\emptyset
;

(2)

\Gamma
has a positive boundary half-edge;

(3)

\Gamma
has a vertex
v
such that there exists an
i\in[a]
such that, for any boundary half-edge
h
attached to the vertex
v
, one has that

\text{tw}_{i}(h)={\text{alt}}_{i}(h)=0.

Otherwise the graph is
relevant
.

t_{x_{2}}
t_{x_{1}}
t_{z_{1}}
t_{x_{3}}
t_{z_{2}}
t_{z_{3}}
v

(a)

The
W
-spin graph
\Gamma

e_{q}
t_{x_{2}}
t_{x_{1}}
t_{z_{1}}
t_{x_{3}}
t_{z_{2}}
t_{z_{3}}
v_{1}
v_{2}

(b)

Exchangeable graph
\Lambda_{1}\in\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma

e_{q}
t_{z_{2}}
t_{x_{3}}

t_{x_{2}}

v_{2}

\Lambda_{2}\in\partial^{+}\Gamma
