Example 2.38
.

Consider the LG model
(x^{4}+y^{5},\mu_{4}\times\mu_{5})
. We consider the smooth connected rooted graded
W
-spin graph
\Gamma_{1}
with 4
r
-tails and two internal tails
t_{z_{1}}
and
t_{z_{2}}
with twist vectors
(2,2)
and
(2,3)
. Then one can check that
\Gamma_{1}
is balanced for
\mathbf{d}=\mathbf{0}
. See Figure
9
(A), where
\times
denotes a root and
r
denotes a (boundary)
r
-tail.
Consider the boundary graph
\Lambda_{1}
as seen in Figure
9
(B). One can see that the vertex
v_{1}
is balanced and
v_{2}
is critical, so
\Lambda_{1}
is a critical boundary graph. Moreover, it is a top boundary for
\Gamma_{1}
.

Secondly, one can consider the smooth connected rooted graded
W
-spin graph
\Gamma_{2}
in Figure
9
(C) with 5
s
-tails and two internal tails
t_{z_{1}}
and
t_{z_{2}}
with twist vectors
(2,2)
and
(2,3)
. Then one can check that
\Gamma_{2}
is balanced for
\mathbf{d}=\mathbf{0}
. Moreover, the graph
\Lambda_{2}
as seen in Figure
9
(D) is top boundary for
\Gamma_{2}
in a similar way. One can notice that these are two instances where the same critical graph appears in two distinct critical boundaries.

Indeed, the vertex
v_{2}
has an
r
-point and an
s
-point. In
\Lambda_{1}
, the
s
-point is a boundary half-edge that corresponds to a half-node in the corresponding
W
-spin disk. Meanwhile the roles of the boundary special points reverse for
\Lambda_{2}
.

Proposition 2.39
.

Let
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
be a smooth, connected graph. Then:

(a)

k_{1}\equiv r(I)\pmod{r}
and
k_{2}\equiv s(I)\pmod{s}
if and only if the space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
is nonempty.

(b)

Assume that the space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
is nonempty. The equation
sk_{1}+rk_{2}=m(I,\mathbf{d})-rs
holds if and only if
\Gamma
is critical for
\mathbf{d}
.

Proof.

This proof follows the same computations as in Proposition
2.31
.
∎

Lastly, we can classify all balanced and critical graphs with a fixed set of internal markings
J\subseteq I
:

Notation 2.40
.

Suppose
\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})
. Using Notation
2.30
, we have that

\sum_{i\in J}a_{i}=r(J)+\ell_{1}r\text{ and }\sum_{i\in J}b_{i}=s(J)+\ell_{2}s

for some
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Since
\Gamma
is a smooth rooted graded graph
\Gamma
with
I(\Gamma)=J
and
balanced with respect to
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
, one can compute using Proposition
2.31
(b), that

(2.30)

k_{1}(\Gamma)=r(J)+pr\text{ and }k_{2}(\Gamma)=s(J)+(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-|J|+1+\sum_{j\in J}d_{j}-p)s,

for some
0\leq p\leq\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-|J|+1+\sum_{j\in J}d_{j}
.
Taking
N:=\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-|J|+1+\sum_{j\in J}d_{j}
, we can enumerate

{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})=\{\Gamma_{J,0},\ldots,\Gamma_{J,p},\ldots,\Gamma_{J,N}\},

where
\Gamma_{J,p}
is the unique balanced graph above that has internal markings
J
and
k_{1}(\Gamma_{J,p})=r(J)+pr
.

Similarly, suppose
\Lambda\in{\text{Crit}}(J,\mathbf{d})
. We can also compute using Proposition
2.39
that

(2.31)

k_{1}(\Gamma)=r(J)+(p-1)r+1\text{ and }k_{2}(\Gamma)=s(J)+(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-|J|-p+1+\sum_{j\in J}d_{j})s+1,

for some
1\leq p\leq N
. Enumerate the set of critical graphs

{\text{Crit}}(J,\mathbf{d})=\{\Lambda_{J,1},\ldots,\Lambda_{J,N}\},

with the same
N
as above, where
\Lambda_{J,p}
is the unique critical graph above with internal markings
J
and
k_{1}(\Lambda_{J,p})=r(J)+(p-1)r+1
.

2.5.
Orientation

In this subsection, we describe the canonical relative orientation of the Witten bundle
\mathcal{W}
over the various moduli spaces considered here, as well as the properties of these orientations. For that, we need some notation and background. We start by recalling the following.

Observation 2.41
.

If
0\to A\xrightarrow{f}B\to C\to 0
is an exact sequence of vector bundles, then there is a canonical isomorphism

(2.32)

\det(A)\otimes\det(C)\simeq\det(B)

given by

(a_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge a_{n})\otimes(c_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge c_{m})\longmapsto\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}f(a_{i})\wedge\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}c^{\prime}_{i},

where
\{a_{i}\}
are
n:=\dim(A)
elements of
A
,
\{c_{i}\}
are
m:=\dim(C)
elements of
C
, and
c^{\prime}_{i}
is an arbitrary element of
B
mapped to
c_{i}
. This isomorphism induces, by writing the elements of
C
first, an isomorphism

(2.33)

\det(C)\otimes\det(A)\simeq\det(B).

We will often use this observation in order to decompose orientations using a fibration structure. We provide a few examples now:

Example 2.42
.

Let
\Gamma\in\partial\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in[l]}}^{W,\textup{labeled}}

be a labeled
{(r,s)}
-graph consisting of two vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
connected by a single edge
e
. Let
N
be the normal bundle of the inclusion
\iota_{\Gamma}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}^{W}\hookrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in[l]}}^{W,\text{labeled}}
. We then have the isomorphism of line bundles using (
2.33
)

(2.34)

\iota_{\Gamma}^{*}\det(T{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in[l]}}^{W,\textup{labeled}})\cong\det(N)\otimes\det(T{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\textup{detach}_{e}(\Gamma)}),

where
\iota_{\Gamma}
is the inclusion map defined in Equation (
2.6
).

Consider a point in the moduli space corresponding to
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}\cup_{q}\Sigma_{2}
, with
\Sigma_{i}
corresponding to the vertex
v_{i}
. Let
T_{q}\Sigma_{i}
be the tangent line at the node
q
. The fiber of the normal line bundle
N
at
\Sigma
can be identified with the tensor product
T_{q}\Sigma_{1}\otimes T_{q}\Sigma_{2}
. These two tangent lines are canonically oriented complex lines when
e
is internal, and are orientable real lines invariant under complex conjugation when
e
is boundary.
In both cases,
N
carries a canonical orientation, where in the second case we use the convention of orienting
N
by the outward-pointing normal.

Example 2.43
.

Consider the forgetful map
\text{For}_{\varnothing,l+1}:{\mathcal{M}}_{0,k,l+1}^{\textup{labeled}}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{0,k,l}^{\textup{labeled}}
that forgets the
(l+1)^{\textup{st}}
internal marked point. Its fiber is a disk with
l
punctures, which carries a canonical (complex) orientation. For
k\geq 1
, we can also consider the forgetful map
\text{For}_{k+1,\varnothing}:{\mathcal{M}}_{0,k+1,l}^{\textup{labeled}}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{0,k,l}^{\textup{labeled}}
, which has a fiber that is a union of open intervals, canonically oriented as a boundary of an oriented disk. We will denote these canonical orientations by
o_{\text{For}_{\varnothing,l+1}^{-1}(\Sigma)}
and
o_{\text{For}_{k+1,\varnothing}^{-1}(\Sigma)}
respectively. Note that by Observation
2.41
this gives two potential decompositions of the orientation
{\mathcal{M}}_{0,k,l}^{\textup{labeled}}
via the two different forgetful maps.

2.5.1.
Background in rank one

In
[
5
]
, the authors constructed families of orientations for the moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,B,I}
of marked disks and the Witten bundles over the
r
-spin moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r,\text{labeled}}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}
. In this subsection, we will recall their results and the properties of these orientations.

We note that our orientations will depend on the cyclic ordering of the boundary markings, hence we will have to discuss each connected component of the moduli spaces individually. To do so, we introduce the following notation. Let
\Gamma
be a genus zero, anchored, pre-stable dual graph. This comes with the set
\hat{\Pi}
of cyclic orders. We denote by
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}^{\text{labeled},\hat{\pi}}
the connected component whose disks have cyclic order
\hat{\pi}\in\hat{\Pi}
. Similarly, if
\Gamma
is a (pre-)graded
W
-spin graph, then we will denote by
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}^{W,\text{labeled},\hat{\pi}}
the connected component associated to the cyclic order
\hat{\pi}
. Note that if we want to consider the unlabeled case, then there is a set of cyclic orderings that correspond to the same connected component. Here, we will continue to use the notation
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}^{\hat{\pi}}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}^{W,\hat{\pi}}
for the corresponding connected component (which may be represented by multiple
\hat{\pi}\in\hat{\Pi}
).

Crucially, the families of orientations also must extend over the boundary in a way that is compatible with the lower dimensional moduli spaces. One key case for this is when one has exactly one boundary edge. We will now give the notation for this key case.

Notation 2.44
.

Let
\Gamma
be a graph with two open vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, connected by an edge
e
. Suppose that each vertex
v_{i}
has internal tails
I_{i}
, boundary tails
B_{i}
, and a unique boundary half-edge
h_{i}
which is part of the edge
e
. Take
B=B_{1}\sqcup B_{2}
and
I=I_{1}\sqcup I_{2}
and assume that the marking functions
m^{I}
and
m^{B}
are injective.

We will also denote by
v_{i}
the graph
\Gamma_{v_{i}}
given by taking the subgraph of
\textup{detach}_{e}\Gamma
which is the connected component containing the vertex
v_{i}
. We write
v_{i}^{\prime}
for the graph
\text{For}_{h_{i}}(v_{i})
.

We can construct an ordering
\pi
on
B
from the orderings
\pi_{i}
on the two vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
in a unique way. Intuitively, this is done by starting at
h_{1}
and following the arrow in
Figure
10
(A). Algorithmically, it is done as follows.

For any
\Sigma\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}
, with normalization
\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}
(where
\Sigma_{i}
corresponds to
v_{i}
), we have an ordering
\hat{\pi}_{v_{i}}
on the boundary half-edges, induced by the natural orientation on
\partial\Sigma_{i}
. Using
\hat{\pi}_{v_{1}}
we can define an ordering
\pi_{1}
on the half-edges of
v_{1}
by starting after the half-edge
h_{1}
and following the natural orientation on
\partial\Sigma_{1}
, keeping
h_{1}
at the end of the ordering. Similarly, we denote by
\pi_{2}
the ordering on the half-edges of
v_{2}
given by starting at
h_{2}
and following the natural orientation. We can restrict the orderings
\pi_{i}
to the boundary tails by omitting the half-edges; in this case, we will still denote this ordering by
\pi_{i}
.

Lastly, we will denote by
\pi
the unique ordering of
B
such that, for any
\Sigma\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}
as above,

•

under
\pi
, the marked points of
\Sigma_{1}
appear before those of
\Sigma_{2}
;

•

when
\pi
is restricted to the marked points of
\Sigma_{i}
, it agrees with the ordering
\pi_{i}
on the boundary tails.

Denote by
\hat{\pi}
a cyclic ordering on
B
induced by
\pi
.

. The arrow in (A) corresponds to the unique ordering

We now define and then construct families of orientations
\{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi}_{0,B,I}\}
and
\{{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\}
on the moduli spaces
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,B,I}^{\text{labeled},\hat{\pi}}
and the Witten bundle over
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r,\text{labeled},\hat{\pi}}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}
, respectively.

Definition 2.45
.

(i) A
family of orientations

\{{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{0,B,I}^{\pi}\}
on the moduli spaces
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,B,I}^{\text{labeled},\hat{\pi}}
consists of the data of an orientation
{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{0,B,I}^{\pi}
on the moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,B,I}^{\text{labeled},\hat{\pi}}
for each tuple
(B,I,\pi,\hat{\pi})
where
|B|+2|I|\geq 3
. Here, each tuple
(B,I,\pi,\hat{\pi})
consists of:
B
a set of boundary marked points,
I
a set of internal marked points,
\hat{\pi}
a cyclic ordering of
B
, and
\pi
an ordering of
B
that induces
\hat{\pi}
.

(ii) A
family of orientations

\{{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\}
on the Witten bundles
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r,\text{labeled},\hat{\pi}}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}
over the moduli space of
r
-spin disks consists of the data of an orientation
{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}
on the Witten bundle over the moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r,\text{labeled},\hat{\pi}}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}
of
r
-spin disks, for each tuple
(B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I},\pi,\hat{\pi})
where
|B|+2|I|\geq 3
. Here, each tuple
(B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I},\pi,\hat{\pi})
consists of:
B
the set of boundary marked points,
\{a_{i}\}
the set of twists on the
|I|
internal marked points,
\hat{\pi}
a cyclic ordering of the boundary marked points, and
\pi
an ordering of
B
that induces
\hat{\pi}
.

Lemma 2.46
.

There exists a unique family of orientations
\{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi}_{0,B,I}\}

for the spaces
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\text{labeled},\hat{\pi}}_{0,B,I}
with the following properties:

(1)

In the zero-dimensional case where
k=l=1
, the orientation is positive, while when
k=3
and
l=0
, the orientations are negative.

(2)

If
f^{B}:B\to B^{\prime}
and
f^{I}:I\to I^{\prime}
are bijections and
F:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,B,I}^{\textup{labeled}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,B^{\prime},I^{\prime}}^{\textup{labeled}}
is the induced map, then
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}_{0,B,I}^{\pi}=F^{*}\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}_{0,B^{\prime},I^{\prime}}^{f^{B}\circ\pi}
.
In particular,
the action of any
g\in\text{Sym}(I)
preserves the orientation on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,B,I}^{\textup{labeled}}
.

(3)

Fix an integer
h
, and let
\pi
be an order of
B,~g\in\text{Sym}(B)

be such that
g
sends
x_{\pi(i)}
to
x_{\pi(i+h)}
cyclically. Then
g
preserves the orientation of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,B,I}^{\textup{labeled},\hat{\pi}}
if and only if
h(|B|-1)
is even.

(4)

The orientation
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi}_{0,k,l+1}
agrees with the orientation induced from
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi}_{0,k,l}
by the fibration
\text{For}_{\varnothing,l+1}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,k,l+1}^{\textup{labeled}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,k,l}^{\textup{labeled}}
and the complex orientation on the fiber. (See Example

2.43
for the forgetful map.)

(5)

On
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,k+1,l}^{\textup{labeled},(1\to 2\to\cdots\to k+1\to 1)}
, we have
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}_{0,k+1,l}^{(1\to 2\to\cdots\to k+1)}=o_{\text{For}_{k+1,\varnothing}^{-1}(\Sigma)}\otimes\text{For}_{k+1,\varnothing}^{*}\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}_{0,k,l}^{(1\to 2\to\cdots\to k)}
.

(6)

Let
\Gamma
be a graph with injective boundary marking, two open vertices,
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, connected by an edge
e
, as in Notation
2.44
.
We have

\det(T{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,B,I}^{\textup{labeled},\hat{\pi}})|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}=\det(N)\otimes\det(Tf_{h_{1}})\otimes(\det(T{\mathcal{M}}_{v^{\prime}_{1}})\boxtimes\det(T{\mathcal{M}}_{v_{2}})),

where
N
is the normal bundle and
Tf_{h_{1}}
is the tangent bundle of the fiber of the map which forgets the tail
h_{1}
(see Example
2.42
). If we write
o_{N}
for the canonical orientation of
N
and
o_{h_{1}}
for the canonical orientation of
Tf_{h_{1}}
described above, then

\iota_{\Gamma}^{*}\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi}=(-1)^{(|B_{1}|-1)|B_{2}|}o_{N}\otimes o_{h_{1}}\otimes(\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi_{1}}_{0,B_{1},I_{1}}\boxtimes\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi_{2}}_{0,\{h_{2}\}\cup B_{2},I_{2}}).

Moreover, using Item (
5
), this equation can also be written as

\iota_{\Gamma}^{*}\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi}=(-1)^{(|B_{1}|-1)|B_{2}|}o_{N}\otimes(\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi_{1}}_{0,B_{1}\cup\{h_{1}\},I_{1}}\boxtimes\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi_{2}}_{0,\{h_{2}\}\cup B_{2},I_{2}}).

(7)

Let
\Gamma
be a connected graph with injective boundary marking, two vertices, an open vertex
v^{o}
and a closed vertex
v^{c}
. We have

\det(T{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma})=\det(N)\otimes(\det(T{\mathcal{M}}_{v^{c}})\boxtimes\det(T{\mathcal{M}}_{v^{o}})),

where
N
is again the normal bundle. Then, for any order
\pi,

(2.35)

\iota_{\Gamma}^{*}\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi}=o_{N}\otimes(\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi}_{v^{o}}\boxtimes\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}_{v^{c}}),

where
o_{N}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}_{v^{c}}
are the canonical complex orientations.

Proof.

Parts (1), (3), (4), (5) are parts (1), (2), (3), and (4) of Proposition 3.12 of
[
5
]
. Part (2) corresponds to the covariant property (Definition 3.11 of loc. cit.) for the unique family of orientations constructed there. Lastly, parts (6) and (7) are Lemma 3.15(1) and (2) of loc. cit., respectively.
∎

Remark 2.47
.

To clean up notation, we will often instead use restriction notation
|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}
to denote pulling back under an inclusion map
\iota_{\Gamma}
, viewing the moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
as a boundary strata of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{{\text{sm}}{\Gamma}}
. For example, in Equation (
2.35
) above, we write
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}
instead of
\iota_{\Gamma}^{*}\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi}
.

We now turn to the family of orientations of the Witten bundles.

Lemma 2.48
.

There exists a family of orientations
{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}

for the Witten bundles
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r,\text{labeled},\hat{\pi}}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}
with the following properties:

(1)

The orientation is positive if the rank is
0
and
I
is non-empty (
|B|=|I|=1,a_{i}=0
).

(2)

Assume
f^{B}:B\to B^{\prime}
and
f^{I}:I\to I^{\prime}
are bijections and

F:(\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,B,I}^{1/r,\textup{labeled}})\to(\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,B^{\prime},I^{\prime}}^{1/r,\textup{labeled}})

is the induced map, lifted to the Witten bundle. Then
{\mathfrak{o}}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}^{\pi}=F^{*}{\mathfrak{o}}_{0,B^{\prime},\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I^{\prime}}}^{f^{B}\circ\pi}
. In particular, the action of any
g\in\text{Sym}(I)
permuting the internal marked points is orientation preserving for
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,B,I}^{1/r,\textup{labeled}}
.

(3)

Fix an integer
h
, and let
\pi
be an order of
B
. Take
g\in\text{Sym}(B)
so that
g
sends
x_{\pi(i)}
to
x_{\pi(i+h)}
cyclically. The action of
g
on the Witten bundle preserves the orientation
{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}
of
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,B,I}^{1/r,\textup{labeled},\hat{\pi}}
if and only if
h(|B|-1)
is even.

(4)

Let
\Gamma
be a graded
r
-spin graph, whose underlying pre-stable dual graph is that in Notation
2.44
. Assume that
\text{tw}(h_{1})={\text{alt}}(h_{1})=0
and recall that
v_{1}^{\prime}
is the vertex obtained when one takes
v_{1}
and forgets the half-edge
h_{1}
. Then

\mathfrak{o}^{\pi}
agrees with

\mathfrak{o}^{\pi_{1}}_{0,B_{1},I_{1}}\boxtimes\mathfrak{o}^{\pi_{2}}_{0,\{h_{2}\}\cup B_{2},I_{2}}
under the isomorphism

\det(\mathcal{W}_{\Gamma})=\det(\mathcal{W}_{v_{1}})\boxtimes\det(\mathcal{W}_{v_{2}})={\textup{For}}_{h_{1}}^{*}\det(\mathcal{W}_{v^{\prime}_{1}})\boxtimes\det(\mathcal{W}_{v_{2}}).

(5)

Let
\Gamma
be a connected graph with injective boundary markings, two vertices, an open vertex
v^{o},
whose tails are cyclically ordered by
\pi,
and a closed vertex
v^{c}
. Then for any order
\pi
whose induced cyclic order is
\hat{\pi},
the pull-back orientation
\iota_{\Gamma}^{*}\mathfrak{o}^{\pi}
on the Witten bundle agrees with
\mathfrak{o}^{\pi}_{v^{o}}\boxtimes\mathfrak{o}_{v^{c}}
under the isomorphism

\det(\mathcal{W}_{\Gamma})\cong{{\textup{Detach}}}^{*}\bigg(\det(\mathcal{W}_{v^{o}})\boxtimes\det(\mathcal{W}_{v^{c}})\bigg).

Proof.

Item (1) is induced by the choice given in Definition 5.10 in
[
5
]
. Item (2) is Theorem 5.2(i) of loc. cit. Item (3) is Lemma 5.6 of loc cit. Item (4) is Lemma 5.13 and the discussion above it in loc. cit. Lastly, Item (5) is Lemma 5.14 of loc. cit.
Here, in Item (5), we note that we have used Observation
2.41
: In case
e
is Neveu-Schwarz, the Witten bundle decomposes as a direct sum by Proposition
2.14
(
i
), but when
e
is Ramond, we use Proposition
2.14
(
iii
) along with Observation
2.41
in order to write
\iota_{\Gamma}^{*}{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi}={{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v^{o}}^{\pi}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v^{c}}
.
∎

Remark 2.49
.

In fact, there are precisely two families
\{\mathfrak{o}^{\pi}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\},\{\mathfrak{o}^{{}^{\prime}\pi}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\}
of orientations of the Witten bundle which satisfy Lemma
2.48
. They are related by
(-1)^{|B|-1}\mathfrak{o}^{\pi}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}=\mathfrak{o}^{{}^{\prime}\pi}_{0,B,\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}}
. In
[
5
, Section 5]
, the family of orientations

\mathfrak{o}^{\pi}
under which
\langle\tau^{1}_{0}\sigma^{2}\rangle^{1/r,o}=1
was chosen, and we continue with this choice.

We require another useful result concerning the induced orientations on the boundary that involves changing the cyclic ordering of the boundary marked points.

Corollary 2.50
.

Suppose
\Gamma
is an
r
-spin boundary graph as described in Notation
2.44
, using the unique ordering
\pi
stated there. Assume further that
\text{tw}(h_{1})={\text{alt}}(h_{i})=0
. Let
\pi^{\prime}
be a different ordering given by shifting
\pi
by
m
steps, i.e.,
\pi^{\prime}(i)=\pi(m+i\pmod{|B|})
.

(1)

If
0\leq m<|B_{1}|
, then we have that

(2.36)

\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi^{\prime}}|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}=(-1)^{(|B_{1}|-1)|B_{2}|+m|B_{2}|}o_{N}\otimes o_{h_{1}}\otimes(\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}_{0,B_{1},I_{1}}\boxtimes\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}_{0,\{h_{2}\}\cup B_{2},I_{2}}),

(2.37)

\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi^{\prime}}|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}=(-1)^{(|B_{1}|-1)|B_{2}|+m(|B_{2}|-1)}o_{N}\otimes(\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}_{0,\{h_{1}\}\cup B_{1},I_{1}}\boxtimes\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}_{0,\{h_{2}\}\cup B_{2},I_{2}}),

and

(2.38)

\mathfrak{o}^{\pi^{\prime}}=(-1)^{m|B_{2}|}\mathfrak{o}^{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}_{0,B_{1},I_{1}}\boxtimes\mathfrak{o}^{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}_{0,\{h_{2}\}\cup B_{2},I_{2}}.

(2)

If
|B_{1}|\leq m<|B|
, then we have that

{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi^{\prime}}=(-1)^{m(|B_{1}|-1)}\mathfrak{o}^{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}_{0,\{h_{2}\}\cup B_{2},I_{2}}\boxtimes\mathfrak{o}^{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}_{0,B_{1},I_{1}}.

Moreover, if
m=|B_{1}|
so
\pi_{2}^{\prime\prime}
is the cyclic order that takes
h_{2}
to be the last tail, then we have that

(2.39)

{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{o}^{\pi^{\prime\prime}_{2}}_{0,B_{2}\cup\{h_{2}\},I_{2}}\boxtimes\mathfrak{o}^{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}_{0,B_{1},I_{1}}.

Proof.

First, we consider the case where
0\leq m<|B_{1}|
. We define
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
as the order of
B_{1}
induced by restricting
\pi^{\prime}
, meaning
\pi^{\prime}_{1}(i)=\pi_{1}(m+i~(\text{mod}|B_{1}|))
. Note that the order of
B_{2}
induced by restricting
\pi^{\prime}
stays the same, i.e.,
\pi^{\prime}_{2}=\pi_{2}
. By applying Lemma
2.46
(
3
) and (
6
), we have that

(2.40)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi^{\prime}}|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m(|B|-1)}\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi}|_{{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m(|B|-1)}\left((-1)^{(|B_{1}|-1)|B_{2}|}o_{N}\otimes o_{h_{1}}\otimes(\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi_{1}}_{0,B_{1},I_{1}}\boxtimes\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi_{2}}_{0,\{h_{2}\}\cup B_{2},I_{2}})\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(|B_{1}|-1)|B_{2}|+m|B_{2}|}o_{N}\otimes o_{h_{1}}\otimes(\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}_{0,B_{1},I_{1}}\boxtimes\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}_{0,\{h_{2}\}\cup B_{2},I_{2}}).

Note that if we want to instead replace
o_{h_{1}}\otimes\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}_{0,B_{1},I_{1}}
with
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}_{0,\{h_{1}\}\cup B_{1},I_{1}}
, we have the analogous computation but using the last line of Lemma
2.46
(
6
) and the fact that
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi_{1}}_{0,B_{1}\cup\{h_{1}\},I_{1}}=(-1)^{m|B_{1}|}\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{\pi_{1}^{\prime}}_{0,B_{1}\cup\{h_{1}\},I_{1}}
. In this fashion, we obtain (
2.37
).
Similarly, we apply Lemma
2.48
(
3
) and (
4
) to obtain that
\mathfrak{o}^{\pi^{\prime}}=(-1)^{m|B_{2}|}\mathfrak{o}^{\pi^{\prime}_{1}}_{0,B_{1},I_{1}}\boxtimes\mathfrak{o}^{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}_{0,\{h_{2}\}\cup B_{2},I_{2}}
.

Next, we consider the case
|B_{1}|\leq m<|B|
. Here, we can see that the induced order on
B_{1}
is the same, i.e.,
\pi^{\prime}_{1}=\pi_{1}
; however, we compute that
\pi^{\prime}_{2}(i)=\pi_{2}(i+m+1-|B_{1}|~\pmod{|B_{2}|+1})
. By Lemma
2.48
(
3
), we then have the following relations using the new orderings:

{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi^{\prime}}=(-1)^{m(|B|-1)}{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi};\qquad{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi^{\prime}_{2}}=(-1)^{(1+m+|B_{1}|)|B_{2}|}{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi_{2}}.

Recall that, by using Observation
2.26
, we can see that the parities of the rank of the bundles
\mathcal{W}_{v_{1}^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{W}_{v_{2}}
are
|B_{1}|-1
and
|B_{2}|
, respectively. We then can see that, by using Lemma
2.48
(
4
), we have that

(2.41)

\displaystyle{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m(|B|-1)}{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m(|B|-1)}\mathfrak{o}^{\pi_{1}}_{0,B_{1},I_{1}}\boxtimes\mathfrak{o}^{\pi_{2}}_{0,\{h_{2}\}\cup B_{2},I_{2}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m(|B|-1)+(1+m+|B_{1}|)|B_{2}|}\mathfrak{o}^{\pi_{1}^{\prime}}_{0,B_{1},I_{1}}\boxtimes\mathfrak{o}^{\pi_{2}^{\prime}}_{0,\{h_{2}\}\cup B_{2},I_{2}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m(|B_{1}|-1)}\mathfrak{o}^{\pi_{2}^{\prime}}_{0,\{h_{2}\}\cup B_{2},I_{2}}\boxtimes\mathfrak{o}^{\pi_{1}^{\prime}}_{0,B_{1},I_{1}}.

∎

2.5.2.
Orientations in Rank two

We now turn to the rank two case to develop a family of orientations on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}^{W}
for graded graphs
\Gamma
and
W=x^{r}+y^{s}
.

Notation 2.51
.

Let
\pi
be an ordering of the boundary markings
B=B_{1}\sqcup B_{2}\sqcup B_{12}
,
where
B_{1}
(resp.
B_{2},B_{12}
) is the set of boundary
r
-tails
(resp. boundary
s
-tails, fully twisted tails).
Write
\hat{\pi}
for the induced cyclic order.
We write
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k_{1}}
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{k_{2}}
for the boundary marked points on a disk with twists
(r-2,0)
and
(0,s-2)
, respectively. Let

N(\pi)=\#\{(x_{i},y_{j})\,|\,\hbox{the $s$-point $y_{j}$ precedes the $r$-point $x_{i}$
in the $\pi$-order}\}.

Denote by
\pi|_{r}
the restriction of
\pi
to
B_{1}\sqcup B_{12},
and by
\pi|_{s}
the restriction to
B_{2}\sqcup B_{12}
.

Given a rooted connected smooth graded
graph
\Gamma
and a cyclic ordering
\hat{\pi}\in\hat{\Pi}_{\Gamma}
, let
\pi^{\text{std}}
be the unique ordering of boundary tails which starts from the root and induces
\hat{\pi}
. Equivalently, on the level of a disk in the moduli space, this is the order of markings along the orientation of the boundary, starting from the root.

Definition 2.52
.

Given a smooth graded graph
\Gamma
with bijective boundary marking, a cyclic order
\hat{\pi}\in\hat{\Pi}_{\Gamma}
, and an order
\pi
which induces the cyclic order
\hat{\pi}
, we define the orientation

{\hat{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi}_{\Gamma}=(-1)^{N(\pi)}{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi},

on the underlying moduli space of disks
{\mathcal{M}}^{\text{labeled},\hat{\pi}}_{\Gamma}
, where
N(\pi)
is defined in Notation
2.51
. Recall that, by Observation
2.12
, we have an isomorphism of vector bundles over
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}

\mathcal{W}_{1}\simeq\text{For}_{\text{spin}\neq 1}^{*}(\mathcal{W}_{1}),

where
\text{For}_{\text{spin}\neq 1}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r}_{\Gamma_{r}}

is as defined in Definition
2.9
, with
the graph
\Gamma_{r}=\text{for}_{\text{spin}\neq 1}(\Gamma)
.

The ordering given by the restriction of
\pi
to unforgotten tails, denoted by
\pi|_{r}
as seen in Notation
2.51
, induces an orientation
{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi|_{r}}_{\Gamma_{r}}
on
\mathcal{W}_{1}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r}_{\Gamma_{r}}
given in Lemma
2.48
. We then use the isomorphism of vector bundles under
\text{For}_{\text{spin}\neq 1}
to pull back this orientation to obtain the orientation on the bundle
\mathcal{W}_{1}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
, which we will denote by
{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi|_{r}}={{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,\Gamma}^{\pi|_{r}}
. Define
{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi|_{s}}={{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s,\Gamma}^{\pi|_{s}}
similarly for
\mathcal{W}_{2}
. Finally, we can combine these three orientations to obtain a relative orientation

o_{\Gamma}^{\pi}:={\hat{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi}_{\Gamma}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi|_{r}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi|_{s}},

of the full Witten bundle
\mathcal{W}
, or equivalently an orientation on the total space of
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
.

When
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}^{\text{labeled}}
, we write

{\hat{\mathfrak{o}}}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}={\hat{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi^{\text{std}}}_{\Gamma},~o_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}=o_{\Gamma}^{\pi^{\text{std}}},

where, on each connected component of the moduli,
\pi^{\text{std}}
is the order of markings along the orientation of the boundary, starting from the root as defined in Notation
2.51
. When
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}^{\text{labeled}}

, we write

{\hat{\mathfrak{o}}}_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}={\hat{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi}_{\Gamma},~o_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}=o_{\Gamma}^{\pi},

where
\pi
is an arbitrary order which agrees with the cyclic order of the points.

This latter orientation a priori depends on the choice of
\pi
, but we have the following proposition.

Proposition 2.53
.

Suppose
\Gamma
is a graph that can be written either in the form

\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}^{\text{labeled}}
or
\Gamma_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}^{\text{labeled}}
.
The following hold:

(1)

The relative orientation

o_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}

is independent of the choice of
\pi
.

(2)

If we change the roles of the
r
- and
s
-bundles in the definitions of
{\hat{\mathfrak{o}}}
and
o
then the orientations
o_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
changes by
(-1)^{k_{1}+k_{2}+1}
while
o_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
does
not change.

(3)

The relative orientations

o_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
and
o_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}

descend to relative orientations of the Witten bundles over the
unlabeled
moduli
spaces
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
and

{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
.

(4)

The orientation

{\hat{\mathfrak{o}}}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
descends to an orientation of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
.

Proof.

(1) Consider the case
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}^{\hat{\pi}}
. Change
\pi
to the ordering
\pi^{\prime}
which induces the same cyclic order but moves the first point of
\pi
to the end. Then, if the first point is an
r
-point, we have the following changes:

{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}
changes by
(-1)^{k_{1}+k_{2}+1}
by Lemma
2.46
(
3
),

N(\pi^{\prime})=N(\pi)+k_{2}+1
, and
{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi^{\prime}|_{r}}=(-1)^{k_{1}}{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi|_{r}}
and
{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi^{\prime}|_{s}}={{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi|_{s}}
, by Lemma
2.48
(
3
).
Thus,
o
does not change. Similar analysis shows it does not change if the
\pi
-first point is an
s
-point.

(2) Changing the roles of
r
and
s
changes
N(\pi)
to
(k_{1}+1)(k_{2}+1)-N(\pi)
and we can compute that swapping
\mathcal{W}_{r},~\mathcal{W}_{s}
adds a sign of
k_{1}k_{2}
as well using Equation (
2.23
). Thus, we have that the relative orientation changes by
(-1)^{(k_{1}+1)(k_{2}+1)+k_{1}k_{2}}=(-1)^{k_{1}+k_{2}+1}
.

In the case where
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}^{\hat{\pi}}
, exchanging the roles of
r
and
s
changes
N(\pi)
to
k_{1}k_{2}-N(\pi)
and swapping
\mathcal{W}_{r},~\mathcal{W}_{s}
adds a sign of
k_{1}k_{2}
as well. Thus, the total expression does not change.

(3) and (4).
Let
G
be the group of automorphisms of the boundary labelings
B
which
preserve twists. Then from Lemmas
2.46
(2) and
2.48
(2), it follows that
G
preserves the orientations on the bundles

\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}^{W,\textup{labeled}}
and
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}^{W,\textup{labeled}}
, and also on the moduli space

{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}^{W,\textup{labeled}}}
.
Thus, the orientations descend to the quotients by
G
.
∎

In light of the previous proposition, we may define:

Definition 2.54
.

We denote by
o_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
and
o_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
the relative orientations induced on the Witten bundles
over the unlabeled moduli spaces
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
respectively and call them the
canonical relative orientations
. Denote by
{\hat{\mathfrak{o}}}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
also the induced orientation on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
and call it the
canonical orientation
of

{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
.
If
\Gamma
is a smooth rooted graded
{(r,s)}
-graph, or a smooth graded
{(r,s)}
-graph with at most one unrooted component we define the
canonical relative orientation

o_{\Gamma}=\boxtimes_{\Lambda\in{{\textup{Conn}}}(\Gamma)}o_{\Lambda}.

Observe that
o_{\Gamma}
is well-defined with respect to the order of connected components. Indeed, we can compute that the dimension of the total space of the Witten bundle over a smooth rooted graded
W
-spin disk is even by using (
2.23
) and the fact that the moduli space has dimension
k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{12}+1\pmod{2}
.

The key point of this definition is that it constructs relative orientations
for moduli spaces associated to balanced graphs and to critcal graphs.

2.5.3.
Behavior of the canonical orientations at the boundary strata

Consider a graded
W
-spin graph that can be written in the form

\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}^{W}
. We now describe the behavior of the canonical relative orientations for the following degenerations of the smooth graph:

•

The graphs
\Lambda\in\partial\Gamma^{W}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
with two vertices and a single internal edge.

•

The graphs
\Lambda\in\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma^{W}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
with two vertices.

•

The graphs
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma^{W}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
with two vertices and a single boundary edge, where the half-node
h
that is adjacent to the rooted vertex is not fully twisted.

Understanding the orientation at these boundaries will be necessary to prove the subsequent results for topological recursion and wall-crossing for open FJRW invariants.

First, consider the boundary graphs that have a single internal edge and no boundary edges.

Corollary 2.55
.

Let
\Lambda\in\partial\Gamma^{W}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
be a graded
{(r,s)}
-graph with two vertices,
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. Assume that
v_{1}
is open and
v_{2}
is closed. Then, under the identification

\iota_{\Lambda}^{*}\det(T{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}})=\det(N)\otimes\left(\det(T{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{v_{1}})\boxtimes\det(T{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{v_{2}})\right),

we have

(2.42)

\iota_{\Lambda}^{*}o_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}=o_{N}\otimes\left(o_{v_{1}}\boxtimes o_{v_{2}}\right),

where
o_{v_{1}},o_{v_{2}}
are the orientations on the total space associated to the two vertices,
o_{v_{2}}
is the standard complex one.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the behavior under internal degenerations of
{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}},{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r},{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}
described in Lemma
2.46
(
7
), and Lemma
2.48
(
5
).
∎

Next, we consider the case where
\Lambda\in\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
is a graded
W
-spin graph. We will use the setup where the underlying dual graph of
\Lambda
is that of
\Gamma
as described in Notation
2.44
. Assume further that the vertex
v_{2}
is the exchangeable vertex. Recall from Definition
1.54
that there exists another graph
\Lambda^{\prime}:=\textup{exchange}(\Lambda)\in\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
, found by swapping the
r
- and
s
-points adjacent to the exchangeable vertex.

Moreover, for any
W
-spin curve
C
representing a point in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
there exists a
W
-spin curve
C^{\prime}
representing a point in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
so that
C\sim_{\textup{Exchange}}C^{\prime}
where
\sim_{\textup{Exchange}}
is as defined in Definition
1.36
. Hence we can define the map

(2.43)

\textup{Exchange}_{v}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}

given by taking the point represented by
C
to the point represented by
C^{\prime}
.

Proposition 2.56
.

Suppose
\Gamma
is a graph that can be written in the form

\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}^{W,\textup{labeled}}
.
Let
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}\in\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
so that
\Lambda\sim_{\textup{Exchange}}\Lambda^{\prime}
and both have exactly one boundary edge.
Then
\textup{Exchange}_{v}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
reverses the orientation
o_{\Gamma}
when restricted to the boundary strata
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
, i.e.,

\iota_{\Lambda}^{*}o_{\Gamma}=-\textup{Exchange}_{v}^{*}\iota^{*}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}o_{\Gamma}.

This orientation reversing isomorphism descends to the unlabeled moduli
spaces.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\Lambda
has a single cyclic order
\hat{\pi}
of its vertices. Let
\hat{\pi}^{\prime}
be the corresponding cyclic order on the boundary tails of
\Lambda^{\prime}
. Recall the definition of standard orderings from Notation
2.51
and let
\pi^{{\text{std}}}
and
(\pi^{{\text{std}}})^{\prime}
be the standard orderings of boundary tails which lift the cyclic orderings
\hat{\pi}
and
\hat{\pi}^{\prime}
. Note that, just like everything else in this case, these orderings differ by transposing an
r
-tail and an
s
-tail.

Note that the orientations induced by
{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi^{{\text{std}}}},{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}^{(\pi^{{\text{std}}})^{\prime}}
on the boundary components
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda},{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
are the same, by applying Lemma
2.46
(
6
) and then using
2.46
(
1
) in either case. Moreover, the orientations of the
r
-spin and
s
-spin Witten bundles ignore the relative order of the
r
-points and
s
-points as they forget one or the other. As
N(\pi^{{\text{std}}})=N((\pi^{{\text{std}}})^{\prime})+1\pmod{2}
, the identification
\textup{Exchange}_{v}
takes the orientation
\hat{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi^{{\text{std}}}}
to
-\hat{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{(\pi^{{\text{std}}})^{\prime}}
, implying the result.
The descent to the unlabeled moduli space follows from Theorem

2.53
(3).
∎

Lastly, consider the boundary graphs that have no internal edges and a single boundary edge.

Notation 2.57
.

For an open vertex
v
in a graded graph
\Gamma
, we write

{K}_{1}(v)=k_{1}(v)+k_{12}(v)-1,~{K}_{2}(v)=k_{2}(v)+k_{12}(v)-1.

Note that
{K}_{1}(v)
and
{K}_{2}(v)
include half-nodes.

Theorem 2.58
.

Let
\Lambda\in\partial\Gamma^{W,\textup{labeled}}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
be a graded
{(r,s)}
-graph where the underlying dual graph is as in Notation
2.44
. Suppose that the vertex
v_{1}
contains the root and that the sets
\hat{\Pi}_{v_{i}}
contain all cyclic orderings for both vertices
v_{i}
. Take
\iota_{\Lambda}
as in Definition
2.6
.

(1)

Suppose that
h_{2}
is fully twisted and consequently
\text{tw}(h_{1})={\text{alt}}(h_{1})=(0,0)
. Then

\iota_{\Lambda}^{*}o_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}=o_{N}\otimes o_{h_{1}}\otimes F_{\Gamma}^{*}(o_{v^{\prime}_{1}}\boxtimes o_{v_{2}}),

where

F_{\Lambda}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{v_{1}}\times{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{v_{2}}\rightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{v^{\prime}_{1}}\times{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{v_{2}}

is the composed map
F_{\Lambda}:={\textup{For}}_{h_{1}}\circ{{\textup{Detach}}}_{e}
and
o_{N}
and
o_{h_{1}}
are the orientations of the bundles
N
and
Tf_{h_{1}}
, respectively, as in Lemma
2.46
(6).

(2)

Suppose that
h_{2}
is singly twisted (and consequently so is
h_{1}
).

(a)

If
h_{1}
is an
r
-point, then

(2.44)

\iota_{\Lambda}^{*}o_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}=(-1)^{{K}_{2}(v_{2})}o_{N}\otimes(o_{v_{1}}\boxtimes o_{v_{2}}),

(b)

If
h_{1}
is an
s
-point, then

(2.45)

\iota_{\Lambda}^{*}o_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}=(-1)^{{K}_{2}(v_{2})+1}o_{N}\otimes(o_{v_{1}}\boxtimes o_{v_{2}}).

We note that in the statement of this theorem, we use the identification

(2.46)

\iota_{\Lambda}^{*}\mathcal{W}=F_{\Lambda}^{*}(\mathcal{W}_{v_{1}^{\prime}}\boxplus\mathcal{W}_{v_{2}}).

Proof.

For the proofs below, we will use some new notation. First, recall Definition
2.52
. For any
\Lambda
, we write

{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi}_{r,s,\Lambda}={{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,\Lambda}^{\pi|_{r}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s,\Lambda}^{\pi|_{s}}.

We have (suppressing the subscript
\Gamma
everywhere)

o^{\pi}=\hat{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s}^{\pi}=(-1)^{N(\pi)}{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s}^{\pi}.

Proof of (1).

Consider first the connected component
{\mathcal{M}}
of
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}

with the property that for any
\Sigma\in{\mathcal{M}}
,
the root directly follows the node
x_{h_{1}}
with respect to the cyclic
order of boundary points on its
v_{1}
-component. In this case, the standard ordering
\pi^{\text{std}}
from Notation
2.51
coincides with the ordering
\pi
given in Notation
2.44
.

By Lemma
2.48
(
4
) applied to
the two Witten bundles separately, (noting that the the cyclic order
in that lemma coincides with the one used here because of the location of
the root), we have

{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi^{\text{std}}|_{r}}={{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi^{\text{std}}_{1}|_{r}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi_{2}|_{r}},\quad{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi^{\text{std}}|_{s}}={{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi^{\text{std}}_{1}|_{s}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi_{2}|_{s}},

where
\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}
is the restriction of
\pi^{\text{std}}
to the tails of
v_{1}^{\prime}
as in Lemma
2.48
and

\pi_{2}
is the restriction of
\pi^{\text{std}}
to the tails of
v_{2}
and extending by adding
h_{2}
in the beginning. We denote by
{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi^{\text{std}}_{1}|_{r}}
the orientation of the
r
-Witten bundle over
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{v_{1}}
which corresponds to the order
\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}
and denote the other orientations similarly.
Thus

\displaystyle{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s}^{\pi}={}

\displaystyle{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}|_{r}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi_{2}|_{r}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}|_{s}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi_{2}|_{s}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{a}{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}|_{r}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}|_{s}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi_{2}|_{r}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi_{2}|_{s}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{a}{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{1}^{\prime}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}},

where
a=\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{W}_{v_{2},r})\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{W}_{v_{1},s})=K_{1}(v_{2})K_{2}(v_{1})\pmod{2}
.

Similarly, by Lemma
2.46
(
6
),

{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi^{\text{std}}}|_{{\mathcal{M}}}=(-1)^{b}o_{N}\otimes o_{h_{1}}\otimes({\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{1}^{\prime}}^{\pi^{\text{std}}_{1}}\boxtimes{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}}),\quad b=({K}_{1}(v_{1})+{K}_{2}(v_{1}))({K}_{1}(v_{2})+{K}_{2}(v_{2}))

where
\pi^{\text{std}}_{1}
is the standard order for the vertex
v_{1}^{\prime}
and
\pi_{2}
is the induced order of boundary markings on the vertex
v_{2}
, as in Notation
2.44
, starting from
h_{2}
. We remark that
\pi_{2}=\pi_{2}^{{\text{std}}}
, as
h_{2}
is a root for
v_{2}
in the detaching in this case.

In addition,

N(\pi^{\text{std}})=N(\pi^{\text{std}}_{1})+N(\pi_{2})+c,\quad c=K_{1}(v_{2})K_{2}(v_{1}).

Note here that, in this special case,
c\equiv a\mod 2
.
We then see that on the Witten bundle over
{\mathcal{M}}
the orientation is

\displaystyle o^{\pi}={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{N(\pi^{\text{std}})}{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s}^{\pi}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{N(\pi^{\text{std}})+a+b}(o_{N}\otimes o_{h_{1}}\otimes{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{1}^{\prime}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}\boxtimes{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}})\otimes({{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{1}^{\prime}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{N(\pi^{\text{std}})+a+b+b}o_{N}\otimes o_{h_{1}}\otimes({\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{1}^{\prime}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{1}^{\prime}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}})\boxtimes({\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{(N(\pi^{\text{std}}_{1})+N(\pi_{2})+a)+a}o_{N}\otimes o_{h_{1}}\otimes({\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{1}^{\prime}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{1}^{\prime}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}})\boxtimes({\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle o_{N}\otimes o_{h_{1}}\otimes((-1)^{N(\pi^{\text{std}}_{1})}{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{1}^{\prime}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{1}^{\prime}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}})\boxtimes((-1)^{N(\pi_{2})}{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle o_{N}\otimes o_{h_{1}}\otimes o_{v_{1}^{\prime}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}\boxtimes o_{v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}}.

Note the additional
(-1)^{b}
in the third line came from swapping
{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}}
and
{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{1}^{\prime}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}
.

We now tackle the general case. Consider a component
{\mathcal{M}}
of
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
where, for any
\Sigma\in{\mathcal{M}}
, if we follow the cyclic order of the boundary points on its
v_{1}
-component from the node
x_{h_{1}}
to the root, then we pass through
m_{1}
boundary points of twist
(r-2,0)
and
m_{2}
of twist
(0,s-2)
.
12
12

12

Note that, for our hypothesis in this case to continue to hold, the root must stay in vertex
v_{1}
.
Now use Corollary
2.50
(
1
) to compute how
a
and
b
change with respect to this new ordering. Here, we see that
a
changes by
{K}_{1}(v_{2})m_{1}+{K}_{2}(v_{2})m_{2}
by Equation (
2.38
), after applying it to both spin bundles. The value of
b
changes by
(m_{1}+m_{2})({K}_{1}(v_{2})+{K}_{2}(v_{2}))
by Equation (
2.36
). Finally,
c
changes by
m_{2}{K}_{1}(v_{2})+m_{1}{K}_{2}(v_{2})
by examining
N(\pi^{\text{std}})
with the shifted position of the root. Combining these alterations of the constants, we see that there is no change of sign.

Proof of (2a).
As before, consider first the component
{\mathcal{M}}
of
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
with the property that, for any
\Sigma\in{\mathcal{M}}
, the root directly follows the node
x_{h_{1}}
with respect to the cyclic order of boundary points on its
v_{1}
-component. Again, the standard ordering
\pi^{\text{std}}
coincides with the ordering
\pi
given in Notation
2.44
. We write
\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}
for the standard ordering on the half-edges of
v_{1},
starting from the root, and
\pi_{2}
for the ordering of the half-edges of
v_{2}
which extends the orientation on tails by putting
h_{2}
first.

For the orientation
{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi^{\text{std}}|_{r}}
, we apply Lemma
2.48
(
4
) in order to decompose the orientation into a tensor product of the orientations
{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}_{r,v_{1}}
and
{{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi_{2}}_{r,v_{2}}
;
however, to apply the lemma, we must swap the roles of
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
since the twist at
h_{2}
is
(0,s-2)
, requiring some care with the cyclic orderings. By Equation (
2.39
) in Corollary
2.50
(
2
),
we get that

{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi^{\text{std}}|_{r}}={{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}|_{r}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi_{2}|_{r}}.

We can directly apply Lemma
2.48
(
4
) for the
s
-spin Witten bundles, giving the decomposition
{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi^{\text{std}}|_{s}}={{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}|_{s}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi_{2}|_{s}}
. Putting this all together, we obtain

(2.47)

\displaystyle{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s}^{\pi^{\text{std}}}={}

\displaystyle{{\displaystyle\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}|_{r}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi_{2}|_{r}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}|_{s}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi_{2}|_{s}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{a}{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}|_{r}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}|_{s}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r}^{\pi_{2}|_{r}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{s}^{\pi_{2}|_{s}},

where

a:=K_{1}(v_{2})K_{2}(v_{1})
.

Secondly, by Lemma
2.46
(
6
), we have that

(2.48)

{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi^{\text{std}}}|_{{\mathcal{M}}}=(-1)^{b}o_{N}\otimes({\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{1}}^{\pi^{\text{std}}_{1}}\boxtimes{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}}),\quad b=({K}_{1}(v_{1})+{K}_{2}(v_{1})+1)({K}_{1}(v_{2})+{K}_{2}(v_{2})+1).

We remark to the reader that we include the half-edge
{h_{2}}
in the count
{K}_{2}(v_{2}),
and the half-edge
{h_{1}}
in
{K}_{1}(v_{1})
; however, the sets
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
in the statement of the lemma do not include half-edges.

Thirdly, we decompose
N(\pi^{\text{std}})

into summands again:

N(\pi^{\text{std}})=N(\pi^{\text{std}}_{1})+N(\pi_{2})+c,~c:=K_{1}(v_{2})(K_{2}(v_{1})-1)-1.

Indeed, we find
c
in the following way. Note that the
r
-point
h_{1}
added at the end of
\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}
adds
K_{2}(v_{1})
such pairs in the vertex
v_{1}
, the
s
-point
h_{2}
at the beginning of
\pi_{2}
adds
k_{1}(v_{2})=K_{1}(v_{2})+1
such pairs in the vertex
v_{2}
and that there are
K_{2}(v_{1})(K_{1}(v_{2})+1)
pairs where the
s
-point is in
v_{1}
and the
r
-point is in
v_{2}
. Putting together, we compute

c=-K_{2}(v_{1})-K_{1}(v_{2})-1+K_{2}(v_{1})(K_{1}(v_{2})+1)
.

Finally, we obtain that:

\displaystyle o^{\pi}={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{N(\pi^{\text{std}})}{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}^{\pi^{\text{std}}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s}^{\pi^{\text{std}}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{N(\pi^{\text{std}})+a+b}(o_{N}\otimes{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{1}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}\boxtimes{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}})\otimes({{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{1}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}\boxtimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{N(\pi^{\text{std}})+a+b+({K}_{1}(v_{1})+{K}_{2}(v_{1}))({K}_{1}(v_{2})+{K}_{2}(v_{2})+1)}o_{N}\otimes({\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{1}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{1}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}})\boxtimes({\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{(N(\pi^{\text{std}}_{1})+N(\pi_{2})+c)+a+b+({K}_{1}(v_{1})+{K}_{2}(v_{1}))({K}_{1}(v_{2})+{K}_{2}(v_{2})+1)}o_{N}\otimes({\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{1}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{1}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}})\boxtimes({\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{a+b+c+({K}_{1}(v_{1})+{K}_{2}(v_{1}))({K}_{1}(v_{2})+{K}_{2}(v_{2})+1)}o_{N}\otimes((-1)^{N(\pi^{\text{std}}_{1})}{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{1}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{1}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}})\boxtimes((-1)^{N(\pi_{2})}{\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}}_{v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}}\otimes{{\mathfrak{o}}}_{r,s,v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{K_{2}(v_{2})}o_{N}\otimes o_{v_{1}}^{\pi_{1}^{\text{std}}}\boxtimes o_{v_{2}}^{\pi_{2}}.

We now consider the general choice of component
{\mathcal{M}}

of
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
. As we did in the proof of Item (1), consider a component
{\mathcal{M}}
of
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}

where, for any
\Sigma\in{\mathcal{M}}
, if we follow the cyclic order of the boundary points on its
v_{1}
-component from the node
x_{h_{1}}
to the root, then we pass through
m_{1}
boundary points of twist
(r-2,0)
and
m_{2}
of twist
(0,s-2)
.
We may now use Corollary
2.50
twice in
order to understand
the analogous version of Equation (
2.47
).
First, considering the
r
-spin bundle, note that the roles of the two vertices are reversed since the
h_{1}
in our context has non-trivial twist, so we are in
the case of Corollary
2.50
(2) with
m=m_{1}+|B_{1}|
. Here,
B_{1}
defined in the Corollary in the current context is the set of boundary marked points (excluding half-nodes) on
v_{2}
with non-trivial twist
\text{tw}_{1}
.
As
|B_{1}|=K_{1}(v_{2})+1
, the sign changes by
(m_{1}+K_{1}(v_{2})+1)K_{1}(v_{2})
. For the
s
-spin bundle, the roles of the two vertices are the same in both contexts, so we can straightforwardly apply Corollary
2.50
(1) with
m=m_{2}
and
|B_{2}|=K_{2}(v_{2})
. So we have that the change in
a
from that stated in (
2.47
) is given by

(m_{1}+K_{1}(v_{2})+1)K_{1}(v_{2})+m_{2}K_{2}(v_{2}).

Next, we provide the analogous analysis for
b
in (
2.48
). Here, we apply Equation (
2.37
) with
m=m_{1}+m_{2}
and
|B_{2}|=K_{1}(v_{2})+K_{2}(v_{2})+1
to obtain that
b
changes by

(m_{1}+m_{2})(K_{1}(v_{2})+K_{2}(v_{2})).

Lastly, we compute
c
. We can do this by re-computing the discrepancy between
N(\pi^{\text{std}})
and
N(\pi^{\text{std}}_{1})+N(\pi_{2})
. There are four different cases where one or the other counts a pair but the other quantity does not:

(1)

When an
s
-tail of
v_{1}
is after the root but before the half-edge, then we need to count the pairs of that
s
-tail and any
r
-tail in
v_{2}
. This is counted in
N(\pi^{\text{std}})
but not in
N(\pi^{\text{std}}_{1})+N(\pi_{2})
. There are
(K_{2}(v_{1})-m_{2})(K_{1}(v_{2})+1)
such pairs.

(2)

When an
r
-tail of
v_{1}
is after the half-edge, then we need to count the pairs with that
r
-tail and any
s
-tail in
v_{2}
. Again, this is counted in
N(\pi^{\text{std}})
but not in
N(\pi^{\text{std}}_{1})+N(\pi_{2})
. There are
m_{1}K_{2}(v_{2})
such pairs.

(3)

When we take the half-edge
h_{1}
and take any
s
-tail in
v_{1}
that is before the half-edge and after the root. This is counted in
N(\pi^{\text{std}}_{1})+N(\pi_{2})
but not in
N(\pi^{\text{std}})
. There are
(K_{2}(v_{1})-m_{2})
such pairs.

(4)

When we take the half-edge
h_{2}
and any
r
-tail in
v_{2}
, we get a contribution as
\pi_{2}
starts at the half-edge. This is counted in
N(\pi^{\text{std}}_{1})+N(\pi_{2})
but not in
N(\pi^{\text{std}})
. There are
(K_{1}(v_{2})+1)
such pairs.

This computes that

c=(K_{2}(v_{1})-m_{2})(K_{1}(v_{2})+1)+m_{1}K_{2}(v_{2})-(K_{2}(v_{1})-m_{2})-(K_{1}(v_{2})+1),

so
c
changes by

-m_{2}(K_{1}(v_{2})+1)+m_{1}K_{2}(v_{2})+m_{2}=-m_{2}K_{1}(v_{2})+m_{1}K_{2}(v_{2}).

Combining all these changes, we therefore get that the changes of
a
,
b
and
c
cancel out modulo
2
, so the sign does not change.

Proof of (2b).
Suppose now that
\Gamma
is as described in (2b). Denote by
o^{\prime}_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
and
o^{\prime}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
the relative orientations of the Witten bundles with the roles of
r
and
s
interchanged.
By Proposition
2.53
, we have that
o^{\prime}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}=o_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
, hence the orientation
o^{\prime}_{v_{1}}=o_{v_{1}}
. Secondly, by the same proposition, we have that

o^{\prime}_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}=(-1)^{k_{1}+k_{2}+1}o_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}},

hence
o_{v_{2}}=(-1)^{{K}_{1}(v_{2})+{K}_{2}(v_{2})+1}o^{\prime}_{v_{2}}
.
By symmetry we would get, using the previous case, that

\iota_{\Gamma}^{*}o^{\prime}_{0,k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}=(-1)^{{K}_{1}(v_{2})}o^{\prime}_{N}\otimes(o^{\prime}_{v_{1}}\boxtimes o^{\prime}_{v_{2}}).

Putting together, we obtain (
2.45
).
∎

3.
Boundary conditions and intersection numbers

In this section, we will define open intersection numbers associated to
W
-spin disks in the case of
W=x^{r}+y^{s}
. They will be defined as counts of zeros of
multisections of the Witten bundle on an open subset of our moduli spaces
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
satisfying certain boundary conditions, which we shall now explain.

3.1.
Boundary strata and intervals

Notation 3.1
.

For an open graded graph
\Gamma
, we define
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
to be the orbifold

{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}:={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}\setminus\left(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Lambda\in\partial\Gamma\text{ such that }\\
{H^{+}}(\Lambda)\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda}\right),

where
{H^{+}}(\Lambda)
, as defined in Definition
1.53
, is the set of half-edges
h
of the graph
\Lambda
such that either
h
or
\sigma_{1}(h)
is positive for the
i^{th}
grading for some
i
.

Recall the subset of graphs
\partial^{+}\Gamma,~\partial^{0}\Gamma
and
\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
, and the relation
{\sim_{\textup{exchange}}}
, from Definitions
1.54
and
1.56
.

Definition 3.2
.

Set

\displaystyle\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\Lambda\in\partial^{+}\Gamma}{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma},

\displaystyle\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma}{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma},

\displaystyle\partial^{\textup{Exchange}}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\Lambda\in\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma}{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}.

Note that
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}\setminus\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
and that
\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}=\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}\cup\partial^{\textup{Exchange}}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
.
Indeed, for
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
to lie in
\partial{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
, it must
either have a contracted boundary edge or a boundary edge, so
\Lambda
must be contained in the union
\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}\cup\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}\cup\partial^{\textup{Exchange}}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
. We remark that if
\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
is nonempty, then
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
is not compact.

Definition 3.3
.

Define

{{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}:={\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\setminus\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}.

Note that even if
\Gamma
is smooth, there may be points of

{{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
corresponding to singular
W
-spin curves with
internal nodes.

Example 3.4
.

For the cases where
\Gamma=\Gamma^{W}_{0,m_{1},m_{2},1,\{(m_{1},m_{2})\}}
, with
m_{1}<r-1,~m_{2}<s-1
, and
\Gamma^{W}_{0,r,0,1,\emptyset},\Gamma^{W}_{0,0,s,1,\emptyset}
, we now show that
\partial^{0}\Gamma=\emptyset
.

Consider the case where
\Gamma=\Gamma^{W}_{0,r,0,1,\emptyset}
. Suppose that
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma
. Note that
\Lambda
cannot have any closed
vertices since there are no internal marked points. Thus
if
e
is an edge in
\Lambda
then
{\text{sm}}_{e}\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\{\Gamma\}
. So, it suffices to show that there are no
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma
such that
\Lambda
has exactly two vertices. If such a
\Lambda
exists, then note that one of the two vertices must have the root, which we call
v_{1}
. Using (
2.22
) and (
2.23
) in the same manner we did in Proposition
2.31
(a), we can see that

k_{1}(v_{1})+k_{12}(v_{1})-1\equiv r(I(v_{1}))=0\pmod{r}.

That is, there must be either
1
or
r+1
boundary half-edges on the vertex
v_{1}
so that
(\text{tw}_{1},{\text{alt}}_{1})=(r-2,1)
. Thus, the vertex
v_{1}
contains at most
r

r
-points and one boundary half-edge, hence there are three cases: (i)
v_{1}
has
r

r
-points and the half-edge does not have the
r
-twisting, (ii)
v_{1}
has
r-1

r
-points and the half-edge does have the
r
-twisting, or (iii)
v_{1}
has no
r
-points and the half-edge does not have the
r
-twisting. However, none of these cases will have two stable vertices, hence
\partial^{0}\Gamma=\emptyset
. Similarly, we have the analogous result for
\Gamma=\Gamma^{W}_{0,0,s,1,\emptyset}
.

Now consider the case
\Gamma=\Gamma^{W}_{0,m_{1},m_{2},1,\{(m_{1},m_{2})\}}
. As before, suppose that there exists a
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma
with exactly two vertices. Let
v_{1}
be the vertex containing the root. Note that, as before using (
2.22
) and (
2.23
), we have that

\displaystyle k_{1}(v_{i})+k_{12}(v_{i})-1

\displaystyle\equiv r(I(v_{i}))\pmod{r},

\displaystyle k_{2}(v_{i})+k_{12}(v_{i})-1

\displaystyle\equiv s(I(v_{i}))\pmod{s}.

If
I(v_{1})=\emptyset
then
k_{1}(v_{1})+k_{12}(v_{1})\equiv 1\pmod{r}
and
k_{2}(v_{1})+k_{12}(v_{1})\equiv 1\pmod{s}
. Since
m_{1}<r-1
and
m_{2}<s-1
, we must then have that
k_{1}(v_{1})=k_{2}(v_{1})=0
. However, this implies that the vertex
v_{1}
is not stable and hence
\Lambda
is not a boundary graph. Thus
v_{1}
must have the internal marking. In this case, however, we then have that
k_{1}(v_{1})+k_{12}(v_{1})-1\equiv m_{1}\pmod{r}
and
k_{2}(v_{1})+k_{12}(v_{1})-1\equiv m_{2}\pmod{s}
. We then have exactly one case that has two stable vertices, which is that of an exchangeable graph.

3.1.1.
The base

We now introduce one of the key concepts of this paper, the base of
a graph. This allows for the inductive structure
of boundary conditions for the canonical multisections to be introduced
in §
3.2
.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\Gamma
be a graded graph. We take
E^{0}(\Gamma)
to be the set of boundary edges such that for each
i
, one of its half-edges has

\text{tw}_{i}={\text{alt}}_{i}=0
. Recall from Definition
2.8
that
\text{for}_{\text{non-alt}}
is the operation that deletes all tails
h
where
\text{tw}_{i}={\text{alt}}_{i}=0
for all
i
.
We define the
base
of
\Gamma
to be

{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma=\text{for}_{\text{non-alt}}(\textup{detach}_{E^{0}(\Gamma)}(\Gamma)).

We call the moduli
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma}
the
base moduli
of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
.
We write

F_{\Gamma}:=(\text{For}_{\text{non-alt}}\circ{{\textup{Detach}}}_{E^{0}(\Gamma)}):{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Gamma}\to{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma}.

On the level of surfaces, the morphism
F_{\Gamma}
normalizes all boundary half-edges in
E^{0}(\Gamma)
and forgets all boundary marked points which have twist
zero and are non-alternating with respect to both spin structures, i.e.,
\text{tw}={\text{alt}}=({0},0)
.
Note that the forgetful map may create unstable connected components. This happens, for example, for exchangeable graphs. We consider the moduli of an unstable connected component to be a point.

Notation 3.6
.

We can extend
F_{\Gamma}
continuously to a map
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma}
. We use the same notation for the extended map.
If we take
\Lambda\in\partial^{!}\Gamma
, it maps
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
under
F_{\Gamma}
to
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
for some
\Lambda^{\prime}\in\partial^{!}{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma
. We can define a function

f_{\Gamma}:\partial^{!}\Gamma\to\partial^{!}{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma

so that
f_{\Gamma}(\Lambda)=\Lambda^{\prime}
.
Explicitly,
\Lambda^{\prime}=\text{for}_{\text{non-alt}}(\textup{detach}_{E^{0}(\Gamma)}(\Lambda))
, where we use the identification of the edges of
\Gamma
as edges of
\Lambda
.

Remark 3.7
.

To motivate the definition of the base moduli,
assume we are given a graded graph
\Gamma
indexing a boundary
stratum of some moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
for
\Gamma^{\prime}
a smooth
graded graph. If
\widehat{\Gamma}=\textup{detach}_{E}\Gamma
for some subset
of edges
E
of
\Gamma
, then the detaching map
{{\textup{Detach}}}_{E}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}\rightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\widehat{\Gamma}}
allows us to relate the former
moduli space to the latter, which is a product of moduli spaces
associated to the connected components of
\Gamma
. In general,
these connected components will not yield moduli spaces
of the sort we consider here: we wish to consider only those moduli
spaces associated to graphs whose boundary tails have twists

(r-2,0)
,
(0,s-2)
or
(r-2,s-2)
.

However,
if
\Gamma\in\partial^{0}\Gamma^{\prime}
, then all boundary half-edges
of
\Gamma
necessarily have twists being either one of the above
three possibilities or twist
(0,0)
. Thus if we detach
\Gamma

along all such boundary half-edges, and forget the newly acquired
boundary tails with twist
(0,0)
, obtaining
{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma
,
the connected components of
{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma
are precisely graded graphs with
boundary tails of the type we are considering in this paper.
Further, as we will see in Observation
3.11
, the
Witten and descendent bundles on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}

pull back from those on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma}
, thus enabling the
construction of inductive boundary conditions. For the remainder
of the boundary conditions near the strata we delete, we rely on positivity conditions.

The following observations are straightforward but useful for later. First, analogously to
[
42
, Observations 3.14 and 3.28]
, we have the following observation, which serves as a compatibility relation for boundary conditions:

Observation 3.8
.

Let
\Gamma
be a graded graph and take
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\partial\Gamma
. Then
F_{\Gamma}
takes
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
to
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{f_{\Gamma}(\Gamma^{\prime})},
and
{\mathcal{B}}f_{\Gamma}(\Gamma^{\prime})={\mathcal{B}}\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Moreover, we have

F_{f_{\Gamma}(\Gamma^{\prime})}\circ F_{\Gamma}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}=F_{\Gamma^{\prime}}.

This follows directly from the definitions.

Recall that when
v
is an exchangeable vertex of an exchangeable graph
\Gamma
, we have defined the graph
\textup{exchange}_{v}(\Gamma)
to be the graph obtained by reversing the cyclic orders at
v
and a corresponding map
\textup{Exchange}_{v}
given in (
2.43
) at the level of moduli. When
\Gamma
has a single exchangeable vertex
v
, we sometimes write
Exchange
instead of
\textup{Exchange}_{v}
.

Another straightforward observation is the following:

Observation 3.9
.

Graphs in the same
{\sim_{\textup{exchange}}}
-equivalence class have the same base. Moreover, if
\Gamma
is exchangeable and
v\in V^{O}(\Gamma)
is an exchangeable vertex, then

F_{\textup{exchange}_{v}(\Gamma)}\circ\textup{Exchange}_{v}=F_{\Gamma}.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
\mathcal{V}(\Gamma)
be the collection of dual graphs consisting of one vertex that may appear as a connected component of
\textup{for}_{\textup{non-alt}}(\textup{detach}_{E(\Lambda)}(\Lambda))
for some
\Lambda\in\partial^{!}\Gamma\setminus\partial^{+}\Gamma
.
We call the elements of
\mathcal{V}(\Gamma)

abstract vertices
.
Let
i\in I(\Gamma)
be a labeling for an internal tail. Let
\mathcal{V}^{i}(\Gamma)\subset\mathcal{V}(\Gamma)
be the collection of abstract vertices which have an internal tail labeled
i
.
For a graph
\Gamma
with
i\in I(\Gamma)
,
denote by
v_{i}^{*}(\Gamma)
the connected component of the graph

\text{for}_{\text{non-alt}}(\textup{detach}_{E(\Gamma)}(\Gamma))

which contains
i
as an internal marking. As we have detached at all edges, this connected component only has a single vertex, hence
v_{i}^{*}(\Gamma)\in\mathcal{V}^{i}(\Gamma)
.

If
v
is a connected component of the graph

\text{for}_{\text{non-alt}}(\textup{detach}_{E(\Gamma)}(\Gamma))
,
denote by
\Phi_{\Gamma,v}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{v}
the natural map,
which is the composition of

\text{For}_{\text{non-alt}}\circ\text{{{{Detach}}}}_{E(\Gamma)}

with the projection to
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{v}
.
Write
\Phi_{\Gamma,i}
for
\Phi_{\Gamma,v_{i}^{*}(\Gamma)}
.

The key observation for the inductive structure of the Witten and
descendent bundles is then:

Observation 3.11
.

Let
\Gamma
be a pre-graded graph. There is a canonical isomorphism between the Witten bundle
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
and the pullback bundle
F_{\Gamma}^{*}\mathcal{W}
. To construct it, we first note that over the locus of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
, all boundary half-edges are Neveu-Schwarz, hence we can apply Proposition
2.14
(1).
Further, in forgetting those resulting boundary tails
with
\text{tw}={\text{alt}}=(0,0)
, we can apply
[
5
, Equation 4.3]
.

Similarly, by Observation 3.32 of
[
42
]
, if the component which contains the internal tails labeled
i
in
{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma
is stable then
{\mathbb{L}}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
is canonically
F_{\Gamma}^{*}\left({\mathbb{L}}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{v^{*}_{i}(\Gamma)}\right)
.

In the rest of the paper, we will construct multisections of
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})

which, using the above observation, are pulled back from the bundle

E_{{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
. For this reason,
it is important to understand the relationship between the dimensions of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma}

and
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
on the one hand and
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
on the
other. We now calculate.

Observation 3.12
.

Fix a smooth graded
W
-spin graph
\Gamma
, and consider

\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
where
\Lambda

is a relevant graph with no internal edges. Let

\mathbf{d}=(d_{i})_{i\in I(\Gamma)}
be a descendent vector, and

E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
the descendent-Witten bundle on

{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
with respect to
\mathbf{d}
. We define the following integers:

(1)

\alpha=\operatorname{rank}E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})-\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
.

(2)

\nu
is the number of (open) vertices of
\Lambda
.

(3)

\beta
is the number of half-edges with
\text{tw}={\text{alt}}=(0,0)
forgotten
in passing from
\textup{detach}_{E^{0}(\Lambda)}\Lambda
to
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
.

(4)

\sigma
is the number of partially stable connected components of

{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
.

Note

\sigma\leq\beta<\nu.

Since we are assuming
\Lambda
is relevant,
necessarily any such partially stable connected component
has two boundary tails and no interior tails. Then

(3.1)

\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}-(\nu-1)

and

(3.2)

\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}-\beta+\sigma,

bearing in mind that the moduli space associated to a partially
stable component is a point, hence one dimension higher than expected.

Denote the connected components of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
as
\Xi_{1},\ldots,\Xi_{\nu}
,
with the last
\sigma
being the partially stable components.
By Observation
3.11
,

\operatorname{rank}E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{i}}(\mathbf{d}).

Combining the previous three equations and using the fact that

\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{i}}(\mathbf{d})=0
for any partially stable component
\Xi_{i}
,
we obtain

(3.3)

\sum_{i=1}^{\nu-\sigma}\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu-\sigma}\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{i}}(\mathbf{d})-\alpha-\beta-(\nu-\sigma)+1.

3.1.2.
Positivity constraints

Recall that the moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
in general is not compact; indeed, we have that
\partial^{+}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{W}=\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{W}\setminus{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
. In order to apply Definition
2.20
to define open
W
-spin invariants, we will require non-vanishing of any global multisection of the Witten bundle in a region “close” to
\partial^{+}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}^{W}
in
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
. To this end, we proceed with the following definitions that aim to provide such multisections which we will call strongly positive.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
\Gamma
be a graded
W
-spin graph and
\Lambda\in\partial^{+}\Gamma
. A
\Lambda
-set with respect to
\Gamma
is an open set
U\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
whose closure intersects the strata
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Xi}
precisely for those graphs
\Xi\in\partial^{!}\Gamma
with
\Lambda\in\partial^{!}\Xi
.

Now further suppose
u\in{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}\subseteq\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
. A
\Lambda
-neighborhood with respect to
\Gamma
of
u\in{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
is a neighborhood
U\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
of
u
which is a
\Lambda
-set with respect to
\Gamma
. We remark that if
\Gamma
is smooth, we will omit the addition “with respect to
\Gamma
” as, for any
\Lambda
, there is a unique smooth graph
\Gamma
for which
\Lambda\in\partial^{!}\Gamma
.

Example 3.14
.

Consider the moduli space given in Example
2.7
with one internal marking and three boundary markings. Let
\Gamma
be the smooth graded graph and let
\Lambda
be a codimension one boundary strata. A
\Lambda
-set with respect to
\Gamma
is any open set whose closure does not intersect any other boundary strata. In Figure
11
(A), we have a
\Lambda
-neighbourhood with respect to
\Gamma
of the point
u\in{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
, while in Figure
11
(B), the neighbourhood’s closure would intersect the codimension two strata, so it is not a
\Lambda
-neighbourhood with respect to
\Gamma
of
u
.

u

(a)

\Lambda
-set with respect to
\Gamma
.

\Lambda

.

.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
\Sigma
be a (possibly nodal) graded marked disk. Write the oriented boundary
\partial\Sigma
as a quotient space
S/{\sim}
, where
S
is the oriented manifold
S^{1}
and
\sim
is an equivalence relation whose equivalence classes are all of size
1
except for a finite number of classes of size
2
and take
q:S\to\partial\Sigma
to be the quotient map. An
interval

\mathbb{I}
is the image of a connected open set in S under
q
. Note that the inverse image
q^{-1}(\mathbb{I})
is the union of an open set with a finite number of isolated points.

Suppose
\Sigma\in{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
and consider the boundary half-node
n_{h}=n_{h}(\Sigma)
, which corresponds to the half-edge
h\in H^{B}(\Lambda)
. We say that
n_{h}

belongs
to the interval
\mathbb{I}
if

•

The corresponding node
n_{\mathrm{edge}(h)}
is contained in
\mathbb{I}
.

•

Consider the boundary
\partial\hat{\Sigma}
of the normalization
\hat{\Sigma}={\nu}^{-1}(\Sigma)
. Then we can write
{\nu}^{-1}(\mathbb{I})=\mathbb{I}_{1}\cup\mathbb{I}_{2}
, where
\mathbb{I}_{1}
is a half-open interval containing boundary point
n_{h}
and
\mathbb{I}_{2}
is a half-open interval containing boundary point
n_{\sigma_{1}h}
. According to the canonical orientation of
\hat{\Sigma}
, we then have that the half-node
n_{h}
is the starting point of
\mathbb{I}_{1}
when viewed as an interval on the (oriented) real line and, consequently, the half-node
n_{\sigma_{1}h}
is the endpoint of
\mathbb{I}_{2}
.

We refer the reader to Figure
12
for a pictorial description of this definition.

Definition 3.16
.

Let
\Gamma
be a graded
W
-spin graph and
\Lambda\in\partial^{+}\Gamma
. Suppose
U
is contained in some
\Lambda
-set with respect to

\Gamma
. A
\Lambda
-family of intervals
for
U
is a family
\{\mathbb{I}_{h}(u)\}_{h\in{H^{+}}(\Lambda),u\in U}
such that the following hold:

(1)

Each
\mathbb{I}_{h}(u)
is an interval in
\partial\Sigma_{u},
where
\Sigma_{u}=\pi^{-1}(u)
is the fiber of the universal curve
\pi:{\mathcal{C}}\to U
at
u
, or equivalently the graded surface which corresponds to
u
. The endpoints of each
\mathbb{I}_{h}(u)
vary smoothly with respect to the smooth structure of the universal curve restricted to
U
.

(2)

Suppose
h
and
h^{\prime}
are two half-edges of
\Lambda
.

(a)

If
h\notin\{h^{\prime},\sigma_{1}h^{\prime}\}
, then
\mathbb{I}_{h}(u)\cap\mathbb{I}_{h^{\prime}}(u)=\emptyset
for any
u\in U
.

(b)

If
h^{\prime}=\sigma_{1}h
, then (i) for any
u\in U\cap{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Xi}
where
h\in{H^{+}}(\Xi)
, we have
\mathbb{I}_{h}(u)\cap\mathbb{I}_{h^{\prime}}(u)=n_{\textup{edge}(h)}
, the node in
\partial\Sigma
corresponding to the half-edges
h
and
\sigma_{1}h
(so that their union forms an
X
-shape near that node, see Figure
12
(D)); (ii) for any
u\in{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Xi}
where
h\notin{H^{+}}(\Xi)
,
\mathbb{I}_{h}(u)\cap\mathbb{I}_{h^{\prime}}(u)=\emptyset
. Here, we use the locally defined injection
\iota:H(\Xi)\to H(\Lambda)
.

(3)

\mathbb{I}_{h}(u)
contains no marked points, and

(4)

the half-node
n_{h}
is the only half-node which belongs to
\mathbb{I}_{h}(u)
, whenever
u\in{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Xi}\cap U
for some partial smoothing
\Xi={\text{sm}}_{E}(\Lambda)
for some
E\subsetneq E(\Lambda)
, where
h\in{H^{+}}(\Xi)
.

When the point
u
corresponds to a stable graded disk
\Sigma
, we sometimes write
\mathbb{I}_{h}(\Sigma)
for
\mathbb{I}_{h}(u)
.

Definition 3.17
.

(1)

Let
A
be a subset of
\partial\Sigma
not containing any alternating special points. An element
w\in({\mathcal{W}_{i}})_{\Sigma}~(i=1,2)

evaluates positively at
A
, if for every
x\in A
it holds that
ev_{x}(w)>0,
i.e.,
ev_{x}(w)\in(\mathcal{J}_{i})^{\tilde{\phi}}_{x}
agrees with the positive direction of the
i^{th}
-grading.

(2)

Let
\Gamma
be any graded graph. Let
U
be a
\Lambda
-set with respect to
\Gamma
and
\mathbb{I}:=\{\mathbb{I}_{h}\}_{h}
a
\Lambda
-family of intervals for
U
. A multisection
\mathbf{s}
of
\mathcal{W}
defined in a subset of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
which includes
U\cap{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
is
(U,\mathbb{I})
-positive
(with respect to
\Gamma
) if, for any
u\in U\cap{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
and
h\in H^{+}_{j}(\Lambda)
, the
\mathcal{W}_{j}
-component of any branch
s_{i}
of
\mathbf{s}
evaluates positively at each
\mathbb{I}_{h}(u)
.
13
13

13

Note that if a positive edge of

\Lambda
is also an edge of
\Gamma
, then
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
is empty.
Thus this positivity is required to hold only along intervals disjoint from
nodes, and hence makes sense.

(3)

Consider a neighborhood
V\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
of
u\in{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
.
A multisection defined in
V\cap{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
is
positive near

u
(with respect to
\Gamma
) if there exists a
\Lambda
-neighborhood
U\subseteq V
of
u
and a
\Lambda
-family of intervals
\mathbb{I}
for
U
such that the multisection is
(U,\mathbb{I})
-positive.

Let
V
be a neighbourhood of
\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
and set

(3.4)

U_{+}=\left(V\cap{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\right)\cup\bigcup_{\Lambda\in\partial^{!}\Gamma~\text{is irrelevant}}{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}

(recalling the definition of an irrelevant graph from Definition
1.57
). We remind the reader that if
\Lambda
is positive, then
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}

is in fact empty.

Definition 3.18
.

A multisection
\mathbf{s}
defined in a set which contains a
U_{+}
as in (
3.4
) is

strongly positive (with respect to
\Gamma
)
if the following hold:

(1)

Let
\Lambda\in\partial^{!}\Gamma
satisfy condition (
1
) of the
definition of irrelevant, Definition
1.57
.
Then
\mathbf{s}(\Sigma)
evaluates positively with respect to the two gradings corresponding to the
r
-spin and
s
-spin structures
at every contracted boundary node of
\Sigma
as described in Definition
1.7
.

(2)

The multisection is positive near each point of
\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
.

(3)

Let
\Lambda\in\partial^{!}\Gamma
satisfy condition (
3
) of
