Definition
1.57
with respect to
i\in\{1,2\}

and let
\Xi
be the component of

\textup{detach}_{E(\Lambda)}(\Lambda)
with vertex
v
as in that condition.
Let
\Sigma\in{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda},
with normalization

{\nu}:\widehat{\Sigma}\to\Sigma
. Let
\widehat{\Sigma}^{\Xi}
denote
the connected component of
\widehat{\Sigma}
corresponding to
\Xi
.
Then:

(a)

The
\mathcal{W}_{i}
-component of each branch of
{\nu}^{*}(\mathbf{s}(\Sigma))

evaluates positively on some non-empty subset of the boundary of

\widehat{\Sigma}^{\Xi}
(note that by (
2.23
), the rank of
\mathcal{W}_{i}
is nonzero).

(b)

If
\Xi
becomes partially stable with no boundary half-edges after forgetting all boundary half-edges
t
with
{\text{alt}}(t)=\text{tw}(t)=(0,0)
, then
{\nu}^{*}(\mathbf{s}(\Sigma))
evaluates positively with respect to the
gradings of the two spin bundles at all of the boundary
of
\widehat{\Sigma}^{\Xi}
.

Again, we omit the extension “with respect to
\Gamma
” whenever
\Gamma
is smooth.

Remark 3.19
.

One possible alternative definition for positivity would be to require that, for all points
\Sigma
of a boundary stratum
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Lambda}
for
\Lambda\in\partial^{+}\Gamma
, the evaluation of the section of the Witten bundle at any non-alternating boundary half-node with positive twist is positive. While in low codimension this idea works, Example 3.23 in
[
6
]
shows that it does not hold in general. In fact, it would imply vanishing of the section at certain points in the moduli space. In
[
6
]
, this issue is solved by working on
{{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
rather than on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
.

We note that other versions of positivity are also possible. For example, one may continue to work on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
while imposing certain positivity constraints on the derivative of the sections of the Witten bundle. At any rate, both approaches will establish equivalent open analogues of the virtual class of the Witten bundle in our setting, while avoiding boundary strata with non-alternating boundary nodes of positive twists.

3.2.
Canonical multisections

Intuitively, canonical multisections are strongly positive multisections
whose restriction to relevant (i.e., not irrelevant in
the sense of Definition
1.57
) boundary strata are pulled back from the base moduli of these boundary strata. With the same intuition, we would like special canonical multisections to satisfy a more restrictive property. For any boundary stratum, the detaching of the dual graph associated to the stratum
is comprised of abstract vertices
v
given in Definition
3.10
.
We require that the restriction of the multisection to this boundary stratum
is pulled back from an assembling of multisections of Witten bundles on these abstract vertices.
Below, we will make this precise.

Definition 3.20
.

Let
\Gamma
be a smooth graded
{(r,s)}
-graph with only open vertices.
Let
U
be an open set of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
so that it contains
\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\cup U_{+}
, where
U_{+}
is a subset of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
as defined in (
3.4
). A smooth multisection
\mathbf{s}
of
\mathcal{W}\to U
is
canonical
if the following both hold:

(1)

The multisection
\mathbf{s}
is strongly positive as in Definition
3.17
;

(2)

For any open
\Xi\in\mathcal{V}(\Gamma)
, there exists a multisection
\mathbf{s}^{\Xi}\in C_{m}^{\infty}({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi},\mathcal{W}_{\Xi})
such that, for any
\Lambda\in\partial^{!}\Gamma\setminus\partial^{+}\Gamma
with only open
vertices,

(3.5)

\mathbf{s}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}=F_{\Lambda}^{*}(\boxplus_{\Xi\in{{\textup{Conn}}}({\mathcal{B}}\Lambda)}\mathbf{s}^{\Xi}),

using Notation
2.18
.

In the case where the set
U
is the entire moduli
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
, we say that
\mathbf{s}
is a
global canonical multisection
.

Note that the requirement (
3.5
) makes sense by Observation

3.11
and is automatic if

\Lambda=\Gamma
and
\Gamma
is connected.

Definition 3.21
.

Let
I\subseteq\Omega
. We define
{(r,s)}\text{-Graph}(I)
to be the collection of
graded open
{(r,s)}
-graphs with all boundary markings being

\emptyset
and
I(\Gamma)\subseteq I
. When
I
is understood, we will often omit
I
.

Definition 3.22
.

A
family of canonical multisections for
\mathcal{W}
bounded by
I
is a collection of (global) canonical multisections
(\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma})_{\Gamma\in{(r,s)}\text{-Graph}(I)}

with
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}\in C_{m}^{\infty}({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},\mathcal{W})
, such that
for all
\Gamma\in{(r,s)}\text{-Graph}(I)
and

\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
, (
3.5
) holds with

\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
. If
I=\Omega
, then we will omit the phrase
“bounded by
I
.”

Note that since there are cases (when
v
is closed and the anchor is Ramond) that the Witten bundle does not decompose as a direct sum, we must use coherent multisections and the assembling map

of §
2.3
(instead of usual multisections and direct sums).

Definition 3.23
.

A multisection
\mathbf{s}
of
\mathcal{W}
over
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}

is
special canonical
if
\mathbf{s}
is strongly positive and, for each abstract vertex
v\in\mathcal{V}(\Gamma)
as defined in Definition
3.10
, there exists data
\mathbf{s}^{v}
as follows:

(1)

If
v
is a closed vertex, then
\mathbf{s}^{v}
is a coherent multisection in

C_{m}^{\infty}({\mathcal{R}}_{v},\mathcal{W})
;

(2)

If
v
is an open vertex, then
\mathbf{s}^{v}\in C_{m}^{\infty}({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{v},\mathcal{W})
.

This data must satisfy the decomposition formula

\mathbf{s}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}=F_{\Lambda}^{*}\circ{{\lx@scalerel@obj{
\hbox to28.45pt{\vbox to28.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.22638pt\lower-14.22638pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0.79 0.79 L 7.87 19.69 L 0 15.75 L -7.87 19.69 L -0.79 0.79 L -19.69 7.87 L -15.75 0 L -19.69 -7.87 L -0.79 -0.79 L -7.87 -19.69 L 0 -15.75 L 7.87 -19.69 L 0.79 -0.79 L 19.69 -7.87 L 15.75 0 L 19.69 7.87 Z}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda,E({\mathcal{B}}\Lambda)}((\mathbf{s}^{v})_{v\in{{\textup{Conn}}}(\textup{detach}({\mathcal{B}}\Lambda))}),

for any
\Lambda\in\partial^{!}\Gamma\setminus\partial^{+}\Gamma
.

We can also define the analogous notions of canonical and special canonical for the cotangent line bundles
{\mathbb{L}}_{i}
.

Definition 3.24
.

A smooth multisection
\mathbf{s}
of
{\mathbb{L}}_{i}\to\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
,
where
\Gamma
is smooth and graded, is called
canonical
if
for any stable open
\Xi\in\mathcal{V}^{i}(\Gamma)
there exists a multisection
\mathbf{s}^{\Xi}\in C_{m}^{\infty}({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi},{\mathbb{L}}_{i})
such that for any
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
with
v_{i}^{*}(\Lambda)=\Xi
, we have

(3.6)

\mathbf{s}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}=\Phi_{\Lambda,i}^{*}(\mathbf{s}^{\Xi}),

where
\Phi_{\Lambda,i}
is as defined in Definition
3.10
.
A global multisection for
{\mathbb{L}}_{i}
is canonical if its restriction to
\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
is canonical.

A multisection
\mathbf{s}\in C_{m}^{\infty}({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},{\mathbb{L}}_{i})
is
special canonical
if for every stable
v\in\mathcal{V}^{i}(\Gamma)
(not necessarily open), there exists a multisection
\mathbf{s}^{v}
of
{\mathbb{L}}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{v}
such that for every
\Lambda\in\partial\Gamma
with
v=v^{*}_{i}(\Lambda)
, we have

(3.7)

\mathbf{s}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}=\Phi_{\Lambda,i}^{*}\mathbf{s}^{v}.

Note that equalities (
3.6
) and (
3.7
) use the identification of cotangent line bundles over
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
and
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma}
outlined in Observation
3.11
. Now, as we have done above for the Witten bundle, we can define a family of canonical multisections for the cotangent line bundle as follows:

Definition 3.25
.

A
family of canonical multisections for
{\mathbb{L}}_{i}
bounded by
I
is a family of canonical multisections
(\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma})_{\Gamma}
, where
\Gamma
runs over all
\Gamma\in{(r,s)}\text{-Graph}
with
i\in I(\Gamma)
and

\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}\in C_{m}^{\infty}({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},{\mathbb{L}}_{i}),
such that (
3.6
) holds. If
I=\Omega
, we omit the suffix “bounded by
I
.”

Remark 3.26
.

It is straightforward to observe that a special canonical multisection for a Witten bundle or for a tautological line
{\mathbb{L}}_{i}
is canonical.

With the definitions of canonical and special canonical outlined for the Witten bundle and for the cotangent line bundles, we have the following for the descendent Witten bundles
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
as in Definition
2.16
:

Definition 3.27
.

A multisection of the descendent Witten bundle
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
is
canonical
(respectively,
special canonical
) if the multisection when restricted to each of its direct summands are canonical (respectively, special canonical).

Definition 3.28
.

A
family of canonical multisections for descendent Witten bundles
bounded by
I
is a collection of multisections
(\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}})
,
where
\Gamma
runs over elements of
{(r,s)}\text{-Graph}
and
\mathbf{d}

runs over elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{I(\Gamma)}
, satisfying the following.
We have
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}
a canonical multisection of
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
such that for all
\Gamma\in{(r,s)}\text{-Graph}
,
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{I(\Gamma)}
, and

\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
,

\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}=F_{\Lambda}^{*}(\boxplus_{\Xi\in{{\textup{Conn}}}({\mathcal{B}}\Lambda)}\mathbf{s}^{\Xi,\mathbf{d}|_{I(\Xi)}}).

We say
(\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}})
is a
family of special canonical multisections for descendent Witten bundles bounded by
I
if it is a family of canonical multisections for descendent Witten bundles bounded by
I
where each multisection
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}
is special canonical.

Theorem 3.29
.

(1)

Consider
\Gamma
a smooth graded
W
-spin graph and a descendent Witten bundle
E=E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
, where
\mathbf{d}=(d_{i})_{i\in I}
is a vector of non-negative integers and
I(\Gamma)\subseteq I
. Then one can construct a transverse special
canonical multisection for
E\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
.

(2)

For any
I\subseteq\Omega
, one can define a family bounded by

I
of transverse special canonical multisections for
E
. In particular,
families of transverse canonical multisections exist.

(3)

We may choose the family of (2) to satisfy
the following stronger transversality condition: Consider any

\Gamma
with
I(\Gamma)\subseteq I
, any
v\in\mathcal{V}(\Gamma)
and any
subbundle
E^{\prime}\subseteq E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
of the form

E^{\prime}=\mathcal{W}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in I(v),~j\in D_{i}}{\mathbb{L}}_{i},

where, for each
i\in I(v)
, we have
D_{i}\subseteq[d_{i}]
.
Then we may require that the multisection
s^{E^{\prime},v}
obtained from
s^{v}

via the projection
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})\to E^{\prime}
is transverse to
0
.

Proof.

This theorem is similar to Lemma 4.10 of
[
6
]
, which is used to show the existence part of Theorem 3.17 in
[
6
]
, and the proof is completely analogous. Here we claim, in addition, that special canonical families can be found. Although it was not claimed in Theorem 3.17 and Lemma 4.10 of
[
6
]
, the inductive construction in the proof of that lemma gives rise automatically to a “special canonical family” in our sense, and the same inductive construction extends automatically to give canonical families in rank
2
as well. The details will be omitted.
∎

Remark 3.30
.

Note when we specify a canonical multisection
\mathbf{s}
on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
, we have also determined uniquely a multisection
\mathbf{s}^{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}
over
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}
for any graph
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
.
Similarly, for any special canonical multisection, we have moreover determined uniquely multisections
\mathbf{s}^{v}
for
v\in\mathcal{V}(\Gamma)
or
v\in\mathcal{V}^{i}(\Gamma)
which satisfy the constraints in Definitions
3.23
and
3.24
. For this reason, when we state that we have the information of a canonical or special canonical multisection
\mathbf{s}
, we will, in turn, take this to mean we have also the data of the multisections
\mathbf{s}^{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}
and
\mathbf{s}^{v}
above.

3.3.
Open and closed extended FJRW invariants

3.3.1.
Closed extended
W
-spin theory

When we perform calculations of open invariants, a generalization of the usual closed
W
-spin intersection numbers naturally emerges.
For any Fermat polynomial
W=\sum_{i=1}^{a}x_{i}^{r_{i}}
,
this generalization is defined by allowing the anchors to have twists

\text{tw}_{i}=-1
for
i
in a
subset of
[a]
, as seen in Observation
2.10
. The remaining twists must be non-negative. In this situation (as in the closed situation with only non-negative twists), one can define the class
c_{W}
analogously as in
[
4
, Section 3.1]
by the formula

c_{W}=e\left(\left(\bigoplus_{i\in[a]}R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{S}_{i}\right)^{\vee}\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}{\mathbb{L}}_{j}^{d_{j}}\right).

We define
closed extended genus 0
W
-spin intersection numbers
to be given by the formula

(3.8)

\left<\tau^{\vec{a}_{1}}_{d_{1}}\cdots\tau^{\vec{a}_{n}}_{d_{n}}\right>^{\text{ext},W}_{0}:=\left(\prod_{i\in[a]}r_{i}\right)\int_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{0,\{\vec{a}_{1},\ldots,\vec{a}_{n}\}}}\hskip-28.45274ptc_{W}.

In particular, in rank
2
for
W=x^{r}+y^{s}
, for an index set
I
with
descendent vector
\mathbf{d}=(d_{i})_{i\in I}
, we have

c_{W}=e\left((R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{S}_{1})^{\vee}\oplus(R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{S}_{2})^{\vee}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in I}{\mathbb{L}}_{i}^{d_{i}}\right),

and

(3.9)

\left<\prod_{i\in I}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\right>^{\text{ext}}:=rs\int_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}}}\hskip-28.45274ptc_{W}

are the
closed extended (genus 0)
{(r,s)}
-spin intersection numbers
.

We make several observations which will be needed later.

Observation 3.31
.

(1) The extended invariant is non-vanishing when the complex rank of the
descendent
Witten bundle agrees with the complex dimension of the moduli space, i.e.,
in the rank two case,

|I|-3={\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}-(r-2)\over r}+{\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}-(s-2)\over s}+\sum_{i\in I}d_{i},

which is equivalent to

m(I,\mathbf{d})+2s+2r=0.

(2) If we are not in the extended case, i.e., if
a_{i}\not=-1
for any
i

and
b_{i}\not=-1
for any
i
, and if furthermore some
a_{i}=r-1
or

b_{i}=r-1
, then the intersection number (
3.9
)
vanishes. This is known as Ramond vanishing, and the argument is identical
to the proof in the rank one case in
[
4
]
, Remark 2.1.

(3) The extended topological recursion relation of
[
4
]
,
Lemma 3.6 also holds for these higher rank invariants via an identical
proof. In the rank two case, this becomes, for
|I|\geq 3
with
1,2,3\in I
,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left<\tau_{d_{1}+1}^{(a_{1},b_{1})}\prod_{i\in I\setminus\{1\}}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right>^{\mathrm{ext}}&=\\
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-1\},\\
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-1\}\end{subarray}}&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\coprod B=I\setminus\{1\}\\
2,3\in A\end{subarray}}\left<\tau_{0}^{(r-2-a,s-2-b)}\prod_{i\in A}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right>^{\text{ext}}\left<\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\tau_{d_{1}}^{(a_{1},b_{1})}\prod_{i\in B}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right>^{\text{ext}}.\end{split}

Note that if none of the
a_{i}
or
b_{i}
are
-1
, then by the Ramond
vanishing of (2), we may sum over only
a\in\{0,\ldots,r-2\}

and
b\in\{0,\ldots,s-2\}
and we obtain the usual topological recursion
relation for FJRW invariants.

3.3.2.
Open intersection numbers

Using Definition
2.20
and Theorem
3.29
, the following definition makes sense:

Definition 3.32
.

Consider the situation of Theorem
3.29
above. The
open intersection number
relative to the (transverse, canonical) multisection
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
for a graded
W
-spin graph
\Gamma
is

(3.11)

\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{12}^{k_{12}(\Gamma)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma},o}:=\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}e(E;\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}|_{U_{+}\cup\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})=\#Z(\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma})\in\mathbb{Q},

where
e(E;\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}|_{U_{+}\cup\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})
is defined as in Definition
2.20
and
\#Z(\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma})
as discussed in §
2.2.3
. We emphasize that this does not just depend on the twists but also the graph
\Gamma
(e.g., this is defined for disconnected graphs).
In the special case when
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}^{W}
, we write

\left\langle\prod_{i\in I}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}}\sigma_{12}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma},o}:=\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}}e(E;\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}|_{U_{+}\cup\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}}).

When we are in the case where
\text{rank}(E)\neq\dim{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
, the integral is defined to be zero.

Remark 3.33
.

The open intersection number
\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{12}^{k_{12}(\Gamma)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s},o}
is independent of the choice of
U_{+}
.

If there are no tautological line bundles associated to a given internal marking, we will sometimes omit the subscript
0
in the notation
\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{0}

inside the brackets
\left<\right>^{\mathbf{s},o}
. If
\mathbf{s}:=(\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma})
is a canonical family of transverse multisections, then we use the notation

\displaystyle\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k(\Gamma)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s},o}:=\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k(\Gamma)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma},o}:=\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{12}^{k_{12}(\Gamma)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma},o},

\displaystyle\left\langle\prod_{i\in[l]}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}}\sigma_{12}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s},o}:=\left\langle\prod_{i\in[l]}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}}\sigma_{12}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s}^{{\Gamma^{W}_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in[l]}}}},o}.

Observation 3.34
.

The intersection numbers decompose over the connected components of the graph
\Gamma
, i.e.,

\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k(\Gamma)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s},o}=\prod_{\Lambda\in{{\textup{Conn}}}(\Gamma)}\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Lambda)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k(\Lambda)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s}^{\Lambda},o}.

We now note that the open invariants only depend on the the boundary conditions given by the canonical multisection.

Proposition 3.35
.

Let
\mathbf{s}^{*}
and
(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{*}
be two families of canonical multisections bounded by
I
. Suppose that
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}=(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{\Gamma}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}
for all boundary strata
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{Exchange}}\Gamma
. Then

\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{12}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma},o}=\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{12}\right\rangle^{(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{\Gamma},o}.

Proof.

By viewing
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
and
(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{\Gamma}
as multisections from
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
via pullback, we can consider the linear homotopy

H:=t(\mathbf{s})^{\Gamma}+(1-t)(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{\Gamma}.

Note that the homotopy
H|_{\partial([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})}
, as defined in Notation
2.18
, is independent in time as
(\mathbf{s})^{\Gamma}|_{\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}=(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{\Gamma}|_{\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}
and non-vanishing. Moreover, since both
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
and
(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{\Gamma}
are canonical, they are also strongly positive. Using Definition
3.18
(2), we have an open neighborhood of the form in (
3.4
) where both
(\mathbf{s})^{\Gamma}
and
(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{\Gamma}
are positive, hence there exists an open neighborhood
V
of
\partial^{+}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}
in
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}
so that
H
is non-vanishing on
[0,1]\times U
where

U:=(V\cap{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})\cup\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}

Perturb
H
inside
[0,1]\times({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\setminus U)
to obtain a homotopy
H^{\prime}
so that
H^{\prime}
is transverse and does not vanish in
[0,1]\times U
. By applying Lemma
2.22
, the claim follows.
∎

3.4.
Canonical homotopies

Recall Definition
2.16
for the descendent Witten bundle
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
with respect to the descendent vector

\mathbf{d}=(d_{i})_{i\in I}
where
I(\Gamma)\subset I
.
We now define a canonical homotopy between two multisections of

E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
.
The definition essentially emulates Definition
3.20
of a canonical multisection above, but with the unit interval added.

Definition 3.36
.

A
canonical homotopy
between two multisections
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
of
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
is a multisection

H\in C_{m}^{\infty}([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},\pi^{*}E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})),~\text{such that }H(0,-)=s_{0},~H(1,-)=s_{1},

where
\pi:[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
is the projection to
the second factor with the following properties:

(1)

For any
t\in[0,1]
, the multisection
H(t,-)
is strongly positive.

(2)

For any
\Lambda\in\partial^{!}\Gamma\setminus\partial^{+}\Gamma
with only open vertices, there exists a positive integer
N_{\Lambda}
and homotopies
H_{1}^{\Xi,\Lambda},\ldots,H_{N_{\Lambda}}^{\Xi,\Lambda}\in C_{m}^{\infty}([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi},\pi^{*}E_{\Xi}(\mathbf{d}))
for each
\Xi\in{{\textup{Conn}}}({\mathcal{B}}\Lambda)
, such that

(3.12)

H|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}=F_{\Lambda}^{*}\left(\biguplus_{i=1}^{N_{\Lambda}}\left(\boxplus_{\Xi\in{{\textup{Conn}}}({\mathcal{B}}\Lambda)}H_{i}^{\Xi,\Lambda}\right)\right).

We say that a canonical homotopy
H
between two multisections
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
is
transverse
if it is transverse as a multisection in
C_{m}^{\infty}([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},\pi^{*}E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d}))
.
The homotopy is said to be
simple
if
N_{\Lambda}=1
for all
\Lambda
and the multisections
H_{1}^{\Xi,\Lambda}
depend only on
\Xi
and not on
\Lambda
. In this case, we denote
H_{i}^{\Xi,\Lambda}
by
H^{\Xi}
.

Definition 3.37
.

A
family of simple canonical homotopies
(bounded by
I\subseteq\Omega
) is a collection of simple canonical homotopies
H^{\Lambda}\in C_{m}^{\infty}([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda},\pi^{*}E_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{d}))
for every graded smooth graph
\Lambda
(with
I(\Lambda)\subseteq I
) such that all
H_{i}^{\Xi,\Lambda}
appearing in (
3.12
) equal
H^{\Xi}
.

First we note that there exists a canonical homotopy between two given
canonical sections.

Lemma 3.38
.

Transverse canonical homotopies between any transverse canonical
multisections
\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathbf{s}_{1}\in C_{m}^{\infty}({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d}))
exist.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma 4.11 in
[
6
]

with
E_{1}=0,~E_{2}=E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d}),~A=\partial^{0}\Gamma
. While the bundle
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
is different in our context rather than the context in
[
6
]
, one can still use Proposition 6.2(3) of
[
6
]
to obtain a finite set of multisections
w_{j}
of
E_{2}
that span each fibre as desired in the proof. The rest of the proof is the same and hence will be omitted.
∎

We will now show that there exists a family of
simple
canonical homotopies that, under certain dimensionality and boundary marking constraints, is transverse to the zero multisection of the bundle
\pi^{*}E_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{d})
. We will also be able to impose other properties on various boundary strata that will help us compute open invariants in later sections. Before we start the lemma, we will require the following observation regarding parities of the Witten bundle and the dimension of the moduli space when one has few roots and no internal tails.

Lemma 3.39
.

Suppose
\Lambda
is a stable, smooth and graded
W
-spin graph without internal tails and with at most
3
boundary tails with twist
(r-2,s-2)
. Then
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{W}_{\Lambda}\neq\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}+1
.

Proof.

As usual, we let
k_{12}=k_{12}(\Lambda)
and
k_{i}=k_{i}(\Lambda)
(see Definition
1.29
(2) or the start of Subsection
2.4
). Then, by using (
2.22
) and (
2.23
) in a similar way as done in Proposition
2.31
(a), we have that

(3.13)

k_{1}+k_{12}=rm_{1}+1;\qquad k_{2}+k_{12}=sm_{2}+1

for some
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and that

\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{W}_{\Lambda}=(r-2)m_{1}+(s-2)m_{2}.

Recall that the dimension formula for the open moduli space in Equation (
2.2
) gives us that

\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}=k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{12}-3=rm_{1}+sm_{2}-k_{12}-1.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{W}_{\Lambda}=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}+1
. Then

k_{12}=2m_{1}+2m_{2}.

This implies that
k_{12}
is even and, since we have assumed that
k_{12}\leq 3
, we then have that it is either
0
or
2
. If
k_{12}=0
then
m_{1}=m_{2}=0
, hence
\Lambda
is not a stable graph. If
k_{12}=2
, then either
m_{1}
or
m_{2}
is
0
; however, if
m_{i}=0
, then
k_{i}+k_{12}\geq k_{12}=2>1
, contradicting Equation (
3.13
).
∎

Lemma 3.40
.

Fix
I\subseteq\Omega
. Suppose
\mathbf{s}_{0}
and
\mathbf{s}_{1}
are two families of transverse canonical multisections bounded by
I
.
Then one can construct a family
H^{*}
of simple canonical homotopies bounded by
I

and for each
A\subseteq I
a distinct choice of time
t_{A}\in(0,1)
between
\mathbf{s}_{0}
and
\mathbf{s}_{1}

with the following properties.

Let
\Gamma
be any smooth, graded

W
-spin graph with only open vertices and
I(\Gamma)\subseteq I
. Fix
\mathbf{d}=(d_{i})_{i\in I}
a vector of descendents.

(1)

Suppose that
\Gamma
satisfies one of the following hypotheses:

(a)

\Gamma
is irrelevant;

(b)

\Gamma
is connected and

\operatorname{rank}E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})\geq\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}+2
;

(c)

\Gamma
contains two connected components
\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2}
with
distinct internal marking sets, i.e.,
I(\Xi_{1})\neq I(\Xi_{2})
, such that
\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{i}}(\mathbf{d})=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{i}}+1
and
k_{12}(\Xi_{i})\leq 2
.

Then
H^{\Gamma}
is nowhere vanishing.

(2)

If
\Gamma
is connected and one of the following holds:

(a)

\operatorname{rank}(E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d}))=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}+1
and
k_{12}(\Gamma)\leq 2,
or

(b)

\operatorname{rank}(E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d}))=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
and
k_{12}(\Gamma)\leq 1
,

then
H^{\Gamma}
is transverse when restricted to any stratum of
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
, that is,
H^{\Gamma}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}
is transverse to the zero multisection of
\pi^{*}E_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{d})\to[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
for any
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
.

(3)

If
\Gamma
is connected,
we have:

(a)

If
\operatorname{rank}(E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d}))=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}+1
and
k_{12}(\Gamma)\leq 2
then
H^{\Gamma}
will be non-zero in

([0,1]\setminus\{t_{I(\Gamma)}\})\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
.
Note by Lemma
3.39
, if
I(\Gamma)=\emptyset
,
this case does not occur.

(b)

If
\operatorname{rank}(E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d}))=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
and
k_{12}(\Gamma)\leq 1
, then for any
A\subseteq I
we have

H^{\Gamma}|_{\{t_{A}\}\times({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\setminus\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})}\pitchfork 0

when considered as a multisection of

E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})\to\{t_{A}\}\times({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\setminus\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})\simeq{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\setminus\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}.

(Note that the point here is that while the multisection
H^{\Gamma}

is transversal to the zero-section of
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
over

[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
, there will be a finite number of times

t
for which the restriction of
H^{\Gamma}
to
\{t\}\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}

is not transversal to the zero-section, and we want the set of such times to be disjoint from the set
\{t_{A}\}_{A\subseteq I}
.)

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathbf{s}_{1}
be as in the statement. We will construct a family of simple canonical homotopies
{H}^{*}
which satisfies the requirements of the various items.

Note that for disconnected
\Gamma
, the homotopies
H^{\Gamma}
are
determined by (
3.12
) (using simplicity)
and the corresponding homotopies

H^{\Lambda}
for
\Lambda\in{{\textup{Conn}}}(\Gamma)
. Thus we will construct homotopies

{H}^{\Gamma}
for smooth connected graded
\Gamma
with
I(\Gamma)\subseteq I
by induction on
n=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
. If Items (2) and (3) are established,
note that (1)(c) then follows from (3)(a).

The case
n=-1
is trivial.
Suppose we have constructed a homotopy for any
\Gamma
such that

\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}<n
.
Now take
\Gamma
satisfying
\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}=n
.
We will analyze boundary strata of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
indexed by

\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
with no internal
edges.
If
\Lambda
has a contracted boundary edge, we define
H^{\Lambda}
as the positive, linear homotopy

H^{\Lambda}(t,-)=(1-t)\mathbf{s}^{\Lambda}_{0}+t\mathbf{s}^{\Lambda}_{1}.

If
\Lambda
does not have a contracted boundary edge, the simple canonical homotopy
H^{\Lambda}
is determined
inductively by (
3.12
) to be

(3.14)

{H}^{\Lambda}=F_{\Lambda}^{*}\big(\boxplus_{i=1}^{v}H^{\Xi_{i}}\big),

where
\Xi_{1},\ldots,\Xi_{v}
are the components of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
, so that
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}=\prod_{i=1}^{v}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{i}}
.
Thus, the homotopy we are trying to construct is determined at times
t=0
and

1
and on the boundary strata of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
. Hence, we now can define a multisection
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
on the boundary

\partial([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})=\big(\{0,1\}\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\big)\cup\big([0,1]\times\partial({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})\big)

via:

\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}|_{\{\epsilon\}\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}(\epsilon,-)=\mathbf{s}_{\epsilon}^{\Gamma},~\epsilon\in\{0,1\},~\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}=H^{\Lambda},~\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma.

Extend
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
to a set
U_{+}
of the form

(3.15)

U_{+}:=\big(V\cap([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})\big)\cup\partial([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}),

where

V
is a neighborhood of
[0,1]\times\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
in
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
.
14
14

14

This
U_{+}
is analogous to that in Equation (
3.4
) but is not the same, due to the interval for the homotopy.

This extension can be done by the same argument of Lemma 6.5 in
[
6
]
, steps 1-3.
We may do this in such a way so
that
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}(t,-)
is strongly positive.

Now that we have established a provisional function
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
on the set
U_{+}
containing the boundary of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\times[0,1]
,
we will adapt
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
on the interior of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\times[0,1]
to obtain a homotopy
H^{\Gamma}
that will satisfy the properties described in the Lemma. This will require a case-by-case analysis depending on the properties of
\Gamma
.

Case 1:
\Gamma
irrelevant.
Suppose
\Gamma
is irrelevant.
Since
\Gamma
is smooth and connected, cases
(
1
) and (
2
) of
Definition
1.57
do not apply
and we are thus in case (
3
). Thus
\Gamma
consists of a single vertex
v
and
there is an
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
\text{tw}_{i}={\text{alt}}_{i}=0
for all boundary tails
adjacent to
v
.

If
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma
, then each edge
of
\Lambda
has exactly one half-edge with
\text{tw}_{i}={\text{alt}}_{i}=0
.
Thus
\Lambda
will have one fewer half-edge with
\text{tw}_{i}\not=0
than
it has vertices.
So by the pigeon-hole principle,
\Lambda
must contain a vertex
with all adjacent half-edges having
\text{tw}_{i}={\text{alt}}_{i}=0
, and
\Lambda

is also irrelevant.
Hence there will be some connected component of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
such that each boundary tail has
\text{tw}_{i}={\text{alt}}_{i}=0
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
. By the condition of Definition
3.18
(
3
) of strongly positive,
we have that for any
(t,\Sigma)\in U_{+}
the
\mathcal{W}_{i}
-component of
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}(t,\Sigma)
evaluates positively on at least one boundary point of
\partial\Sigma
.

We can extend
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
continuously from
U_{+}

to an open neighbourhood
U
of
U_{+}

which contains a neighborhood
W
of
\partial([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})
.
Further, by continuity,
we may do this in such a way so that

\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}(t,\Sigma)
evaluates positively on at least one boundary point of
\partial\Sigma
for each
(t,\Sigma)\in U
,
after possibly shrinking
W
.

Now let
U^{\prime}
be another open neighborhood of
U_{+}
whose closure is contained in
U
, and take two non-negative functions
\rho_{0}
and
\rho_{1}
over the moduli
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
that sum to the constant function
1
and so that

(3.16)

\displaystyle\rho_{0}|_{U^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=1\text{ and has support }\text{Supp}(\rho_{0})\subseteq U\text{ while }

\displaystyle\rho_{1}|_{([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})\setminus U}

\displaystyle=1\text{ and has support }\text{Supp}(\rho_{1})\subseteq([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})\setminus U^{\prime}.

By
[
6
]
, Observation 7.6, we may find a section

s_{+}
of
C^{\infty}({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},\mathcal{W}_{i})
which is nowhere vanishing
on
{{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
(see Definition
3.3
),
and with the property that for
\Sigma\in{{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
,

s_{+}(\Sigma)
evaluates positively on
\partial\Sigma
.
15
15

15

In
[
6
]
, such a section was constructed on the moduli
of smooth disks, but there is no difficulty in constructing the section
on the whole moduli away from the boundary, i.e., extending the
construction across singular disks with only internal nodes.

Viewing
s_{+}
as a section of
C^{\infty}({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d}))
, we may pull it back to a section
\tilde{s}_{+}
of
C^{\infty}([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d}))
and define the homotopy

H^{\Gamma}=\rho_{0}\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}+\rho_{1}\tilde{s}_{+}.

This homotopy satisfies our requirements, namely, it is nowhere vanishing.
In particular, statement (1)(a) of the lemma is satisfied
if
\Gamma
is irrelevant and connected. If
\Gamma
is irrelevant
but not connected, then one of its connected components is irrelevant and (1)(a)
still holds.

Case 2:
\Gamma
relevant.
Suppose now that
\Gamma
is relevant.
Recall that we are proceeding by induction on
\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
. We
take special care in three cases for which the statement of the lemma
requires additional structure, namely

(a)

\operatorname{rank}E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})\geq\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}+2
;

(b)

\operatorname{rank}E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}+1
and
k_{12}(\Gamma)\leq 2
;

(c)

\operatorname{rank}E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
and
k_{12}(\Gamma)\leq 1
.

For all other relevant graphs
\Gamma
,
we will extend
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
to an arbitrary homotopy
H^{\Gamma}

on
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
at the end of each induction step.

We will go through the three cases consecutively, extending
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
to a homotopy
H^{\Gamma}
that satisfies the required properties given in the statement of the lemma. In doing so, we will be considering boundary
strata corresponding to

\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
with no internal
edges, and
study the vanishing properties of
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}
.
If
\Lambda
is irrelevant, then it follows inductively from (1)(a)
that
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
does not vanish on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
. We note that the property that the homotopy is nonvanishing is a stronger condition than any other condition that we require in the statement of the lemma. Hence
we will be able to restrict attention below to the case
that
\Lambda
is relevant. In particular,
Observation
3.12
applies, and we use the notation
\alpha,\beta,\nu
and
\sigma
of that observation.

(a)

\alpha\geq 2
. In this case, we extend
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}

to
H^{\Gamma}
so that
H^{\Gamma}
is nonvanishing:
from (
3.3
) and
the pigeonhole principle, as
\alpha+\beta-1\geq 1
,
there must be a connected component

\Xi
of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
such that
\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}\leq\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi}(\mathbf{d})-2
.
By the inductive hypothesis of (1)(b) of the statement of the lemma,

H^{\Xi}
is nowhere vanishing, and therefore also
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
will not vanish at
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
. Extend
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
transversally to all
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},
and call the result
H^{\Gamma}
. This extension
may be constructed as in Lemma 3.54 of
[
42
]
or Lemma 4.11 of

[
6
]
.
Note that, in this case, since
\dim([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})<\operatorname{rank}E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
, the transversality assures that
H^{\Gamma}
is nowhere vanishing, proving the inductive step for the case given in (1)(b) of the statement of the lemma.

(b)

\alpha=1
and
k_{12}(\Gamma)\leq 2
. Again consider a relevant

\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
. Suppose first
that
\beta\geq 1
. Then
\alpha+\beta-1\geq 1
and
(
3.3
) shows again there is a stable component

\Xi_{i}
of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
with
\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{i}}\leq\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{i}}(\mathbf{d})-2
.
Thus
\widetilde{H}_{\Gamma}
again inductively does not vanish on

{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
.

Next, assume
\beta=0
, so that necessarily
\sigma=0
. There are now two possible
subcases:
(i)
\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{i}}\leq\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi}(\mathbf{d})-2
for some
i
;
(ii)
\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{i}}=\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{i}}(\mathbf{d})-1
for all
i
.
In case (i), then inductively as before
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
is
non-vanishing on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
.

In case (ii), first note that in any event
\beta=0
implies
that no half-node of
\Lambda
has twist

(r-2,s-2)
, i.e., is fully twisted.
Indeed, if such a half-edge
h
exists, then the opposite half-edge

\sigma_{1}(h)
has
\text{tw}={\text{alt}}=(0,0)
,
and hence
\sigma_{1}(h)
is forgotten in
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
, i.e.,
\beta\geq 1
.

By (
2.23
), we have for each
i
,

(3.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}k_{1}(\Xi_{i})+k_{2}(\Xi_{i})+k_{12}(\Xi_{i})-3\equiv{}&\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{i}}\\
\equiv{}&\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{i}}(\mathbf{d})-1\\
\equiv{}&k_{1}(\Xi_{i})+k_{2}(\Xi_{i})-1\pmod{2}.\end{split}

As
k_{12}(\Gamma)
is assumed to be at
most
2
, we thus see that each component of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
has either
zero or two fully twisted boundary points and at most one component can have two fully twisted boundary points.
Note by Lemma
3.39
that

I(\Xi_{i})\not=\emptyset
for any
i
, and necessarily the
I(\Xi_{i})

are disjoint. Thus (1)(c) applies inductively to conclude that

H^{\Lambda}
is nowhere vanishing in this case also.

Thus over
U_{+}
, we have that the function
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
is transverse to the zero section, so we may then extend it as before to a global transverse homotopy. It will have a finite number of zeroes.
By slightly perturbing the homotopy, we may assume that there is no
x\in{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
with two different times
t_{1},t_{2}
such that (local branches of)
the homotopy vanishes at
(t_{1},x),(t_{2},x)
.

We further modify the homotopy to guarantee that all zeroes occur
at time
t_{I(\Gamma)}
. Let
\pi_{i}
,
i=1,2
be the projection
of
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
onto the
i^{th}
factor.
Let
S
be the collection of zeroes of branches where the homotopy vanishes. Choose a diffeomorphism
\psi:[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\to[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
which satisfies

(i)

\pi_{2}\circ\psi=\pi_{2}
;

(ii)

\psi|_{([0,1]\times\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})\cup U_{+}}=\textup{id}
;

(iii)

\pi_{1}(\psi(S))=t_{I(\Gamma)}
.

We define
{H}^{\Gamma}=\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}\circ\psi^{-1},
where we use the canonical trivialization of
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})|_{[0,1]\times x},
for any
x\in{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
. This proves the inductive steps for the cases given in (2)(a) and (3)(a).

(c)

\operatorname{rank}E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}

and
k_{12}(\Gamma)\leq 1
. By the analogous parity computation as in (
3.17
), we have that
k_{12}(\Gamma)=1
.
Now let
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
be relevant
as before. Again, if
\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{i}}\leq\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{i}}(\mathbf{d})-2

for some connected component
\Xi_{i}
of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
, then necessarily
\widetilde{H}_{\Gamma}

is non-vanishing on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
. So assume now this does not
occur. Then we have a further subdivision of cases, with notation as
in (
3.3
).

(i)

\beta=0
;

(ii)

\beta\geq 1
.

In case (i), necessarily
\sigma=0
, and (
3.3
) then
gives

\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}=\operatorname{rank}E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})-(\nu-1)
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
k_{12}(\Xi_{1})=1
and
k_{12}(\Xi_{j})=0
for all
2\leq j\leq\nu
. By a parity computation similar to (
3.17
) above, we have that

(3.18)

\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{j}}=\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{j}}(\mathbf{d})+k_{12}(\Xi_{j})-1+2p_{j}

for some integers
p_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
and with the constraint that
\sum_{j}p_{j}=0
.
If any of these
p_{j}
are nonzero, then necessarily we have

\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{i}}\leq\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{i}}(\mathbf{d})-2
for some
i
, which we
assumed did not occur.
Thus we may assume that
p_{j}=0
for all
j
.
If
\nu\geq 3
, then
\Xi_{2},\Xi_{3}
satisfy

\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{j}}+1=\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{j}}(\mathbf{d})
.
Thus we see inductively from statement (1)(c) of the lemma
that
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
restricted to
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
is
nowhere vanishing, hence again transversal. Next, suppose
\nu=2
.
We know that
H^{\Xi_{2}}
on
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{2}}

is only zero when
t=t_{A}
, where
A=I(\Xi_{2})
.
Thus when
t\not=t_{A}
, the homotopy
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}

restricted to
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
is non-vanishing,
hence again transversal.
On the other hand, the homotopy
H^{\Xi_{1}}
on
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{1}}
,
restricted to
\{t_{A}\}\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{1}}
, is transverse to the
zero section.
Thus the homotopy
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
restricted to
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}

is transverse to the zero section at time
t_{A}
,
and so is transverse at all times.

In case (ii),

\beta=\sum_{j=1}^{\nu-\sigma}k_{12}(\Xi_{j})-1

(bearing in mind that
k_{12}(\Gamma)=1
and none of the partially
stable components contain a root).
Note that (
3.18
) still holds for

1\leq j\leq\nu-\sigma

for some integers
p_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Thus by (
3.3
)
we obtain
\sum_{j=1}^{\nu-\sigma}(k_{12}(\Xi_{j})-1+2p_{j})=-\beta-(\nu-\sigma)+1
.
Simplifying, we have that

\sum_{j=1}^{v-\sigma}(k_{12}(\Xi_{j})+2p_{j})=-\beta+1.

Consequently, if
\beta>1
, by the pigeonhole principle, there must exist a

j
such that
\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{j}}\leq\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{j}}(\mathbf{d})-2
,
again contradicting our assumption.
If
\beta=1
and
\nu-\sigma\geq 2
,
we must have either again

\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{j}}\leq\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{j}}(\mathbf{d})-2

for some
j
or
\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{j}}=\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{j}}(\mathbf{d})-1

for two choices of
j
. Thus by (1)(b) or (c)
again the restriction of
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
to
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}

will not vanish. Finally, if
\beta=1
and
\nu-\sigma=1
, we necessarily
have
k_{12}(\Xi_{1})=2
and

\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{1}}=\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{1}}(\mathbf{d})-1
, so by Item (2)(a)
of the statement of the lemma applied inductively,
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}

is transverse to the zero section on
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
in this case also.
Thus,
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
is transverse on
\partial([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})
, showing item (2)(b) of the lemma.

Finally, to ensure (3)(b) of the lemma, we proceed as follows.
For any

t\in T:=\{t_{I(\Gamma)}\ |\ I(\Gamma)\subseteq I,I(\Gamma)\neq\emptyset\}

and a boundary point
(t,x)
in which
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
vanishes, extend
\widetilde{H}_{\Gamma}
to a neighborhood
U_{t,x}
of
(t,x)
in

[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}

in a transverse way.
There is no difficulty in choosing the extension such that the
restriction of this extension to

U_{t,x}\cap(\{t\}\times({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\setminus\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}))
is transverse.
Now, extend
\widetilde{H}_{\Gamma}
to each
{\{t\}\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}

for
t\in T
so that, as a multisection of
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})

on
\{t\}\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
, it is transversal on
\{t\}\times{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
.
Again extend
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
transversally to the whole space

[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
. We call the result
H^{\Gamma}
.

Lastly, as stated above, for the remaining graded, connected, relevant graphs
\Gamma
that do not fit in the above cases, we extend
\widetilde{H}^{\Gamma}
to an arbitrary homotopy
H^{\Gamma}
, as the claim requires no additional structure for them.
∎

Finally, we note that there is a family of transverse simple canonical homotopies between multisections of tautological bundles.

Lemma 3.41
.

Suppose
d_{1}>0
and let
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}=\mathbf{d}-(1,0\ldots,0)
be a vector of descendents. Take
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
to be a graph associated to a boundary strata. Let
\mathbf{s}
be a family of canonical transverse multisections for
E_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime})\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
, and let
(\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\Lambda})_{\Lambda},(\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\Lambda})_{\Lambda}
be families of transverse multisections of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
. Assume that
\mathbf{s}_{0}^{*}
is a canonical family. Then one can find another family
\rho^{*}
of
canonical multisections of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}

such that the family of homotopies
H^{*}
between
\mathbf{s}^{*}\boxplus\mathbf{s}_{0}^{*}
and
\mathbf{s}^{*}\boxplus\mathbf{s}_{1}^{*}

(3.19)

H^{\Gamma}=\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}\boxplus\left((1-t)\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\Gamma}+t(1-t)\rho^{\Gamma}+t\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\Gamma}\right)

is transverse when restricted to any stratum of
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
with
I(\Gamma)\subseteq I,~\operatorname{rank}E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})\geq\dim([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})
.

Proof.

The proof here is identical to the proof of Lemma 4.11 in
[
6
]
in the case where
E_{1}=E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d}^{\prime}),E_{2}={\mathbb{L}}_{1}
, and
A=\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
. There, the claim only involved a single graph and did not involve families, but adapting the proof for families adds no difficulty.
∎

3.5.
Chamber Indices and Symmetric canonical multisections

In this subsection, we give the notation necessary to discuss the
set of all possible tuples of
open FJRW invariants. In particular, we first recall
Definition
2.32
that
{\text{Balanced}}(I,\mathbf{d})
is defined to be the collection of connected, rooted, graphs with internal markings
I
balanced with respect to
\mathbf{d}
. We recall from
(
0.3
) that for

I\subseteq\Omega
we set

{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)=\prod_{J\subseteq I}\prod_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}}\mathbb{Q}^{{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})}.

We may now define the crucial expression
\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},{\nu})
.
While it may appear intimidating, it arises naturally from the period
integrals discussed in the next section.

Notation 3.42
.

Let
{\nu}:=({\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}})\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(J)
.
We define
\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},{\nu})
to be

\sum_{h=1}^{|J|}\frac{1}{h!}\sum_{J_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup J_{h}=J}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{k_{1}(i)\}_{i=1}^{h},~k_{1}(i)=r(J_{i})~(mod~r),\\
\{k_{2}(i)\}_{i=1}^{h},~k_{2}(i)=s(J_{i})~(mod~s),\\
sk_{1}(i)+rk_{2}(i)=m(J_{i},\mathbf{d})\end{subarray}}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1+\sum_{i=1}^{h}k_{1}(i)}{r})\Gamma(\frac{1+\sum_{i=1}^{h}k_{2}(i)}{s})}{\Gamma(\frac{1+r(J)}{r})\Gamma(\frac{1+s(J)}{s})}\prod_{i=1}^{h}{\nu}_{\Gamma_{0,k_{1}(i),k_{2}(i),1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in J_{j}},\mathbf{d}|_{J_{i}}}},

where the numbers
r(J),s(J)
and
m(J,\mathbf{d})
are as defined in Notation
2.30
, the graph
\Gamma_{0,k_{1}(i),k_{2}(i),1,J_{i}}
is as defined in Notation
2.27
, and
\Gamma(\cdot)
denotes the Gamma function.

In general for an element
{\nu}\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)
,
{\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}

may depend on the individual labels of the internal legs of
\Gamma

and not just their twists. However, let
\sigma:I\rightarrow I
be a
twist-preserving bijection, i.e., satisfying
\text{tw}\circ\sigma=\text{tw}
.
We define for any graph
\Gamma
with

I(\Gamma)\subseteq I
the graph
\sigma(\Gamma)
to be the same as
\Gamma

but with the internal label
i
replaced with
\sigma(i)
for all
i\in I(\Gamma)
. We define
\mathbf{d}\circ\sigma
to be the analogous
action on the descendent vectors in
\mathbb{Z}^{I(\Gamma)}_{\geq 0}
.
This induces a permutation transformation

\sigma^{*}:{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)\rightarrow{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)

via
\sigma^{*}({\nu})_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}={\nu}_{\sigma(\Gamma),\mathbf{d}\circ\sigma}
.
We then define

{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)\subseteq{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)

to be the subset of elements fixed under
\sigma^{*}
for all
twist-preserving bijections
\sigma:I\rightarrow I
.

For any family of canonical multisections
\mathbf{s}
bounded by
I
,
we obtain
{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}}\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)
via

(3.20)

{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}=\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{12}^{k_{12}(\Gamma)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s},o}.

Definition 3.43
.

A family
\mathbf{s}
of canonical multisections bounded by
I
is
symmetric

if, for any
\sigma:I\rightarrow I
a twist preserving bijection, and

\Gamma
with
I(\Gamma)\subseteq I
,
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{I(\Gamma)}
,
the multisections
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
and
\mathbf{s}^{\sigma(\Gamma)}
of

E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
and
E_{\sigma(\Gamma)}(\mathbf{d}\circ\sigma)

agree under the natural isomorphism
\Psi_{\sigma}:{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\cong{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\sigma(\Gamma)}
.

Note that if
\mathbf{s}
is a symmetric canonical family bounded by
I
, then

{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}}\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
.

It is elementary and useful to know that symmetric families exist:

Observation 3.44
.

Given any family
\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}^{*}
of canonical multisections
bounded by a finite set
I
, define a family of multisections as follows. For

\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})
for
J\subseteq I
, define the multisection

\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
of
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
via

\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}:=\biguplus_{\sigma\in{\text{Maps}}(I,I)}\Psi_{\sigma}^{*}\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}^{\Gamma}

where
{\text{Maps}}(I,I)
is the set of twist-preserving bijections.
Then
\mathbf{s}
is a symmetric family of canonical multisections.

We can now define several key subsets of
{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)
and
{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
.

Definition 3.45
.

We define

\displaystyle\Omega_{r,s}(I):={}

\displaystyle\{{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}}\,|\,\hbox{$\mathbf{s}$ is a family of canonical
multisections bounded by $I$}\},

\displaystyle{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I):={}

\displaystyle\{{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}}\,|\,\hbox{$\mathbf{s}$ is a symmetric
family of canonical multisections bounded by $I$}\}.

Proposition 3.46
.

When
I
is finite, we have that
{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)=\Omega_{r,s}(I)\cap{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
.

Proof.

The forward inclusion is trivial. For the reverse inclusion,
let
\mathbf{s}
be a canonical family of multisections with
{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}}\in\Omega_{r,s}(I)\cap{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
. Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}
be the symmetric
family constructed in Observation
3.44
.
Then it is immediate
that
{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}}={\nu}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}}
, hence the result.
∎

One of the key results of the paper (Corollary
5.14
) will be a characterization of the sets
\Omega_{r,s}(I)
,
{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
. They will coincide with the set of
chamber indices:

Definition 3.47
.

A
chamber index
is a point

{\nu}=({\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}})\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)

where the following hold:

(1)

{\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{0}}=1
if
|I(\Gamma)|=1
and
{\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{0}}=-1
if
|I(\Gamma)|=0
for all
\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(\subseteq I)
.

(2)

For
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
with
|I^{\prime}|=1
,
\mathbf{d}=(d)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{I^{\prime}}
,
we have

\mathcal{A}(I^{\prime},\mathbf{d},{\nu})=(-1)^{d}.

(3)

For any
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
with
|I^{\prime}|\geq 2
, and
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{I^{\prime}}
with
m(I^{\prime},\mathbf{d})\geq sr(I^{\prime})+rs(I^{\prime})
, we have

\mathcal{A}(I^{\prime},\mathbf{d},{\nu})=0.

We define

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{r,s}(I):={}

\displaystyle\{{\nu}\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)\,|\,\hbox{${\nu}$ is a chamber index}\}

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}(I):={}

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)\cap{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I).

We call a chamber index
\nu\in\Upsilon_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}(I)

symmetric
.

4.
The
B
-Model

In this section, we describe the enumerative theory associated to a Landau-Ginzburg
B
-model, due to Saito and Givental. We use the description given in the case of the
B
-model of FJRW theory
by He, Li, Li, Saito, Shen and Webb in
[
33
]
and

[
24
]
. We describe the
B
-model in terms
of oscillatory integrals, reviewing some of the details from

[
19
]
, and show how to interpret these oscillatory
integrals in terms of the
\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},{\nu})
of
Notation
3.42
. In doing so, we will see how the
set
\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)
defined in Definition
3.47

plays the role of tuples of mirror
B
-model invariants.
We will then explore the wall-crossing group and show that

\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)
is a torsor for this group, hence giving a clear
structure to this set.

4.1.
The state space

We consider the Landau-Ginzburg model

\left(X=\mathbb{C}^{n},W=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{r_{i}}\right).

We do not allow for a group of symmetries. We shall quickly review
Saito-Givental theory in this context, sending the reader to

[
19
]
for more details. For a much more in-depth
exposition, see
[
23
]
, Chapter 2 and references therein.

A principal object of study is the twisted de Rham complex

(4.1)

(\Omega_{X}^{\bullet},d+\hbar^{-1}dW\wedge-),

where
\Omega_{X}^{i}
is the sheaf of algebraic

i
-forms on
X
and
\hbar\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
is an auxiliary parameter.

We then have the following calculation of the hypercohomology of
the twisted de Rham complex (see
[
19
]
, Propositon 2.1):

Proposition 4.1
.

Consider the Landau-Ginzburg model
(X,W)
in (
4.1
). Then the hypercohomology group
\mathbb{H}^{n}(X,(\Omega_{X}^{\bullet},d+\hbar^{-1}dW\wedge-))
has dimension
\prod_{i=1}^{n}(r_{i}-1)
and is generated by the basis

M=\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{a_{i}}d\mathbf{x}\ \middle|\ 0\leq a_{i}\leq r_{i}-2\right\},

where
d\mathbf{x}=dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}
.

There is a homology group dual to the hypercohomology group
\mathbb{H}^{n}(X,(\Omega_{X}^{\bullet},d+\hbar^{-1}dW\wedge-))
. Consider the relative homology

H_{n}(X,\operatorname{Re}W/\hbar\ll 0;\mathbb{C}).

There is a natural perfect pairing

(4.2)

\displaystyle H_{n}(X,\operatorname{Re}W/\hbar\ll 0;\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{H}^{n}(X,(\Omega_{X}^{\bullet},d+\hbar^{-1}dW\wedge-))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{C},

\displaystyle(\Xi,\omega)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\int_{\Xi}e^{W/\hbar}\omega.

Thus there must be a dual basis for
H_{n}(X,\operatorname{Re}W/\hbar\ll 0;\mathbb{C})
for any basis of the hypercohomology group.
In fact, in the case of
W=x^{r}
, Li, Li, Saito and Shen in
[
33
]
define a
good basis
for

H_{n}(X,\operatorname{Re}W/\hbar\ll 0;\mathbb{C})
as a basis of cycles

\{\Xi_{\mu}\,|\,\text{$0\leq\mu\leq r-2$}\}

satisfying the condition

\int_{\Xi_{\mu}}x^{\mu^{\prime}}e^{x^{r}/\hbar}d\mathbf{x}=\delta_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\,\text{ for all $0\leq\mu,\mu^{\prime}\leq r-2$},

where
\delta
denotes the Kronecker delta function. In

[
19
]
, we give a concrete description of this good basis
in the case where
W=x^{r}
, but only its existence is relevant here.
If instead
W=\sum_{i}x_{i}^{r_{i}}
, and
\Xi^{r_{i}}_{0},\ldots,\Xi^{r_{i}}_{r_{i}-2}

is the good basis for
x_{i}^{r_{i}}
, then one can bootstrap from the good basis given in
[
33
]
, as is done in
[
24
]
. Consider the following basis for
H_{n}(X,\operatorname{Re}W/\hbar\ll 0;\mathbb{C})
:

\{\Xi_{\mu}:=\Xi^{r_{1}}_{\mu_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Xi^{r_{n}}_{\mu_{n}}\,|\,\mu\in D\}.

where

D=\{\mu=(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})\,|\,\hbox{$0\leq\mu_{i}\leq r_{i}-2$ for
$1\leq i\leq n$}\}.

This is a good basis for
W
. We remark that the basis
\Xi_{\mu}
varies with
\hbar
.
Note that there is a clear bijection between
D
and
M
given by mapping
\mu
to
x^{\mu}d\mathbf{x}
where
x^{\mu}:=\prod_{i}x_{i}^{\mu_{i}}
for
\mu\in D
. We then
have the identity

(4.3)

\int_{\Xi_{\mu}}x^{\mu^{\prime}}e^{(x_{1}^{r_{1}}+\cdots+x_{n}^{r_{n}})/\hbar}d\mathbf{x}=\delta_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}.

The following more general integrals will also be important for us,
see also
[
19
]
, Lemma 2.4:

Lemma 4.2
.

For any cycle
\Xi\in H_{1}(\mathbb{C},\re x^{r}/\hbar;\mathbb{C})
, and
for all
r,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have:

(4.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Xi}x^{nr+k}e^{x^{r}/\hbar}dx={}&(-1)^{n}\hbar^{n}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}(i-1+\frac{k+1}{r})\right)\int_{\Xi}x^{k}e^{x^{r}/\hbar}dx\\
={}&(-1)^{n}\hbar^{n}\frac{\Gamma\left(n+\frac{k+1}{r}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{k+1}{r}\right)}\int_{\Xi}x^{k}e^{x^{r}/\hbar}dx.\end{split}

Proof.

Integration by parts.
∎

Now write
W_{0}=\sum_{i}x_{i}^{r_{i}}
.
Recall that the elements
x^{\mu}
for
\mu\in D
are a basis for the Milnor ring viewed as a vector space. We introduce coordinates
y_{\mu}
on the universal unfolding of
W_{0}
, parameterized
by a germ
\mathcal{M}
of the origin in a vector space with basis
\{x^{\mu}\,|\,\mu\in D\}
.
The versal deformation
W
for
W_{0}
on
\mathcal{M}\times X
is then
given by

W=x_{1}^{r_{1}}+\cdots+x_{n}^{r_{n}}+\sum_{\mu\in D}y_{\mu}x^{\mu}.

Given
f\in\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}][[y_{\mu}]]
,
we can consider the following collection of oscillatory integrals, each of
which we view as a formal power series:

\int_{\Xi_{\mu}}e^{W/\hbar}fd\mathbf{x}=\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\varphi_{\mu,j}(\mathbf{y})\hbar^{-j}

where
\varphi_{\mu,j}(\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{C}[[y_{\mu}]]
.

The following is an oversimplification of Saito’s theory of primitive
forms, but will be sufficient for our purposes:

Definition 4.3
.

If
\varphi_{\mu,j}\equiv 0
for all
j<0
and

\varphi_{\mu,0}=\delta_{\mu\mathbf{0}}
, then we say
that
fd\mathbf{x}
is a
primitive form
. Further, in this case,

\varphi_{\mu,1}
form a set of coordinates on the universal unfolding

\mathcal{M}
called
flat coordinates
.

For more details and explicit examples in the one-dimensional case,
see
[
19
]
.

Notation 4.4
.

We typically will use the variables
t_{\mu}:=\varphi_{\mu,1}
for the flat coordinates.

Remark 4.5
.

In recent papers proving Landau-Ginzburg mirror symmetry

[
33
,
24
]
, the authors use
Saito’s general framework for constructing Frobenius manifold structures
on the universal unfoldings of potentials
(see
[
44
,
43
]
or Section III.8 of
[
36
]
) to construct
the
B
-model
W
-spin Frobenius manifold. Our definition of flat
coordinates coincides with the definition given in
Equation (13) in §2.2.2 of
[
24
]
, with somewhat
different notation. For explicit examples in the one-dimensional case,
see
[
19
]
.

4.2.
Period integrals in the rank
2
case

In the prequel
[
19
]
, we consider period integrals in the
r
-spin case.
Here we consider period integrals in the rank two case. We will first
consider the not-necessarily symmetric case.

Definition 4.6
.

Fix a finite
marking set for internal points
I\subseteq\Omega
, and define the ring

A_{I}:=\mathbb{Q}[\{u_{i,j}\,|\,i\in I,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\}]/\langle u_{i,j}u_{i,j^{\prime}}\,|\,i\in I,j,j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\rangle.

Let
{\nu}=({\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}})\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)
. Then we define the potential with
descendents associated to
{\nu}
to be

W^{{\nu}}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq I,\,\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}\\
\,\,\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|J|-1}{\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}x^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}y^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\prod_{i\in J}u_{i,d_{i}}\in A_{I}[[x,y]].

Given a canonical family of multisections
\mathbf{s}
bounded by
I
,
we define

W^{\mathbf{s}}:=W^{{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}}},

where
{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}}
is defined in (
3.20
).

Remark 4.7
.

We remark that for a fixed
k_{1},k_{2}
, it follows
from Proposition
2.31
(b) and the definition of
m(J,\mathbf{d})

that there are only a finite number of
J\subseteq I
and
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}

such that the set
\{\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})\,|\,k_{i}(\Gamma)=k_{i},i=1,2\}

is finite. Hence the coefficient of

x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}
is a polynomial, so the potential is indeed
an element of
A_{I}[[x,y]]
.

Definition 4.8
.

Let
J\subseteq I
be a finite index set,
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
. Then we
define

d(J,\mathbf{d}):={sr(J)+rs(J)-m(J,\mathbf{d})\over rs}-1.

We now have a direct calculation of the period integral, which motivates
the definition of
\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},{\nu})
from Notation
3.42
:

Theorem 4.9
.

If
W^{{\nu}}=x^{r}+y^{s}\mod\langle u_{i,j}\,|\,i\in I,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\rangle
, then for
0\leq a\leq r-2
,
0\leq b\leq s-2
,

\int_{\Xi_{(a,b)}}e^{W^{{\nu}}/\hbar}d\mathbf{x}=\delta_{a,0}\delta_{b,0}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq I\\
J\not=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}}(-1)^{|J|}(-\hbar)^{-d(J,\mathbf{d})-2}\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},{\nu})\delta_{r(J),a}\delta_{s(J),b}\prod_{j\in J}u_{j,d_{j}}.

We remark that this equality holds when both sides are viewed as Laurent series in the
\hbar
variable with coefficients in
A_{I}
.

Proof.

Let
J\subseteq I
and
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
.
Using Notation
2.30
, Proposition
2.31
shows
that the graph
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(a_{j},b_{j})\}_{j\in J}}

is balanced with respect to
\mathbf{d}
if and only if

(4.5)

k_{1}\equiv r(J)\pmod{r},\quad k_{2}\equiv s(J)\pmod{s},\text{ and }sk_{1}+rk_{2}=m(J,\mathbf{d}).

By definition of
r(J),s(J)
, there exists nonnegative integers
\ell_{1}(J),\ell_{2}(J)\in\mathbb{Z}
so that

\sum_{j\in J}a_{j}=r(J)+\ell_{1}(J)r,\quad\sum_{j\in J}b_{j}=s(J)+\ell_{2}(J)s.

We may write

(4.6)

\int_{\Xi_{(a,b)}}e^{W^{{\nu}}/\hbar}d\mathbf{x}=\int_{\Xi_{(a,b)}}\left(\sum_{h\geq 0}\frac{(W^{{\nu}}-x^{r}-y^{s})^{h}}{h!\hbar^{h}}\right)e^{(x^{r}+y^{s})/\hbar}d\mathbf{x}.

Noting by assumption that

W^{{\nu}}-x^{r}-y^{s}\equiv 0\mod\langle u_{i,j}\rangle
,
we may now expand the summation.
We can see that if
J\not=\varnothing
, then
the coefficient of
u_{J,\mathbf{d}}:=\prod_{j\in J}u_{j,d_{j}}

in (
4.6
) is

(4.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Lambda_{J,\mathbf{d}}:={}&\int_{\Xi_{(a,b)}}\sum_{h=1}^{|J|}\frac{1}{h!}\sum_{J_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup J_{h}=J}\quad\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{k_{1}(j)\}_{j=1}^{h},~k_{1}(j)=r(J_{j})~(mod~r),\\
\{k_{2}(j)\}_{j=1}^{h},~k_{2}(j)=s(J_{j})~(mod~s),\\
sk_{1}(j)+rk_{2}(j)=m(J_{j},\mathbf{d})\end{subarray}}\\
&\Bigg(x^{\sum_{j}k_{1}(j)}y^{\sum_{j}k_{2}(j)}\hbar^{-h}\prod_{j=1}^{h}(-1)^{|J_{j}|-1}{\nu}_{\Gamma_{0,k_{1}(j),k_{2}(j),1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in J_{j}}},\mathbf{d}|_{J_{j}}}\Bigg)e^{(x^{r}+y^{s})/\hbar}d\mathbf{x}.\end{split}

Performing integration by parts using
Lemma
4.2
and using (
4.3
),
we can see that
the integral is
0
unless
(a,b)=(r(J),s(J))
. As the monomial in

x,y
occurring in the integrand is
x^{\sum_{j}k_{1}(j)}y^{\sum_{j}k_{2}(j)}
,
the integration by parts produces a power
\hbar^{n_{1}+n_{2}}
, where

\sum_{j}k_{1}(j)=r(J)+n_{1}r,\quad\sum_{j}k_{2}(j)=s(J)+n_{2}s.

Putting this together with Lemma
4.2
and
Equation (
4.3
), the coefficient
is given by

(4.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Lambda_{J,\mathbf{d}}={}&\sum_{h=1}^{|J|}\frac{1}{h!}\sum_{J_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup J_{h}=J}\quad\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{k_{1}(j)\}_{j=1}^{h},~k_{1}(j)=r(J_{j})~(mod~r),\\
\{k_{2}(j)\}_{j=1}^{h},~k_{2}(j)=s(J_{j})~(mod~s),\\
sk_{1}(j)+rk_{2}(j)=m(J_{j},\mathbf{d})\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|J|-h+n_{1}+n_{2}}\\
&\Bigg(\frac{\Gamma(\tfrac{1+\sum_{j}k_{1}(j)}{r})\Gamma(\tfrac{1+\sum_{j}k_{2}(j)}{s})}{\Gamma(\tfrac{1+r(J)}{r})\Gamma(\tfrac{1+s(J)}{s})}\hbar^{n_{1}+n_{2}-h}\prod_{j=1}^{h}{\nu}_{\Gamma_{0,k_{1}(j),k_{2}(j),1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in J_{j}}},\mathbf{d}|_{J_{j}}}\Bigg)\delta_{r(J),a}\delta_{s(J),b}\end{split}

Using (
2.24
), note that

\sum_{j=1}^{h}m(J_{j},\mathbf{d})=(h-1)rs+m(J,\mathbf{d}).

From
sk_{1}(j)+rk_{2}(j)=m(J_{j},\mathbf{d})
, we obtain

\displaystyle n_{1}+n_{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{j}k_{1}(j)-r(J)}{r}+\frac{\sum_{j}k_{2}(j)-s(J)}{s}=\frac{(\sum_{j}sk_{1}(j)+rk_{2}(j))-sr(J)-rs(J)}{rs}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{j}m(J_{j},\mathbf{d})-sr(J)-rs(J)}{rs}=\frac{(h-1)rs+m(J,\mathbf{d})-sr(J)-rs(J)}{rs}.

Hence

(4.9)

(n_{1}+n_{2}-h+1)rs=m(J,\mathbf{d})-sr(J)-rs(J).

In particular,
n_{1}+n_{2}-h
only depends on
J
, and we may
rewrite (
4.8
) as

(4.10)

\Lambda_{J,\mathbf{d}}=(-1)^{|J|}(-\hbar)^{(m(J,\mathbf{d})-sr(J)-rs(J)-rs)/rs}\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},{\nu})\delta_{r(J),a}\delta_{s(J),b}.

This agrees with the stated result via the definition of
d(J,\mathbf{d})
.
On the other hand, the contribution to the integral from the case

J=\varnothing
follows directly the definition of a good basis to be

\delta_{(a,b),(0,0)}
. This gives the desired result.
∎

We next consider the symmetric case.

Definition 4.10
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}\subseteq\Omega
be a finite set of labels.
Set

A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}:=\mathbb{Q}[t_{\alpha,\beta,d}\,|\,0\leq\alpha\leq r-2,0\leq\beta\leq s-2,d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}]/\mathrm{Ideal}(I),

where
\mathrm{Ideal}(I)
is the ideal generated by monomials of the form

\prod_{d}t_{\alpha,\beta,d}^{n_{d}}

such that
\sum n_{d}>|(\text{tw}|_{I})^{-1}(\alpha,\beta)|
.

Definition 4.11
.

For each
l\geq 0
, let
\mathcal{A}_{l}
denote the collection of
multi-sets of size
l
of the form

\{(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},d_{1}),\ldots,(\alpha_{l},\beta_{l},d_{l})\}

with
0\leq\alpha_{i}\leq r-2
,
0\leq\beta_{i}\leq s-2
, and
d_{i}\geq 0
.
For
A\in\mathcal{A}_{l}
,
denote by
\text{Aut}(A)
the group of permutations
\sigma:[l]\rightarrow[l]

such that
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i})=(\alpha_{\sigma(i)},\beta_{\sigma(i)},d_{\sigma(i)})
for all
i\in[l]
.

For
A=\{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i})\}\in\mathcal{A}_{l}
such that there exists
distinct
j_{1},\ldots,j_{l}\in I
with
\text{tw}(j_{i})=(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})
, we
write for
{\nu}\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)

\mathcal{A}(A,{\nu}):=\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},{\nu}),\quad\quad d(A):=d(J,\mathbf{d}),

where
J=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{l}\}
and
\mathbf{d}=(d_{i})_{1\leq i\leq l}
. Note
that since
{\nu}
is symmetric, the first quantity is independent of
the choice of
j_{i}
, while the second is independent by definition.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
{\nu}=({\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}})\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
.
We define the potential with descendents

(4.11)

W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}=\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 0,l\geq 0}\,\,\sum_{A=\{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i})\}\in\mathcal{A}_{l}}(-1)^{l-1}\frac{{\nu}_{\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,\{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})\}},(d_{i})}}{|\text{Aut}(A)|}(\textstyle{\prod}_{i=1}^{l}t_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i}})x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}\in A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}[[x,y]].

Given a symmetric canonical family of multisections
\mathbf{s}
,
we define

W^{\mathbf{s},\mathrm{sym}}=W^{{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}},\mathrm{sym}}.

Again, similarly as in Remark
4.7
, there are only a finite
number of non-zero terms containing a fixed monomial
x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}
, so that indeed
W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}\in A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}[[x,y]].

Definition 4.13
.

Given a finite subset
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
we define a map

\displaystyle\psi_{I}:A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}\rightarrow

\displaystyle A_{I}

\displaystyle t_{\alpha,\beta,d}\mapsto

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I:(a_{i},b_{i})=(\alpha,\beta)}u_{i,d}.

This also induces a map

\psi_{I}:A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}[[x,y]]\rightarrow A_{I}[[x,y]].

Lemma 4.14
.

The homomorphism
\psi_{I}
is well-defined and injective. Further,
let
A\in\mathcal{A}_{l}
with
A=\{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i})\}
. Then

(4.12)

\psi_{I}\left({\prod_{i=1}^{l}t_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i}}\over|\textup{Aut}(A)|}\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq I,\\
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in S\end{subarray}}\prod_{j\in J}u_{j,d^{\prime}_{j}},

where the sum is over all subsets
J\subseteq I
and
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in S
where
S\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
is the subset of descendent vectors
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}:=(d_{i}^{\prime})
for which there exists a bijection
\phi:J\rightarrow[l]
with

(a_{j},b_{j})=(\alpha_{\phi(j)},\beta_{\phi(j)})
and
d^{\prime}_{j}=d_{\phi(j)}
.

Proof.

We begin with the second statement by viewing
\psi_{I}
as
a clearly well-defined map
\mathbb{Q}[\{t_{\alpha,\beta,d}\}]\rightarrow A_{I}
.
We have

\displaystyle\psi_{I}\left({\prod_{i=1}^{l}t_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i}}\over|\text{Aut}(A)|}\right)={}

\displaystyle|\text{Aut}(A)|^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\sum_{j\in I:(a_{j},b_{j})=(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})}u_{j,d_{i}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle|\text{Aut}(A)|^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq I\\
\phi:J\rightarrow[l]\end{subarray}}\prod_{j\in J}u_{j,d_{\phi(j)}}

where we sum over all bijections
\phi:J\rightarrow[l]
such that
for all
j\in J
,
(a_{j},b_{j})=(\alpha_{\phi(j)},\beta_{\phi(j)})
; here
we use the fact that
u_{i,j}u_{i,j^{\prime}}=0
in
A_{I}
for all

i\in I,j,j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Given such a bijection
\phi
, we may
define
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
by
d^{\prime}_{j}=d_{\phi(j)}
. Thus we may rewrite
the above expression as

|\text{Aut}(A)|^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J\subseteq I,\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}\\
\phi:J\rightarrow[l]\end{subarray}}\prod_{j\in J}u_{j,d^{\prime}_{j}},

where now the sum is over bijections
\phi
satisfying

(a_{j},b_{j})=(\alpha_{\phi(j)},\beta_{\phi(j)})
,
d^{\prime}_{j}=d_{\phi(j)}
. Now
assuming such a bijection exists, there are precisely
|\text{Aut}(A)|

such bijections. Thus summing over all such bijections gives the desired
formula (
4.12
).

To finish the proof, we just need to show
\ker\psi_{I}=\mathrm{Ideal}(I)\subseteq\mathbb{Q}[\{t_{\alpha,\beta,d}\}]
. Note that if

M=\prod_{i=1}^{l}t_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i}}
, then
\psi_{I}(M)=0
if and only
if the sum in the right-hand side of (
4.12
) is empty, i.e., there is no

J\subseteq I
and bijection

\phi:J\rightarrow[l]
with
(a_{j},b_{j})=(\alpha_{\phi(j)},\beta_{\phi(j)})
. However, this is the case if and only if there
is some
i\in[l]
such that
|\{j\in[l]\,|\,(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})=(\alpha_{j},\beta_{j})\}|>|(\text{tw}|_{I})^{-1}(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})|
. Put another way, this occurs
if
I
does not contain enough elements
j
with
\text{tw}(j)=(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})
.
However, by definition of
\mathrm{Ideal}(I)
, this occurs if and only if

M\in\mathrm{Ideal}(I)
. In particular
\mathrm{Ideal}(I)\subseteq\ker{\psi_{I}}
.

We now note that given two distinct monomials
M_{1},M_{2}
in the

t_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i}}
, the sets of monomials appearing on the
right-hand side of (
4.12
) are disjoint. Thus
if
f=\sum a_{i}M_{i}\in A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}
with
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}
,
M_{i}
monomials,
and
\psi_{I}(f)=0
,
we must have
\psi_{I}(M_{i})=0
for each
i
. But then each
M_{i}
, hence
f
,
lies in
\mathrm{Ideal}(I)
. This proves
\ker\psi_{I}=\mathrm{Ideal}(I)
.
∎

The following two corollaries are then immediate consequences of the
above lemma, the formulas for
W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}
,

W^{{\nu}}
, and the formula of Theorem
4.9
.

Corollary 4.15
.

Let
{\nu}=({\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}})\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
. Then

\psi_{I}(W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}})=W^{{\nu}}.

Corollary 4.16
.

Let
{\nu}
be as in Corollary
4.15
.
If

W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}=x^{r}+y^{s}\mod\langle t_{\alpha,\beta,d}\,|\,0\leq\alpha\leq r-2,0\leq\beta\leq s-2,d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\rangle,

then

\displaystyle\int_{\Xi_{(a,b)}}e^{W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}/\hbar}dx\wedge dy

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\delta_{a,0}\delta_{b,0}+\sum_{l\geq 1}\sum_{A=\{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i})\}\in\mathcal{A}_{l}}(-1)^{l}(-\hbar)^{-d(A)-2}\mathcal{A}(A,{\nu})\delta_{r(A),a}\delta_{s(A),b}\left({\prod_{j=1}^{l}t_{\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},d_{j}}\over|\mathrm{Aut}(A)|}\right).

We now can see immediately that a symmetric chamber index
\nu
from Definition
3.47
provides flat coordinates for the Landau-Ginzburg model
W
. This will be used in proving the last paragraph of Theorem
0.3
.

Corollary 4.17
.

Let
{\nu}
be a symmetric chamber index bounded by
I
. Then we have

\int_{\Xi_{(a,b)}}e^{W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}/\hbar}dx\wedge dy=\delta_{(a,b),(0,0)}+\hbar^{-1}t_{a,b,0}+O(\hbar^{-2})\mod\langle t_{(\alpha,\beta,d)}\,|\,0\leq\alpha\leq r-2,0\leq\beta\leq s-2,d>0\rangle.

In particular,
dx\wedge dy
is a primitive form and
the
t_{a,b,0}
are flat coordinates.

Proof.

We first note that by Condition (1) of Definition
3.47

that the hypothesis on
W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}
of
Corollary
4.16
holds, and
hence we may use the formula for the integral given there.
Further, by Condition (3)
of Definition
3.47
, there is no contribution
of the form
(-\hbar)^{-d(A)-2}\mathcal{A}(A,{\nu})
with

d(A)<0
and
|A|\geq 2
. On the other hand, if
A=\{(a,b,0)\}
,
then it follows from Condition (1) of Definition
3.47

and the formula for
\mathcal{A}(A,{\nu})
that the coefficient of

\hbar^{-1}
is the stated one.
∎

Example 4.18
.

Let
W=x^{4}+y^{5}
. Working modulo any cubic term in the
t_{(\alpha,\beta,0)}
variables and any
t_{(\alpha,\beta,d)}
with
d>0
, we can write a deformation
W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}
. To compress notation, we write
t_{ij}:=t_{(i,j,0)}
. From the constraints given in Definition
3.47
, we can see that

\displaystyle W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}

\displaystyle=x^{4}+y^{5}+t_{23}x^{2}y^{3}+t_{22}x^{2}y^{2}+t_{21}x^{2}y+t_{20}x^{2}+t_{13}xy^{3}+t_{12}xy^{2}+t_{11}xy+t_{10}x

\displaystyle\qquad+t_{03}y^{3}+t_{02}y^{2}+t_{01}y+t_{00}+{c_{x}}t_{22}t_{23}x^{4}+{c_{y}}t_{22}t_{23}y^{5}+d_{x}t_{23}^{2}x^{4}y+d_{y}t_{23}^{2}y^{6}

\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{4}t_{23}t_{21}y^{4}+\tfrac{1}{4}t_{23}t_{20}y^{3}+\tfrac{2}{5}t_{23}t_{13}x^{3}y+\tfrac{1}{5}t_{23}t_{12}x^{3}+\tfrac{2}{5}t_{23}t_{03}x^{2}y+\tfrac{1}{5}t_{23}t_{02}x^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{8}t_{22}^{2}y^{4}+\tfrac{1}{4}t_{22}t_{21}y^{3}+\tfrac{1}{4}t_{22}t_{20}y^{2}+\tfrac{1}{5}t_{22}t_{13}x^{3}+\tfrac{1}{5}t_{22}t_{03}x^{2}+\tfrac{1}{8}t_{21}^{2}y^{2}+\tfrac{1}{4}t_{21}t_{20}y

\displaystyle\qquad+\tfrac{1}{8}t_{20}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{5}t_{13}^{2}x^{2}y+\tfrac{1}{5}t_{13}t_{12}x^{2}+\tfrac{2}{5}t_{13}t_{03}xy+\tfrac{1}{5}t_{13}t_{02}x+\tfrac{1}{5}t_{12}t_{03}x+\tfrac{2}{5}t_{03}^{2}y+\tfrac{1}{5}t_{03}t_{02},

subject to the constraints

(4.13)

\tfrac{c_{x}}{4}+\tfrac{c_{y}}{5}=\tfrac{1}{20}\text{ and }\tfrac{d_{x}}{4}+\tfrac{2d_{y}}{5}=\tfrac{1}{20};\quad\text{ with $c_{x},c_{y},d_{x},d_{y}\in\mathbb{Q}$}.

Note that any choice of
\mathbb{Q}
-solutions to (
4.13
) will give a chamber index.

Comparing with
[
33
, Appendix A, Type
W_{12}
]
, the authors start with a versal deformation, and then find a primitive form that in our notation is of the form

\zeta=dx\wedge dy(1-\tfrac{1}{20}t_{22}t_{23}-\tfrac{1}{20}t_{23}^{2}y+\cdots).

One can apply Lemma
4.4
and the constraint (
4.13
) to see that

\displaystyle\int_{\Xi_{(a,b)}}

\displaystyle e^{W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}/\hbar}dx\wedge dy=

\displaystyle\int_{\Xi_{(a,b)}}e^{(W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}-({c_{x}}t_{22}t_{23}x^{4}+{c_{y}}t_{22}t_{23}y^{5}+d_{x}t_{23}^{2}x^{4}y+d_{y}t_{23}^{2}y^{6}))/\hbar}(1-\tfrac{1}{20}t_{22}t_{23}-\tfrac{1}{20}t_{23}^{3}y+\ldots)dx\wedge dy

since

e^{({c_{x}}t_{22}t_{23}x^{4}+{c_{y}}t_{22}t_{23}y^{5}+d_{x}t_{23}^{2}x^{4}y+d_{y}t_{23}^{2}y^{6})/\hbar}dx\wedge dy=(1-\tfrac{1}{20}t_{22}t_{23}-\tfrac{1}{20}t_{23}^{3}y+\ldots)dx\wedge dy

in the hypercohomology
\mathbb{H}^{n}(X,(\Omega_{X}^{\bullet},d+\hbar^{-1}dW\wedge-))

We note that the potential

W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}-({c_{x}}t_{22}t_{23}x^{4}+{c_{y}}t_{22}t_{23}y^{5}+d_{x}t_{23}^{2}x^{4}y+d_{y}t_{23}^{2}y^{6})

would be equal to the
flat
deformation (up to order 2) found by the approach in
[
33
]
once viewed in
\mathbb{H}^{n}(X,(\Omega_{X}^{\bullet},d+\hbar^{-1}dW\wedge-))
. Essentially, our choice of deformation of
W
has yielded a less complicated primitive form.

4.3.
The Landau-Ginzburg wall-crossing Group

To motivate the discussion of this subsection, let us consider
a rank
n
