Fermat potential
W=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{r_{i}}
, and let

A
be some coefficient ring parameterizing a perturbation
of
W
. Fix a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq A
and
let
W^{\prime}\in A[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]]
be such that
W\equiv W^{\prime}\mod\mathfrak{m}
.
We are interested in changes to
W^{\prime}
which do not affect the
oscillatory integrals
\int e^{W^{\prime}/\hbar}dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}
. Indeed, take an automorphism

\psi\in\text{Aut}_{A}(A[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]])

which is the identity modulo the ideal
\mathfrak{m}
and satisfies the relation

\psi^{*}(dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n})=dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}.

Then

(4.14)

\int_{\Xi}e^{W^{\prime}/\hbar}dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}=\int_{\Xi}\psi^{*}\left(e^{W^{\prime}/\hbar}dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}\right)=\int_{\Xi}e^{\psi(W^{\prime})/\hbar}dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}.

Note that we have not changed the cycle of integration
\Xi
and in
fact should use
\psi^{-1}(\Xi)
in the second and third integrals,
but because
\psi
is just an infinitesimal extension of the identity,
this has no effect on the integral.
Thus we may change
W^{\prime}
by applying
\psi
without changing
the integrals.

We now study the Lie algebra of the group of such
\psi
. Some care
is necessary to handle the completion, so let

R_{k}:=A[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]/(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})^{k+1},

so that
A[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]]
is the inverse limit of the
R_{k}
.

Consider the
module
\Theta_{k}
of derivations of

R_{k}
over
A
:

\Theta_{k}:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}R_{k}\partial_{x_{i}}.

This module comes equipped with the usual Lie bracket, i.e.,

(4.15)

\left[\left(\prod_{i}x_{i}^{n_{i}}\right)\partial_{x_{j}},\left(\prod_{i}x_{i}^{n_{i}^{\prime}}\right)\partial_{x_{k}}\right]=\left(n_{j}^{\prime}\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{n_{i}+n_{i}^{\prime}-\delta_{ij}}\right)\partial_{x_{k}}-\left(n_{k}\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{n_{i}+n_{i}^{\prime}-\delta_{ik}}\right)\partial_{x_{j}}.

We next define a linear subspace of
\Theta_{k}
,

(4.16)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{g}_{A,k}:={}&\bigoplus_{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})}\mathfrak{g}_{A,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})}\\
:={}&\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}\setminus\{0\}\\
\sum a_{i}\leq k\end{subarray}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{m}\cdot(x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{a_{n}}((a_{i}+1)x_{1}\partial_{x_{1}}-(a_{1}+1)x_{i}\partial_{x_{i}})).\end{split}

To show
\mathfrak{g}_{A,k}
is closed under Lie bracket,
one tediously calculates

(4.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left[\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{a_{i}}\right)((a_{j}+1)x_{1}\partial_{x_{1}}-(a_{1}+1)x_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}),\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{b_{i}}\right)((b_{\ell}+1)x_{1}\partial_{x_{1}}-(b_{1}+1)x_{\ell}\partial_{x_{\ell}})\right]\\
={}&\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{a_{i}+b_{i}}\right)\big((a_{j}+b_{j}+1)\alpha+(a_{\ell}+b_{\ell}+1)\beta\big)x_{1}\partial_{x_{1}}-(a_{1}+b_{1}+1)(\alpha x_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}+\beta x_{\ell}\partial_{x_{\ell}}),\end{split}

with

\alpha={-(b_{\ell}+1)(a_{1}+1)a_{1}+(b_{1}+1)(a_{1}+1)a_{\ell}\over a_{1}+b_{1}+1},\quad\beta={(a_{j}+1)(b_{1}+1)b_{1}-(a_{1}+1)(b_{1}+1)b_{j}\over a_{1}+b_{1}+1}.

Further,
if
v\in\mathfrak{g}_{A,k}
,
then the Lie derivative
{\mathcal{L}}_{v}(dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n})=0
.
In detail, since
dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}
is a closed form, we have

{\mathcal{L}}_{v}(dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n})=d(\iota(v)(dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n})).

Taking
v=x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{a_{n}}((a_{i}+1)x_{1}\partial_{x_{1}}-(a_{1}+1)x_{i}\partial_{x_{i}})
, we see this latter quantity is

\displaystyle d\left(x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{a_{n}}((a_{i}+1)x_{1}dx_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}-(-1)^{i-1}(a_{1}+1)x_{i}dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{dx_{i}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{a_{n}}((a_{1}+1)(a_{i}+1)-(a_{i}+1)(a_{1}+1))dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 0.

Thus vector fields in
\mathfrak{g}_{A,k}
indeed preserve
dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}
.

We note that this is not the algebra of all vector fields preserving

dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}
. Indeed, vector fields of the form

f(x_{1},\ldots,\widehat{x_{i}},\ldots,x_{n})\partial_{x_{i}}
also preserve

dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}
. But such a vector field will not play a role in our theory.
This is a non-trivial point, which in the rank two case is in fact
enforced by Condition (3)(a) of the definition of strongly positive,
Definition
3.18
.

Definition 4.19
.

As
\mathfrak{g}_{A,k}
is a nilpotent Lie algebra, we may define a group

G_{A,k}:=\exp(\mathfrak{g}_{A,k})
. This is a group
whose underlying set is
\mathfrak{g}_{A,k}
but where
multiplication is given by the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula.
We may then define
G_{A}
as the pro-nilpotent Lie group given as
the inverse limit of the
G_{A,k}
, identified as a set with

\mathfrak{g}_{A}
, the inverse limit of the
\mathfrak{g}_{A,k}
.
We call
G_{A}
the
Landau-Ginzburg wall-crossing group of rank
n

(over
A
)
.

We note that
G_{A}
acts by automorphisms of

A[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]]
which preserve
dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{n}
via the exponential: i.e., if
v\in\mathfrak{g}_{A}
, then

\exp(v)
acts on elements of
A[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]]
by

(4.18)

f\mapsto\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{v^{n}(f)\over n!},

where
v^{n}
denotes differentiating with respect to the vector field

n
times.

Remark 4.20
.

A similar group of automorphisms of
A[x_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,x_{n}^{\pm 1}]

preserving
d\log x_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\log x_{n}

or a symplectic holomorphic form
has been studied extensively in the literature and applied
in many different settings, with initial study in
the two-dimensional case by Kontsevich and Soibelman in
[
30
]
and
in higher dimension by Gross and Siebert in
[
21
]
. The particular use
here follows closely the application of the wall-crossing group
by Gross in
[
22
]
.

Restricting now to the two-dimensional case, we may write

(4.19)

\mathfrak{g}_{A,k}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}\\
a+b\leq k\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{m}\cdot(x^{a}y^{b}((b+1)x\partial_{x}-(a+1)y\partial_{y}))=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}\\
a+b\leq k\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{g}_{A,(a,b)}.

The action of
G_{A}
on
A[[x,y]]
can be described explicitly through the following computation:

Lemma 4.21
.

For
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
, consider

v=gx^{a}y^{b}((b+1)x\partial_{x}-(a+1)y\partial_{y})\in\mathfrak{g}_{A,(a,b)},

with
g\in\mathfrak{m}\subseteq A
.

(1)

If
a\not=b
,
then
\exp(v)
acts on
A[[x,y]]
via

(4.20)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto x(1+(b-a)gx^{a}y^{b})^{(b+1)/(b-a)}

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\longmapsto y(1+(b-a)gx^{a}y^{b})^{(a+1)/(a-b)}.

(2)

If
a=b
, then
\exp(v)
acts on
A[[x,y]]
via

(4.21)

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto x\exp((a+1)gx^{a}y^{b})

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\longmapsto y\exp(-(a+1)gx^{a}y^{b}).

Proof.

We first note the calculation of (2) is straightforward, using the
fact that
v
vanishes on
(xy)^{n}
for any
n
.
For (1), note that given the description (
4.18
)
for the action of
\exp(v)
,
we can rewrite the automorphism
\psi=\exp(v)
on the elements
x,y\in A[[x,y]]
as

\displaystyle\psi(x)={}

\displaystyle x\left(1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}f_{x,n}(x^{a}y^{b})^{n}\right)=xf_{x}

\displaystyle\psi(y)={}

\displaystyle y\left(1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}f_{y,n}(x^{a}y^{b})^{n}\right)=yf_{y}

for some
f_{x,n},f_{y,n}\in A
defining
f_{x},f_{y}\in A[[x^{a}y^{b}]]

with
f_{x},f_{y}\equiv 1\mod\mathfrak{m}
. Further, since
v(x^{a+1}y^{b+1})=0
,

\psi(x^{a+1}y^{b+1})=x^{a+1}y^{b+1}
, so
f_{x}^{a+1}=f_{y}^{-(b+1)}
. Thus by taking roots as formal
power series, there is a unique function
f\in A[[q]]
so
that
f_{x}=f(x^{a}y^{b})^{b+1}
,
f_{y}=f(x^{a}y^{b})^{-(a+1)}
and
f\equiv 1\mod q
.
Now recall that
\psi^{*}(dx\wedge dy)=dx\wedge dy
.
Writing
f^{\prime}
for the derivative of
f
with respect to
q
, and
substituting
q=x^{a}y^{b}
, we can compute that

dx\wedge dy=\psi^{*}(dx\wedge dy)=(f^{b-a}+(a-b)x^{a}y^{b}f^{\prime}f^{b-a-1})dx\wedge dy.

Thus, with
q=x^{a}y^{b}
, we require that

f^{b-a}+(a-b)qf^{\prime}f^{b-a-1}=1.

If
a=b
, we obtain no restriction on
f
, and hence the explicit
calculation is necessary. However, if
a\not=b
, then any solution
to this differential equation is of the form

f(q)=(1+Cq)^{1/(b-a)}
for
C
a constant, which we may
view as an element of
A
. This now gives the
form (
4.20
) up to determining the constant
C
. However,
we may expand
\psi(x)
as a Taylor series in
C
to first order
to obtain

\psi(x)=x(1+{(b+1)C\over b-a}x^{a}y^{b}+\cdots).

Noting that

\psi(x)=x+v(x)+\cdots=x+(b+1)gx^{a+1}y^{b}+\cdots,

we see we must have
C=(b-a)g
, as desired.
∎

We now fix a Landau-Ginzburg model
W=x^{r}+y^{s}
. We will
fix a finite
I\subseteq\Omega
and take
A=A_{I}

or
A=A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}
. In both cases, we take
\mathfrak{m}
to
be the ideal generated by the variables (the
u_{i,d}

or
t_{\alpha,\beta,d}
in the two cases). We thus obtain the groups

G_{A_{I}}
or
G_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
.

Remark 4.22
.

The morphism
\psi_{I}:A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}\rightarrow A_{I}
induces a
homomorphism of nilpotent Lie algebras
\widetilde{\psi}_{I}:\mathfrak{g}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}},k}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}_{A_{I},k}

and a homomorphism of
groups
\widetilde{\psi}_{I}:G_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}\rightarrow G_{A_{I}}
which is compatible with the action of these
two groups on
A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}[[x,y]]
and
A_{I}[[x,y]]
respectively,
i.e., for
g\in G_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
,
W\in A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}[[x,y]]
,
we have

(4.22)

\psi_{I}(g(W))=\widetilde{\psi}_{I}(g)(\psi_{I}(W)).

The choice of
W
determines natural subgroups as follows.
We want to emphasize that these subgroups consist of direct summands
that are parameterised by critical graphs.

Definition 4.23
.

Fix
I\subseteq\Omega
a finite set of markings.

(1)

For
J\subseteq I
,
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
,
we define

u_{J,\mathbf{d}}:=\prod_{j\in J}u_{j,d_{j}}.

Define for
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 0
,

(k_{1},k_{2})\not=(0,0)
,

(4.23)

\mathfrak{g}_{A_{I},(k_{1},k_{2})}^{r,s}:=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varnothing\not=J\subseteq I,\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}\\
k_{1}=r(J)\text{ mod }r,\hskip 8.19447ptk_{2}=s(J)\text{ mod }s\\
sk_{1}+rk_{2}=m(J,\mathbf{d})-rs\end{subarray}}u_{J,\mathbf{d}}x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}((k_{2}+1)x\partial_{x}-(k_{1}+1)y\partial_{y})\mathbb{Q}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{(k_{1},k_{2})}.

Set

\mathfrak{g}_{A_{I},k}^{r,s}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2}\geq 0\\
0<k_{1}+k_{2}\leq k\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{g}^{r,s}_{(k_{1},k_{2})}.

(2)

Define

\mathfrak{g}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}},(k_{1},k_{2})}^{r,s}:=\widetilde{\psi}_{I}^{-1}(\mathfrak{g}^{r,s}_{A_{I},(k_{1},k_{2})})

and

\mathfrak{g}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}},k}^{r,s}:=\widetilde{\psi}_{I}^{-1}(\mathfrak{g}^{r,s}_{A_{I},k}).

Remark 4.24
.

The introduction gave different descriptions of the groups

G_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
and
G_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}^{r,s}
.
It is not difficult to see that the explicit constructions
of these groups here agree with the characterizations given in
§
0.4
. Indeed, for
G_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
,
automorphisms which are
the identity modulo
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}

are given as exponentials of derivations which are zero modulo

\mathfrak{m}
. A computation of the Lie bracket

\mathcal{L}_{v}(dx\wedge dy)
shows that any derivation preserving

dx\wedge dy
must be a linear combination of derivations of
the form
x^{a}y^{b}((b+1)x\partial_{x}-(a+1)y\partial_{y})
with
a,b\geq-1
,
(a,b)\not=(-1,-1)
. However
the requirement that the derivation take
xy
into the ideal generated by

xy
forces
a,b\geq 0
. Thus these three conditions yield the group

G_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
. The additional restrictions for

\mathfrak{g}^{r,s}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}},k}
arise as follows. The condition that
[E,\cdot]

acts by multiplication by
rs
, where
E
is the Euler
field of (
0.6
), imposes the condition that
sk_{1}+sk_{2}=m(J,\mathbf{d})-rs
. Finally invariance under the action

x\mapsto\xi_{r}x
,
y\mapsto\xi_{s},y
,
t_{a,b,d}\mapsto\xi^{-a}_{r}\xi^{-b}_{s}t_{a,b,d}
imposes the condition

k_{1}=r(J)\mod r
,
k_{2}=s(J)\mod r
.

We omit the details as
we will not need the description given in the introduction.

Proposition 4.25
.

The subalgebra
\mathfrak{g}^{r,s}_{A,k}
is closed under Lie bracket in
either case
A=A_{I}
or
A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to
check this in the case where
A=A_{I}
. Using (
4.17
),
we have, for
J,J^{\prime}\subseteq I
disjoint,
k_{1},k_{2},\mathbf{d},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}

satisfying the conditions in (
4.23
),

\displaystyle[u_{J,\mathbf{d}}x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}((k_{2}+1)x\partial_{x}-(k_{1}+1)y\partial_{y},u_{J^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}x^{k^{\prime}_{1}}y^{k^{\prime}_{2}}((k^{\prime}_{2}+1)x\partial_{x}-(k^{\prime}_{1}+1)y\partial_{y}]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle u_{J,\mathbf{d}}u_{J^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}x^{k_{1}+k_{1}^{\prime}}x^{k_{2}+k_{2}^{\prime}}C((k_{2}+k_{2}^{\prime}+1)x_{1}\partial_{x_{1}}-(k_{1}+k_{1}^{\prime}+1)x_{2}\partial_{x_{2}})

for some
C\in\mathbb{Q}
.
If
\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J\cup J^{\prime}}
denotes the
descendent vector agreeing with
\mathbf{d}
on
J
and
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
on
J^{\prime}
,
then we just need to check (1)
k_{1}+k_{1}^{\prime}=r(J\cup J^{\prime})\mod r
,

k_{2}+k_{2}^{\prime}=s(J\cup J^{\prime})\mod s
, which is automatic, and (2)

s(k_{1}+k_{1}^{\prime})+r(k_{2}+k_{2}^{\prime})=m(J\cup J^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime})-rs
. But this follows
immediately from the definition of
m
.
∎

Definition 4.26
.

We write
G_{A}^{r,s}\subseteq G_{A}
for the subgroups obtained from
the system of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{g}^{r,s}_{A,k}
for
A=A_{I}

or
A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}
.

The key observation is then:

Theorem 4.27
.

Let
I\subseteq\Omega
be finite.
There is a well-defined action of the group
G_{A_{I}}^{r,s}
on
the set of chamber indices

\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)
(see Definition
3.47
) given by

g(W^{{\nu}})=W^{g({\nu})}.

This action is faithful and transitive. Similarly, there is a well-defined
action of the group
G^{r,s}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
on

\Upsilon_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}(I)
defined via the same formula. This action is
faithful and transitive.

Proof.

Step 1. Existence of the action
.
We first consider the non-symmetric case.
There is an injection from
\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)
into

A_{I}[[x,y]]
given by
{\nu}\mapsto W^{{\nu}}
.
Given
g\in G_{A_{I}}^{r,s}
, we thus obtain
g(W^{{\nu}})\in A_{I}[[x,y]]
. To
obtain the desired action, we need to show that there exists a chamber index

{\nu}^{\prime}
such that
W^{{\nu}^{\prime}}=g(W^{{\nu}})
.

Since elements of the form
\exp(v)
for
v\in\mathfrak{g}^{r,s}_{A_{I},(k_{1},k_{2})}
generate the group
G^{r,s}_{A_{I}}
,
it is enough to check this statement for

(4.24)

g=\exp(Cu_{J,\mathbf{d}}x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}((k_{2}+1)x\partial_{x}-(k_{1}+1)y\partial_{y})),\quad C\in\mathbb{Q},

the exponential of a generator of
\mathfrak{g}^{r,s}_{A_{I},(k_{1},k_{2})}
. Note that since
g
corresponds to a generator of
\mathfrak{g}^{r,s}_{A_{I},(k_{1},k_{2})}
, we have

(4.25)

k_{1}=r(J)\pmod{r},\quad k_{2}=s(J)\pmod{s},\text{ and }sk_{1}+rk_{2}=m(J,\mathbf{d})-rs.

So let
C^{\prime}u_{J^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}x^{k_{1}^{\prime}}y^{k_{2}^{\prime}}
be a term in
W^{{\nu}}
.
By the definition of chamber index, this term is non-zero only if

\Gamma_{0,k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}^{\prime},1,J^{\prime}}
is balanced with respect to
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}
,
i.e.,

(4.26)

k_{1}^{\prime}=r(J^{\prime})\pmod{r},\quad k_{2}^{\prime}=s(J^{\prime})\pmod{s},\text{ and }sk_{1}^{\prime}+rk_{2}^{\prime}=m(J^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}).

Applying

g
to this term produces no new terms if
J\cap J^{\prime}\not=\varnothing
,
as then
u_{J,\mathbf{d}}u_{J^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}=0
. Otherwise,
we see that,
by applying Equation (
4.18
) and noting
u_{J,\mathbf{d}}^{2}=0
,
we obtain a new term of the form

C^{\prime\prime}u_{J\cup J^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime}}x^{k_{1}+k_{1}^{\prime}}y^{k_{2}+k_{2}^{\prime}}

for some
C^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}
. Now

k_{1}+k_{1}^{\prime}=r(J)+r(J^{\prime})=r(J\cup J^{\prime})\pmod{r}
and

k_{2}+k_{2}^{\prime}=s(J)+s(J^{\prime})=s(J\cup J^{\prime})\pmod{s}
. Furthermore, using (
4.25
) and (
4.26
)

m(J\cup J^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime})=m(J,\mathbf{d})+m(J^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime})-rs=sk_{1}+rk_{2}+rs+sk_{1}^{\prime}+rk_{2}^{\prime}-rs=s(k_{1}+k_{1}^{\prime})+r(k_{2}+k_{2}^{\prime}),

showing that
\Gamma_{0,k_{1}+k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}+k_{2}^{\prime},1,J\cup J^{\prime}}
is balanced with
respect to
\mathbf{d}^{\prime\prime}
.
This shows that
g(W^{{\nu}})
arises from an element
g({\nu})=(g({\nu})_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}})\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)
.

We now only need to show that
g({\nu})

satisfies the conditions of Definition
3.47
, still
with
g
as given in (
4.24
).
First we show Condition (1) of the definition.
Suppose that
\mathbf{d}=0
. Then necessarily
|J|\geq 2
. Indeed,
using Notation
2.40
, there are
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-|J|+1

choices of
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 0
satisfying the conditions in (
4.25
). However, if
J=\{j\}
, then

r(J)=a_{j},s(J)=b_{j}
, and thus
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}=0
and there are no such

k_{1},k_{2}
. Thus in this case,

g
is the identity modulo
\mathfrak{m}^{2}
, and
we have
g({\nu})_{\Gamma,\mathbf{0}}={\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{0}}
when

|I(\Gamma)|=0
or
1
, as desired.

For Conditions (2) and (3) of Definition
3.47
, consider the two potentials
W^{\nu}
and
W^{g(\nu)}
. For any
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
and
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{I^{\prime}}
, it follows from (
4.14
) that

\int_{\Xi_{r(I^{\prime}),s(I^{\prime})}}e^{W^{\nu}/\hbar}dx\wedge dy=\int_{\Xi_{r(I^{\prime}),s(I^{\prime})}}e^{W^{g(\nu)}/\hbar}dx\wedge dy,

which implies by Theorem
4.9
that

\mathcal{A}(I^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime},{\nu})=\mathcal{A}(I^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime},g({\nu}))
,
and hence the required value of
\mathcal{A}(I^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime},g({\nu}))

follows from the same requirement for
\mathcal{A}(I^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime},{\nu})
.

For the symmetric case, we note that if
g\in G^{r,s}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
,
then
\widetilde{\psi}_{I}(g)
acts on
{\nu}\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
as above
giving
\widetilde{\psi}_{I}(g)({\nu})\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}}(I)
. However, since

\widetilde{\psi}_{I}(g)(\psi_{I}(W^{{\nu}}))=\psi_{I}(g(W^{{\nu}}))
by
(
4.22
), we see in fact that
\widetilde{\psi}_{I}(g)({\nu})\in{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
. As we have already seen above this action takes
chamber indices to chamber indices, we get the desired action on

\Upsilon_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}(I)
.

Step 2. Faithfulness of the action
.
Take
A=A_{I}
or
A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}
, and suppose that
{\nu}\in\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)
or
\Upsilon_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}(I)
in the two cases. Suppose

g=\exp(v)\in G_{A}^{r,s}
satisfies
g({\nu})={\nu}
. Let
k\geq 2
be the smallest
integer such that
v\equiv 0\mod\mathfrak{m}^{k}
but

v\not\equiv 0\mod\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
. Then working modulo

\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
, we have

g(W^{{\nu}})=W^{{\nu}}+v(W^{{\nu}})=W^{{\nu}}+v(x^{r}+y^{s})
.
Thus it suffices to check that
v(x^{r}+y^{s})=0

implies
v=0
, still modulo
\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
.

Now
v
is a linear combination
of vector fields of the form given in (
4.23
).
Let
k_{1}
be minimal so that
v
has a non-zero summand in

\mathfrak{g}^{r,s}_{A,(k_{1},k_{2})}
for some
k_{2}
,
and let
k_{2}
be minimal among such choices
of
k_{2}
fixing
k_{1}
. In other words, we consider the minimal pair

(k_{1},k_{2})
occuring in
v\mod\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
with respect to the
lexicographic ordering. Thus we may write the

\mathfrak{g}^{r,s}_{A,(k_{1},k_{2})}
summand of
v
as

v^{\prime}=Cx^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}((k_{2}+1)x\partial_{x}-(k_{1}+1)y\partial_{y})

for some
C\in\mathfrak{m}^{k}\setminus\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
.
Now

v^{\prime}(x^{r}+y^{s})=C\left(r(k_{2}+1)x^{k_{1}+r}y^{k_{2}}-s(k_{1}+1)x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}+s}\right).

Note that neither of the two terms in this expression is zero.
On the other hand,
because of the minimality of the pair
(k_{1},k_{2})
, this expression
does not cancel with any other terms appearing in
v(x^{r}+y^{s})
. This
shows
C=0
, hence
v=0\mod\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
, contradicting the
definition of
k
. Hence
v=0
.

Step 3. Transitivity of the action.

We will consider the case
A=A_{I}
, the symmetric case being similar.
Let
{\nu}
,
{\nu}^{\prime}\in\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)
.
We proceed inductively, showing that
for any positive integer
k
, there exists an element
g\in G^{r,s}_{A_{I}}

such that
g({\nu})_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}={\nu}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}
for any
\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})
with
sk_{1}(\Gamma)+rk_{2}(\Gamma)<k
.
Note that the base case of
k=0
is automatic,
as we may take
g
to be the identity. Define an ideal
\mathcal{I}_{k}:=\langle x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}\in A_{I}[[x,y]]\ |\ sk_{1}+rk_{2}\geq k\rangle
.

Now assume we have found a
g
which works for a given
k
. By
replacing
{\nu}
with
g({\nu})
, we may assume that
W^{{\nu}}=W^{{\nu}^{\prime}}\mod\mathcal{I}_{k}
. Now consider
J\subseteq I,\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}

such that there exists a

\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})
with
sk_{1}(\Gamma)+rk_{2}(\Gamma)=k
.
Consider the coefficient of
u_{J,\mathbf{d}}
in
W^{{\nu}^{\prime}}-W^{{\nu}}
, namely

(-1)^{|J|-1}\sum_{\Gamma=\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},1,J}\in{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})}({\nu}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}-{\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}})x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}

Recall from Notation
2.40

that we have a complete classification of graphs
\Gamma_{J,p}\in{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})
where
\Gamma_{J,p}
is the unique balanced graph with
k_{1}(\Gamma_{J,p})=r(J)+pr
and
k_{2}(\Gamma_{J,p})=s(J)+(N-p)s
, and
I(\Gamma_{J,p})=J
for
p=0,\ldots,N=\frac{\sum_{i\in J}a_{i}-r(J)}{r}+\frac{\sum_{i\in J}b_{i}-s(J)}{s}-|J|+1+\sum_{j\in J}d_{j}
.
Note that
sk_{1}(\Gamma_{J,p})+rk_{2}(\Gamma_{J,p})
is independent of
p
,
and hence is always
k
.

In particular, we may write
the
u_{J,\mathbf{d}}
coefficient of
W^{{\nu}^{\prime}}-W^{{\nu}}
as

(-1)^{|J|-1}\sum_{p=0}^{N}({\nu}^{\prime}_{\Gamma_{J,p},\mathbf{d}}-{\nu}_{\Gamma_{J,p},\mathbf{d}})x^{r(J)+pr}y^{s(J)+(N-p)s}.

Let
p^{\prime}\geq 0
be the smallest
p
such that
{\nu}^{\prime}_{\Gamma_{J,p},\mathbf{d}}\not={\nu}_{\Gamma_{J,p},\mathbf{d}}
.
Now consider

\displaystyle v=(-1)^{|J|}u_{J,\mathbf{d}}{{\nu}^{\prime}_{\Gamma_{J,p^{\prime}},\mathbf{d}}-{\nu}_{\Gamma_{J,p^{\prime}},\mathbf{d}}\over s(r(J)+p^{\prime}r+1)}

\displaystyle x^{r(J)+p^{\prime}r}y^{s(J)+(N-p^{\prime}-1)s}

\displaystyle((s(J)+(N-p^{\prime}-1)s+1)x\partial_{x}-(r(J)+p^{\prime}r+1)y\partial_{y}).

One checks easily that this lies in
\mathfrak{g}^{r,s}_{A_{I}}
and corresponds to the critical graph
\Lambda_{J,p^{\prime}+1}
in Notation
2.40
.
Modulo
\mathcal{I}_{k+1}
,
\exp(v)(W^{{\nu}})
differs from

W^{{\nu}}
only in the coefficients of
u_{J,\mathbf{d}}x^{r(J)+p^{\prime}r}y^{s(J)+(N-p^{\prime})s}

and
u_{J,\mathbf{d}}x^{r(J)+(p^{\prime}+1)r}y^{s(J)+(n-p^{\prime}-1)s}
, and further
the coefficient of
u_{J,\mathbf{d}}x^{r(J)+p^{\prime}r}y^{s(J)+(N-p^{\prime})s}
in
\exp(v)(W^{{\nu}})
agrees with
the coefficient of the same monomial in
W^{{\nu}^{\prime}}
. Thus by replacing

W^{{\nu}}
with
\exp(v)(W^{{\nu}})
and repeating this process inductively on
p^{\prime}
, we may assume that
the coefficient of
u_{J,\mathbf{d}}
in
W^{{\nu}^{\prime}}-W^{{\nu}}
is

(-1)^{k}({\nu}^{\prime}_{\Gamma_{J,N},\mathbf{d}}-{\nu}_{\Gamma_{J,N},\mathbf{d}})x^{r(J)+Nr}y^{s(J)},

i.e., there is at most one
\Gamma
with
I(\Gamma)=J
with

{\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}\not={\nu}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}
.
Further, inductively we
have assumed
{\nu}_{\Gamma^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}={\nu}^{\prime}_{\Gamma^{\prime},\mathbf{d}^{\prime}}

whenever
I(\Gamma^{\prime})\subsetneq J
.
Since
\Gamma
is balanced for
\mathbf{d}
, we have
m(J,\mathbf{d})=sk_{1}(\Gamma)+rk_{2}(\Gamma)=sr(J)+rs(J)+rsN\geq sr(J)+rs(J)
,
so if
|J|\geq 2
,
the inequality of Condition (3) of Definition
3.47

holds and
\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},{\nu})=\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},{\nu}^{\prime})=0
. On
the other hand, if
|J|=1
, Condition (2) similarly implies that
\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},{\nu})=\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},{\nu}^{\prime})
.
Looking at the form of these expressions, one notes they agree
except for the contribution from the terms
{\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}

and
{\nu}^{\prime}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}
,
and that implies equality of these two terms.

Repeating this for all
J,\mathbf{d}
such that there exists
a
\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})
with
sk_{1}(\Gamma)+rk_{2}(\Gamma)=k
then gives
the induction step. Note there are a finite number of
J\subseteq I
and graphs
\Gamma
with
I(\Gamma)=J
with
sk_{1}(\Gamma)+rk_{2}(\Gamma)\leq k
and balanced with respect to some descendent vector
\mathbf{d}
.
Thus by working modulo
\mathcal{I}_{k+1}
the automorphism we build only involves a
composition of a finite number of automorphisms.
∎

We end this section with a result that will be of use in §
5.3
and clarifies the computational result from Corollary
4.16
.

Corollary 4.28
.

Let
I
be a finite set of
\Omega
. Take
{\nu}
and
{\nu}^{\prime}
to be two chamber indices bounded by
I
. Then,

\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},{\nu})=\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},{\nu}^{\prime})

for all
J\subseteq I
and
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.27
, there exists an element
g\in G_{A_{I}}^{r,s}
so that
g\cdot{\nu}={\nu}^{\prime}
. Next, note that, by (
4.14
), since
{\nu}
and
{\nu}^{\prime}
are in the same orbit of the wall-crossing group action we have that

\int_{\Xi}e^{W^{{\nu}}/\hbar}dx\wedge dy=\int_{\Xi}e^{W^{{\nu}^{\prime}}/\hbar}dx\wedge dy.

The result then follows from comparing coefficients in the result from Corollary
4.16
.
∎

5.
Invariance, wall-crossing, mirror symmetry, and open topological recursion

5.1.
Invariance

Unlike the open
r
-spin invariants defined in
[
5
,
6
]
, the open
W
-spin invariants for
W=x^{r}+y^{s}
defined here
do depend on the choice of the canonical multisection
. However, some simple invariants are independent of these choices:

Theorem 5.1
.

We have

\langle\sigma_{1}^{r}\sigma_{12}\rangle^{\mathbf{s},o}=\langle\sigma_{2}^{s}\sigma_{12}\rangle^{\mathbf{s},o}=-1.

For
0\leq m_{1}\leq r-1,0\leq m_{2}\leq s-1
, we have

\langle\tau^{(m_{1},m_{2})}_{0}\sigma_{1}^{m_{1}}\sigma_{2}^{m_{2}}\sigma_{12}\rangle^{\mathbf{s},o}=1.

In particular these numbers are independent of
the choice of
\mathbf{s}
and

W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}
satisfies (
0.5
).

Remark 5.2
.

We note that this justifies item (1) in the definition of chamber
index, see Definition
3.47
.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Let
\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathbf{s}_{1}
be two canonical multisections for
E\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},
for a smooth rooted graded (possibly disconnected)
\Gamma
that
is balanced with respect to a descendent vector
\mathbf{d}
.
By Lemma
3.40
(
2
) we can find a canonical transverse homotopy
H\in C_{m}^{\infty}([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},\pi^{*}E)
with
H(\epsilon,-)=\mathbf{s}_{\epsilon},
for
\epsilon=0,1
, and it holds, by Lemma
2.22
, that

(5.1)

\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k(\Gamma)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\Gamma},o}-\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k(\Gamma)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s}_{0}^{\Gamma},o}=\#Z(H|_{([0,1]\times\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})\cup([0,1]\times\partial^{\textup{Exchange}}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})}).

Recall that we showed in Example
3.4
that if
\Gamma\in\{\Gamma_{0,m_{1},m_{2},1,\{(m_{1},m_{2})\}},\Gamma_{0,r,0,1,\emptyset},\Gamma_{0,0,s,1,\emptyset}\}
, then
\partial^{0}\Gamma=\emptyset
. Here, the set
\partial^{\textup{Exchange}}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
need not be empty; however, by Lemma
5.3
below, we have that
\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times\partial^{\textup{Exchange}}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})=0
.
Thus, the quantity in (
5.1
) vanishes and we conclude that the intersection number is independent of the choice of the canonical multisection.

When
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0,m_{1},m_{2},1,\{(m_{1},m_{2})\}}
, let

\Gamma_{i}=\text{for}_{\text{spin}\neq i}(\Gamma)
.
Note that
\Gamma_{1}
is the graded
r
-spin graph with a single internal tail with twist
m_{1}
and
m_{1}+1
boundary tails, and
\Gamma_{2}
is the similarly defined graded
s
-spin graph.
We may then take
\mathbf{s}=\text{For}_{\text{spin}\neq 1}^{*}\zeta_{1}\oplus\text{For}_{\text{spin}\neq 2}^{*}\zeta_{2}
,
where
\zeta_{i}
is a transverse global canonical multisection of the Witten bundle over
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma_{i}}
. By transversality we may assume that it vanishes only in
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Gamma_{i}}
.
Using
[
6
, Appendix A]
, and specifically Notation A.4 there, we can write

Z(\zeta_{i})=\sum_{p_{j}^{i}\in Z(\zeta_{i})}\varepsilon_{j}^{i}p_{j}^{i},

where
\varepsilon_{j}^{i}
is the weight of
p_{j}^{i}
in the zero locus of
\zeta_{i}.

Hence,

\#Z(\mathbf{s})=\sum_{p_{j_{1}}^{1}\in Z(\zeta_{1})}\sum_{p_{j_{2}}^{2}\in Z(\zeta_{2})}\varepsilon_{j_{1}}^{1}\varepsilon_{j_{2}}^{2}\#\left(\pi_{1}^{-1}(p_{j_{1}}^{1})\cap\pi_{2}^{-1}(p_{j_{2}}^{2})\right).

For smooth moduli points
p^{i}\in{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Gamma_{i}}
,
i=1,2
,

\pi_{1}^{-1}(p^{1})\cap\pi_{2}^{-1}(p^{2})
is a singleton set, the unique point of
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Gamma}
whose projection to each
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Gamma_{i}}
is
p^{i}
.
16
16

16

As both projections share a common internal and boundary marking given by the unique internal marking and the root, we may represent each point
p^{1}
and
p^{2}
as the unit disk model with the internal marking at
0
and root marking at
1
. This kills off all automorphisms given by the action of
PSL(2,\mathbb{R})
. Thus, knowing the points
p^{i}\in{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma_{i}}
,
i=1,2
is sufficient to
identify the point
p\in{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
with
\pi_{i}(p)=p^{i}
,
i=1,2
.

Hence

\#Z(\mathbf{s})=\sum_{p_{j_{1}}^{1}\in Z(\zeta_{1})}\sum_{p_{j_{2}}^{2}\in Z(\zeta_{2})}\varepsilon_{j_{1}}^{1}\varepsilon_{j_{2}}^{2}=\frac{\langle\tau_{0}^{m_{1}}\sigma^{m_{1}+1}\rangle^{1/r,o}}{m_{1}!}\frac{\langle\tau_{0}^{m_{2}}\sigma^{m_{2}+1}\rangle^{1/s,o}}{m_{2}!}.

We note that the division by
m_{1}!m_{2}!
is because of our convention that we do not mark the boundary points, unlike
[
5
,
6
]
. By Theorem 1.2
of
[
6
]
,

\#Z(\mathbf{s})=1.

When
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0,r,0,1,\emptyset}
, the
s
-Witten bundle is
rank
0
.
Hence, the multisection
\mathbf{s}
is a pull back of a canonical multisection of
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma_{1}},
where
\Gamma_{1}=\text{for}_{\text{spin}\neq 1}(\Gamma)

is the smooth graded
r
-spin graph with no internal tails and
r+1
boundary tails. Consequently,

\langle\sigma_{1}^{r}\sigma_{12}\rangle^{\mathbf{s},o}=\langle\sigma^{r+1}\rangle^{1/r,o}/r!=-1,

where the last equality used Theorem 1.2 of
[
6
]
. The same argument shows
\langle\sigma_{2}^{s}\sigma_{12}\rangle^{\mathbf{s},o}=-1
.
∎

The reason open
W
-spin invariants depend on the choice of canonical section is that homotopies between these canonical sections may have zeroes on boundary strata. However, we can now check that this does not occur at exchangeable strata.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},k_{12},\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
be a smooth, connected, graded
W
-spin graph which is
balanced with respect to

\mathbf{d}
. Let
\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathbf{s}_{1}
be two canonical multisections for
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
. Take
H
to be a canonical homotopy with
H(\epsilon,-)=\mathbf{s}_{\epsilon}
for
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
that is a part of a family of canonical homotopies as built in Lemma
3.40
. Then

\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times\partial^{\textup{exchange}}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})=0.

Proof.

Suppose
\Xi\in\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
and
\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}\leq\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}-2
. Then by the pigeon-hole principle and (
3.3
),
there either exists a connected component
\Lambda\in{{\textup{Conn}}}({\mathcal{B}}\Xi)
so that
\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}\leq\operatorname{rank}E_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{d})-2
, or two connected components

\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\in{{\textup{Conn}}}({\mathcal{B}}\Xi)
so that
\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda_{i}}=\operatorname{rank}E_{\Lambda_{i}}(\mathbf{d})-1
for
i=1,2
. In either case,
the canonical homotopy
H
will not vanish on the strata
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Xi}
by Lemma
3.40
(1)(b) or (c). In turn, by the canonicity of
H
, it will also not vanish on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}
. Thus, in order for
H
to vanish, we must have that
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}
has codimension one.
Thus, we can write

\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times\partial^{\textup{Exchange}}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Xi\in\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma\\
\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}-1\end{subarray}}\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}}).

Recall that
\Gamma
contains all cyclic orderings of the vertex, and
that the graphs
\Xi\in\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
of codimension one have two open vertices: a rooted vertex and an exchangeable vertex
v
. This exchangeable vertex has one half-edge
h
and two boundary tails
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
Note that
v
has two possible cyclic orderings on its half-edges, namely
\pi_{1}:=(h\to t_{1}\to t_{2})
and
\pi_{2}:=(h\to t_{2}\to t_{1})
, which correspond to two collections of connected components of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}
.

We now make two new
W
-spin graphs
\Xi_{1}
and
\Xi_{2}
that are the same as
\Xi
, but the only cyclic ordering on
v
for
\Xi_{i}
is
\pi_{i}
.
The moduli
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{1}}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{2}}
are related by the (involutive) map
\textup{Exchange}_{v}
. Since
H
is a canonical homotopy, we have by Observation
3.9
that

|\#Z(H_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{1}}]})|=|\#Z(H_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\textup{Exchange}_{v}(\Xi_{1})}})|=|\#Z([H_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{2}}})|,

while Proposition
2.56
shows that their orientations as boundary strata in
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
are opposite. Thus, for any
\Xi\in\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
of codimension one, we have that

\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})=\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{1}}})+\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi_{2}}})=0.

∎

The main theorem of this subsection is:

Theorem 5.4
.

\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d},{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}})
is independent of the choice of family
of canonical multisections
\mathbf{s}
.

We will now assemble some of the key notation for keeping track of
terms in
{\mathcal{A}}(I,\mathbf{d},{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}})

and critical boundary graphs which
play a role in changes of the individual open invariants appearing
in
{\mathcal{A}}(I,\mathbf{d},{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}})
.
(See Definition
2.33
for critical boundary graphs).
This notation will be used both in
the proof of the above theorem and in the proof of the open topological
recursion formula of the next subsection.

Notation 5.5
.

Let
I
be a set of internal markings with
|I|=l
.
For
h\leq l
, write
\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
for the collection of sets
of the form

\{(I_{1},k_{1}(1),k_{2}(1)),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}

where
I_{1},\ldots,I_{h}
is an ordered partition of
I
into
h
non-empty pieces,
k_{1}(i),k_{2}(i)\geq 0
for all
i
and

(5.2)

k_{1}(i)=r(I_{i})~(\text{mod}~r),~k_{2}(i)=s(I_{i})~(\text{mod}~s),~\text{and }sk_{1}(i)+rk_{2}(i)=m(I_{i},\mathbf{d}).

Write
\mathcal{P}(I,\mathbf{d})=\bigcup_{h\in[l]}\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
.
For
P={\{(I_{1},k_{1}(1),k_{2}(1)),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}}\in\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d}),
let
\Gamma_{P}
be the following disjoint union of graded rooted
graphs:

(5.3)

\Gamma_{P}:=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{h}\Gamma_{P,j}:=\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{h}\Gamma_{0,k_{1}(j),k_{2}(j),1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I_{j}}}.

The graphs
\Gamma_{P}
for
P\in\mathcal{P}(I,\mathbf{d})
are precisely the smooth graphs which correspond to the product of open invariants in
Notation
3.42
for a given summand. With notation as in the first paragraph of the proof of Theorem

5.1
, note that
E=E_{\Gamma_{P}}(\mathbf{d})

naturally decomposes
as
E=\boxplus_{\Lambda\in{{\textup{Conn}}}(\Gamma_{P})}E_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{d})
.
The requirement (
5.2
) then guarantees that
for each
\Lambda\in{{\textup{Conn}}}(\Gamma_{P})
,

\operatorname{rank}E_{\Lambda}(\mathbf{d})=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
, i.e.,
\Lambda
is balanced,
see Proposition
2.31
.

Notation 5.6
.

We now consider notation for accounting for critical boundaries of
graphs
\Gamma_{P}
.
Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
and take

, and

(5.4)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq k_{1}(0)\leq k_{1}(j),

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq k_{2}(0)\leq k_{2}(j),

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq R_{0}\leq k_{1}(j)-k_{1}(0),

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq S_{0}\leq k_{2}(j)-k_{2}(0).

We will construct a boundary graph
\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}\in\partial^{0}\Gamma_{P}\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma_{P}
.
This graph keeps all but the

with a graph

•

•

above; where

–

If

This will correspond to wall-crossing phenomena for the open invariants

Notation 5.7
.

We next introduce an analogue of the set
\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
that corresponds to bases of critical boundaries. For
h\geq 0
, let
\mathcal{Q}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
be the collection of sets

\{(I_{0},k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}

so that the following hold:

•

\{I_{0},\ldots,I_{h}\}
is an ordered partition of
[h]
such that

is nonempty and at most one
I_{j}
is empty;

•

k_{1}(i)\equiv r(I_{i})\pmod{r}
for all

;

•

k_{2}(i)\equiv s(I_{i})\pmod{s}
for all

; and

•

for all

i
.

These conditions arise from Propositions
2.31
and
2.39
. Set

\mathcal{Q}(I,\mathbf{d})=\bigcup_{h=1}^{l}\mathcal{Q}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})

with
l=|I|
.
Given a
Q=\{(I_{0},k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}\in\mathcal{Q}(I,\mathbf{d})
, we can construct a graph
\Lambda_{Q}
with
h+1
connected components so that

•

one connected component is the critical graph

\Lambda_{Q,0}:=\Gamma_{0,k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1,0,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I_{0}}};

•

h
connected components are the balanced graphs

\Lambda_{Q,j}:=\Gamma_{0,k_{1}(j),k_{2}(j),1,\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I_{j}}}

for
1\leq j\leq h
.

These graphs are the graphs corresponding to the
bases of critical boundaries
for some graph
\Gamma_{P}
associated to
P\in\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
.
In other words,
{\mathcal{B}}{\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}}

is a critical boundary graph of
\Gamma_{P}
if and only if

\Lambda_{Q}\succ{\mathcal{B}}{\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}}

for some
Q
,
using the notation in Definition
1.47
.
We remark that there is not necessarily a unique
P
which satisfies this property, and indeed, it is the combinatorics of how the same base graph

\Lambda_{Q}
contributes to critical boundaries of different

{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma_{P}}
that is key to many of the arguments in the rest of
this paper. This kind of argument first appears in Part (B) of the proof of
Theorem
5.4
.

Consider
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
with

I_{j}=\emptyset
for some
1\leq j\leq h
. Then the graph
\Lambda_{Q,j}
is rooted and has either
k_{1}(j)=r,k_{2}(j)=0
or
k_{1}(j)=0,k_{2}(j)=s
, by Proposition
2.31
(a) and (b). Thus, we can partition the set
\mathcal{Q}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
into three subsets
\mathcal{Q}^{r}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})\cup\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})\cup\mathcal{Q}^{\neq\emptyset}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})

defined as follows:

(1)

\mathcal{Q}^{r}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
is made of partitions with some
1\leq j\leq h
so that
I_{j}=\emptyset
,
k_{1}(j)=r,k_{2}(j)=0
,

(2)

\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
is made of partitions which contain some empty
I_{j}
with
k_{1}(j)=0,k_{2}(j)=s
, and

(3)

\mathcal{Q}^{\neq\emptyset}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
is made of partitions where
I_{j}
is nonempty for all
j
.

We define

{\mathcal{Q}}^{\not=\emptyset}(I,\mathbf{d})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{l}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{\not=\emptyset}(I,\mathbf{d}),\qquad{\mathcal{Q}}^{\not=\emptyset}_{\leq h}(I,\mathbf{d})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{h}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}^{\not=\emptyset}(I,\mathbf{d}),

and similar notation for
{\mathcal{Q}}^{r}(I,\mathbf{d})
and

{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}(I,\mathbf{d})
.

We then also have various maps between these sets. First, we have

(5.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}_{h}^{\not=\emptyset}(I,\mathbf{d})\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{h+1}^{r}(I,\mathbf{d}),\quad\quad&Q\mapsto Q^{+r}\\
\mathcal{Q}_{h}^{\not=\emptyset}(I,\mathbf{d})\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{h+1}^{s}(I,\mathbf{d}),\quad\quad&Q\mapsto Q^{+s}\end{split}

with

(5.6)

\displaystyle{Q}^{+r}

\displaystyle=Q\cup\{(\emptyset,r,0)\}

\displaystyle{Q}^{+s}

\displaystyle=Q\cup\{(\emptyset,0,s)\},

with the convention on ordering the elements of
Q^{+r}
or
Q^{+s}

that the additional element is added at the end. We may define the inverse
maps

Q^{r}_{h+1}(I,\mathbf{d})\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{h}^{\not=\emptyset}(I,\mathbf{d}),\quad\quad Q\mapsto Q^{-}

via, if
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{r}_{h+1}(I,\mathbf{d})
with
I_{i}=\emptyset
,
the element
Q^{-}
is given by

(5.7)

(I^{Q^{-}}_{j},k_{1}^{Q^{-}}(j),k_{2}^{Q^{-}}(j))=\begin{cases}(I^{Q}_{j},k_{1}^{Q}(j),k_{2}^{Q}(j)),~~\text{if $j<i$,}\\
(I^{Q}_{j+1},k_{1}^{Q}(j+1),k_{2}^{Q}(j+1)),~~\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Similarly we obtain

Q^{s}_{h+1}(I,\mathbf{d})\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{h}^{\not=\emptyset}(I,\mathbf{d}),\quad\quad Q\mapsto Q^{-}.

Example 5.8
.

Consider the LG model
(x^{4}+y^{5},\mu_{4}\times\mu_{5})
. Take
|I|=2
where
(a_{1},b_{1})=(2,2)
and
(a_{2},b_{2})=(2,3)
. Then we have examples where

\{(I,1,1),(\emptyset,4,0)\}\in\mathcal{Q}^{r}_{1}(I,\mathbf{0});\qquad\{(I,1,1),(\emptyset,0,5)\}\in\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{1}(I,\mathbf{0}).

The graphs corresponding to these partitions are the detachings of the graphs found in Figure
9
(B) and (D). If we instead take
|I|=3
where
(a_{1},b_{1})=(1,1)
,
(a_{2},b_{2})=(2,2)
, and
(a_{3},b_{3})=(2,3)
, then we have

\{(\{2,3\},1,1),(\{1\},1,1)\}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\neq 0}_{1}(I,\mathbf{0}).

This graph corresponding to this partition is the detaching of the graph found in Figure
7
(D). We remark that the vertex
v_{2}
in the figure above serves as
v_{0}
in the present notation.

Proof of Theorem
5.4
.

We outline the proof by explaining the five steps we will take:

(A)

We identify the strata on which a canonical homotopy may vanish: these are precisely those strata which correspond to critical boundary graphs in Definition
2.33
. We then show these coincide with the graphs introduced
in Notation
5.6
.

(B)

We classify all the ways that we can glue the bases of critical boundaries to create critical boundaries so that they can be smoothed to a balanced graph.

(C)

We compute how much an introduction of a zero by a homotopy for a critical graph will change the invariants associated to such a moduli problem.

(D)

We provide closed formulas for how the invariants change with respect to the number of zeros in a given homotopy.

(E)

We show that, for any introduction of a zero through the homotopy for a critical graph,
\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d},{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}})
remains invariant.

Part (A). Identification of strata where the homotopy may vanish
and classification of critical boundary graphs.

Given two families of canonical multisections
\mathbf{s}_{1}
and
\mathbf{s}_{2}
, we may compare them via the family of simple canonical homotopies
H
constructed in Lemma
3.40
. In this context,
as we will use Lemma
2.22
to understand
changes in open invariants appearing in

\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d},{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}})
, we identify boundary strata where
H

may vanish.

To this end, start with a smooth balanced connected graph
\Gamma

with respect to the descendent vector
\mathbf{d}
.
We now identify all boundary graphs
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma
such that the homotopy
H^{\Gamma}
between
\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\Gamma}
and

\mathbf{s}_{2}^{\Gamma}
may vanish in
[0,1]\times{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
.

First, if
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma
is irrelevant, then
as in the first paragraph of Case 1 of the proof of Lemma
3.40
a connected component of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
is irrelevant as well. By Lemma
3.40
(1)(a) and canonicity,
H^{\Gamma}
will then not vanish on
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
.

Second, suppose
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma
is relevant.
We claim that, for
H^{\Gamma}|_{[0,1]\times{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}}
to vanish,
we must have that
\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}-1
. Indeed, if
\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}<\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}-1
, then in the notation of
Observation
3.12
,
\nu\geq 3
. By the fact that

\Gamma
is balanced for
\mathbf{d}
, we also always have
\alpha=0
and
\beta\geq\sigma
in (
3.3
), and hence by
the pigeon-hole principal,
there exists a connected component
\Xi
of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
such that

\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Xi}\leq\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi}(\mathbf{d})-2
or there exists
two connected components
\Xi_{1},\Xi_{2}
of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
such that

\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Xi_{i}}=\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{i}}(\mathbf{d})-1
for
i=1,2
. In either case,

H^{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}
does
not vanish on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}
by Lemma
3.40
(1)(b)
or (c),
and, in turn,
H^{\Gamma}
does not vanish on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
.

Note that the only codimension one relevant boundary strata
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
are those with a single boundary node.
Hence if
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma
is such that
H^{\Gamma}
vanishes somewhere on
[0,1]\times{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
, then
\Lambda
consists of two open vertices connected by a boundary edge, and a number of boundary tails. Take
v_{1}
to be the rooted vertex with corresponding boundary half-edge
h_{1}
and let
v_{2}
be the additional vertex with half-edge
h_{2}
.

Since
\Lambda
is relevant we only have the following possibilities for the twist at the boundary edge:

(i)

\text{tw}(h_{1})=(r-2,0),{\text{alt}}(h_{1})=(1,0),\text{tw}(h_{2})=(0,s-2),
and
{\text{alt}}(h_{2})=(0,1)
;

(ii)

\text{tw}(h_{1})=(0,s-2),{\text{alt}}(h_{1})=(0,1),\text{tw}(h_{2})=(r-2,0),
and
{\text{alt}}(h_{2})=(1,0)
;

(iii)

\text{tw}(h_{1})=(r-2,s-2),{\text{alt}}(h_{1})=(1,1),\text{tw}(h_{2})=(0,0),
and
{\text{alt}}(h_{2})=(0,0)
;

(iv)

\text{tw}(h_{1})=(0,0),{\text{alt}}(h_{1})=(0,0),\text{tw}(h_{2})=(r-2,s-2),
and
{\text{alt}}(h_{2})=(1,1)
.

When we pass to the base
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
in case (iii) (resp. (iv)), we forget the half-edge
h_{2}
(resp.
h_{1}
), so
\beta=1
. Here we have two cases, depending on whether forgetting the half-edge will keep the corresponding vertex stable or make it partially stable, i.e., if
\sigma=0
or
1
in the notation of
Observation
3.12
.
If the corresponding vertex remains stable, so that
\sigma=0
in
(
3.3
), we again have one connected component
\Xi
of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
with

\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Xi}\leq\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi}(\mathbf{d})-2
or two connected components
\Xi_{i}
of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
with
\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Xi_{i}}\leq\operatorname{rank}E_{\Xi_{i}}(\mathbf{d})-1
. Hence again by Lemma
3.40
(1)(b)
or (c),

H^{\Gamma}
is nowhere vanishing on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
.
If on the other hand
\sigma=1
, we may describe the partially stable vertex. Indeed, if it had two
r
-points or two
s
-points then the constraints given for a pre-graded
{(r,s)}
-spin structure in Observation
2.26
do not hold. Consequently, it must have exactly one
r
-point and one
s
-point, which implies that

\Lambda
is an exchangeable critical boundary graph. In this case, we have by Lemma
5.3
that while
H^{\Gamma}
may vanish on

{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
, this vanishing will not contribute to a change
of the total number of zeroes between
\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\Gamma}
and

\mathbf{s}_{2}^{\Gamma}
. Thus we may ignore any contributions from these strata.
17
17

17

One could view these strata as irrelevant relevant boundary strata.

It is left to analyze cases (i) and (ii) above. Indeed, both these cases can happen, but we can deduce further restrictions on the vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, since
v_{1}
is the vertex which has a root.
Using (
2.23
) we obtain

\operatorname{rank}\,E_{v_{1}}(\mathbf{d})\equiv\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{1}}\pmod{2},~\operatorname{rank}\,E_{v_{2}}(\mathbf{d})\equiv\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{2}}+1\pmod{2}.

By Lemma
3.40
, this requires that

\operatorname{rank}\,E_{v_{1}}(\mathbf{d})=\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{1}},~\operatorname{rank}\,E_{v_{2}}(\mathbf{d})=\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{2}}+1

as otherwise
H
will not vanish on
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
.

We summarize the above discussion. Suppose
H^{\Gamma}
vanishes on

[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
with
\Lambda
not an exchangeable graph.
Take the two connected components
\Xi_{1}
and
\Xi_{2}
of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda

corresponding to the vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. Then
\Xi_{1}
is a balanced graph and
\Xi_{2}
is a critical graph, so
\Lambda
is a critical boundary graph in the terminology of Definitions
2.32
and
2.33
.

We now remove the connectedness assumption on
\Gamma
. In this case we may
write
\Gamma=\Gamma_{P}
for some
P\in\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
as defined in Equation (
5.3
) in Notation
5.5
.
Let
\Lambda
be a boundary graph for
\Gamma
. In order for
H^{\Gamma}
to vanish on
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
, each connected component of
\Lambda
must be either smooth or a critical boundary graph as deduced above. Moreover,
if there exists two connected components
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}
of
\Lambda
that are critical boundary graphs, then by Lemma
3.40
(1)(c),

H^{\Gamma}
is non-vanishing on
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
.
Hence
\Lambda
has at most one critical boundary component, thus
has only one boundary edge. This implies that, if the graph
\Lambda
is not exchangeable and
H
vanishes on
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
, then the graph
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
consists of
h
graded, balanced, rooted vertices and one critical graded open vertex, and all the boundary half-edges of
\Lambda
which are not tails are singly twisted.

From this description, it is now immediately clear that, given

P\in\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
, any non-exchangeable critical boundary graph
for
P
is of the form

\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}
of Notation

5.6

with indices as given subject to the constraints of
(
5.4
).
18
18

18

We remark that
k_{i}(0)
must be non-negative as the graph
\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}
must be relevant. Otherwise, the graph would satisfy condition (3) of Definition
1.57
and be irrelevant.
Further, in order for such a graph
to be a critical boundary graph, the vertex
v_{0}
must be critical
and the vertex
v_{j}
must be balanced. In particular, we then
have
\Lambda_{Q}\succ{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}

where, using the notation of Notation
5.7
, if

P=\{(I_{1},k_{1}(1),k_{2}(1)),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}
,

(5.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q=Q_{P,j,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0)}:=\{(I_{0},&k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1),(I_{1},k_{1}(1),k_{2}(1)),\ldots,\\
&(I_{j}\setminus I_{0},k_{1}(j)-k_{1}(0),k_{2}(j)-k_{2}(0)),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}\in\mathcal{Q}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d}).\end{split}

We write

(5.9)

{\widetilde{\text{Crit}}}(P):=\{\Lambda\in\partial\Gamma_{P}\,|\,\hbox{$\Lambda$ is a critical
boundary graph}\}.

Elements of
{\widetilde{\text{Crit}}}(P)
are all of the form
\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}
. We then define

{\widetilde{\text{Crit}}}(I,\mathbf{d}):=\bigcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}(I,\mathbf{d})}{\widetilde{\text{Crit}}}(P)

to be the set of all critical boundary graphs that have the set
I
of internal markings with descendent vector
\mathbf{d}
.

Part (B). Classification of smooth graded balanced graphs whose bases of critical boundaries include a fixed critical graph.

We now classify, given
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
, the critical boundary graphs with base
\Lambda_{Q}
. In particular, define

A^{\epsilon}_{Q}:=\{\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),I_{0},R_{0},S_{0}}\,|\,{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}\prec\Lambda_{Q}\}.

Note that
P\in{\mathcal{P}}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
is not fixed here. We may describe
A^{\epsilon}_{Q}

depending on whether
Q
lies in
\mathcal{Q}^{r}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
,

\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
or
\mathcal{Q}^{\not=\emptyset}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
.

Given
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{r}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
,
there exists a unique
j\in[h]
so that
(I_{j},k_{1}(j),k_{2}(j))=(\emptyset,r,0)
.
Define

(5.10)

\begin{split}\widehat{Q}_{j}:=\{(I_{1},k_{1}(1),k_{2}(1)),\ldots,&(I_{j-1},k_{1}(j-1),k_{2}(j-1)),\\
&(I_{0},k_{1}(0)+r,k_{2}(0)),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}\in\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d}).\end{split}

We may take the graph
\Lambda_{Q}
and attach an
s
-tail on the vertex
v_{0}
to an
r
-tail on the vertex
v_{j}
in order to obtain the critical boundary graph
\Gamma_{\widehat{Q}_{j},j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}
introduced
in Notation
5.6
, where

•

R_{0}\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\}
, indicating how many
r
-points
lie after the root and before the node in the cyclic order associated
to the graph; and

•

S_{0}=0
as there are no
s
-tails attached to
v_{j}
.

Note that the smoothing of
\Gamma_{\widehat{Q}_{j},j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}

agrees with
\Gamma_{\widehat{Q}_{j}}
modulo cyclic orders.
Therefore, for
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{h}^{r}(I,\mathbf{d})
, we have that
A^{1}_{Q}
is the collection of such critical boundary graphs
\Gamma_{\widehat{Q}_{j},j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}
above and
A^{2}_{Q}=\varnothing
. Note that by the above discussion, the moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma_{\widehat{Q}_{j}}}
has
r
boundary strata of the form
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}
, with
\Xi\in A_{Q}^{1}
,
depending on the choice of
R_{0}
.

For
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{h}^{s}(I,\mathbf{d})
, there exists a unique
j\in[h]
so that
(I_{j},k_{1}(j),k_{2}(j))=(\emptyset,0,s)
. In this case, we have that
A^{2}_{Q}
is the analogous collection of critical boundary graphs
\Gamma_{\widehat{Q}_{j},j,2,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}
as above and
A^{1}_{Q}=\varnothing
,
with
R_{0}=0
, and
S_{0}\in\{0,\ldots,s-1\}
.
Similarly as before,

(5.11)

\begin{split}\widehat{Q}_{j}:=\{(I_{1},k_{1}(1),k_{2}(1)),\ldots,&(I_{j-1},k_{1}(j-1),k_{2}(j-1)),\\
&(I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0)+s),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}\in\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d}).\end{split}

The moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma_{\widehat{Q}_{j}}}
has
s
boundary strata

{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}
, with
\Xi\in A_{Q}^{2}
depending on the choice of
S_{0}
.

Lastly, given
Q=\{(I_{0},k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\neq\emptyset}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
, we may attach the vertex
v_{0}
to any other vertex
v_{1},\ldots,v_{h}
. We thus
define, for any
j\in[h]
,

(5.12)

\begin{split}\widehat{Q}_{j}:=\{(I_{1},k_{1}(1),k_{2}(1)),\ldots,(I_{0}\cup I_{j},k_{1}(0)+k_{1}(j),&k_{2}(0)+k_{2}(j)),\\
&\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}\in\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d}).\end{split}

This keeps all triplets the same from
1
to
h
except the
j
th one. There are potentially many ways to attach the vertices
v_{0}
and
v_{j}
, depending on the number of
r
-points and
s
-points on the
j
th disk.

•

If
k_{1}(j)>0
then there is an
r
-point on
v_{j}
to attach to an
s
-point on
v_{0}
, hence there is a critical boundary graph
\Gamma_{\widehat{Q}_{j},j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}
where
0\leq R_{0}\leq k_{1}(j)-1
and
0\leq S_{0}\leq k_{2}(j)
. There are
k_{1}(j)(k_{2}(j)+1)
such boundaries, forming
the set
A^{1}_{Q}
.

•

Analogously, if
k_{2}(j)>0
, then there is an
s
-point on
v_{j}
to attach to an
r
-point on
v_{0}
, hence there is a critical boundary graph
\Gamma_{\widehat{Q}_{j},j,2,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}
where
0\leq R_{0}\leq k_{1}(j)
and
0\leq S_{0}\leq k_{2}(j)-1
. There are
(k_{1}(j)+1)k_{2}(j)
such boundaries,
forming the set
A^{2}_{Q}
.

Note now that

(5.13)

\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}(I,\mathbf{d}),\epsilon\in\{1,2\}}A_{Q}^{\epsilon}={\widetilde{\text{Crit}}}(I,\mathbf{d}).

The containment of the first set into the second is by our discussion above. The opposite containment is by the discussion in Part (A).

It will be useful to partition the sets
A^{\epsilon}_{Q}
as follows.
Define the sets

(5.14)

\displaystyle A^{1}_{Q,j,R_{0}}

\displaystyle=\{\Gamma_{\widehat{Q}_{j},j,1,k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}:0\leq S_{0}\leq k_{2}(j)\};

\displaystyle A^{2}_{Q,j,S_{0}}

\displaystyle=\{\Gamma_{\widehat{Q}_{j},j,2,k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}:0\leq R_{0}\leq k_{1}(j)\}.

Here, the set
A^{1}_{Q,j,R_{0}}
(resp.
A^{2}_{Q,j,S_{0}}
) enumerates all possible choices of
S_{0}
(resp.
R_{0}
) once we fix
Q
,
j
and
R_{0}
(resp.
S_{0}
).

To summarize:

•

If
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{r}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
and
I_{j}=\varnothing
, then we have that

(5.15)

A^{1}_{Q}=\bigcup_{R_{0}=0}^{r-1}A^{1}_{Q,j,R_{0}};\quad A^{2}_{Q}=\varnothing.

•

If
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
and
I_{j}=\varnothing
, then we have that

(5.16)

A^{1}_{Q}=\varnothing;\quad A^{2}_{Q}=\bigcup_{S_{0}=0}^{s-1}A^{2}_{Q,j,S_{0}}.

•

If
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{\neq 0}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
, then we have that

(5.17)

A^{1}_{Q}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{h}\bigcup_{R_{0}=0}^{k_{1}(j)-1}A^{1}_{Q,j,R_{0}};\quad A^{2}_{Q}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{h}\bigcup_{S_{0}=0}^{k_{2}(j)-1}A^{2}_{Q,j,S_{0}}.

We lastly reorganize the description of critical boundaries
using the notation
Q^{+r}
,
Q^{+s}
of (
5.6
).
We then have

(5.18)

\bigsqcup_{P\in\mathcal{P}(I,\mathbf{d})}{\widetilde{\text{Crit}}}(P)=\bigsqcup_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{\not=\emptyset}(I,\mathbf{d})}(A_{Q}^{1}\sqcup A_{Q}^{2}\sqcup A_{Q^{+r}}^{1}\sqcup A_{Q^{+s}}^{2}).

Part (C) Zeros of homotopies, from boundary to base.

Denote by
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0}}
(respectively
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{P,j,2,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),S_{0}}
) the following union of boundary strata of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma_{P}}
:

(5.19)

{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0}}:=\bigcup_{\Xi\in A^{1}_{Q,j,R_{0}}}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}\qquad\left(\textrm{resp. }{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{P,j,2,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),S_{0}}:=\bigcup_{\Xi\in A^{2}_{Q,j,S_{0}}}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}\right),

with
Q=Q_{P,j,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0)}
given in (
5.8
).

Recall that for
\Gamma_{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}\in A^{1}_{Q}
, we have

{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma_{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}\prec\Lambda_{Q}
.
Hence, if

Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{h}^{r}(I,\mathbf{d})\cup\mathcal{Q}_{h}^{\neq 0}(I,\mathbf{d})
, we have a map

F_{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0}}:{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0}}\rightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{Q}},

which is defined to be
F_{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0}}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}}=F_{\Xi}
for any
\Xi\in A^{1}_{Q,j,R_{0}}
.
Here
F_{\Xi}
is the map defined in Definition
3.5
.
The map
F_{P,j,2,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),S_{0}}
is defined analogously.

We can see that the map
F_{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0}}
is surjective. Indeed, an element of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{Q}}
is a disjoint union of
graded
W
-spin disks
\cup_{i=0}^{h}\Sigma_{i}
. We may then obtain
an element of the domain of
F_{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0}}
which maps to this given
element of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{Q}}
as follows.
In the cyclic order of points on

\Sigma_{j}
, we glue the
(R_{0}+1)^{st}

r
-point on
\Sigma_{j}
after the root to
some choice of
s
-point on
\Sigma_{0}
. This, in turn, determines the number
S_{0}
of
s
-points that follow the root and precede the glued node.
Then the resulting glued disk lies in

{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma_{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0}}.

Since we had
k_{2}(0)+1
choices of
s
-point on the vertex
v_{0}
to attach to the vertex
v_{j}
, this map is of degree
\pm(k_{2}(0)+1)
. Analogously,
F_{P,j,2,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),S_{0}}
is surjective with degree
\pm(k_{1}(0)+1)
.

We now aim to relate the orientation of the total space of the Witten bundle over
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma_{P}}
near the boundary
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0}}}
to the orientation of the Witten bundle over
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{Q}}
.
To do so, we consider the following diagram:

(5.20)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma_{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\Gamma_{P}}}

Here, Theorem
2.58
(
2
) relates the orientation on the total space of the Witten bundle over
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0}}}
to that over
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{Q}}
. This is done by inducing the orientation on the total space of the Witten bundle over
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0}}}
by contracting
\iota_{\Gamma_{P}}^{*}o_{\Gamma_{P}}
with the outward pointing normal for the inclusion
\iota_{\Gamma_{P}}
. Note that the term
K_{2}(v_{2})
appearing in Theorem
2.58
(
2
) is defined in Notation
2.57
, and here takes the value
k_{2}(0)
.

We then can conclude that if we consider a homotopy
H^{\Gamma}
where

(5.21)

H^{\Gamma}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}}}=(\mathrm{id}_{[0,1]}\times F_{\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}})^{*}\boxplus_{\Lambda\in{{\textup{Conn}}}({\mathcal{B}}\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}})}H^{\Lambda},

then, by taking union over boundary strata given in Equation (
5.19
), we see that

(5.22)

\displaystyle\#Z(H^{\Gamma}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{P,j,1,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0}}}})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k_{2}(0)}(k_{2}(0)+1)\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q}}),

\displaystyle\#Z(H^{\Gamma}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{P,j,2,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),S_{0}}}})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k_{2}(0)-1}(k_{1}(0)+1)\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q}}).

Part (D): Number of zeros for a given simple homotopy.

Recall that we have chosen the family of transverse homotopies

H^{*}
between the given
families of transverse multisections
\mathbf{s}_{1}
and
\mathbf{s}_{2}

to be simple.
By Part (A) of this proof, we know that this homotopy may only vanish in two cases: exchangeable critical boundaries and boundaries corresponding to graphs
\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}
. By Lemma
5.3
, we know that

\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times\partial^{\textup{exchange}}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})=0.

Thus, from here until the end of the proof we will only need to consider homotopies of the form given in (
5.21
). Thus we are in a context where we may use (
5.22
) above.

We now claim that, in the notation of
(
5.7
),
given
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{r}_{h+1}(I,\mathbf{d})\cup\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{h+1}(I,\mathbf{d})
where
I_{i}=\varnothing
,

(5.23)

\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q}})=-\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q^{-}}}).

Indeed, recall from Example
3.4
that, for the graphs
\Xi=\Gamma_{0,r,0,1,\emptyset}
and
\Xi=\Gamma_{0,0,s,1,\emptyset}
, the boundary
\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}=\emptyset
, hence

\#Z(H^{\Xi}|_{\{t\}\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})
is independent of
t
by Lemma
2.22
. By Theorem
5.1
, the constant invariant associated to this component is

\langle\sigma_{1}^{r}\sigma_{12}\rangle^{o}=\langle\sigma_{2}^{s}\sigma_{12}\rangle^{o}=-1.

Thus
\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q}})=\#Z(H^{\Xi}|_{\{t\}\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})\cdot\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q^{-}}})=-\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q^{-}}})
, proving (
5.23
).

We now give closed formulas for combinations of

\#Z(H^{\Xi})
for various graphs

\Xi
which contribute to changes in the open invariants appearing
in
\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d},{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}})
.
For the formulas below, take
\bullet
to mean the smoothing
{\text{sm}}(\Xi)={\text{sm}}_{E(\Xi)}\Xi,
for any critical boundary graphs
\Xi
that can be found in Equation (
5.13
) above.

Consider any
Q=\{(I_{0},k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1),\ldots,(I_{h+1},k_{1}(h+1),k_{2}(h+1))\}\in\mathcal{Q}^{r}_{h+1}(I,\mathbf{d})
. Then
I_{j}=\emptyset
for some
1\leq j\leq h+1
and we can use Equations (
5.22
) and (
5.23
) to see that

(5.24)

\displaystyle\sum_{\Xi\in A^{1}_{Q}}\#Z(H^{\bullet}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{R_{0}=0}^{r-1}\ \sum_{\Xi\in A^{1}_{Q,j,R_{0}}}\#Z(H^{\bullet}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{R_{0}=0}^{r-1}(-1)^{k_{2}(0)}(k_{2}(0)+1)\cdot\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q}})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k_{2}(0)+1}\cdot r(k_{2}(0)+1)\cdot\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q^{-}}}).

Similarly, for
any
Q=\{(I_{0},k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1),\ldots,(I_{h+1},k_{1}(h+1),k_{2}(h+1))\}\in\mathcal{Q}^{s}_{h+1}(I,\mathbf{d})
,

(5.25)

\sum_{\Xi\in A^{2}_{Q}}\#Z(H^{\bullet}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})=(-1)^{k_{2}(0)}\cdot s(k_{1}(0)+1)\cdot\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q^{-}}}).

For any
Q=\{(I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0)),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}\in\mathcal{Q}^{\neq\emptyset}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
, then the contributions from critical boundary graphs in
A^{1}_{Q}
are

(5.26)

\displaystyle\sum_{\Xi\in A^{1}_{Q}}\#Z(H^{\bullet}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{h}\ \sum_{R_{0}=0}^{k_{1}(j)-1}\ \sum_{\Xi\in A^{1}_{Q,j,R_{0}}}\#Z(H^{\bullet}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{h}\ \sum_{R_{0}=0}^{k_{1}(j)-1}\left((-1)^{k_{2}(0)}(k_{2}(0)+1)\right)\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q}})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k_{2}(0)}(k_{2}(0)+1)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{h}k_{1}(j)\right)\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q}}).

Similarly, for
A^{2}_{Q}
we have

(5.27)

\sum_{\Xi\in A^{2}_{Q}}\#Z(H^{\bullet}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})=(-1)^{k_{2}(0)+1}(k_{1}(0)+1)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{h}k_{2}(j)\right)\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q}}).

Part (E): Showing that
\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d},{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}})
is independent of
\mathbf{s}
.
We now show that

(5.28)

\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d},{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}_{1}})-\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d},{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}_{2}})=0.

for any two canonical families of multisections
\mathbf{s}_{1}
and
\mathbf{s}_{2}
. This is equivalent to showing that
\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d},{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}})
remains invariant under the family of homotopies
H
.
Only the invariants corresponding to graphs
\Gamma_{P}
for
P\in\mathcal{P}(I,\mathbf{d})
where there exists a critical boundary
\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}\in{\widetilde{\text{Crit}}}(I,\mathbf{d})
may change. In particular, we can see that the left-hand side of Equation (
5.28
) is of the form

(5.29)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}\in{\widetilde{\text{Crit}}}(I,\mathbf{d}),\\
P=\{(I_{1},k_{1}(1),k_{2}(1)),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}\in\mathcal{P}(I,\mathbf{d})\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{h!}&\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1+\sum_{i=1}^{h}k_{1}(i)}{r})\Gamma(\frac{1+\sum_{i=1}^{h}k_{2}(i)}{s})}{\Gamma(\frac{1+r(I)}{r})\Gamma(\frac{1+s(I)}{s})}\cdot\\
&\cdot\#Z(H^{\Gamma_{P}}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}}}).\end{split}

We see that this summation breaks down further into summands that correspond to different possible bases of critical boundaries. Take
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(I,\mathbf{d})
to be the collection of

Q=\{(I_{0},k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d}),\qquad h\geq 0

such that

•

I_{j}\neq\varnothing
for all
0\leq j\leq h
;

•

the elements
(I_{1},k_{1}(1),k_{2}(1)),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))
are ordered in a way so that, for any two indices
i
and
j
, we have that
\text{min}(I_{i})<\text{min}(I_{j})
if and only if
i<j
.

In the case
h=0
, we just have the data of a critical graph with internal markings
I
. Note that if
h\geq 1
, then
Q
is an element of
\mathcal{Q}^{\neq\emptyset}(I,\mathbf{d})
.
Moreover, in this case, if we consider the action of the symmetric group

S_{h}
on
\mathcal{Q}^{\neq\emptyset}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
given by permuting
the last
h
elements in the ordered partitions in

\mathcal{Q}^{\neq\emptyset}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
, we can see that

\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(I,\mathbf{d})
contains a unique element of each
orbit of this
S_{h}
-action.
Thus
\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(I,\mathbf{d})
has precisely one element
for each graph
\Lambda_{Q}
, with the order of connected
components not taken into account.

Recall the notation, from (
5.6
),

Q^{+r}\in\mathcal{Q}^{r}(I,\mathbf{d})
and
Q^{+s}\in\mathcal{Q}^{s}(I,\mathbf{d})
for any
Q\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(I,\mathbf{d})
. We see for any
Q\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(I,\mathbf{d})
that, by combining Equations (
5.24
) and (
5.26
),

(5.30)

\sum_{\Xi\in A^{1}_{Q}}\#Z(H^{{\text{sm}}_{E(\Xi)}(\Xi)}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})+\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{h}k_{1}(i)}{r}\sum_{\Xi\in A^{1}_{Q^{+r}}}\#Z(H^{{\text{sm}}_{E(\Xi)}(\Xi)}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})=0,

and, by combining Equations (
5.25
) and (
5.27
),

(5.31)

\sum_{\Xi\in A^{2}_{Q}}\#Z(H^{{\text{sm}}_{E(\Xi)}(\Xi)}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})+\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{h}k_{2}(i)}{s}\sum_{\Xi\in A^{2}_{Q^{+s}}}\#Z(H^{{\text{sm}}_{E(\Xi)}(\Xi)}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})=0.

Consequently, using the above two equations and (
5.24
), (
5.25
)
applied with
Q=Q^{+r}
or
Q=Q^{+s}
, we get a new relation

(5.32)

\displaystyle\sum_{\Xi\in A^{1}_{Q}\cup A^{2}_{Q}}\#Z(H^{{\text{sm}}_{E(\Xi)}(\Xi)}

\displaystyle|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})+\frac{1+k_{1}(0)+\sum_{i=1}^{h}k_{1}(i)}{r}\sum_{\Xi\in A^{1}_{Q^{+r}}}\#Z(H^{{\text{sm}}_{E(\Xi)}(\Xi)}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})

\displaystyle+\frac{1+k_{2}(0)+\sum_{i=1}^{h}k_{2}(i)}{s}\sum_{\Xi\in A^{2}_{Q^{+s}}}\#Z(H^{{\text{sm}}_{E(\Xi)}(\Xi)}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})

\displaystyle=((-1)^{k_{2}(0)}+(-1)^{k_{2}(0)+1})(k_{1}(0)+1)(k_{2}(0)+1)\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q}})=0.

Thus, the difference expressed in Equation (
5.29
) simplifies to:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\overline{\mathcal{Q}}(I,\mathbf{d}),\\
Q=\{(I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0)),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}\end{subarray}}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1+\sum_{i=0}^{h}k_{1}(i)}{r})\Gamma(\frac{1+\sum_{i=0}^{h}k_{2}(i)}{s})}{\Gamma(\frac{1+r(I)}{r})\Gamma(\frac{1+s(I)}{s})}\left(\sum_{\Xi\in A^{1}_{Q}\cup A^{2}_{Q}}\#Z(H^{\bullet}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{1+\sum_{i=0}^{h}k_{1}(i)}{r}\sum_{\Xi\in A^{1}_{Q^{+r}}}\#Z(H^{\bullet}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})+\frac{1+\sum_{i=0}^{h}k_{2}(i)}{s}\sum_{\Xi\in A^{1}_{Q^{+s}}}\#Z(H^{\bullet}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})\right)=0,

where
\bullet={\text{sm}}_{E(\Xi)}\Xi
.
The fact that the expression on the left-hand side above agrees with that of
(
5.29
)
follows by: (1) using the partition found in (
5.13
);
(2) noting that we are summing the
k_{\epsilon}(i)
from
0
to
h
rather
than
1
to
h
because we are summing over possible
Q
rather than
P
;
(3) the standard relation
\Gamma(x+1)=x\Gamma(x)
. The
stated vanishing uses the relation in (
5.32
). Therefore, the expression in Notation
3.42
is invariant with respect to choice of family of canonical multisections
\mathbf{s}
.
∎

We now show that the open FJRW invariants corresponding to two families of canonical multisections are related by the action of an element in the Landau-Ginzburg wall-crossing group.

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
\mathbf{s}_{0},\mathbf{s}_{1}
be two families of canonical multisections of the descendent Witten bundles.
Then there exists an element
g\in G_{A_{I}}^{r,s}
such that

W^{\mathbf{s}_{1}}=g(W^{\mathbf{s}_{0}}).

Proof.

By Lemma
3.40
, there exists a family of simple canonical homotopies
H
from
\mathbf{s}_{0}
to
\mathbf{s}_{1}
that satisfies the properties stated therein. By item (3) of that lemma,
we have for each non-empty
A\subseteq I
a time
t_{A}\in(0,1)

so that properties (3)(a) and (b) are satisfied. Further, all these times are
distinct. Order the non-empty subsets
A_{1},\ldots,A_{2^{|I|}-1}
of
I

so that
t_{A_{1}}<\cdots<t_{A_{2^{|I|}-1}}
. We write
t_{i}:=t_{A_{i}}
.

For any descendent
vector
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{A_{i}}
and
\Gamma\in{\text{Crit}}(A_{i},\mathbf{d})
, we define

(5.33)

\Delta(\Gamma):=\#Z(H^{\Gamma}).

Note by property (3)(a), these zeroes occur at time
t_{i}
. We also remark that the descendent vector
\mathbf{d}
is implicitly used and fixed in the construction of
H^{\Gamma}
so we omit it in the notation.

Now set

\displaystyle g_{i}:=\exp\Bigg(\sum_{\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{A_{i}}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\{(A_{i},k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1)\}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}(A_{i},\mathbf{d})}(-1)^{|A_{i}|+k_{2}(0)}\Delta(\Lambda_{Q,0})\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\Bigg(\prod_{j\in A_{i}}u_{j,d_{j}}\Bigg)x^{k_{1}(0)}y^{k_{2}(0)}((k_{2}(0)+1)x\partial_{x}-(k_{1}(0)+1)y\partial_{y})\Bigg).

Note that
g_{i}\in G^{r,s}_{A_{I}}
by the definition of

\mathcal{Q}_{0}(A_{i},\mathbf{d})
.
We will ultimately show that the following choice of
g
gives the desired
result:

g:=g_{2^{|I|}-1}\circ\cdots\circ g_{1}.

For any
t\in[0,1]
, we write
\mathbf{s}_{t}

for the family of canonical sections obtained by
restricting
H
to time
t
.

We first claim that, for any

t\in\tau:=[0,1]\setminus\{t_{i}\,|\,1\leq i\leq 2^{|I|}-1\},

and
\Gamma
a balanced graph with respect to some descendent
vector
\mathbf{d}
,
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}_{t}|_{\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}

is non-vanishing, and hence the degree of the Euler class

Z^{\Gamma}(t):=\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}e(E;\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}_{t}|_{U_{+}\cup\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})

is well-defined.

To show the claim, we now fix some
J\subseteq I
,
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
and

\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})
. Thus
\Gamma=\Gamma_{P}
for some
P=\{(J,k_{1}(\Gamma),k_{2}(\Gamma))\}\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(J,\mathbf{d})
. The analysis of the proof of Theorem
5.4

tells us that the zeros of
H^{\Gamma}|_{[0,1]\times\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}

occur only on two sorts of codimension one boundary strata.

The
first type are codimension one exchangeable boundary strata. If
\Lambda\in\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
represents a codimension one stratum,
then
\Lambda
has two vertices
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
, with
v_{0}
exchangeable,
so that
I(v_{0})=\varnothing
, and thus
I(v_{1})=J
. In particular,
by canonicity of
H^{\Gamma}
,
H^{\Gamma}|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}

can vanish only when
H^{v_{1}}
vanishes, which occurs at time

t_{J}
.

The second type are codimension one boundary strata corresponding to graphs
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma
described as follows. For each
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}(J,\mathbf{d})
,
we constructed sets
A^{1}_{Q},A^{2}_{Q}
of critical boundary graphs.
All boundary zeroes of
H^{\Gamma}
on non-exchangeable strata
lie on some strata
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
where
\Lambda\in A^{1}_{Q}\cup A^{2}_{Q}
for some
Q\in Q_{1}(J,\mathbf{d})
with
\widehat{Q}_{1}=P
.
In particular, we consider those
Q
of the form

(5.34)

Q=\{(I_{0},k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1),(I_{1},k_{1}(1),k_{2}(1))\}

with
I_{0}\not=\varnothing
,
k_{i}(0)+k_{i}(1)=k_{i}(\Gamma)
,
and
I_{0}\cup I_{1}=J
.
Then by Lemma
3.40
(3)(a) and canonicity of
H^{*}
,
if
\Lambda\in A^{1}_{Q}\cup A^{2}_{Q}
, then

H^{\Lambda}
only vanishes at time
t_{I_{0}}
. Thus
we have now seen in particular that
{\bf s}^{\Gamma}_{t}|_{\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}

is non-vanishing whenever
t\in\tau
, hence showing the claim.

Note that for those
t
for which
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}_{t}
is
transversal to the zero section, we have

Z^{\Gamma}(t)=\#Z(\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}_{t}).

For each
t\in\tau
, we obtain
a potential
W^{\mathbf{s}_{t}}
, and by Lemma
2.22
,
this will only depend on the connected component of
\tau
containing

t
by the claimed non-vanishing of such
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}_{t}
on

\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
.
For each
i
, choose
t_{i}^{\pm}

with
t_{i-1}<t_{i}^{-}<t_{i}<t_{i}^{+}<t_{i+1}
(taking
t_{0}=0
and
t_{2^{|I|}}=1
).
Then it will be sufficient to show that

W^{\mathbf{s}_{t_{i}^{+}}}=g_{i}(W^{\mathbf{s}_{t_{i}^{-}}})
.

By Lemma
2.22
,

Z^{\Gamma}(t_{i}^{+})=Z^{\Gamma}(t_{i}^{-})+\#Z(H^{\Gamma}|_{[t_{i}^{-},t_{i}^{+}]\times\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}).

Now
\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}=\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
, and
by Lemma
5.3
we have that

\#Z(H^{\Gamma}|_{[t_{i}^{-},t_{i}^{+}]\times\partial^{\textup{exchange}}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})=0,

hence

(5.35)

Z^{\Gamma}(t_{i}^{+})=Z^{\Gamma}(t_{i}^{-})+\#Z(H^{\Gamma}|_{[t_{i}^{-},t_{i}^{+}]\times\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}).

Thus we only get contributions to
Z^{\Gamma}(t_{i}^{+})-Z^{\Gamma}(t_{i}^{-})
from
those
Q
as in (
5.34
) with
I_{0}=A_{i}
.

Thus, we can use (
5.24
), (
5.25
), (
5.26
), and (
5.27
),
and compute that, with the summation over
Q

ranging over those
Q
as in (
5.34
)
with
I_{0}=A_{i}
,

(5.36)

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z^{\Gamma}(t_{i}^{+})-Z^{\Gamma}(t_{i}^{-})={}&\#Z(H^{\Gamma}|_{[t_{i}^{-},t_{i}^{+}]\times\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})\\
={}&\sum_{Q}\sum_{\Xi\in A^{1}_{Q}\cup A^{2}_{Q}}\#Z(H^{\Gamma}|_{[t_{i}^{-},t_{i}^{+}]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}})\\
={}&\sum_{Q}\bigg((-1)^{k_{2}(0)}(k_{2}(0)+1)k_{1}(1)+(-1)^{k_{2}(0)+1}(k_{1}(0)+1)k_{2}(1)\bigg)\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q}}).\end{split}

Using the definition of
\Delta
in (
5.33
) and the fact that

H^{\Lambda_{Q,1}}
does not have any zeros near time
t_{i}
on the
boundary of
\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{Q,1}}
as

A_{i}\not\subseteq I(\Lambda_{Q,1})
, we can see that

(5.37)

\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q}})=\#Z(H^{\Lambda_{Q,0}})\cdot\#Z({\bf s}^{\Lambda_{Q,1}}_{t_{i}})=\Delta(\Lambda_{Q,0})Z^{\Lambda_{Q,1}}(t_{i}^{-}),

We now calculate the coefficient of
x^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}y^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\prod_{j\in J}u_{j,d_{j}}
in
g_{i}(W^{\mathbf{s}_{t_{i}^{-}}})
and show it coincides
with the corresponding coefficient in
W^{\mathbf{s}_{t_{i}^{+}}}
.
We write
g_{i}=\exp(v_{i})
. As the monomial
\prod_{j\in A_{i}}u_{j,d_{j}}
has square zero,
v_{i}\circ v_{i}=0
, hence
g_{i}=\mathrm{Id}+v_{i}
.
The term with monomial
x^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}y^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\prod_{j\in J}u_{j,d_{j}}
in
W^{\mathbf{s}_{t_{i}^{-}}}
is

(-1)^{|J|-1}Z^{\Gamma}(t_{i}^{-})x^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}y^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\prod_{j\in J}u_{j,d_{j}},

and applying
\mathrm{Id}
to this gives the same term.
All other terms involving the same monomial arise from applying a summand
of
v_{i}
arising from
Q_{0}:=\{(A_{i},k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1)\}\in\mathcal{Q}_{0}(A_{i},\mathbf{d})
to a term of
W^{\mathbf{s}_{t^{-}_{i}}}
arising from
a balanced (with respect to
\mathbf{d}
) graph
\Gamma_{P_{1}}
with

P_{1}=\{(J\setminus A_{i},k_{1}(1),k_{2}(1))\}

with
k_{i}(0)+k_{i}(1)=k_{i}(\Gamma)
. Thus
Q=Q_{0}\cup P_{1}\in\mathcal{Q}_{1}(J,\mathbf{d})
and
\widehat{Q}_{1}=P
, and all such
Q
