with

I_{0}=A_{i}
and
\widehat{Q}_{1}=P
occur in this way.
Thus the coefficient of
x^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}y^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\prod_{j\in J}u_{j,d_{j}}
, after applying
g_{i}
, becomes, with again the sum over

Q
ranging over those
Q
as in (
5.34
) with
I_{0}=A_{i}
,

\displaystyle(-1)^{|J|-1}Z^{\Gamma}(t_{i}^{-})+

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\sum_{Q}\Big((-1)^{(|A_{i}|+k_{2}(0))+(|J\setminus A_{i}|-1)}\big((k_{2}(0)+1)k_{1}(1)-(k_{1}(0)+1)k_{2}(1)\big)\Delta(\Lambda_{Q,0})Z^{\Lambda_{Q,1}}(t_{i}^{-})\Big)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{|J|-1}\left(Z^{\Gamma}(t_{i}^{-})+\sum_{Q}\Big((-1)^{k_{2}(0)}\big((k_{2}(0)+1)k_{1}(1)-(k_{1}(0)+1)k_{2}(1)\big)\Delta(\Lambda_{Q,0})Z^{\Lambda_{Q,1}}(t_{i}^{-})\Big)\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(-1)^{|J|-1}Z^{\Gamma}(t_{i}^{+})

by (
5.36
) and (
5.37
). This proves the desired result.
∎

5.2.
Open topological recursion relations and mirror symmetry: statement
of results

This subsection states the open topological recursion
relations and proves their immediate corollaries. This is expressed in terms of the quantities

{\mathcal{A}}(I,\mathbf{d},{\nu}^{\mathbf{s}})
, which by Theorem
5.4
is known to be independent of
\mathbf{s}
, so we will now refer to it as just
\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d})
. For ease of notation in stating the topological recursion relations, we take the index set of interior
markings
I
to coincide with
[\ell]
. For a descendent vector
\mathbf{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{\ell})
,
we write
{\bf e}_{1}=(1,0,\ldots,0)
, so that

\mathbf{d}+{\bf e}_{1}=(d_{1}+1,d_{2},\ldots,d_{\ell})
.

Theorem 5.10
.

Given a canonical family of multisections
{\bf s}
, the following
identities hold. If
|I|\geq 1
, then

(5.38)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle(I,\mathbf{d}+{{\mathbf{e}}}_{1})=

\displaystyle\quad\quad\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-2\}\\
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-2\}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\coprod B=\{2,\ldots,l\}\\
A\not=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\tau_{d_{1}}^{(a_{1},b_{1})}\prod_{i\in A}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\textup{ext}}\mathcal{A}(B\cup\{z_{a,b}\},\mathbf{d})-\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d}).

If
|I|\geq 2
, then

(5.39)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle(I,\mathbf{d}+{{\mathbf{e}}}_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-2\}\\
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-2\}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\coprod B=\{2,\ldots,l\},2\in B\\
A\not=\varnothing\end{subarray}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\tau_{d_{1}}^{(a_{1},b_{1})}\prod_{i\in A}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\textup{ext}}\mathcal{A}(B\cup\{z_{a,b}\},\mathbf{d}).

Here
z_{a,b}
is an additional internal marking with twist

(r-2-a,s-2-b)
and no descendent. We extend
\mathbf{d}
in a natural way so that
d_{z_{a,b}}=0
.

The next section will be devoted to the proof of this result. Now, we state and prove two key corollaries that establish open mirror symmetry for the Landau-Ginzburg mirror pair.

Corollary 5.11
.

(1)

If
I=\{i\}
is a singleton, then
\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d})=(-1)^{d_{i}}
.

(2)

If
|I|\geq 2
, then

(5.40)

\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-2\}\\
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-2\}\end{subarray}}\sum_{A\coprod B=I\setminus\{1,2\}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\prod_{i\in A\cup\{1,2\}}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\textup{ext}}\mathcal{A}\big(B\cup\{z_{a,b}\},\mathbf{d}\,\big).

(3)

Suppose
|I|\geq 2
.
Let
d(I,\mathbf{d})
be as defined in Definition
4.8
. Then

\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d})=\begin{cases}0&d(I,\mathbf{d})<0\\
\left\langle\tau_{d(I,\mathbf{d})}^{(r-r(I)-2,s-s(I)-2)}\prod_{i\in I}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\textup{ext}}&d(I,\mathbf{d})\geq 0.\end{cases}

Proof.

For (1), note that
if
I
is a singleton
\{i\}
, then we obtain

\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d})=(-1)^{d_{i}}\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{0})=(-1)^{d_{i}}\langle\tau_{0}^{(a_{1},b_{1})}\sigma_{1}^{a_{1}}\sigma_{2}^{b_{2}}\sigma_{12}\rangle^{o}=(-1)^{d_{i}}.

Here the first equality follows from repeated use of
(
5.38
) as the first summand on the right-hand side of
(
5.38
) vanishes. Then the second equality comes from
the definition of
\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{0})
and the third
equality is Theorem
5.1
.

For (2), we may equate the right-hand sides of (
5.38
)
and (
5.39
) and solve for
\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d})
to obtain

(5.41)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d})={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-2\},\\
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-2\}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\coprod B=\{2,\ldots,l\}\\
A\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\tau_{d_{1}}^{(a_{1},b_{1})}\prod_{i\in A}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\text{ext}}\mathcal{A}(B\cup\{z_{a,b}\},\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-2\},\\
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-2\}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\coprod B=\{2,\ldots,l\},2\in B\\
A\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\tau_{d_{1}}^{(a_{1},b_{1})}\prod_{i\in A}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\text{ext}}\mathcal{A}(B\cup\{z_{a,b}\},\mathbf{d})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-2\},\\
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-2\}\end{subarray}}\sum_{A\coprod B=\{2,\ldots,l\},2\in A}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\tau_{d_{1}}^{(a_{1},b_{1})}\prod_{i\in A}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\text{ext}}\mathcal{A}(B\cup\{z_{a,b}\},\mathbf{d}).

This gives (2).

Finally, we prove (3). Suppose
I
is an index set with
|I|\geq 2

and
1,2\in I
.
Assume by induction the result
for all
I^{\prime}
with
2\leq|I^{\prime}|<|I|
.
To prove (3), we aim to apply (
5.40
), but, to do so effectively, we must establish a relationship between
d(B\cup\{z_{a,b}\},\mathbf{d})
and
d(I,\mathbf{d})
. Let us consider those sets
B\subseteq I\setminus\{1,2\}
for which the contribution in the summation of
(
5.40
) is non-zero,
i.e.,

(5.42)

\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\prod_{i\in I\setminus B}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\text{ext}}\not=0.

By Observation
3.31
, (1), this requires

(5.43)

0=m((I\setminus B)\cup\{(a,b)\},\mathbf{d})+2r+2s=m(I\setminus B,\mathbf{d})+sa+rb-rs+2r+2s.

With
B^{\prime}:=B\cup\{z_{a,b}\}
where
z_{a,b}
is as in Theorem
5.10
, we will now show
that (
5.43
) implies

(5.44)

d(B^{\prime},\mathbf{d})=d(I,\mathbf{d})-1.

We note that

m(I\setminus B,\mathbf{d})=m(I,\mathbf{d})-m(B,\mathbf{d})+rs

and

m(B^{\prime},\mathbf{d})=m(B,\mathbf{d})+r(s-b-2)+s(r-a-2)-rs.

Putting this together, we see that (
5.43
) holds
if and only if

m(B^{\prime},\mathbf{d})=m(I,\mathbf{d})+rs.

Note further that (
5.42
) also implies from
(
2.8
) that

a+2+\sum_{i\in I\setminus B}a_{i}\equiv 0\mod r
, and thus

(5.45)

r(I)\equiv(a+2)+(r-a-2)+\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\equiv(r-a-2)+\sum_{i\in B}a_{i}\equiv r(B^{\prime})\mod r,

giving the equality
r(I)=r(B^{\prime})
. Similarly,
s(I)=s(B^{\prime})
.
Thus we conclude that

m(B^{\prime},\mathbf{d})-(sr(B^{\prime})+rs(B^{\prime}))=m(I,\mathbf{d})-(sr(I)+rs(I))+rs,

showing (
5.44
).

Thus, if
B
is non-empty, so that
|I|>|B^{\prime}|\geq 2
,
then by the induction hypothesis, (
5.44
), and (
5.45
),

{\mathcal{A}}(B^{\prime},\mathbf{d})=\left\langle\tau_{d(I,\mathbf{d})-1}^{(r-r(I)-2,s-s(I)-2)}\tau_{0}^{(r-2-a,s-2-b)}\prod_{i\in B}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\text{ext}},

where for convenience we interpret this as
0
if
d(I,\mathbf{d})-1<0
and
are using (
5.44
) to write this formula
using
d(I,\mathbf{d})
instead of
d(B^{\prime},\mathbf{d})
.

We first consider the case that
d(I,\mathbf{d})<0
, so that
d(B^{\prime},\mathbf{d})<-1
if
(
5.42
) holds.
Then by the induction hypothesis, the only terms in
(
5.40
) which are not obviously zero arise with
B=\varnothing
,
but we claim that in this case, the invariant in
(
5.42
) vanishes. Indeed, in this case

m(B^{\prime},\mathbf{d})=s(r-2-a)+r(s-2-b)=sr(B^{\prime})+rs(B^{\prime})
, hence
d(B^{\prime},\mathbf{d})=-1
, contradicting

d(B^{\prime},\mathbf{d})<-1
.
Thus all terms in (
5.40
) vanish, showing the desired
inductive vanishing of
{\mathcal{A}}(I,\mathbf{d})
in this case.

If, on the other hand,
d(I,\mathbf{d})=0
, then again by induction all terms
in the double summation of (
5.40
) vanish except
when
B=\varnothing
. Moreover, a term in the first summation can only be non-zero if

a=r-r(I)-2,b=s-s(I)-2

by Observation
2.25
. Thus, after using
\mathcal{A}(\{z_{a,b}\},\mathbf{d})=1
by
(1), we obtain

\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d})=\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(r-r(I)-2,s-s(I)-2)}\prod_{i\in I}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\text{ext}}

as desired.

Finally, if
d(I,\mathbf{d})>0
,
we write (
5.40
) by splitting the sum according
to whether
B
is empty or not, use item (1), and the induction hypothesis
to get:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}(I,\mathbf{d})={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-2\},\\
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-2\}\end{subarray}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\prod_{i\in I}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\text{ext}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-2\},\\
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-2\}\end{subarray}}\sum_{A\coprod B=I\setminus\{1,2\},B\not=\varnothing}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\prod_{i\in A\cup\{1,2\}}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\text{ext}}\cdot

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\cdot\left\langle\tau_{d(I,\mathbf{d})-1}^{(r-r(I)-2,s-s(I)-2)}\tau_{0}^{(r-2-a,s-2-b)}\prod_{i\in B}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\text{ext}}.

By the closed extended topological recursion and Ramond vanishing of
Observation
3.31
(2),(3), this now agrees with

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-2\},\\
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-2\}\end{subarray}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\prod_{i\in I}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\text{ext}}+\left\langle\tau_{d(I,\mathbf{d})}^{(r-r(I)-2,s-s(I)-2)}\prod_{i\in I}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\text{ext}}.

However, a term in the first summation can only be non-zero if

a=r-r(I)-2,b=s-s(I)-2
by Observation
2.25
. Using this prescribed twist and the definition of
d(I,\mathbf{d})
, one can compute that
m(I\cup\{z\},\mathbf{d})+2r+2s=-rsd(I,\mathbf{d})<0
where
z
is an internal marking with twist
(a,b)
. Thus, by Observation
3.31
(1), all the terms in the first summation vanish, giving the result.
∎

Corollary 5.12

(Open Mirror Symmetry for 2-dimensional Fermat polynomials)
.

If
\mathbf{s}
is a canonical symmetric family of multisections bounded by a finite index set
I
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\Xi_{a,b}}e^{W^{{\nu},\mathrm{sym}}/\hbar}dx\wedge dy={}\delta_{a,0}\delta_{b,0}+\sum_{d\geq 0}t_{a,b,d}\hbar^{d-1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{l\geq 2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\in\mathcal{A}_{l}\\
d(A)\geq 0\end{subarray}}(-1)^{l}(-\hbar)^{-d(A)-2}{\delta_{r(A),a}\delta_{s(A),b}\over|\textup{Aut}(A)|}\left\langle\tau_{d(A)}^{(r-r(A)-2,s-s(A)-2)}\prod_{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i})\in A}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})}\right\rangle^{\textup{ext}}\prod_{(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},d_{i})\in A}t_{\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},d_{j}}.

as an equality of Laurent series in the variable
\hbar
with coefficients in
A_{I,\textrm{sym}}
.

In particular, working modulo
\langle t_{a,b,d}\,|\,0\leq a\leq r-2,0\leq b\leq s-2,d>0\rangle
,
dx\wedge dy
is a primitive form
and the
t_{a,b,0}
are flat coordinates.

Proof.

The formula follows from Corollary
4.16
.
Here, for the summation over
l=1
and
l\geq 2
, we use Corollary

5.11
,(1) and (3) respectively
for the value of
\mathcal{A}(A,\mathbf{d})
. Note that for
l=1
,
if
A=\{(a_{1},b_{1},d_{1})\}
, then
r(A)=a_{1},s(A)=b_{1}
, and thus

d(A)=-d_{1}-1
. The final statement is then just Corollary
4.17
.
∎

5.3.
The action of the wall-crossing group on open FJRW invariants

In this subsection we will prove the following result:

Theorem 5.13
.

We have the containment
\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)\subseteq\Omega_{r,s}(I)
. That is, every chamber index bounded by
I
can be realized as the open FJRW invariants for some canonical family of multisections bounded by
I
.

In particular, we now show that Theorem
0.7
will follow from Theorem
5.13
.

Corollary 5.14
.

We have

\Omega_{r,s}(I)=\Upsilon_{r,s}(I).

Moreover, for a finite set
I\subseteq\Omega
,

{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)=\Upsilon_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}(I).

In particular, when
I
is finite, the group
G^{r,s}_{A_{I}}
(resp.
G^{r,s}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
) acts faithfully and transitively
on
\Omega_{r,s}(I)
(resp.
{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
).

Proof.

Note that the equalities
\Omega_{r,s}(I)=\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)
and
{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)=\Upsilon_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}(I)
imply the rest of the statement in the corollary. This is because Theorem
4.27
shows that
G_{A_{I}}^{r,s}
(resp.
G^{r,s}_{A_{I,\mathrm{sym}}}
) acts faithfully and transitively on

\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)
(resp.
\Upsilon_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}(I)
) via the formula

(5.46)

g(W^{\nu})=W^{g(\nu)}.

We remark the formula for the action given here is in line with the relation between open FJRW invariants associated to two canonical families of multisections bounded by
I
given in Theorem
5.9
.

Moreover, note that Corollary
5.11
implies the containments
\Omega_{r,s}(I)\subseteq\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)
and

{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)\subseteq\Upsilon_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}(I)
. Thus by using Theorem
5.13
, we have the equality
\Omega_{r,s}(I)=\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)
.
Lastly, note that, by Proposition
3.46
, the containment
\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)\subseteq\Omega_{r,s}(I)
and equality
\Upsilon_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}(I)=\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)\cap{\mathbb{Q}\textendash\mathrm{Bal}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
imply that
\Upsilon_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}(I)\subseteq{\Omega_{r,s}^{\mathrm{sym}}}(I)
.
∎

We need a couple of lemmas before we start to prove Theorem
5.13
. The first ingredient in the proof is the following lemma:

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
\Gamma
be a smooth connected open graded
W
-spin graph.
Let
\zeta
be a multisection of
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
defined over
\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
.
Suppose that, for any
\Lambda\in\partial\Gamma\setminus\partial^{+}\Gamma
, we can write

\zeta|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}=F_{\Lambda}^{*}\zeta^{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda},

where
\zeta^{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}\in C_{m}^{\infty}({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda},E_{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}(\mathbf{d}))
is a transverse multisection that is strongly positive with respect to
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
, and
\textup{Aut}({\mathcal{B}}\Gamma)
-invariant.
Then one can extend
\zeta
to a transverse, strongly positive,
\textup{Aut}(\Gamma)
-invariant
multisection of
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
.

Proof.

The lemma is analogous to Lemma 6.5 in
[
6
]
.
The proof is the same.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

Note that Lemma
5.15
also holds when
\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
is the empty set. In this case, the lemma is restating the existence of a transverse, strongly positive,
\text{Aut}(\Gamma)
-invariant multisection of
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
.

The second lemma constructs a homotopy between multisections of orbifold bundles that will have a desired number of zeros.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
E\to M
be an oriented orbifold bundle over an oriented orbifold
M
with corners, such that the generic isotropy of
E\to M
is trivial and
\operatorname{rank}_{\mathbb{R}}E=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}M+1=n
.
Let
s\in C_{m}^{\infty}(M,E)
be a nowhere vanishing multisection and
c\in\mathbb{Q}
. Then one can construct a multisection
s^{\prime}
which agrees with
s
away from a compact set
K\subset\textup{Interior}(M)
and a transverse homotopy
H:[0,1]\times M\to E
, so that

H(0,-)=s,H(1,-)=s^{\prime},~H(t,p)=s(p)\text{ for all }p\in M\setminus K

and
\#Z(H)=c
.

Proof.

Write
c=a/b
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and
b\in\mathbb{N}
.
Choose both a point
x\in M
with trivial isotropy and an open neighborhood
U
of

x
such that there is a diffeomorphism
\phi:U\rightarrow B
with
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
being the unit ball.
We can write the multisection
s|_{U}=\biguplus_{i=1}^{m}n_{i}s_{i},
where
s_{i}\in C^{\infty}(U,E)
and
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
.
Now we can rewrite
s
in the neighborhood
U
as

s=\biguplus_{i=1}^{b\sum_{i=1}^{m}n_{i}}\tilde{s}_{j},~~\tilde{s}_{j}=s_{i}~\text{ when }b\sum_{q=1}^{i-1}n_{q}<j\leq b\sum_{q=1}^{i}n_{q}.

We may choose a trivialization
(E|_{U}\to U)\simeq(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times U\to U)
so that
\tilde{s}_{1}|_{U}
is mapped to the constant section
e_{1}=(1,0,\ldots,0)
. From now on, we use this trivialization to identify the fibers of
E|_{U}\to U
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Denote by
S^{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
the unit sphere. Take
g:S^{n-1}\to S^{n-1}
to be a degree
a(\sum_{i=1}^{m}n_{i})
map that is chosen so that the lower hemisphere is mapped to
e_{1}
. Let
\pi:S^{n-1}\to\bar{B}
be the projection to the first
n-1
coordinates and
\pi^{\prime}:B\to S^{n-1}
be its inverse projection to the upper hemisphere, i.e.,
\pi\circ\pi^{\prime}=id
and
\text{Image}(\pi^{\prime})
has positive last coordinate.
We define
s^{\prime}
so that

s^{\prime}|_{(M\setminus U)}=s|_{(M\setminus U)},\text{ and }s^{\prime}|_{U}=\biguplus_{i=1}^{b\sum_{i=1}^{m}n_{i}}\tilde{s}^{\prime}_{j},

where

\tilde{s}^{\prime}_{j}(y)=\begin{cases}g(\pi^{\prime}(\phi(y))),&\text{ if $j=1$},\\
\tilde{s}_{j},&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

The multisection
s^{\prime}
glues to give a multisection. Finally, there must exist a transverse homotopy
H
from
s^{\prime}
to
s
that is constant for all except the first branch, that is constant on the first branch outside of
U
and satisfies
\#Z(H)=\frac{a\sum_{i=1}^{m}n_{i}}{b\sum_{i=1}^{m}n_{i}}=c
.
19
19

19

Here,
\#Z(H)
is computed using the discussion above Notation A.4 in
[
6
]
.

∎

Proof of Theorem
5.13
.

Fix a chamber index
\nu:=({\nu}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}})\in\Upsilon_{r,s}(I)
. We will construct a family of canonical multisections
\mathbf{s}
of the bundles
E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
bounded by
I
so that

(5.47)

\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{12}^{k_{12}(\Gamma)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s},o}=\nu_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}

for all graphs
\Gamma
contained in some
{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})
for some
J\subseteq I
and
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
. We do so via induction on the number
l=|J|
of internal markings.

First, when
l=0
, consider any family of canonical multisections
\mathbf{s}
of
\mathcal{W}
bounded by
I
. Such a family exists by Theorem
3.29
. Then by Theorem
5.1
, the condition in (
5.47
) holds.

Let
{(r,s)}\text{-Graph}(l)
be the set of all graded open
{(r,s)}
-graphs
\Gamma
where
I(\Gamma)\subset I
and
|I(\Gamma)|\leq l
. For the induction step, assume that we have constructed a family of canonical multisections
(\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma})_{\Gamma\in{(r,s)}\text{-Graph}(l)}
so that

•

If
\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(I(\Gamma),\mathbf{d})
for some
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{I(\Gamma)}

and
|I(\Gamma)|<l
, then (
5.47
) holds.

•

If
\Gamma\in{(r,s)}\text{-Graph}(l)
then

\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}=F_{\Gamma}^{*}(\boxplus_{\Xi\in{{\textup{Conn}}}({\mathcal{B}}\Gamma)}\mathbf{s}^{\Xi}).

Let
J\subseteq I
be a set of size
l
. We will construct a canonical family of multisections
\mathbf{s}
bounded by
J
using induction on a new number

n(\Gamma):=sk_{1}(\Gamma)+rk_{2}(\Gamma)+(r+s)k_{12}(\Gamma),

a weighted count of boundary markings on the graph
\Gamma
. Note
n(\Gamma)\geq 0
, so we may vacuously take the base case of this induction to be

n(\Gamma)=-1
. Inside this induction step, i.e., once we fix the number
n(\Gamma)
, we now perform downward induction on the number
k_{12}(\Gamma)
. Here, we may take the base case of the induction to be
k_{12}(\Gamma)=\lfloor\tfrac{n(\Gamma)}{(r+s)}\rfloor+1
, which will be vacuous as well.

Next, suppose we have constructed the canonical multisection
\mathbf{s}
for any graded graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
that satisfies any of the following:

(i)

I(\Gamma^{\prime})\subsetneq I(\Gamma)
,

(ii)

I(\Gamma^{\prime})=I(\Gamma)
, and
n(\Gamma^{\prime})<n(\Gamma)
, or

(iii)

I(\Gamma^{\prime})=I(\Gamma)
,
n(\Gamma^{\prime})=n(\Gamma)
, and
k_{12}(\Gamma^{\prime})>k_{12}(\Gamma)
.

We now construct a canonical multisection
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
that will be in our canonical family of multisections bounded by
J
. We do this via a case-by-case analysis in order to respect the canonical property.

If
\Gamma
is not connected, then each connected component
\Xi
of
\Gamma

satisfies either
I(\Xi)\subsetneq I(\Gamma)
or
n(\Xi)<n(\Gamma)
, hence the multisection
\mathbf{s}^{\Xi}
is already defined and we write
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}=\boxplus_{\Xi\in{{\textup{Conn}}}(\Gamma)}\mathbf{s}^{\Xi}
.

Analogously, if
\Gamma\neq{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma
and
\Gamma
does not have an exchangeable vertex, then any connected component
\Xi
of
{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma
has either the property (i) or (ii) in the inductive hypotheses above. Thus, the multisection
\mathbf{s}^{\Xi}
is already constructed, so we construct
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
as

(5.48)

\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}=F^{*}_{\Gamma}(\boxplus_{\Xi\in{{\textup{Conn}}}({\mathcal{B}}\Gamma)}\mathbf{s}^{\Xi}).

Lastly, if
\Gamma
has an exchangeable vertex
v
with incident edge
e
, then
\textup{detach}_{e}\Gamma
has two connected components, the exchangeable vertex and the rest of the graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Further
\Gamma^{\prime}
has the property (iii) above, so we can construct
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
as in (
5.48
) by induction.

Otherwise, we define the multisection
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
as follows. We start by constructing the multisection at the boundary. First note that, for any
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
and
\Gamma={\text{sm}}_{E}\Lambda
, we have the relation

(5.49)

n(\Gamma)=\sum_{v\in\mathrm{Vert}(\Lambda)}n(v)-(r+s)|E|.

Thus, if
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{Exchange}}\Gamma
then
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
consists of connected components
\Xi_{1},\ldots,\Xi_{f}
such that, for each
i
, one of the following holds:

(i)

I(\Xi_{i})\subsetneq J
,

(ii)

I(\Xi_{i})=J
and
n(\Xi_{i})<n(\Gamma)
, or

(iii)

I(\Xi_{i})=J
,
n(\Xi_{i})=n(\Gamma)
and
k_{12}(\Xi_{i})>k_{12}(\Gamma)
.

Thus we can inductively define the transverse multisections
\mathbf{s}^{\Lambda}
for all
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{Exchange}}\Gamma
via the formula (
5.48
). We then can use Lemma
5.15
to extend the multisection over the entire moduli space. By Proposition
3.35
, we have that the intersection number

\left\langle\prod_{i\in I(\Gamma)}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{12}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s},o}

is independent of the choice of the extension
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
from the boundary.

Consider the corresponding chamber index
\nu=(\nu_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}})
given above and the chamber index
\nu^{\mathbf{s}}=(\nu^{\mathbf{s}}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}})
corresponding to the family of multisections
\mathbf{s}
(bounded by
J
) as defined in (
3.20
). For any
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
and
\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})
,
we now write

\Delta_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{s}):=\nu^{\mathbf{s}}_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}-\nu_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}.

For any
\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(\subseteq J,\mathbf{d})
where
|I(\Gamma)|<l
, we have by induction that
\Delta_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{s})=0
. The goal now is to construct a new family of multisections
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
bounded by
J
so that
\Delta_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})
vanishes for all
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
and
\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})
.

Consider the quantities
\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},\nu^{\mathbf{s}})
and
\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},\nu)
defined in Notation
3.42
.
Using that
\nu
is a chamber index by assumption
and that
\nu^{\mathbf{s}}
is a chamber index by Corollary
5.11
(3),
we have from Corollary
4.28
that

(5.50)

0=\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},\nu^{\mathbf{s}})-\mathcal{A}(J,\mathbf{d},\nu)=\sum_{\Gamma\in{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})}\frac{\Gamma(\tfrac{1+k_{1}(\Gamma)}{r})\Gamma(\tfrac{1+k_{2}(\Gamma)}{s})}{\Gamma(\tfrac{1+r(J)}{r})\Gamma(\tfrac{1+s(J)}{s})}\Delta_{\Gamma,\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{s}).

Recall the notation for all balanced and critical graphs given in Notation
2.40
of balanced graphs and critical graphs with internal markings
J
. If
N=0
, then
{\text{Balanced}}(J,\mathbf{d})=\{\Gamma_{0}\}
and the equality
\Delta_{\Gamma_{J,0}}=0
follows directly from (
5.50
). If
N>0
then we can classify the type of top critical boundary graphs corresponding to each
\Gamma_{J,i}
. Recall from Definition
2.36
that a top boundary graph will be a critical boundary graph with two vertices
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
with a boundary edge
e
so that

•

the vertex
v_{1}
is rooted, balanced and has no internal markings; and

•

the vertex
v_{0}
is of the form
\Lambda_{J,p}\in{\text{Crit}}(J,\mathbf{d})
from Notation
2.40
.

Given a balanced graph
\Gamma_{J,i}
, there are only two possible types of top critical boundaries: (i) when
v_{1}
is
\Gamma^{W}_{0,r,0,1,\emptyset}
and
v_{0}
is
\Lambda_{J,i}
, and (ii) when
v_{1}
is
\Gamma_{0,0,s,1,\emptyset}^{W}
and
v_{0}
is
\Lambda_{J,i+1}
. For
\Gamma_{J,0}
, there are not enough
r
-points to have type (i) and, for
\Gamma_{J,N}
, there are not enough
s
-points to have type (ii). Otherwise, for
1\leq i\leq N-1
, the graph
\Gamma_{J,i+1}
has precisely two types of top critical boundaries,
such that both have two vertices and one boundary edge and one of its half-edges is a
r
-point and one is a
s
-point. As a quick remark, we note that type (i) (respectively type (ii)) corresponds to critical boundary graphs with
\epsilon=1
(respectively
\epsilon=2
).

Suppose we change the multisection
\mathbf{s}^{\Lambda_{J,i}}
to another multisection
(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{\Lambda_{J,i}}
, which agrees with
\mathbf{s}^{\Lambda_{J,i}}
near
\partial{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Lambda_{J,i}}
.
Given a transverse homotopy
H\in C_{m}^{\infty}\big([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{J,i}},\pi^{*}E_{\Lambda_{J,i}}(\mathbf{d})\big)
which is constant in time near
[0,1]\times\partial{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Lambda_{J,i}}

we define

h_{i,\mathbf{d}}:=h_{i,\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{s}^{\Lambda_{J,i}},(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{\Lambda_{J,i}}):=\int_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{J,i}}}e(\pi^{*}E_{\Lambda_{J,i}}(\mathbf{d});H|_{V}),

where
V
can be taken to be the union of
\{0,1\}\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{J,i}}
with the locus where
H
is constant in time.

We claim that the quantity
h_{i,\mathbf{d}}
depends only on the multisections
\mathbf{s}^{\Lambda_{J,i}}
and
(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{\Lambda_{J,i}}
, but not on the homotopy chosen between them. Choose another homotopy
H^{\prime}\in C_{m}^{\infty}([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{J,i}},\pi^{*}E_{\Lambda_{J,i}}(\mathbf{d}))

which is the constant in time near
[0,1]\times\partial{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Lambda_{J,i}}
. While
H
and
H^{\prime}
will have different neighborhoods
V
and
V^{\prime}
of
[0,1]\times\partial{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Lambda_{J,i}}
where they are constant in time, we will have that
H|_{V\cap V^{\prime}}=H^{\prime}|_{V\cap V^{\prime}}
. Thus, by an argument analogous to that in the proof of Proposition
3.35
we have

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{J,i}}}e(\pi^{*}E_{\Lambda_{J,i}}(\mathbf{d});H|_{V})={}

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{J,i}}}e(\pi^{*}E_{\Lambda_{J,i}}(\mathbf{d});H|_{V\cap V^{\prime}})

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{J,i}}}e(\pi^{*}E_{\Lambda_{J,i}}(\mathbf{d});H^{\prime}|_{V^{\prime}}).

We now construct this new family of canonical multisections
\mathbf{s}^{\prime}
bounded by
J
by taking
\mathbf{s}
, but then replacing the multisection
\mathbf{s}^{\Lambda_{J,i}}
for the graphs
\Lambda_{J,i}
with
\mathbf{s^{\prime}}^{\Lambda_{J,i}}
. This will require us to change the canonical multisections
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
for all
\Gamma
such that there exists a boundary strata
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
for which
\Lambda_{J,i}
is a connected component of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
. We now see which balanced graphs have this property up to the induction step where we construct the multisection for the graph
\Gamma_{J,i}
.

We can use (
5.49
) to see that for

n(\Lambda_{J,i})=n(\Gamma_{J,i})-n(\Gamma^{W}_{0,r,0,1,\emptyset})+(r+s)=n(\Gamma_{J,i})-rs.

Since
n(\Lambda_{J,i})<n(\Gamma_{J,i})
, we are modifying the
sections
\mathbf{s}^{\Lambda_{J,i}}
which was already determined at a previous
step of the induction. Take a graded graph
\Gamma
whose multisection has already been constructed and so that there exists
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
with
\Lambda_{J,i}
a connected component of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
. Thus
I(\Gamma)\subseteq J
but if
\Lambda_{J,i}
is a vertex in the graph
\Gamma
then
I(\Gamma)=J
.

Since
I(\Gamma)=J
, all other vertices of
\Lambda
aside from
\Lambda_{J,i}
must have no internal markings. Moreover, since
\Gamma
is graded, all other vertices must be stable and have no untwisted boundary tails. For any vertex
v
in
\Lambda
with no internal markings, we must have by combining (
2.22
) and (
2.23
) as in the proof of Proposition
2.31
(a) that

(5.51)

r\ |\ k_{1}(v)+k_{12}(v)-1\text{ and }s\ |\ k_{2}(v)+k_{12}(v)-1.

Since we are at the induction step for the graph
\Gamma_{J,i}
, we know that
n(\Gamma)\leq n(\Gamma_{J,i})
. We now can start to order all possible vertices with no internal markings in increasing order with respect to the weighted number
n(v)
. The first two such vertices
v
that satisfy (
5.51
) have
n(v)=r+s
and have the following number of boundary half-edges

•

k_{1}(v)=k_{2}(v)=0
and
k_{12}(v)=1
;

•

k_{1}(v)=k_{2}(v)=1
and
k_{12}(v)=0
.

To guarantee stability, any vertex
v
with this number of boundary half-edges with non-trivial twists will require at least one additional boundary half-edge adjacent to
v
that is untwisted. Suppose
\Lambda
has one of these vertices.
Then
\Lambda
must have additional vertices with no internal markings and a fully twisted half-edge on which we glue the untwisted boundary half-edge. If such an additional vertex
v^{\prime}
has
n(v^{\prime})=r+s
, then we can repeat this argument. Thus, eventually, we must find a vertex
v
with no internal markings and
n(v)>r+s
. After these two cases, we have the graphs
\Gamma^{W}_{0,r,0,1,\emptyset}
and
\Gamma^{W}_{0,0,s,1,\emptyset}
in the ordering with respect to
n(v)
, where
n(\Gamma^{W}_{0,r,0,1,\emptyset})=n(\Gamma^{W}_{0,0,s,1,\emptyset})=rs+(r+s)
. Since
n(\Gamma)\leq n(\Gamma_{J,i})
this means that
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
must consist of
\Lambda_{J,i}
, one of the graphs
\Gamma^{W}_{0,r,0,1,\emptyset}
and
\Gamma^{W}_{0,0,s,1,\emptyset}
, and possibly some vertices
v
with
n(v)=r+s
by (
5.49
). However, if we have any vertices
v
with
n(v)=r+s
, we will then have
n(\Gamma)=n(\Gamma_{J,i})
with
k_{12}(\Gamma)<k_{12}(\Gamma_{J,i})
. Otherwise,
\Gamma
coincides with
\Gamma_{J,i-1}
or
\Gamma_{J,i}
.

Thus we have proved the following: if
\Gamma
is any graph for which
there exists
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma

with
\Lambda_{J,i}
a connected component of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
, then
either (i)
J\subsetneq I(\Gamma)
; (ii)
n(\Gamma)>n(\Gamma_{J,i})
;
(iii)
n(\Gamma)=n(\Gamma_{J,i})
but
k_{12}(\Gamma)<k_{12}(\Gamma_{J,i})
;
or (iv)

\Gamma=\Gamma_{J,i-1}
or
\Gamma=\Gamma_{J,i}
. Therefore, changing
\mathbf{s}^{\Lambda_{J,i}}

will have no further effect on the inductive construction.

Replace all
\mathbf{s}^{\Lambda_{J,i}}
with
(\mathbf{s^{\prime}})^{\Lambda_{J,i}}
, and then apply Lemma
5.15
to define
(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{\Gamma}
at this induction step for those
\Gamma
with boundary graphs
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma\cup\partial^{\textup{exchange}}\Gamma
so that
\Lambda_{J,i}
is a connected component of
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
. Here, the intersection numbers corresponding to the balanced graphs
\Gamma_{J,i}
will change. Define

o^{\prime}_{J,p,\mathbf{d}}:=\left\langle\prod_{i\in J}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}(\Gamma_{J,p})}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}(\Gamma_{J,p})}\sigma_{12}\right\rangle^{(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{\Gamma_{J,p}},o}.

Using Lemma
2.22
along with (
5.22
), (
5.24
), and (
5.25
), we compute the new invariants
o^{\prime}_{J,p,\mathbf{d}}
to be as follows:

•

For
p=0
, it will change to

(5.52)

o^{\prime}_{J,0,\mathbf{d}}=\nu_{\Gamma_{J,0},\mathbf{d}}+\Delta_{\Gamma_{J,0},\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{s})+(-1)^{k_{2}(\Lambda_{J,1})}sk_{1}(\Lambda_{J,1})h_{1,\mathbf{d}};

•

For
0<p<N
, it will change to

(5.53)

o^{\prime}_{J,p,\mathbf{d}}=\nu_{\Gamma_{J,p},\mathbf{d}}+\Delta_{\Gamma_{J,p},\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{s})+(-1)^{k_{2}(\Lambda_{J,p})+1}rk_{2}(\Lambda_{J,p})h_{p,\mathbf{d}}+(-1)^{k_{2}(\Lambda_{J,p+1})}sk_{1}(\Lambda_{J,p+1})h_{p+1,\mathbf{d}};\text{ and }

•

For
p=N
, it will change to

(5.54)

o^{\prime}_{J,N,\mathbf{d}}=\nu_{\Gamma_{J,N},\mathbf{d}}+\Delta_{\Gamma_{J,N},\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{s})+(-1)^{k_{2}(\Lambda_{J,N})+1}rk_{2}(\Lambda_{J,N})h_{N,\mathbf{d}}.

By Lemma
5.17
, there exists a multisection
(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{\Lambda_{J,1}}
where
c
in the lemma is taken to be
h_{1,\mathbf{d}}=(-1)^{k_{2}(\Lambda_{J,1})+1}\frac{\Delta_{\Gamma_{J,0},\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{s})}{sk_{1}(\Lambda_{J,1})}
. Then we will have that
o^{\prime}_{J,0,\mathbf{d}}=\nu_{\Gamma_{J,0},\mathbf{d}}
.

We inductively choose
h_{p,\mathbf{d}}
so that we obtain
o_{J,p,\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}=\nu_{\Gamma_{J,p},\mathbf{d}}
for all
0\leq p<N
. This is done by assuming
given
h_{1,\mathbf{d}},\ldots,h_{p,\mathbf{d}}
so that
o_{J,p-1,\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}=\nu_{\Gamma_{J,p-1},\mathbf{d}}
. Then take

h_{p+1,\mathbf{d}}=\frac{-\Delta_{\Gamma_{J,p},\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{s})+(-1)^{k_{2}(\Lambda_{J,p})}rk_{2}(\Lambda_{J,p})h_{p,\mathbf{d}}}{(-1)^{k_{2}(\Lambda_{J,p+1})}sk_{1}(\Lambda_{J,p+1})}.

Then the expression for
o_{J,p,\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}
in Equation (
5.53
) will simplify to
o_{J,p,\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}=\nu_{\Gamma_{J,p},\mathbf{d}}
. Again, the multisection
(\mathbf{s}^{\prime})^{\Lambda_{J,p}}
is guaranteed to exist by Lemma
5.17
.
Note that this does not change the boundary behavior for these multisections and that these critical boundaries all have internal markings
J
, so they do not affect any previously specified open intersection numbers in the inductive step.

Now we have that
\Delta_{\Gamma_{J,p},\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{s})=0
for
p=0,\ldots,N-1
. Combining this with Equation (
5.50
), we obtain that
\Delta_{\Gamma_{J,N},\mathbf{d}}(\mathbf{s})=0
. We perform the above modifications
in this way for all graphs
\Gamma
with internal markings
J
and descendent vectors
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{J}
such that
\Gamma
is balanced with respect
to
\mathbf{d}
. It is reassuring to note that the top boundary critical graphs with different descendent vectors will all have independent inductive structures at top boundary strata, so the steps above will not rely on each other. Continue to do the above for all
J\subseteq I
with
|J|=l
to complete the induction step. Thus we have found that
\nu^{\mathbf{s}}=\nu
, hence
\nu\in\Omega_{r,s}(I)
.
∎

6.
Proof of the open Topological Recursion Relations

We now outline the proof of Theorem
5.10
.
By Theorem
5.4
,
\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d})
is independent of the canonical family used to define it. We prove Theorem
5.10
by calculating
\mathcal{A}(I,\mathbf{d}+{\bf e}_{1})
in two ways, with respect to a family
of transverse special canonical multisections.

For convenience in this subsection, we assume that
\Gamma
is a rooted smooth graded
W
-spin graph
\Gamma
with
I(\Gamma)=[l]
, with
l>0
. Let

(6.1)

E=E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d}+{\bf e}_{1})\text{ and }E_{1}=E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})

be the descendent Witten bundles with respect to descendent vectors
\mathbf{d}+{\bf e}_{1}
and
\mathbf{d}
respectively. Suppose that
\Gamma
is balanced with respect to the descendent vector
\mathbf{d}+{\bf e}_{1}
.
Consider the following two transverse special canonical multisections for
E
and
E_{1}
respectively:

(6.2)

\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}=s^{\Gamma}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in\left[l\right],~j\in\left[d_{i}+\delta_{i=1}\right]}s_{ij}^{\Gamma}

and

(6.3)

\mathbf{s}_{1}=\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\Gamma}=s^{\Gamma}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[l],~j\in[d_{i}]\end{subarray}}s_{ij}^{\Gamma},

where
s^{\Gamma}
is the
\mathcal{W}
-component of
\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}
and
s_{ij}^{\Gamma}
is the projection to the
j^{th}

{\mathbb{L}}_{i}
summand.

Since
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{s}_{1}
are canonical, they are strongly positive, so there exists a set
U_{+}=U_{+}^{\Gamma}
as in (
3.4
) on which
s^{\Gamma}
does not vanish.
Our goal is to compute
\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}e(E;\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}|_{U_{+}\cap\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})
in terms of
\mathbf{s}_{1}
; however, doing this directly is difficult. Instead, given a marked point
w
other than the first internal marked
point
z_{1}
, we will look at a specific
transverse multisection,
\mathbf{s}_{1}\oplus t_{w}
, with a distinguished multisection
t_{w}\in C^{\infty}({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},{\mathbb{L}}_{1})
that we will define below. This multisection, however, will not be canonical so we must take a homotopy to a canonical multisection.

A priori, we would like to find the invariant

\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}e(E;\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma}|_{U_{+}\cap\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})

by computing
\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}e(E;\mathbf{s}_{1}^{\Gamma}\oplus t_{w}|_{U_{+}\cap\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})
, and then finding the difference of the two integrals as the number of zeros introduced by the homotopy between the two sections.
This naive approach fails, as the integrals become unwieldy.
However, it turns out that we can control the contributions of certain linear combinations of these integrals. A key technique introduced here is that we can use two cleverly chosen special linear combinations of multisections that are multisums corresponding to distinguished multisections of
E
to prove the recurrence relations of Theorem
5.10
.

6.1.
The distinguished multisections
t_{w}
of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}

Notation 6.1
.

Consider a graded graph
\Gamma
with at least two tails, one of which is internal. We can define the distinguished multisection
t=t_{w}\in C^{\infty}({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},{\mathbb{L}}_{1})
mentioned above as follows. Consider a smooth graded
W
-spin disk
\Sigma
with an internal marking
z_{1}
and additional marking
w
. After identifying
\Sigma
with the upper half-plane, we set

(6.4)

\tilde{t}\left(\Sigma\right)=dz\left.\left(\frac{1}{z-w}-\frac{1}{z-\bar{z}_{1}}\right)\right|_{z=z_{1}}\in T_{z_{1}}^{*}\Sigma.

This provides a section of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}
over
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Gamma}
. Furthermore, this
extends to a section of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}
over
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
via exactly
the same argument as given in
[
42
]
, §4.3, which we briefly
review. If
\Sigma
is singular, let
C
be the underlying complex
curve. Then there is a unique meromorphic differential
\omega

on the normalization
of
C
with the following properties.

First, it has simple poles with residue

+1
and
-1
respectively at
w
and
\bar{z}_{1}
. Second, consider a path in
C
from
w
to
\bar{z}_{1}
that passes through the minimal number of irreducible components and nodes. Since
C
has genus 0, there is a unique choice of nodes and irreducible components for the path to traverse. For any node that the path traverses through,
the two preimages have at most simple poles, and the residues at these poles
sum to zero. If we normalise at such a node, then one node will be in the connected component associated to
w
and the other in the connected component containing
\bar{z}_{1}
. The residue at the half-node sharing the connected component with
w
is
-1
and the residue for the half-node sharing the connected component with
\bar{z}_{1}
is
+1
. For all other irreducible components where the path does not pass through,
\omega
is holomorphic and hence vanishes. One then defines

\tilde{t}(\Sigma)
to be the value of
\omega
at
z_{1}
.

We call
t_{w}
the section (of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}
)
pointing at
w
.

Remark 6.2
.

One special case of a section
t_{w}
occurs when the graded
W
-spin disk

\Sigma
contains a boundary node
n
and the normalization
of
\Sigma
at this node
n
is
\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\Sigma_{2}
with

z_{1}\in\Sigma_{1}
and
w\in\Sigma_{2}
. Write
n_{i}\in\Sigma_{i}
for the half-nodes
corresponding to
n
. Then it is easy to see from
the description of Notation
6.1
in the singular case that

t_{w}
coincides with
t_{n_{1}}
, the section of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}
pointing at

n_{1}
.

Notation 6.3
.

Let
\Gamma
be a smooth graded
W
-spin graph with at least two tails, one of which is internal. Let
I_{1}
be the set of internal tails in the connected component containing the internal tail
1
.

Given the data of a subset
A\subseteq I_{1}\setminus\{1\}
and two numbers
a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-2\}
and
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-2\}
, we denote by
\Gamma^{A,a,b}
the graded graph obtained from
\Gamma
by replacing the vertex containing internal tail
1
with another graph with one open vertex
v_{o}
and one closed vertex
v_{c}
so that:

•

the vertices
v_{o}
and
v_{c}
are attached with one edge. Here, the half-edge belonging to
v_{c}
has twist
(a,b)
and the half-edge belonging to
v_{o}
has twist
(r-2-a,s-2-b)
;

•

the closed vertex
v_{c}
has internal tails labeled by
\{1\}\cup A
with the same twists as the original tails on
\Gamma
;

•

the open vertex
v_{o}
has internal tails labeled by
I_{1}\setminus(A\cup\{1\})
with the same twists as the original tails on
\Gamma
and the boundary tails with the same twists as in
\Gamma
.

Such graphs
\Gamma^{A,a,b}
correspond to the stable disks seen in Figure
14
. We note that
a
is allowed to take
the value
-1
instead of
r-1
as the corresponding half-node will be an
anchor (and similarly for
b
).

Let
\Gamma
be a rooted smooth graded
W
-spin graph
\Gamma
with internal markings
I
with
1\in I
. As in (
6.1
), let
E=E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d}+{\bf e}_{1})
and
E_{1}=E_{\Gamma}(\mathbf{d})
be the two descendent Witten bundles with respect to
\mathbf{d}+\mathbf{e}_{1}
and
\mathbf{d}
, where
\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{Z}^{I}_{\geq 0}
and
{\bf e}_{1}=(\delta_{i1})_{i\in I}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{I}
. Consider the two special canonical multisections
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{s}_{1}
for
E
and
E_{1}
given in (
6.2
) and (
6.3
)
The first observation is the following, which will be the only place where we use the fact that
\mathbf{s}_{1}
is special canonical rather than canonical.

Lemma 6.4
.

Suppose
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},k_{12},\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in I}}
is a smooth connected graded
W
-spin graph with at
least two tails, one of which is internal. Write
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{s}_{1}\oplus t_{w}
and
k_{\bullet}=k_{\bullet}(\Gamma)
. If
A\subseteq I
, write
z_{A,a,b}
for an internal marking labelled by the union of labels of the marked points in
A
with twist
(r-2-a,s-2-b)
. Take
v_{A,a,b}
to be the graph
\Gamma_{0,k_{1},k_{2},k_{12},\{(a_{i},b_{i})\}_{i\in B\cup\{z_{A,a,b}\}}}
where
A\sqcup B=I
.

(1)

If
w
is a boundary marking, then the integral
\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}e(E;\mathbf{t}|_{U_{+}\cap\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})
equals

(6.5)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup B=I\\
1\in A\\
a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-2\},\\
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-2\}\end{subarray}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\prod_{i\in A}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\textup{ext}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(r-2-a,s-2-b)}\prod_{i\in B}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}}\sigma_{12}^{k_{12}}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s}^{v_{A,a,b}},o}.

(2)

If
w
is an internal marking, say
z_{2},
then the integral
\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}e(E;\mathbf{t})
equals

(6.6)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup B=I\\
1\in A,\ 2\in B\\
a\in\{-1,\ldots,r-2\},\\
b\in\{-1,\ldots,s-2\}\end{subarray}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(a,b)}\prod_{i\in A}\tau_{d_{i}}^{(a_{i},b_{i})}\right\rangle^{\textup{ext}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{(r-2-a,s-2-b)}\prod_{i\in B}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}}\sigma_{12}^{k_{12}}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s}^{v_{A,a,b}},o}.

\bullet_{z_{1}}
A
I_{1}\setminus A
\bullet
\bullet^{w=z_{2}}

(a)

w
an internal node

\bullet_{z_{1}}
A
I_{1}\setminus A
\bullet
\bullet_{w}

(b)

w
a boundary node

Figure 14
.
Stable disks associated to
\Gamma^{A,a,b}
for the two choices of
w
and the boundaries contributing in Lemma
6.4

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is completely analogous to the proof of Lemma 4.14 in
[
6
]
(which in turn is inspired by the proof of Lemma 4.7 in
[
42
]
) and will be only be sketched.

Let
{{\mathcal{G}}_{oc}}\subset\partial\Gamma
be the collection of graphs
\Lambda
with the following properties:

•

\Lambda
has exactly one edge
e
which connects an open vertex
v^{o}_{\Lambda}
and a closed vertex
v^{c}_{\Lambda}
,

•

the internal tail
z_{1}
is adjacent to
v^{c}_{\Lambda}
, i.e.,
1\in I(v^{c}_{\Lambda})
, and

•

if
w
is an internal marking, say
w=z_{2}
, then
~2\in I(v^{o}_{\Lambda})
.

We refer the reader to the corresponding stable disks in Figure
14
.
By Lemma 4.13 of
[
6
]
, the zero locus of
t_{w}
is
\bigcup_{\Lambda\in{{\mathcal{G}}_{oc}}}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
. By adapting the proof with the number of automorphisms as computed in Observation
1.15
in mind, we can see that the zero locus has multiplicity
d
.

Moreover, by Corollary
2.55
, the induced relative orientation on
\mathcal{W}
restricted to the zero locus is the one induced from the canonical complex orientation for the closed part, and the canonical relative orientation for the open part. See Lemma 4.13 of
[
6
]
for more details. Note that, by Observation
1.15
and
Proposition
1.26
, the map

{{\textup{Detach}}}_{e}:{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{v^{o}_{\Lambda}}\times{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v^{c}_{\Lambda}}

has degree
rs/d
.

Following the argument of Lemma 4.14 in
[
6
]
, we can restrict to the zero locus of
t_{w}
with the correct multiplicity, and in turn, use the detaching map with the decomposition properties of the special canonical multisection

\mathbf{s}_{1}
(see Definition
3.27
) to see that

(6.7)

\displaystyle\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}e(E;\mathbf{t}|_{U_{+}\cap\partial^{0}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\Lambda\in\mathcal{G}_{oc}}\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}d\cdot e(E_{1};\mathbf{s}_{1}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\Lambda\in\mathcal{G}_{oc}}\left(rs\int_{{{\mathcal{PM}}}_{v_{\Lambda}^{c}}}e(E_{1};\mathbf{s}_{1}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{v^{c}_{\Lambda}}})\right)\cdot\int_{{{\mathcal{PM}}}_{v_{\Lambda}^{o}}}e(E_{1};\mathbf{s}_{1}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{v^{o}_{\Lambda}}}).

Lastly, using the definition of closed extended invariants in (
3.8
), we obtain (
6.5
) and (
6.6
).
∎

Note that in Lemma
6.4
(1), when
w
is a boundary point, the integral is independent of the choice of the specific boundary point, hence item (1) of the lemma will also hold if we replace
\tilde{t}_{w}
by a
\uplus
-sum of similar sections defined using different boundary marked points in the same connected component.

Notation 6.5
.

We denote by
t_{r}
the multisection of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
that is the
\uplus
-sum of multisections pointing to all boundary markings in the connected component of
z_{1}
that are
r
-points.
Define similarly
t_{s}
. Lastly, let
t_{\times}
be the multisection which points to the root of the connected component of
z_{1}
.

Notation 6.6
.

Consider a section
t_{w}
of any of the above types. Recall that
\mathbf{s}_{1}
is a multisection of
E_{1}
and we write
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{s}_{1}\oplus t_{w}
for a multisection of
E
. Note that
t_{w}
need not be canonical, hence
\mathbf{t}
need not be. As a consequence, we will need a homotopy from

t_{w}
to a canonical multisection.
Write

(6.8)

E=E_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{L}}_{1}

with
E_{1}
as in (
6.1
). Let
\mathbf{s}_{2}
be a canonical multisection of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}
. Then we can consider the canonical multisection
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}_{1}\boxplus\mathbf{s}_{2}
of
E
.

We apply Lemma
3.41
with respect to
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{s}_{1}\oplus t_{w}
to find a canonical family of multisections
\rho^{*}

of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}
, such that, for each balanced
\Gamma
, the homotopy given at time

u
by

(6.9)

H^{\Gamma}(t_{w})(u,-):=\mathbf{s}_{1}\boxplus\left((1-u)t_{w}+u(1-u)\rho^{\Gamma}+u\mathbf{s}_{2}\right)

is transverse. Here, as we did in Proposition
3.35
, we are viewing multisections
\mathbf{s}_{1},t_{w},\mathbf{s}_{2}
with base
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
as multisections with base
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
via pulling back the bundle under the projection.

Notation 6.7
.

Let
\Gamma
be a smooth graded
W
-spin graph with an internal tail
1\in I(\Gamma)
, and let
\Xi_{1},\ldots,\Xi_{f}
be the connected components of
\Gamma
. Suppose that
\Xi_{j}
contains the tail
1
. Consider graded graphs
\Lambda\in\partial\Gamma
where

•

\Lambda
has connected components
\Lambda_{1},\ldots,\Lambda_{f}
where
\Lambda_{j}\in\partial\Xi_{j}
and
\Lambda_{i}=\Xi_{i}
for
i\not=j
;

•

\Lambda_{j}
has two open vertices
v_{0}
and
v_{j}
, with one edge
e
between them;

(a)

Denote by
{\mathcal{G}}_{WC}
the collection of

wall-crossing graphs
, i.e., all
\Lambda
as above where the following are also satisfied:

•

the vertex
v_{0}
contains the internal tail
1
and is critical
with respect to the descendent vector
\mathbf{d}+{\bf e}_{1}
;

•

the vertex
v_{j}
is rooted and balanced with respect to the descendent vector
\mathbf{d}+{\bf e}_{1}
;

•

the half edges
h_{0}
and
h_{j}
of
e
must fall into one of the following two cases:

(i)

\text{tw}(h_{1})=(r-2,0),{\text{alt}}(h_{1})=(1,0),\text{tw}(h_{2})=(0,s-2),
and
{\text{alt}}(h_{2})=(0,1)
;

(ii)

\text{tw}(h_{1})=(0,s-2),{\text{alt}}(h_{1})=(0,1),\text{tw}(h_{2})=(r-2,0),
and
{\text{alt}}(h_{2})=(1,0)
;

(b)

Denote by
\mathcal{G}_{\textup{Exchange}}
the set of all
\Lambda
as above where the following are satisfied:

•

the vertex
v_{0}
is rooted and has the internal tail
1
;

•

the vertex
v_{j}
is an exchangeable vertex; and

•

the half-edges satisfy the following:

\text{tw}(h_{0})=(r-2,s-2),{\text{alt}}(h_{0})=(1,1),\text{tw}(h_{j})=(0,0),\text{ and }{\text{alt}}(h_{j})=(0,0).

(c)

Denote by
{\mathcal{G}}_{\textup{Cont}}
the set of all
\Lambda
as above where the following are satisfied:

•

the vertex
v_{0}
is rooted and has the internal tail
1
;

•

{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
is balanced with respect to the descendent vector
\mathbf{d}
;

•

the half-edges satisfy the following:

\text{tw}(h_{0})=(0,0),{\text{alt}}(h_{0})=(0,0),\text{tw}(h_{j})=(r-2,s-2),\text{ and }{\text{alt}}(h_{j})=(1,1).

Recall the definition of a degeneration of a pre-graded
W
-spin graph from Definition
1.49
(4). If we let
\Gamma:=\Gamma_{P}
for some
P\in\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d}+{{\mathbf{e}}}_{1})
(see Notation
5.5
),
then we denote by

(6.10)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathrm{WC}}(P)

\displaystyle:=\{\Lambda\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathrm{WC}}\ |\ \Lambda\text{ is a degeneration of }\Gamma_{P}\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{\textup{Exchange}}(P)

\displaystyle:=\{\Lambda\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\textup{Exchange}}\ |\ \Lambda\text{ is a degeneration of }\Gamma_{P}\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{\textup{Cont}}(P)

\displaystyle:=\{\Lambda\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\textup{Cont}}\ |\ \Lambda\text{ is a degeneration of }\Gamma_{P}\}.

Remark 6.8
.

The graphs in the collection
{\mathcal{G}}_{WC}(P)
are precisely the subcollection of all graphs of the form
\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}
described in Part (B) of the proof of Theorem
5.4
, with the additional constraint that
1\in I_{0}
.

Using this notation, we can provide a new formula for
\left\langle\prod_{i\in{I(\Gamma)}}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}+\delta_{1i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{12}^{k_{12}(\Gamma)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma},o}
:

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d}+{{\mathbf{e}}}_{1})
. Take
\Gamma:=\Gamma_{P}
as in (
5.3
).
Let
\mathbf{s}_{1}
be a canonical multisection of
E_{1}
and

\mathbf{s}_{2}
a canonical multisection of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}
. Take
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{s}_{1}+\mathbf{s}_{2}
and
\mathbf{t}_{w}=\mathbf{s}_{1}+t_{w}
and consider the homotopy
H:=H^{\Gamma}(t)
between them, as in Equation (
6.9
). Then

(6.11)

\left\langle\prod_{i\in{I(\Gamma)}}\tau^{(a_{i},b_{i})}_{d_{i}+\delta_{1i}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}(\Gamma)}\sigma_{12}^{k_{12}(\Gamma)}\right\rangle^{\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma},o}=\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}e(E,\mathbf{t}_{w})+Z_{WC}^{\Gamma}(\mathbf{t}_{w})+Z_{\textup{Cont}}^{\Gamma}(\mathbf{t}_{w})+Z_{\textup{Exchange}}^{\Gamma}(\mathbf{t}_{w}),

where

(6.12)

\displaystyle Z^{\Gamma}_{WC}(\mathbf{t}_{w})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\Lambda\in{\mathcal{G}}_{WC}(P)}\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}});

\displaystyle Z^{\Gamma}_{\textup{Cont}}(\mathbf{t}_{w})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\Lambda\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\textup{Cont}}(P)}\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}});

\displaystyle Z^{\Gamma}_{\textup{Exchange}}(\mathbf{t}_{w})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\Lambda\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\textup{Exchange}}(P)}\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}).

Proof.

By Lemma
3.41
,
the homotopy
H
of
(
6.9
) from
\mathbf{t}_{w}
to
\mathbf{s}
is transverse to zero on each stratum
[0,1]\times{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
and the projection of
H
to
E_{1}
equals
\mathbf{s}_{1}
at all times. Moreover,
H
is non-vanishing on
[0,1]\times U_{+}
, where
U_{+}
is as in (
3.4
), as
\mathbf{s}_{1}
is strongly positive
(see Definition
3.18
).
Lemma
2.22
then gives

(6.13)

\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}e(E;\mathbf{s})-\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}}e(E;\mathbf{t}_{w})=\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times(\partial{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma})}).

We will now show that
H
only vanishes on those boundary strata indexed by graphs that are in the collections
{\mathcal{G}}_{WC}
,
{\mathcal{G}}_{\textup{Cont}}
, and
{\mathcal{G}}_{\textup{Exchange}}
, yielding (
6.11
). For
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma
with
\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}\leq\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Gamma}-2
,

H
will not vanish as it is a transverse homotopy. If instead
\Lambda\in\partial^{0}\Gamma
is irrelevant, then
\mathbf{s}_{1}
is non-vanishing
on
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
as
\mathbf{s}_{1}
is strongly positive,
and hence
H
is non-vanishing on
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}
.
We thus may assume that
\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}=\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Gamma}-1
and
\Lambda
is relevant.

We can then describe
\Lambda
. Write
\Xi_{1},\ldots,\Xi_{f}
for the connected components of
\Lambda
.
Using the assumption that
\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}=\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Gamma}-1
, we see

\Lambda
must have a single boundary edge
e
which connects open vertices

v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. Thus we may assume that
\Xi_{1}
contains the edge
e

and vertices
v_{1},v_{2}
, and the remaining connected components
\Xi_{2},\ldots,\Xi_{f}
are rooted smooth open graded graphs.
Then one can decompose

(6.14)

E_{1}=(E_{1})_{v_{1}}\boxplus{(E_{1})_{v_{2}}}\boxplus\Big((E_{1})_{2}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus(E_{1})_{f}\Big),\quad\quad E=E_{v_{1}}\boxplus{E_{v_{2}}}\boxplus\Big(E_{2}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus E_{f}\Big),

where
(E_{1})_{v_{i}}=E_{v_{i}}(\mathbf{d}),~E_{v_{i}}=E_{v_{i}}(\mathbf{d}+{\bf e}_{1})
and
(E_{1})_{i}=E_{\Xi_{i}}(\mathbf{d}),~E_{i}=E_{\Xi_{i}}(\mathbf{d}+{\bf e}_{1})
.

We next observe that for
H
to vanish on this stratum, the internal tail

1
must be contained in
\Xi_{1}
. Indeed, if not, the component of

H
in
E_{v_{1}}\boxplus E_{v_{2}}
is constant, coinciding with the

E_{v_{1}}\boxplus E_{v_{2}}
component of
\mathbf{s}
, and hence by transversality
has no zeros. Thus for
i\neq 1
, the bundle
E_{i}
is the same as
(E_{1})_{i}
.

As in Part (A) of the proof of Theorem
5.4
, there are four cases which we must discuss, depending on the different potential twists for the half-edges in the first connected component. Without loss of generality take
v_{1}
to be the rooted vertex of

\Xi_{1}
with corresponding boundary half-edge
h_{1}
and
v_{2}
to be the additional vertex with half-edge
h_{2}
. We can have:

(i)

\text{tw}(h_{1})=(r-2,0),{\text{alt}}(h_{1})=(1,0),\text{tw}(h_{2})=(0,s-2),
and
{\text{alt}}(h_{2})=(0,1)
;

(ii)

\text{tw}(h_{1})=(0,s-2),{\text{alt}}(h_{1})=(0,1),\text{tw}(h_{2})=(r-2,0),
and
{\text{alt}}(h_{2})=(1,0)
;

(iii)

\text{tw}(h_{1})=(r-2,s-2),{\text{alt}}(h_{1})=(1,1),\text{tw}(h_{2})=(0,0),
and
{\text{alt}}(h_{2})=(0,0)
;

(iv)

\text{tw}(h_{1})=(0,0),{\text{alt}}(h_{1})=(0,0),\text{tw}(h_{2})=(r-2,s-2),
and
{\text{alt}}(h_{2})=(1,1)
.

Recall from Equation (
2.23
) that
the rank of the Witten bundle associated to a given vertex
v

satisfies

(6.15)

\operatorname{rank}E_{v}\equiv k_{1}(v)+k_{2}(v)\pmod{2}.

On the other hand,

(6.16)

\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v}\equiv 1+\#\hbox{(boundary half-edges incident to $v$)}\pmod{2}.

Note that since
H
agrees
with
\mathbf{s}
on
E_{i}
,
2\leq i\leq f
and
\mathbf{s}
is transversal,
in order for
H
to vanish we need that

(6.17)

\operatorname{rank}E_{i}\leq\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Xi_{i}},\quad 2\leq i\leq f.

Since
\operatorname{rank}E=\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{\Lambda}^{W}+1
by the fact that
\Gamma
is
balanced with respect to
\mathbf{d}+\mathbf{e}_{1}
, we must also have

(6.18)

\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{1}}+\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{2}}+\sum_{i=2}^{f}\operatorname{rank}E_{i}=1+\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{v_{1}}^{W}+\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{v_{2}}^{W}+\sum_{i=2}^{f}\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{\Xi_{i}}^{W}.

We now carry out a more careful analysis of ranks and dimensions
for the vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
in the four cases to determine when

H
may vanish.

(i) & (ii)

Cases (i) and (ii) are similar to one another.
By (
6.15
) and (
6.16
), we have

(6.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{1}}\equiv{}&k_{1}(v_{1})+k_{2}(v_{1})\equiv k_{1}(v_{1})+k_{2}(v_{1})+k_{12}(v_{1})+1\equiv\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{1}}\pmod{2};\\
\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{2}}\equiv{}&k_{1}(v_{2})+k_{2}(v_{2})\equiv k_{1}(v_{2})+k_{2}(v_{2})+1-1\equiv\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{2}}-1\pmod{2}.\end{split}

If
1\in I(v_{1})
, then the homotopy
H
will also be constant in time for the

v_{2}
component.
Thus, in order for
H
to vanish, we need by transversality that

\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{2}}\leq\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{2}}
.
By (
6.19
), we know then that
\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{2}}<\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{2}}
, but the parities of
\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{1}}
and
\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{1}}
agree.
Thus by (
6.18
),
we then must have
\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{1}}>\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{1}}
, and hence
by parity,

\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{1}}\geq\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{1}}+2
. By transversality, the
v_{1}
-component of the homotopy
H|_{[0,1]\times{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}}
cannot vanish, hence
H|_{[0,1]\times{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}}
does not vanish.

When
1\in I(v_{2})
and
H
vanishes, we claim that the graph
\Lambda
is a critical boundary graph with respect to the descendent vector
\mathbf{d}+{\bf e}_{1}
.
Indeed, note that now
H
is constant
on the
v_{1}
component, so necessarily
\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{1}}\leq\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{v_{1}}^{W}
. Thus by (
6.18
),
\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{2}}\geq\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{v_{2}}^{W}+1
. If the inequality is strict, then
H
will
non-vanishing on
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Lambda}^{W}
by transversality. Thus in this
case we have
\operatorname{rank}E_{i}=\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{\Xi_{i}}^{W}
for
2\leq i\leq f
,

\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{1}}=\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{v_{1}}^{W}
and
\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{2}}=\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{v_{2}}^{W}+1
,
i.e.,
\Lambda
is a critical boundary.
We denote the collection of graphs which parameterize such boundaries by
{\mathcal{G}}_{WC}
, as in Notation
6.7
.

Case (i) here corresponds to the case where
\epsilon=1
and case (ii) corresponds to the case where
\epsilon=2
in Remark
6.8
.

(iii)

In case (iii), when we pass to the base, we will forget the half-edge
h_{2}
. As in (
3.1
) and (
3.2
),
we must have
\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}=\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Gamma}-2+\sigma
, where

\sigma=1
if
v_{2}
becomes partially stable after forgetting
h_{2}

and
\sigma=0
otherwise.

Suppose
\sigma=1
. Then, as discussed in Case (iii) of Part (A) of the proof of Theorem
5.4
, since
\Lambda
is assumed to be relevant, the vertex

v_{2}
must be exchangeable. In this case,
\Lambda
is one of the
the exchangeable graphs in the set
{\mathcal{G}}_{\textup{Exchange}}
, as defined in Notation
6.7
.

Now suppose
\sigma=0
. We have two subcases.

(a)

1\in I(v_{1})
.
Recall the map
F_{\Lambda}:{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}^{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}^{W}
.
Here by definition,
F_{\Lambda}=\text{For}_{\text{non-alt}}\circ{{\textup{Detach}}}_{E^{0}(\Lambda)}
. We write

\Xi=\textup{detach}_{E^{0}(\Lambda)}\Lambda
and
F={{\textup{Detach}}}_{E^{0}(\Lambda)}
. There is a canonical identification of the bundles
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}
on the moduli spaces
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}
under the map
F_{\Lambda}
by Observation
2.15
(i).
Note that

F
is a finite covering map. Indeed, given two
W
-spin disks
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}

representing elements of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{v_{1}}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{v_{2}}
respectively,
we may obtain an element of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
in two different ways,
by attaching the unique boundary point of
\Sigma_{2}
with twist
(0,0)

to one of the two boundary points of
\Sigma_{1}
with twist
(r-2,s-2)
.
Recall
t_{\times}
is the section of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}
pointing towards the root.
Then
t_{\times}
is not the pull-back of a section of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}
on

{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}
as on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}
we cannot in general
distinguish between the root
and the boundary point corresponding to
h_{1}
. Similarly, if
t_{h_{1}}

denotes the section on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
pointing from
z_{1}
to the
half-node corresponding to
h_{1}
,
t_{h_{1}}
is not the pull-back of a
section from
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}
. Lastly,
t_{r},t_{s}
or
t_{w}
for

w\in I(v_{2})

may contain
t_{h_{1}}
as a weighted summand, see Remark

6.2
. Thus it is possible that none of these
sections are pulled back from
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Xi}
. On the other hand, recalling
the definition of pushforward from Notation
2.21
,
it is easy to see that
F_{*}t_{\times}=F_{*}t_{h_{1}}=t_{(r,s)}
,
the
\uplus
-sum of multisections pointing to all boundary markings
which are fully twisted. Further,
t_{(r,s)}
is then pulled back from

{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}
. Hence in any event,
F_{*}t_{w}
is pulled back from

{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}
. Now, the remaining multisections used to construct
the homotopy
H
in Lemma
3.41
are canonical, so we have that

F_{*}H
is also pulled back from
[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda}
.
Since
\dim([0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{W}_{{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda})=\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{W}_{\Gamma}-1
, we have by transversality that
F_{*}H
does not vanish, and hence

H
itself does not vanish.
Thus we do not get any contributions to the number of zeroes of the homotopy from this case.

(b)

1\in I(v_{2})
.
Let
k_{\varnothing}(v_{i})
denote the number of boundary half-edges where
(\text{tw}_{i},{\text{alt}}_{i})=(0,0)
for all
i
. We use (
6.15
) and (
6.16
) to see that

(6.20)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{1}}\equiv{}k_{1}(v_{1})+k_{2}(v_{1})\equiv{}&k_{1}(v_{1})+k_{2}(v_{1})+k_{12}(v_{1})+1+1\\
\equiv{}&\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{1}}+1\pmod{2};\\
\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{2}}\equiv{}k_{1}(v_{2})+k_{2}(v_{2})\equiv{}&k_{1}(v_{2})+k_{2}(v_{2})+k_{\varnothing}(v_{2})+1\\
\equiv{}&\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{2}}\pmod{2}.\end{split}

Combining the facts that
H
is constant on the
v_{1}
component with the parities computed in (
6.20
), we must have
\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{1}}<\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{v_{1}}^{W}
if
H|_{[0,1]\times{\mathcal{M}}_{\Lambda}}
is to vanish. Thus, by applying (
6.17
) and (
6.18
), we have that
\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{2}}>\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{v_{2}}^{W}
. In turn, (
6.20
) implies that

\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{2}}\geq\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{v_{2}}^{W}+2.

Thus, by transversality, the
v_{2}
-component of the homotopy
H|_{[0,1]\times{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}}
cannot vanish, hence
H|_{[0,1]\times{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}}
does not vanish.

(iv)

We again can compute using (
6.15
) and (
6.16
) that in case (iv)

(6.21)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{1}}\equiv{}&\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{1}}+1\pmod{2};\\
\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{2}}\equiv{}&\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{2}}\pmod{2}.\end{split}

Note that the parities in case (iv) are the same as in (
6.20
). Thus, if
1\in I(v_{2})
, we can apply (
6.21
), (
6.17
) and (
6.18
) as before to imply that

\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{2}}\geq\dim{\mathcal{M}}_{v_{2}}^{W}+2.

This again implies by transversality that the
v_{2}
-component of the homotopy
H|_{[0,1]\times{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}}
cannot vanish, hence
H|_{[0,1]\times{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Lambda}}
does not vanish.

If
1\in I(v_{1})
, then it is possible that
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
is balanced with respect to
\mathbf{d}
. Indeed, note that the base
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda
forgets the half-edge
h_{1}
. Then the rank of
(E_{1})_{v_{1}}=E_{v_{1}}(\mathbf{d})
has the same parity as
{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{{\mathcal{B}}v_{1}}
, hence one can have that
\operatorname{rank}(E_{1})_{v_{1}}=\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{{\mathcal{B}}v_{1}}
, and so

\quad\qquad\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{1}}=\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{{\mathcal{B}}v_{1}}+2,\quad\operatorname{rank}E_{v_{2}}=\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{v_{2}},\text{ and }\operatorname{rank}E_{i}=\dim{\mathcal{M}}^{W}_{\Xi_{i}},\quad 2\leq i\leq f.

These graphs make up the collection

{\mathcal{G}}_{\textup{Cont}}
.

Using the analysis of contributing boundaries and (
6.13
), we
conclude the lemma.
∎

The bulk of the analysis involves treating each of the summands
on the right-hand side of (
6.11
)
separately. We begin with the easy case:

Lemma 6.10
.

Z^{\Gamma}_{\textup{Exchange}}(\mathbf{t}_{w})=0
.

Proof.

The argument is similar to the corresponding argument in Lemma
5.3
. Recall that the homotopy
H^{\Gamma}(t_{w})

of (
6.9
)
depends only on the multisections
\mathbf{s}_{1},\rho,\mathbf{s}_{2}
, and
t_{w}
. For any
\Lambda\in\mathcal{G}_{\textup{Exchange}}(P)
, we have the corresponding graph
\Lambda^{\prime}:=\textup{exchange}(\Lambda)\in\mathcal{G}_{\textup{Exchange}}(P)
. By Observation
3.9
, we know that the canonical multisections
\mathbf{s}_{1},\rho
, and
\mathbf{s}_{2}
are the same on
\Lambda
and
\Lambda^{\prime}
. Moreover, the multisection
t_{w}
is independent of the cyclic ordering on the exchangeable graph, so we have that
\textup{Exchange}^{*}t_{w}=t_{w}
. Thus we have that

|\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}})|=|\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}})|.

Recalling that, by Proposition
2.56
, the map
\textup{Exchange}:{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
is orientation reversing, we conclude that

\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}})=-\#Z(H|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}}).

Summing over all
\Lambda\in\mathcal{G}_{\textup{Exchange}}(P)
implies the result.∎

We now restrict our attention to computing
Z^{\Gamma}_{WC}(\mathbf{t}_{w})
. This requires understanding the boundary graphs
\Lambda\in\mathcal{G}_{WC}(P)
for a fixed
P\in\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d}+{{\mathbf{e}}}_{1})
. As said in Remark
6.8
, the graphs in the collection
{\mathcal{G}}_{WC}(P)
are precisely the subcollection of all graphs of the form
\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}
in Notation
5.6
, with the additional constraint that
1\in I_{0}
. Recall that such a graph
\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}\in\mathcal{G}_{WC}(P)
will have the property that

{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}\prec\Lambda_{Q}

for some

(6.22)

Q=\{(I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0)),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}\in\mathcal{Q}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d}+{{\mathbf{e}}}_{1}),

where
1\in I_{0}
and
\widehat{Q}_{j}=P
where
\widehat{Q}_{j}
is as in (
5.12
). We can then classify all graphs in
{\mathcal{G}}_{WC}(P)
as

(6.23)

{\mathcal{G}}_{WC}(P)=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{Q}(I,\mathbf{d}+{{\mathbf{e}}}_{1})\\
\text{where $1\in I_{0}$ and}\\
P=\widehat{Q}_{j}\text{ for some $j$}\end{subarray}}\left(\bigcup_{R_{0}=0}^{k_{1}(j)-1}A^{1}_{Q,j,R_{0}}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{S_{0}=1}^{k_{2}(j)-1}A^{2}_{Q,j,S_{0}}\right).

with notation as in (
5.14
).

Observation 6.11
.

Suppose
\Lambda=\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}\in\mathcal{G}_{WC}
where
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda=\Lambda_{Q}
with
Q
as in (
6.22
). This implies that the
j
th connected component in the critical boundary graph consists of two open vertices
v_{0}
and
v_{j}
sharing a boundary edge so that

•

1\in I(v_{0})
;

•

v_{0}
is a critical vertex;

•

v_{j}
is a balanced vertex;

•

h_{0}
is the boundary half-edge in vertex
v_{0}
;

•

h_{j}
is the boundary half-edge in vertex
v_{j}
.

By definition, if
\epsilon=1
then
h_{0}
is an
s
-point and if
\epsilon=2
, then
h_{0}
is an
r
-point. Consider the multisections of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{v_{0}}
given by
t_{r}
,
t_{s}
,
t_{\times}
and
t_{z_{2}}
from Notation
6.5
. By using the canonical identification of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{v_{0}}
and
({\mathbb{L}}_{1}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Gamma})|_{{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{W}_{\Lambda}}}
and Definition
2.19
and Remark
6.2
, we compute that

(6.24)

\displaystyle t_{r}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}

\displaystyle=(k_{1}(0)+1)t^{v_{0}}_{r}\uplus(k_{1}(j)-1)t^{v_{0}}_{h_{0}};

\displaystyle t_{s}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}

\displaystyle=(k_{2}(0)+1)t^{v_{0}}_{s}\uplus(k_{2}(j)-1)t^{v_{0}}_{h_{0}};

\displaystyle t_{\times}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}

\displaystyle=t^{v_{0}}_{h_{0}};

\displaystyle t_{z_{2}}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}t^{v_{0}}_{z_{2}},&\text{if $2\in I(v_{0})$;}\\
t^{v_{0}}_{h_{0}},&\text{if $2\in I(v_{j})$.}\end{cases}

While almost all multisections that factor into the construction in a homotopy
H(t_{\bullet})
are canonical, the multisection
t_{h_{0}}
on the boundary is not, but we can prove the following technical lemma that deals with this issue.

Lemma 6.12
.

Suppose
P\in\mathcal{P}(I,\mathbf{d})
and
\Lambda:=\Gamma_{P,j,\epsilon,I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0),R_{0},S_{0}}\in\mathcal{G}_{WC}(P)
so that
{\mathcal{B}}\Lambda=\Lambda_{Q}
for some
Q\in\mathcal{Q}(I,\mathbf{d})
. Take the multisection
t_{h_{0}}^{\Lambda}
pointing at the half-node corresponding to the half-edge
h_{0}
on the graph
\Lambda
. Then

\#Z(H(t^{\Lambda}_{h_{0}})|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}})=\begin{cases}\#Z(H(t_{s}^{v_{0}})|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}})&\text{ if $\epsilon=1$},\\
\#Z(H(t_{r}^{v_{0}})|_{[0,1]\times{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}})&\text{ if $\epsilon=2$}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that the graph
\Lambda
is connected. If it were disconnected, then the homotopy as defined in (
6.9
) restricted to the components not containing the tail
1
is constant so the number of zeroes is fixed and we reduce to the connected case.

Now consider a fixed connected component
{\mathcal{M}}
of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
. Recall from §
2.5.1
, since we are in the unlabeled case, there can be multiple cyclic orderings of the boundary tails that correspond to the same connected component. We say two cyclic orderings are equivalent if they correspond to the same connected component in
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
. Note the data encoded in each equivalence class
[\hat{\pi}]
of cyclic orderings may be viewed as the
pattern of
r
- and
s
-points following the root in the anti-clockwise
direction on the boundary of the stable disk.

Any cyclic ordering
\hat{\pi}
in the equivalence class
[\hat{\pi}]
induces a cyclic ordering
\hat{\pi}_{0}
on all
half-edges attached to
v_{0}
in
\Lambda
.
This further induces an ordering
\pi_{0}
on the boundary half-edges of the vertex
v_{0}
by putting the half-edge
h_{0}
at the beginning of the ordering (as seen in Notation
2.44
and Figure
10
). Analogously as above, we say two orderings on
v_{0}
are equivalent if they differ by a permutation of the half-edges of
v_{0}
preserving
r
- points and
s
-points.

Let us assume that
\epsilon=1
and take the group
G:=\mathbb{Z}/(k_{2}(0)+1)\mathbb{Z}
. It acts on the set

{{\textup{Conn}}}({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}):=\{{\mathcal{M}}\ |\ {\mathcal{M}}\text{ a connected component of ${\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}$}\}

as follows. Given a connected component
{\mathcal{M}}
of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
, there is an equivalence class of orderings
[\pi_{0}]
as above.
Take
n\in\{0,\ldots,k_{2}(0)\}
representing an element of
\mathbb{Z}/(k_{2}(0)+1)\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
s(n)
be the positive integer such that
\pi_{0}(s(n)+1)
is the
n
th

s
-point in the ordering
\pi_{0}
, not counting the initial half-node.
In particular, we take
s(0)=0
, i.e., the half-node is in the
0^{th}
location in the ordering.
There exists a connected component
n\cdot{\mathcal{M}}
of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
where the analogous equivalence class of orderings
[\pi_{n}]
on the boundary half edges of
v_{0}
has the property that

\text{tw}(\pi_{0}(i+s(n)))=\text{tw}(\pi_{n}(i))

for any
i
(viewed modulo
k_{2}(0)+1
) and representatives
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{n}
of
[\pi_{0}]
and
[\pi_{n}]
. Geometrically, in the unlabeled case, this can be viewed as changing which
s
-point on the disk corresponding to
v_{0}
is the half-node by ‘rotating to the
n
th
s
-point found anti-clockwise.’ Note that there is a canonical isomorphism

\rho_{n}:{\mathcal{M}}\to{n\cdot{\mathcal{M}}}

given by changing the half-node on the disk corresponding to the vertex
v_{0}
.

As orbits of the group action partition the set of connected components, we treat each orbit of connected components of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
separately to prove the lemma. Fix an orbit
O({\mathcal{M}})
of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
. The orbit has cardinality
\#O({\mathcal{M}})
where
\#O({\mathcal{M}})|(k_{2}(0)+1)
. If
\#O({\mathcal{M}})\neq k_{2}(0)+1
, then this means that there is a rotational symmetry in the cyclic ordering
\hat{\pi}_{0}
that preserves the pattern of
r
- and
s
-points on the vertex
v_{0}
. Take the minimal representative
\tau\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}(0)+1\}
of a generator of the stabilizer subgroup
G_{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}/(k_{2}(0)+1)\mathbb{Z}
for
{\mathcal{M}}
. This means

\text{tw}(\pi_{0}(i+s(n)))=\text{tw}(\pi_{n}(i))\text{ for all $i$}\Longleftrightarrow n=k\tau.

Take the map to the base given in Definition
3.5

F_{\Lambda}:{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda_{Q}}.

As in Observation
2.15
(i), there is a canonical identification for the cotangent bundle
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}
on the moduli spaces above.
Recall that the map
F_{\Lambda}
is finite with degree
(-1)^{k_{2}(0)}(k_{2}(0)+1)
.

Consider the multisection
t_{h_{0}}^{v_{0}}
on
{\mathcal{M}}
. Once we detach this edge, the half-edge
h_{0}
will become indistinguishable from the tails that are
s(k\tau)\,(\text{mod }k_{1}(0)+k_{2}(0)+2)
places away from
h_{0}
for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Define the following subset of boundary marked points

T({\mathcal{M}})=\{x_{s(k\tau)}\ |\ 0\leq k<\tfrac{k_{2}(0)+1}{\tau}\}.

Thus we have that

(F_{\Lambda})_{*}(t_{h_{0}}^{v_{0}})=\biguplus_{x\in T({\mathcal{M}})}t_{x},

with the pushforward as defined in Notation
2.21
.
Using this fact and that
\rho,\mathbf{s}_{1}
, and
\mathbf{s}_{2}
are pulled back from the base, we can see that the pushforward of
H(t_{h_{0}}^{v_{0}})
on this connected component of the boundary stratum
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda}
is

(6.25)

(F_{\Lambda})_{*}(H(t_{h_{0}}^{v_{0}})|_{{\mathcal{M}}})=H(\uplus_{x\in T({\mathcal{M}})}t_{x})|_{F_{\Lambda}({\mathcal{M}})}.

Note that we also have that

F_{\Lambda}^{*}\big(H(\uplus_{x\in T({\mathcal{M}})}t_{x})|_{F_{\Lambda}({\mathcal{M}})}\big)=H(\uplus_{x\in T({\mathcal{M}})}t_{x})|_{{\mathcal{M}}}.

This means that the number of zeros of the homotopy
H(\uplus_{x\in T({\mathcal{M}})}t_{x})|_{{\mathcal{M}}}
will not depend on which half-edge of
v_{0}
corresponds to the half-node, i.e., for all
n\in G
we have

(6.26)

H(\uplus_{x\in T({\mathcal{M}})}t_{x})|_{{\mathcal{M}}}=\rho_{n}^{*}(H(\uplus_{x\in T({\mathcal{M}})}t_{x})|_{(n\cdot{\mathcal{M}})}).

By applying the pullback
F_{\Lambda}^{*}
to Equation (
6.25
) and taking zeros, we have that

(6.27)

Z({\mathcal{M}}):=\#Z(H(t_{h_{0}}^{v_{0}})|_{{\mathcal{M}}})=\#Z(H(\uplus_{x\in T({\mathcal{M}})}t_{x})|_{{\mathcal{M}}}).

Note that we have a natural bijection between the
s
-points on
v_{0}
and the set
\bigcup_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\in O({\mathcal{M}})}T({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})
. Using this bijection and the fact that
|T({\mathcal{M}})|=|T({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})|
for all
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\in O({\mathcal{M}})
, we have that

(6.28)

\biguplus_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\in O({\mathcal{M}})}\biguplus_{x\in T({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})}t_{x}=t_{s}.

We then see from (
6.26
), (
6.27
), and (
6.28
) that

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\in O({\mathcal{M}})}Z({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\in O({\mathcal{M}})}\#Z(H(\uplus_{x\in T({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})}t_{x})|_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}\in O({\mathcal{M}})}\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime\prime}\in O({\mathcal{M}})}\frac{1}{|O({\mathcal{M}})|}\#Z(H(\uplus_{x\in T({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})}t_{x})|_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime\prime}\in O({\mathcal{M}})}\#Z(H(t_{s})|_{{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime\prime}}).

We can perform the same computation for all orbits of the action on the set
{{\textup{Conn}}}({\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Lambda})
to prove the
\epsilon=1
case. The case where
\epsilon=2
is the same.
∎

Using Lemma
6.12
, we now give a closed form expression for the contribution of
\mathcal{G}_{WC}
for
H(t_{\bullet})
.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
P\in\mathcal{P}_{h}(I,\mathbf{d})
. Take
\Gamma:=\Gamma_{P}
as in (
5.3
). Let
t_{\bullet}
be one of the multisections of
{\mathbb{L}}_{1}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
defined in Notation
6.5
or
t_{z_{2}}
for an internal marking
z_{2}
. Take the corresponding homotopy
H^{\Gamma}(t_{\bullet})
as defined in (
6.9
). Then

(6.29)

\displaystyle Z^{\Gamma}_{WC}(\mathbf{t}_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in\mathcal{Q}(I,\mathbf{d}+{{\mathbf{e}}}_{1})\\
Q=\{(I_{0},k_{1}(0),k_{2}(0)),\ldots,(I_{h},k_{1}(h),k_{2}(h))\}\\
\text{where $1\in I_{0}$ and}\\
P=\widehat{Q}_{j}\text{ for some $j$}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{k_{2}(0)}\prod_{i=1}^{h}\left\langle\prod_{\ell\in I_{i}}\tau^{(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})}_{d_{\ell}}\sigma_{1}^{k_{1}(i)}\sigma_{2}^{k_{2}(i)}\sigma_{12}\right\rangle^{(\mathbf{s}^{\Gamma_{0,k_{1}(i),k_{2}(i),1,I_{i}}}),o}

\displaystyle\cdot\Big((k_{2}(0)+1)k_{1}(j)\alpha^{\Gamma_{0,k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1,0,I_{0}}}_{\bullet,1}-(k_{1}(0)+1)k_{2}(j)\alpha^{\Gamma_{0,k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1,0,I_{0}}}_{\bullet,2}\Big),

where
\alpha^{\Gamma_{0,k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1,0,I_{0}}}_{\bullet,\epsilon}

is defined as follows depending on
\epsilon
and
\bullet\in\{r,s,{\times},z_{2}\}
. Taking
\Xi:=\Gamma_{0,k_{1}(0)+1,k_{2}(0)+1,0,I_{0}}
, we have:

(6.30)

\displaystyle\alpha^{\Xi}_{r,1}
